Reference edition: Saints Holy Bible 70-book edition, pinned Saints-Translation English records. Greek-based source numbering is retained.

Isaiah 1:1
The vision that Isaiah son of Amos saw, which he saw against Judea and against Jerusalem during the reign of Oziah, Joatham, Achaz and Hezekiah, who reigned over Judea.
  Hosea 1:1 — parallel prophetic dating under the same Judean kings: Isaiah and Hosea both date their prophetic revelation under Uzziah, Jotham, Ahaz and Hezekiah, using the respective supplied name forms. Hosea additionally names Israel's Jeroboam.

Isaiah 1:2
Listen, heaven, and give ear, earth, because the Lord has spoken: “I begot sons and raised them high, but they rejected Me.
  Deuteronomy 32:1-6 — close heavenly-earthly summons and unfaithful children: Both summon heaven and earth to hear before describing Israel as children who fail their divine Father. Isaiah speaks of sons raised high who reject Him; Deuteronomy calls the generation blemished and asks how it repays the Father who made it.

Isaiah 1:3
An ox knew the one who acquired it, and a donkey its lord’s manger; but Israel did not know Me, and the people did not understand Me.”
  Jeremiah 8:7 — close animals knowing what God's people do not: Isaiah contrasts an ox knowing its owner and a donkey its master's manger with Israel's failure to know God; Jeremiah contrasts birds observing their appointed times with the people's ignorance of the Lord's judgments. The animals and the knowledge specified differ.

Isaiah 1:4
Woe, sinful nation, people full of sins, evil offspring, lawless sons! You abandoned the Lord and provoked the Holy One of Israel to anger.
  Deuteronomy 32:15-20 — close abandonment of the divine Father and provocation: Isaiah indicts lawless sons for abandoning the Lord and provoking Him; Deuteronomy likewise describes Israel forsaking the God who begot and nourished it and provoking Him through unfaithfulness.

Isaiah 1:5
Why should you be struck again as you add lawlessness? Every head is in pain, and every heart in grief.
  Jeremiah 5:3 — parallel punishment failing to produce correction: Isaiah asks why further blows should fall as lawlessness increases; Jeremiah says the scourged people refuse instruction and harden themselves instead of returning. Neither presents the blows as having secured repentance.

Isaiah 1:6
From feet to head: for neither a wound nor a bruise nor an inflamed blow is there a poultice to apply, nor oil nor bandages.
  Jeremiah 6:13-14 — related untreated injury as a figure for the people: Isaiah describes wounds without poultice, oil or bandages; Jeremiah likewise depicts the people's fracture being treated with contempt by those announcing peace. The latter specifies misleading religious leaders rather than Isaiah's catalogue of untreated injuries.

Isaiah 1:7
Your land is deserted, your cities burned by fire. Your country—foreigners consume it before you; it has been deserted, overturned by foreign peoples.
  Leviticus 26:31-33 — covenant-judgment counterpart to devastated land and cities: Isaiah describes deserted land, burned cities and foreign consumption; Leviticus threatens desolated cities and land, enemies dwelling there and scattering among nations. Burning and consumption are Isaiah's particular details.

Isaiah 1:8
Daughter Zion will be left like a tent in a vineyard, like a fruit-watcher’s shelter in a cucumber field, like a besieged city.
  Isaiah 24:20 — internal repeated fruit-watcher shelter image: Isaiah 1 compares isolated Daughter Zion to a fruit-watcher's shelter; Isaiah 24 compares the shaken earth to the same kind of shelter. The fragile rural structure depicts distinct scales of devastation.

Isaiah 1:9
If the Lord Sabaoth had not left us seed, we would have become like Sodom and been made like Gomorrah.
  Romans 9:29 — explicit quotation of the preserved seed and Sodom comparison: Romans expressly introduces Isaiah's words and repeats that without the Lord Sabaoth leaving seed, the people would have become like Sodom and Gomorrah.

Isaiah 1:10
Hear the Lord’s word, rulers of Sodom! Attend to God’s law, people of Gomorrah!
  Ezekiel 16:46-50 — parallel prophetic comparison of Jerusalem with Sodom: Isaiah addresses Jerusalem's rulers and people under Sodom and Gomorrah's names; Ezekiel calls Sodom Jerusalem's sister and says Jerusalem surpassed her lawlessness. These are moral comparisons, not a claim that the cities are geographically identical.

Isaiah 1:11
“What is the multitude of your sacrifices to Me?” says the Lord. “I am full of burnt offerings of rams; fat of lambs and blood of bulls and male goats I do not desire,
  Psalms 49:8-13 — close divine rejection of reliance on sacrificial animals: Isaiah says the Lord does not desire the abundance of rams, bulls' blood and goats; the psalm likewise refuses calves and goats and asks whether God eats bulls' flesh or drinks goats' blood. Their respective arguments challenge dependence on the offerings themselves.

Isaiah 1:12
not even if you come to appear before Me. For who demanded these things from your hands? To trample My court,
  Micah 6:6-8 — parallel questioning of what worship the Lord seeks: Isaiah asks who demanded the worshipers' coming and trampling of His court; Micah asks whether offerings secure approach to God and answers with justice, mercy and readiness to walk with Him. Isaiah's surrounding summons to ethical reform supplies the comparable setting.

Isaiah 1:13
you will no longer continue. If you bring fine flour, it is futile. Incense is an abomination to Me. Your new moons and sabbaths and a great day I cannot endure; fasting and idleness,
  Amos 5:21-23 — close rejected festivals offerings and ritual sounds: Isaiah calls flour offerings futile and incense abominable and refuses the named observances; Amos likewise rejects festivals and offerings and refuses to hear their music. Their lists overlap in rejected ritual rather than every individual item.

Isaiah 1:14
and your new moons and your festivals My soul hates. You have become more than enough for Me; I will no longer let your sins go.
  Amos 5:21 — close divine hatred of the people's festivals: Both directly state the Lord's hatred or rejection of the people's festivals. Isaiah additionally names new moons and refuses to continue overlooking their sins.

Isaiah 1:15
Whenever you stretch your hands toward Me, I will turn My eyes away from you. Even if you multiply supplication, I will not listen to you, for your hands are full of blood.
  Isaiah 59:1-3 — internal bloodstained hands and withdrawn divine attention: Isaiah 1 joins unanswered prayer and God's turned-away eyes with hands full of blood; Isaiah 59 likewise joins God's turned-away face with offenses and hands stained by blood. The latter explains that the barrier is sin, not inability to hear or save.

Isaiah 1:16
Wash yourselves, become clean. Remove the evils from your souls, before My eyes. Stop your evil deeds.
  James 4:8 — close cleansing of hands hearts and conduct: Isaiah summons the people to wash, remove evils from their souls and stop evil deeds; James likewise commands sinners to cleanse their hands and purify their hearts as they draw near to God.

Isaiah 1:17
Learn to do good, seek judgment, rescue the wronged. Give judgment for the orphan and vindicate the widow.
  Jeremiah 22:3 — close justice for the wronged orphan and widow: Both command justice and rescue of the wronged and expressly protect orphans and widows. Jeremiah additionally names the newcomer and prohibits innocent bloodshed.

Isaiah 1:18
Come, let us reason together, says the Lord. Though your sins are like purple-red, I will whiten them like snow; though they are like scarlet, I will whiten them like wool.
  Psalms 50:9 — close cleansing from sin expressed through whiteness and snow: Isaiah promises that deeply colored sins will be whitened like snow and wool; the psalmist likewise expects divine washing to make him whiter than snow. Wool and the purple-red and scarlet colors belong to Isaiah.

Isaiah 1:19
If you are willing and listen to Me, you will eat the land’s good things.
  Leviticus 26:3-5 — covenant blessing of obedient enjoyment of the land: Isaiah promises the land's good things to willing hearers; Leviticus likewise links keeping God's commandments to the land's produce and eating bread to satisfaction.

Isaiah 1:20
But if you are unwilling and do not listen to Me, a sword will devour you, for the Lord’s mouth has spoken these things.”
  Leviticus 26:23-25 — covenant penalty of the sword after refusal to obey: Isaiah warns that the sword will devour those unwilling to listen; Leviticus likewise threatens a covenant-avenging sword when the people resist correction and persist in crooked conduct.

Isaiah 1:21
How the faithful city Zion has become a prostitute, full of judgment, in which righteousness lay at rest, but now murderers!
  Ezekiel 16:8-15 — parallel Jerusalem portrayed as an unfaithful woman: Isaiah contrasts formerly faithful and righteous Zion with a prostitute and murderers; Ezekiel likewise describes Jerusalem brought into God's covenant and then prostituting herself through trust in her beauty. The accounts develop different details of the city's betrayal.

Isaiah 1:22
Your silver is unapproved; your retailers mix the wine with water.
  Jeremiah 6:28-30 — close rejected silver as a figure for corruption: Isaiah calls the city's silver unapproved; Jeremiah calls the corrupt people rejected silver because wickedness was not melted away and the Lord rejected them. Isaiah's diluted wine is a separate accompanying image.

Isaiah 1:23
Your rulers disobey, partners of thieves, loving gifts and pursuing repayment, not judging for orphans and not attending to widows’ judgment.
  Deuteronomy 10:17-18 — contrast between corrupt rulers and God's impartial justice: Isaiah's rulers love gifts and neglect orphans and widows; Deuteronomy describes God as taking no bribe and executing judgment for the orphan and widow. The rulers contradict the particular judicial qualities attributed to God.

Isaiah 1:24
Therefore thus says the Master, the Lord Sabaoth: “Woe, mighty ones of Israel! For My anger will not cease among My opponents, and I will exact judgment from My enemies.
  Deuteronomy 32:41-43 — close divine repayment of enemies through judgment: Isaiah announces the Lord's judgment against His enemies; Deuteronomy likewise joins God's hand taking hold of judgment with repayment to enemies and those who hate Him.

Isaiah 1:25
I will bring My hand against you and burn you to purity. I will destroy the disobedient, remove all lawless people from you and humble all the arrogant.
  Malachi 3:2-3 — parallel divine purification by burning and refining: Isaiah promises to burn the city to purity and remove lawless people; Malachi depicts the Lord's arrival as a smelting fire that purifies Levi's sons like precious metal. City and priestly group are distinct objects of purification.

Isaiah 1:26
I will establish your judges as before and your counselors as at the beginning. After these things you will be called City of Righteousness, Faithful Metropolis Zion.
  Zechariah 8:3 — close renewed Jerusalem receiving a faithful name: Isaiah promises restored judges and the name City of Righteousness, Faithful Metropolis Zion; Zechariah likewise promises God's return to Jerusalem and its designation as the True City and His mountain as holy.

Isaiah 1:27
For her captivity will be saved through judgment and through mercy.
  Hosea 1:7 — parallel mercy and salvation for Judah: Isaiah says Zion's captivity will be saved through judgment and mercy; Hosea likewise promises mercy and salvation to Judah's sons through the Lord their God. Hosea does not repeat Isaiah's captivity-and-judgment formulation.

Isaiah 1:28
The lawless and sinners will be shattered together, and those abandoning the Lord will be brought to an end.
  Psalms 36:38 — close collective destruction of the lawless: Isaiah says lawless people and sinners will be shattered together; the psalm likewise says the lawless will be destroyed together and the ungodly remnant destroyed.

Isaiah 1:29
For they will be ashamed of their idols, which they themselves wanted, and they were disgraced by their gardens, which they desired.
  Isaiah 65:3 — internal gardens associated with provocative worship: Isaiah 1 joins shame over desired idols and gardens; Isaiah 65 explicitly describes sacrifices in gardens among practices that provoke God. The later passage clarifies a comparable illicit use of gardens without identifying a particular garden here.

Isaiah 1:30
For they will be like a terebinth that has shed its leaves, like a garden that has no water.
  Job 8:11-13 — parallel waterless growth picturing the ungodly's failure: Isaiah compares the judged people to a leafless tree and a waterless garden; Bildad likewise asks whether wetland plants grow without water before applying their failure to those forgetting the Lord. Plant species and the speaker's immediate setting differ.

Isaiah 1:31
Their strength will become a stalk of tow and their works sparks of fire. The lawless and sinners will be burned together, with no one to quench.”
  Matthew 3:10-12 — parallel combustible growth and unquenchable judgment: Isaiah makes strength a stalk of tow and works sparks of fire and says lawless people burn with no quencher; John the Baptist likewise uses rejected trees and chaff for judgment and names unquenchable fire. The images concern different forms of rejected growth and do not identify Isaiah's historical scene with the baptismal address.

Isaiah 2:1
The word that came from the Lord to Isaiah son of Amos concerning Judea and Jerusalem.
  2 Chronicles 32:32 — named counterpart to Isaiah's prophetic record: Isaiah's heading names the Lord's revelation to Isaiah son of Amos concerning Judea and Jerusalem; Chronicles likewise attributes prophecy to Isaiah son of Amoz concerning Hezekiah's deeds. The shared named prophet is specific, while Chronicles does not identify this particular oracle.

Isaiah 2:2
In the last days the Lord’s mountain and God’s house will be clearly visible on the summits of the mountains and be raised above the hills; all the nations will come to it,
  Micah 4:1 — close parallel of the exalted mountain attracting peoples: Both place the Lord's mountain on mountain summits and above the hills in the last days and describe peoples coming toward it. Isaiah explicitly includes God's house and all nations.

Isaiah 2:3
and many nations will go and say, “Come, let us go up to the Lord’s mountain and to the house of Jacob’s God. He will announce His way to us, and we will walk in it.” For law will go out from Zion, and the Lord’s word from Jerusalem.
  Micah 4:2 — close parallel of nations seeking Zion's instruction: Both give the nations' invitation to ascend the Lord's mountain and Jacob's God's house, learn His way and walk in it; both conclude that law goes from Zion and the Lord's word from Jerusalem.

Isaiah 2:4
He will judge between the nations and reprove a numerous people. They will beat their swords into plows and their spears into sickles. Nation will no longer take sword against nation, and they will certainly no longer learn to wage war.
  Micah 4:3 — close parallel of judgment and weapons remade for farming: Both join divine judgment among peoples with swords made into plows, spears into sickles and the cessation of nations learning war. Micah adds strong nations reaching a distant land.

Isaiah 2:5
And now, house of Jacob, come, let us walk by the Lord’s light!
  Ephesians 5:8-10 — related exhortation to walk in the Lord's light: Isaiah invites Jacob's house to walk by the Lord's light; Ephesians likewise describes people as light in the Lord and commands walking as children of light, explaining its fruit as goodness, righteousness and truth.

Isaiah 2:6
For He abandoned His people, Israel’s house, because their country was filled, as from the beginning, with omens like the foreigners’ country, and many foreign children were born to them.
  Deuteronomy 18:9-14 — law background for foreign omen practices: Isaiah indicts the country for omens like foreign peoples' practices; Deuteronomy forbids learning the nations' divination and omens. The separate reference to foreign children is not explained by this law.

Isaiah 2:7
For their country was filled with silver and gold, and their treasures had no number; the land was filled with horses, and their chariots had no number.
  Deuteronomy 17:16-17 — related warning about multiplying horses and precious wealth: Isaiah describes a country full of horses, silver and gold; Deuteronomy forbids the ruler to multiply horses and greatly multiply silver and gold. The law addresses the ruler specifically, whereas Isaiah describes the land.

Isaiah 2:8
The land was filled with abominations, the works of their hands, and they bowed before what their fingers made.
  Psalms 113:12-16 — close worship of handmade idols: Isaiah indicts people for bowing to what their hands and fingers made; the psalm likewise calls the nations' idols works of human hands and addresses their makers and those trusting them.

Isaiah 2:9
A human being bowed down, and a man was humbled; I will certainly not let them go.
  Psalms 96:7 — related humiliation of those bowing to idols: Within Isaiah's indictment of handmade worship, the human bows and is humbled without release; the psalm likewise calls those bowing to carved images to shame. The forms of humiliation are expressed differently.

Isaiah 2:10
And now enter the rocks and hide in the earth from the Lord’s terror and the glory of His strength, whenever He rises to shatter the earth.
  Amos 9:2-3 — parallel attempts to hide from divine judgment: Isaiah commands entering rocks and hiding in earth before the Lord's terror; Amos likewise depicts people burrowing below or hiding on Carmel as God searches them out. Amos explicitly denies successful escape, which Isaiah's individual sentence does not state.

Isaiah 2:11
For the Lord’s eyes are high, but the human being is low; humanity’s height will be humbled, and the Lord alone will be exalted on that day.
  Psalms 17:28 — related divine humbling of human height: Isaiah contrasts the Lord's high eyes with humanity brought low; the psalm likewise attributes humbling the arrogant's eyes to God. The high eyes in Isaiah belong to the Lord, not to the arrogant people described in the psalm.

Isaiah 2:12
For the Lord Sabaoth has a day against every insolent and arrogant person, against everyone high and elevated; they will be humbled,
  Proverbs 16:18 — parallel downfall of insolence: Isaiah announces the Lord's day against insolent and elevated people who will be humbled; Proverbs likewise places insolence before ruin and evil-mindedness before a fall. It does not name the prophetic day.

Isaiah 2:13
against every cedar of Lebanon among the high and elevated, and against every oak tree of Bashan,
  Zechariah 11:1-2 — close judgment imagery of Lebanon's cedars and Bashan's oaks: Both place Lebanon's cedars and Bashan's oaks under devastation. Isaiah lists them among the high things facing the Lord's day; Zechariah commands lament because the mighty forest has fallen.

Isaiah 2:14
against every mountain and every high hill,
  Micah 1:3-4 — close mountains and heights affected by divine arrival: Isaiah includes every mountain and high hill in the Lord's judgment; Micah likewise describes the Lord descending upon earth's heights and mountains shaking beneath Him.

Isaiah 2:15
against every high tower and every high wall,
  Ezekiel 26:3-4 — parallel divine judgment against walls and towers: Isaiah lists high towers and walls under the Lord's day; Ezekiel gives a specific city example in the Lord's announcement that Tyre's walls and towers will be thrown down. The cities and the scope differ.

Isaiah 2:16
against every ship of the sea and every beautiful display of ships.
  Psalms 47:8 — parallel destruction of impressive sea shipping: Isaiah places sea ships and their beautiful display under judgment; the psalm likewise addresses God shattering Tarshish's ships with a violent wind. Tarshish is named only by the target, not substituted into Isaiah's wording.

Isaiah 2:17
Every human being will be humbled, humanity’s height will fall, and the Lord alone will be exalted on that day.
  Isaiah 5:15-16 — internal repeated humbling of humans and exaltation of God: Isaiah 2 contrasts falling human height with the Lord alone exalted; Isaiah 5 likewise joins human humiliation with the Lord Sabaoth exalted through judgment and righteousness.

Isaiah 2:18
They will hide away all the handmade things,
  Genesis 35:4 — Narrative parallel: hiding objects of foreign worship: Isaiah says the handmade things will be hidden; the preceding description identifies objects made by fingers and worshiped, and the following unit locates their concealment in caves and earth. Genesis describes Jacob hiding the foreign gods surrendered by his household beneath the terebinth. Both passages remove worshiped objects from sight, although Genesis additionally says Jacob destroyed them and Isaiah's unit does not assert destruction.

Isaiah 2:19
carrying them into caves, into clefts in the rocks and into holes in the earth, from the Lord’s terror and the glory of His strength, whenever He rises to shatter the earth.
  Jeremiah 16:16-18 — related rocky concealment within divine judgment: Isaiah describes carrying handmade things into caves and rock clefts before the Lord's terror; Jeremiah describes God's hunters searching people out from rocky openings because their wrongdoings are not hidden from Him. Jeremiah's quarry is people, not the objects carried in Isaiah.

Isaiah 2:20
For on that day a person will cast out his silver and gold abominations, which they made to worship, to the worthless ones and the bats,
  Isaiah 30:22 — internal rejection of silver-and-gold idols: Both describe people casting away or removing silver-and-gold idols. Isaiah 2 names worthless ones and bats as the destination; Isaiah 30 instead describes smashing and pushing the idols away through defilement images.

Isaiah 2:21
to enter holes in the solid rock and clefts in the rocks from the Lord’s terror and the glory of His strength, whenever He rises to shatter the earth.
  Luke 23:28-30 — related desperate turning to rocky cover in threatened calamity: Isaiah describes entering rock holes and clefts in terror; Jesus warns Jerusalem's daughters of days when people ask mountains to fall and hills to cover them. Seeking a cleft and asking the heights to cover are distinct expressions of desperation.

Isaiah 3:1
Look now, the Master, the Lord Sabaoth, will remove from Judea and Jerusalem strong man and strong woman, the strength of bread and the strength of water,
  Ezekiel 4:16-17 — close removal of Jerusalem's bread and water support: Isaiah says the Lord will remove the strength of bread and water; Ezekiel likewise says God breaks Jerusalem's support of bread and reduces bread and water to scarcity.

Isaiah 3:2
giant and strong man and warrior and judge and prophet and conjecturer and elder,
  2 Kings 24:14-16 — historical counterpart to removal of civic and military strength: Isaiah includes warriors, judges, prophets and elders among the supports removed; Kings describes Jerusalem losing rulers and mighty fighting men through deportation. It supports the loss of leadership and military strength without supplying every office in Isaiah's list.

Isaiah 3:3
commander of fifty and admirable counselor and wise master builder and discerning listener.
  2 Kings 24:14-16 — historical counterpart to loss of officials and skilled workers: Isaiah includes commanders, counselors and a wise master builder in the removed supports; Kings likewise describes deportation of rulers, powerful men and craftsmen. The precise professional lists are not identical.

Isaiah 3:4
I will establish young men as their rulers, and mockers will lord it over them.
  Ecclesiastes 10:16 — close warning about a young ruler: Isaiah describes young men installed as rulers amid judgment; Ecclesiastes likewise pronounces woe on a city whose king is young. Isaiah's mockers and Ecclesiastes's morning feasting are their separate details.

Isaiah 3:5
The people will clash, person against person and each against his neighbor. The child will collide with the elder, the dishonored with the honored.
  Micah 7:6 — parallel generational and household disorder: Isaiah describes children colliding with elders and people against neighbors; Micah likewise depicts sons dishonoring fathers and hostility within households. Each depicts broken social relations through its own specified pairs.

Isaiah 3:6
For a man will seize his brother or a member of his father’s household, saying, “You have a cloak; become our leader, and let my food be under you!”
  Isaiah 4:1 — parallel image: Isaiah 3:6 has a man seize his brother, offering leadership because he has a cloak and speaking of food, and Isaiah 4:1 has seven women take hold of one man, promising their own bread and clothes. Both picture desperate people seizing a man over bread and clothing in the coming collapse.

Isaiah 3:8
For Jerusalem has been abandoned and Judea has collapsed; their tongues accompany lawlessness, disobeying what concerns the Lord, because now their glory has been humbled,
  Psalms 11:4-5 — related rebellious speech and divine opposition: Isaiah links Jerusalem's fall with tongues accompanying lawlessness and disobedience; the psalm likewise seeks the destruction of boastful tongues that claim their lips are their own and ask who is their lord.

Isaiah 3:9
and the shame of their face stood against them. They announced their sin like Sodom’s and made it manifest. Woe to their soul, for they have planned an evil plan against themselves,
  Genesis 19:4-9 — narrative background for brazen sin like Sodom's: Isaiah says the people publicly announce their sin like Sodom; Genesis depicts Sodom's crowd openly demanding access to the guests and threatening Lot. The narrative illustrates public lawlessness without asserting that every act in Isaiah is the same offense.

Isaiah 3:10
saying, “Let us bind the righteous person, because he is troublesome to us.” Therefore they will eat the produce of their works.
  Wisdom of Solomon 2:12 — close plot against a troublesome righteous person: Isaiah reports the proposal to bind the righteous because he is troublesome; Wisdom's ungodly speakers likewise propose lying in wait for the righteous because he is troublesome and opposes their works.

Isaiah 3:11
Woe to the lawless! Evils according to the works of his hands will happen to him.
  Romans 2:6-9 — parallel evil repaid according to deeds: Isaiah says evil will befall the lawless according to his hands' works; Romans likewise says God repays each according to works and describes wrath and affliction for those doing evil.

Isaiah 3:12
My people, your collectors glean you, and those demanding payment lord it over you. My people, those pronouncing you blessed lead you astray and disturb the path of your feet.
  Micah 3:5 — parallel misleading favorable speech linked with exploitation: Isaiah joins oppressive payment demands with misleading pronouncements of blessing; Micah describes prophets who mislead the people, proclaiming peace when fed and war when unfed. Collectors and prophets are distinct groups in the respective accusations.

Isaiah 3:13
But now the Lord will establish Himself for judgment and will set His people before judgment.
  Psalms 81:1-4 — parallel God standing as judge over corrupt judgment: Isaiah says the Lord establishes Himself for judgment of His people; the psalm likewise pictures God standing in the judging assembly and challenging unjust judgments against the vulnerable. It describes the assembly as gods rather than repeating Isaiah's wording for the people.

Isaiah 3:14
The Lord Himself will come to judgment with the people’s elders and its rulers: “Why have you burned My vineyard, while the poor person’s plunder is in your houses?
  Micah 2:1-3 — parallel divine indictment of plundered houses and inheritance: Isaiah accuses rulers whose houses contain the poor person's plunder; Micah likewise condemns people plundering a man, his house and inheritance, followed by the Lord's announced calamity. Isaiah additionally speaks of God's vineyard being burned.

Isaiah 3:15
Why do you wrong My people and shame the face of the poor?”
  Proverbs 22:22-23 — close prohibition of shaming and oppressing the poor: Isaiah asks why the people are wronged and the poor person's face shamed; Proverbs likewise forbids force against the needy and dishonoring the weak at the gates, because the Lord judges their case.

Isaiah 3:16
Thus says the Lord: “Because Zion’s daughters were exalted and walked with high neck and glancing eyes, dragging their tunics as their feet moved and playing with their feet,
  1 Peter 3:3-4 — related critique of outward display: Isaiah indicts Zion's daughters for exalted bearing and conspicuous display; First Peter contrasts outward adornment with the precious beauty of a gentle and quiet spirit. Its exhortation to wives is a different setting from Isaiah's judgment oracle.

Isaiah 3:17
God will humble Zion’s ruling daughters, and the Lord will uncover their appearance
  Ezekiel 16:37-39 — parallel humiliating exposure associated with Jerusalem: Isaiah announces the humbling of Zion's daughters and uncovering of their appearance; Ezekiel likewise depicts Jerusalem's shame revealed and her clothing stripped away. The latter personifies the city as one woman.

Isaiah 3:18
on that day, and the Lord will remove the glory of their clothing and their ornaments, hair braids, fringes and crescents,
  Ezekiel 16:39 — close removal of Jerusalem's clothing and adornment: Isaiah begins a list of clothing and ornaments whose glory the Lord removes; Ezekiel likewise describes Jerusalem being stripped of clothing and vessels of boasting. Isaiah's braids, fringes and crescents are more specific details.

Isaiah 3:19
the necklace and the adornment of their face,
  Ezekiel 16:11-12 — parallel Jerusalem adorned with necklace and facial ornaments: Isaiah's judgment list names a necklace and adornment of the face; Ezekiel's account of Jerusalem's former adornment explicitly names a necklace, a nostril ring and ear hoops. The target recounts gifts whose later removal is described in the separately read 16:39.

Isaiah 3:20
the arrangement of glorious adornment, armlets, bracelets, a hair braid, armbands, rings and earrings,
  Ezekiel 16:11-12 — parallel bracelets and ear ornaments in Jerusalem's adornment: Isaiah's removed ornaments include bracelets and earrings; Ezekiel likewise names bracelets around Jerusalem's hands and hoops upon her ears in its account of her earlier adornment. Isaiah's longer catalogue is not claimed to match item for item.

Isaiah 3:22
the coverings used about the house and the transparent Laconian garments,
  Esther 1:6 — shared item: Both name transparent fabrics among luxury goods. Isaiah 3:22 lists 'the coverings used about the house and the transparent Laconian garments', and Esther 1:6 describes 'transparent coverings adorned with varied flowers' among the furnishings of a lavish setting.

Isaiah 3:23
the fine-linen, blue-purple and scarlet garments, and fine linen interwoven with gold and blue-purple, and trailing summer wraps.
  Ezekiel 16:10-13 — related costly linen and precious adornment of Jerusalem: Isaiah lists fine linen, colored fabrics and gold-work among the removed attire; Ezekiel likewise portrays Jerusalem clothed in fine linen and patterned coverings and adorned with gold and silver. The individual colors and weaving details differ, and the target initially describes adornment bestowed rather than removed.

Isaiah 3:24
Instead of sweet fragrance there will be dust; instead of a belt you will gird yourself with a cord; instead of golden head adornment you will have baldness because of your deeds; instead of the tunic with purple at its center you will gird yourself with sackcloth.
  Amos 8:10 — close reversal to sackcloth and baldness: Isaiah replaces fine dress and golden head adornment with sackcloth and baldness; Amos likewise announces sackcloth on every waist and baldness on every head as festivity turns to mourning.

Isaiah 3:25
Your most beautiful son, whom you love, will fall by the sword, and your strong men will fall by the sword.
  Lamentations 2:21 — related Jerusalem's bereavement through the sword: Isaiah announces the beloved son's and strong men's fall by the sword; Jerusalem's lament likewise describes God's killing with sword and famine amid the collapse and captivity of its people. The particular fates and groups in the two sentences are not identical.

Isaiah 3:26
The cases of your adornment will be humbled and mourn; you will be left alone and dashed to the ground.”
  Lamentations 1:1 — close devastated Jerusalem left alone: Isaiah ends with the city left alone and dashed down; Lamentations likewise opens with once-populous Jerusalem sitting alone like a widow. Isaiah's mourning adornment-cases are not replaced with city gates.

Isaiah 4:1
Seven women will take hold of one man, saying, “We will eat our own bread and put on our own clothes; only let your name be called over us. Take away our disgrace!”
  Isaiah 3:25-26 — preceding context: Isaiah 3:25-26 says your strong men will fall by the sword and she will be left alone, and Isaiah 4:1 has seven women take hold of one man. The loss of the men in war is the situation in which the women seek a single man.
  Luke 1:25 — verbal echo: The women ask, Take away our disgrace, and in Luke 1:25 Elizabeth says the Lord looked upon me to take away my disgrace among people. Both use the plea or thanks for the removal of a woman's disgrace, though Luke's occasion is a pregnancy.

Isaiah 4:2
On that day God will shine out in counsel with glory upon the earth, to exalt and glorify what remains of Israel,
  Isaiah 60:1-3 — internal divine glory shining upon Jerusalem: Isaiah 4 says God shines in glory to exalt Israel's remnant; Isaiah 60 likewise announces Jerusalem's light and the Lord's glory rising and appearing upon her. The source is not rewritten as a statement about a branch.

Isaiah 4:3
and those remaining in Zion and left in Jerusalem will be called holy, all those recorded for life in Jerusalem,
  Daniel 12:1 — related register marking a people for divine honor: Isaiah calls Jerusalem's survivors holy and recorded for life; Daniel likewise says the people found inscribed in the book will be exalted on the day of unparalleled affliction. Daniel's supplied wording says exalted, rather than repeating Isaiah's description of holy survivors in Jerusalem.

Isaiah 4:4
because the Lord will wash away the filth of Zion’s sons and daughters and thoroughly cleanse the blood from their midst, by a spirit of judgment and a spirit of burning.
  Ezekiel 36:25-27 — parallel divine cleansing expressed with spirit language: Isaiah promises removal of Zion's filth and blood by a spirit of judgment and burning; Ezekiel likewise promises cleansing from uncleanness and idols and God's Spirit within the people. Sprinkled water and inward renewal are Ezekiel's particular formulation.
  Malachi 3:2-3 — related purification through divine fiery judgment: Isaiah's cleansing of Zion comes by a spirit of judgment and burning; Malachi describes the Lord's purifying arrival as smelting fire cleansing Levi's sons. This addresses the fiery-purification facet separately from Ezekiel's water and Spirit promise.

Isaiah 4:5
He will come, and it will be: a cloud will shade every place of Mount Zion and everything around her by day, and as of smoke and as of a light of fire burning by night; with all the glory it will be covered.
  Exodus 13:21-22 — close cloud by day and fire by night: Isaiah promises cloud-covering by day and fiery light by night over Zion; Exodus likewise describes God's persistent pillar of cloud by day and fire by night before Israel. The promised Zion covering and the wilderness guidance are distinct settings.

Isaiah 4:6
It will be shade from heat, shelter and concealment from harshness and rain.
  Psalms 120:5-6 — related divine shelter from scorching heat: Isaiah promises shade from heat as part of God's shelter; the psalm likewise names the Lord as shelter and says the daytime sun will not scorch. Isaiah additionally includes harshness and rain.

Isaiah 5:1
I will sing now to the beloved a song of the loved one for my vineyard. The beloved had a vineyard on a horn, in a fertile place.
  Psalms 79:9-12 — parallel cultivation of Israel as a vine: Isaiah introduces the beloved's vineyard, identified as Israel in 5:7; the psalm portrays Israel as God's vine transplanted from Egypt and carefully rooted until its branches spread across the land.

Isaiah 5:2
I put a fence around it, surrounded it with a palisade, planted a Sorech vine, built a tower in its midst and dug a wine vat in it. I waited for it to produce grapes, but it produced thorns.
  Matthew 21:33 — shared vineyard fence, tower, and winepress imagery: Isaiah's carefully planted vineyard has a fence, tower, and wine vat; Jesus' vineyard parable repeats that distinctive arrangement. Isaiah's immediate failure is the vine's thorns, whereas the parable develops the tenants' wrongdoing.

Isaiah 5:3
Now, man of Judah and residents of Jerusalem, judge My case and the matter of My vineyard.
  Micah 6:1-2 — parallel presentation of God's case against His people: Isaiah calls Judah and Jerusalem to judge the case concerning His vineyard; Micah explicitly announces the Lord's case against Israel before the hills and foundations of the earth. The judicial setting corresponds while the summoned audience differs.

Isaiah 5:4
What more shall I do for My vineyard that I did not do for it? For I waited for it to produce grapes, but it produced thorns.
  Micah 6:3-4 — parallel divine questions after benefits bestowed: The vineyard owner asks what more he could have done after expecting grapes; the Lord asks Israel how He has wronged them and recalls their redemption from Egypt. Both questions expose an unjustified response to prior divine care.
  Hebrews 6:7-8 — parallel failed crop despite provision: Isaiah contrasts expected grapes with thorns after careful cultivation; Hebrews contrasts useful vegetation from rain-fed ground with thorns and thistles facing rejection. The shared agricultural argument concerns the response to received provision.

Isaiah 5:5
Now I will announce to you what I will do to My vineyard: I will remove its fence, and it will be plundered; I will pull down its wall, and it will be trampled,
  Psalms 79:13-14 — parallel removal of a vine's protective boundary: Isaiah announces that removal of the vineyard's fence will expose it to plunder and trampling; the psalm asks why God tore down the vine's fence, allowing passersby and wild creatures to ravage it.

Isaiah 5:6
and I will abandon My vineyard. It will certainly not be pruned or dug, and thorns will rise in it as in fallow ground. I will command the clouds not to rain upon it.
  Deuteronomy 28:23-24 — parallel judgment through loss of nourishing rain: God commands the clouds not to rain upon the abandoned vineyard; Deuteronomy's covenant warning replaces the land's rain with dust and earth. The target supports the withheld-rain judgment, not every detail of pruning and thorns.

Isaiah 5:7
For the vineyard of the Lord Sabaoth is Israel’s house, and the man of Judah a beloved new planting. I waited for it to produce judgment, but it produced lawlessness, not righteousness but an outcry.
  Jeremiah 2:21 — parallel Israel as a good planting turned bad: Isaiah identifies the vineyard as Israel and contrasts expected justice with lawlessness; Jeremiah addresses Israel as a fruitful, genuine vine that has changed into bitterness. Both use failed vine cultivation to expose the people's corruption.

Isaiah 5:8
Woe to those joining house to house and bringing field beside field, so that they may take something from their neighbor! Surely you will not dwell alone upon the earth?
  Micah 2:1-2 — parallel seizure of neighbors' houses and fields: Isaiah condemns joining houses and fields so as to take from a neighbor; Micah describes people coveting fields and plundering a man, his house, and his inheritance. Both connect expanding property with dispossession.

Isaiah 5:9
For these things were heard in the ears of the Lord Sabaoth: “If many houses come to be, great and beautiful, they will become deserted, and no one will inhabit them.
  Amos 3:14-15 — related judgment upon costly houses: Isaiah's great and beautiful houses become deserted; Amos announces judgment in which colonnaded and summer houses are struck and ivory houses perish. Both portray impressive housing as unable to escape judgment, with different stated outcomes.

Isaiah 5:10
For where ten pairs of oxen work, it will yield one jar, and the one sowing six artabas will yield three measures.”
  Deuteronomy 28:38-39 — parallel disappointing grain and vineyard yields: Isaiah gives sharply reduced yields from cultivated land and sown grain; Deuteronomy warns that much seed will yield little and cultivated vines will supply no wine. The correspondence concerns failed harvests, without equating the different measurements or causes.

Isaiah 5:11
Woe to those rising in the morning and pursuing strong drink, lingering into the evening, for wine will burn them up!
  Proverbs 23:29-35 — parallel warning against prolonged pursuit of wine: Isaiah condemns drinkers who pursue strong drink from morning and linger until evening; Proverbs identifies those lingering over wines and seeking drinking parties, then describes their injury and renewed craving.

Isaiah 5:12
For with lyre and harp, drums and flutes they drink wine, but they do not look at the Lord’s works or consider the works of His hands.
  Amos 6:4-6 — parallel music and wine amid culpable indifference: Isaiah's musical wine feast disregards the Lord's works; Amos describes luxurious eating, instruments, and wine without suffering over Joseph's shattering. Both connect festive indulgence with neglected obligations, while the neglected object differs.

Isaiah 5:13
Therefore My people became captive because they did not know the Lord, and there was a multitude of dead through famine and thirst for water.
  Hosea 4:6 — parallel judgment associated with failure to know God: Isaiah explains captivity and deadly deprivation by the people's not knowing the Lord; Hosea connects the people's lack of knowledge and rejection of recognition with divine rejection. Hosea does not repeat Isaiah's particular famine and captivity details.

Isaiah 5:14
Hades enlarged its appetite and opened its mouth without ceasing, and her glorious ones, great ones, rich ones and pestilent ones will go down.
  Habakkuk 2:5 — shared image of Hades' enlarged appetite: Isaiah portrays Hades opening an unceasing mouth to receive the city's great and rich; Habakkuk compares a boastful man's widened, unsatisfied appetite to Hades and death. The image corresponds, while Isaiah describes Hades itself and Habakkuk a human resemblance.

Isaiah 5:15
A human being will be humbled, a man dishonored, and lofty eyes humbled.
  Psalms 17:28 — parallel humbling of lofty eyes: Isaiah announces the dishonoring of human beings and humbling of lofty eyes; the psalm addresses God as the One who humbles arrogant eyes while saving a humble people.

Isaiah 5:16
The Lord Sabaoth will be exalted through judgment, and the holy God will be glorified through righteousness.
  Ezekiel 28:22 — parallel divine glory displayed through judgment: Isaiah says the holy God will be exalted through judgment and glorified through righteousness; Ezekiel says the Lord will be glorified and sanctified in Sidon when He executes judgments there. The settings differ, but both explicitly join judgment and God's manifested honor.

Isaiah 5:17
Those who were plundered will be pastured like bulls, and lambs will feed upon the deserted places of those wiped out.
  Isaiah 7:25 — related pasture imagery after the land's upheaval: Isaiah 5 portrays plundered people pastured like bulls and lambs feeding in deserted places; Isaiah 7 describes land becoming sheep pasture and cattle trampling ground. Both use cattle and sheep in transformed land, without equating the figurative people of 5:17 with the animals of 7:25.

Isaiah 5:18
Woe to those drawing sins as with a long cord and lawless deeds as with the strap of a heifer’s yoke,
  Proverbs 5:22 — shared cords-of-sin imagery with differing agency: Isaiah condemns people drawing sins with a long cord; Proverbs depicts people bound tightly by the cords of their own sins. The same binding image exposes attachment to wrongdoing, but Isaiah emphasizes drawing sins and Proverbs being caught by them.

Isaiah 5:19
who say, “Let what He will do approach quickly, so that we may see, and let the counsel of the Holy One of Israel come, so that we may know!”
  Jeremiah 17:15 — parallel taunt demanding arrival of the Lord's word: Isaiah quotes those insisting that God's work and counsel come quickly so they may see; Jeremiah reports opponents asking where the Lord's word is and demanding that it come. Both challenge prophetic warning by demanding its realization.

Isaiah 5:20
Woe to those calling evil good and good evil, making darkness light and light darkness, making bitter sweet and sweet bitter!
  Proverbs 17:15 — particular judicial instance of reversing good and evil: Isaiah condemns calling evil good and good evil; Proverbs calls judging the unjust righteous and the righteous unjust detestable before God. The target supplies a concrete moral-verdict reversal, rather than repeating Isaiah's light and flavor images.

Isaiah 5:21
Woe to those who are discerning in their own judgment and knowledgeable in their own view!
  Proverbs 3:7 — parallel rejection of self-estimated wisdom: Isaiah pronounces woe on those discerning in their own judgment and knowledgeable in their own view; Proverbs commands a person not to be prudent in one's own estimation, but to fear God and turn from evil.

Isaiah 5:22
Woe to your strong ones who drink wine and the powerful who mix strong drink,
  Proverbs 31:4-5 — parallel warning about powerful drinkers: Isaiah condemns strong and powerful people drinking and mixing intoxicants; Proverbs warns the powerful against wine lest they forget wisdom and become unable to judge the weak rightly. Isaiah's following verse similarly exposes corrupted judgment.

Isaiah 5:23
who justify the ungodly for gifts and take away the righteous person’s right!
  Deuteronomy 16:19 — parallel condemnation of bribed judgments: Isaiah describes gifts used to justify the ungodly and remove the righteous person's right; Deuteronomy prohibits bribes because they blind the wise and overthrow the words of the righteous.

Isaiah 5:24
Therefore, as a stalk is burned by a fiery coal and consumed by a rising flame, their root will become like chaff and their blossom rise like dust. For they did not desire the law of the Lord Sabaoth, but provoked the utterance of the Holy One of Israel.
  Malachi 3:19 — parallel fire judgment expressed through root and plant destruction: Isaiah compares the condemned to a stalk burned by fire, with root like chaff and blossom like dust; Malachi depicts practitioners of lawlessness as straw burned until neither root nor branch remains. Both join fiery plant imagery to judgment on wrongdoing.

Isaiah 5:25
The Lord Sabaoth was enraged with wrath against His people, brought His hand against them and struck them. The mountains were roused, and their carcasses became like dung in the middle of the road. In all these things the anger did not turn away, but the hand is still high.
  Isaiah 9:11 — repeated refrain of unspent anger and the high hand: Both passages conclude that despite the announced blows the anger has not turned away and the hand is still high. This is a distinctive repeated judgment refrain, while the surrounding descriptions of the blows differ.

Isaiah 5:26
Therefore He will raise a signal among the distant nations and whistle to them from the earth’s end. Behold, they come quickly and lightly!
  Deuteronomy 28:49-50 — parallel summoning of a swift distant nation: Isaiah describes God signaling nations from the earth's end, who arrive quickly; Deuteronomy warns that the Lord will bring a nation from afar and the earth's end with eagle-like speed. The target also characterizes that invader's ruthlessness.

Isaiah 5:27
They will not hunger or grow weary, slumber or sleep, nor loosen the belts from their waists; their sandal straps will certainly not break.
  Isaiah 40:28 — verbal echo: Isaiah 5:27 says the invaders will not hunger or grow weary, and Isaiah 40:28 says God will not hunger or grow weary. The same phrase is applied to an invading force and to God.
  Isaiah 40:31 — verbal echo: Isaiah 5:27 describes a force that does not hunger or grow weary, and Isaiah 40:31 says those enduring for God will run without growing weary and walk without hunger. Both describe tireless strength in the same terms.

Isaiah 5:28
Their arrows are sharp and their bows drawn. Their horses’ feet were reckoned like solid rock, and their chariot wheels like a storm.
  Jeremiah 4:13 — parallel comparison of attacking chariots to a storm: Isaiah likens the invaders' chariot wheels to a storm; Jeremiah likens the approaching enemy's chariots to a storm and adds eagle-swift horses. The shared martial-weather image does not supply Isaiah's rock-like hoof detail.

Isaiah 5:29
They rush like lions and have taken their stand like a lion’s cub. It will seize and cry out as a beast does and cast out, and no one will rescue them.
  Hosea 5:14 — parallel lion-like seizure without rescue: Isaiah's attacking force rushes like lions and seizes without anyone rescuing; Hosea portrays the Lord Himself as a lion to Judah, seizing and taking with no rescuer. The image and helplessness correspond, while the immediate agents differ.

Isaiah 5:30
It will cry out because of them on that day like the sound of a surging sea. They will look to the earth, and behold, harsh darkness in their perplexity.
  Amos 5:18-20 — parallel darkness as the character of judgment: Isaiah's threatened people look toward the earth and find harsh darkness and perplexity; Amos warns that the Lord's day is darkness rather than light. Both use darkness to describe the experience of judgment.
  Isaiah 17:12-13 — parallel cry compared with a surging sea: Isaiah 5 compares the cry to a surging sea; Isaiah 17 explicitly compares many nations' roaring and disturbance to a surging sea and rushing water. This counterpart concerns sound within accounts of invasion.

Isaiah 6:1
In the year that King Oziah died, I saw the Lord seated on a throne, high and lifted up; and the house was full of His glory.
  1 Kings 22:19 — parallel prophetic vision of the enthroned Lord: Isaiah sees the Lord on a high throne with the house full of His glory; Micaiah sees the Lord on His throne surrounded by heaven's army. Both introduce a heavenly scene preceding a commission, without identifying their different attendants.
  John 12:39-41 — New Testament identification of Isaiah's vision of glory: After citing Isaiah's words about blinded eyes and a hardened heart, John says Isaiah saw His glory and spoke concerning Him. In John's discussion of unbelief toward Jesus, this explicitly connects Isaiah's glory vision with Jesus.

Isaiah 6:2
And seraphim stood around Him: each one had six wings, and each one had six wings; with two they covered their faces, and with two they covered their feet, and with two they flew.
  Ezekiel 1:6-11 — related throne-vision creatures using wings to cover themselves: Isaiah's seraphim have six wings, with pairs covering faces and feet and enabling flight; Ezekiel's living creatures have four wings, two of which cover their bodies. The comparison concerns winged attendants and covering, while preserving their different kinds, numbers, and arrangements.

Isaiah 6:3
And one cried to another, saying: Holy, holy, holy is the Lord Sabaoth; all the earth is full of His glory.
  Numbers 14:21 — parallel declaration of the Lord's glory throughout the earth: The seraphim proclaim that all the earth is full of the Lord's glory; in Numbers the Lord declares that His glory shall fill all the earth. The universal glory claim corresponds, while Isaiah adds the threefold acclamation of holiness.

Isaiah 6:4
And the lintel was lifted at the sound of their cry, and the house was filled with smoke.
  Exodus 19:16-19 — related smoke and powerful sound at divine manifestation: Isaiah's house fills with smoke at the attendants' cry; Sinai is covered with smoke at God's fiery descent, accompanied by strong trumpet sounds. Both associate the divine manifestation with smoke and overwhelming sound; Exodus here does not state that Isaiah's lintel event recurs.

Isaiah 6:5
And I said, Woe is me, for I am pierced to the heart! For I am a man of unclean lips, and I dwell among a people of unclean lips; and my eyes have seen the King, the Lord Sabaoth.
  Luke 5:8-10 — parallel confession of unworthiness before a manifestation of the Lord: Isaiah confesses unclean lips and a pierced heart after seeing the King; Peter confesses that he is a sinful man after Jesus' extraordinary catch of fish. Both confessions precede a mission, with different encounters and forms of sin-consciousness.

Isaiah 6:6
And one of the seraphim was sent to me, with a coal in his hand that he had taken from the altar with the tongs;
  Leviticus 16:12-13 — related altar coals brought into the divine presence: Isaiah's seraph takes a coal from the altar for the encounter with the prophet; Aaron brings burning altar coals within the veil and uses incense before the Lord. The altar-coal setting corresponds, but the priestly incense rite is not the same action as Isaiah's cleansing.

Isaiah 6:7
and he touched my mouth and said, Behold, this has touched your lips, and will take away your iniquities and cleanse your sins.
  Jeremiah 1:9 — parallel touching of a prophet's mouth before commission: Isaiah's lips are touched with the coal to remove iniquity and cleanse sins; the Lord touches Jeremiah's mouth to put His words there. Both prepare a prophet's speech through a touch, while the explicitly stated effects differ.

Isaiah 6:8
And I heard the voice of the Lord, saying, Whom shall I send, and who will go to this people? And I said, Behold, here I am; send me.
  1 Kings 22:20-22 — parallel heavenly question followed by a volunteer: The Lord asks whom to send and Isaiah volunteers; in Micaiah's vision the Lord asks who will deceive Ahab and a spirit volunteers. The council question-and-response pattern corresponds; Isaiah's prophetic mission is not equated with the spirit's deceptive mission.

Isaiah 6:9
And He said, Go, and say to this people: Hearing, you will hear and certainly not understand; and seeing, you will see and certainly not perceive.
  Matthew 13:14-15 — explicit quotation of Isaiah's uncomprehending hearing and sight: Matthew names Isaiah's prophecy and quotes the repeated hearing without understanding and seeing without perception, applying it to the hearers of Jesus' parables.

Isaiah 6:10
For the heart of this people has grown dull, and with their ears they have heard heavily, and they have closed their eyes; lest they see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and understand with their heart, and turn, and I will heal them.
  Acts 28:25-27 — explicit quotation of Isaiah's unresponsive people: Paul attributes the words to the Holy Spirit through Isaiah and repeats the dull heart, heavy ears, closed eyes, and the clause about turning and healing. Acts applies the quotation amid divided responses to Paul's witness about Jesus.
  John 12:39-41 — related quotation with a different statement of agency: John uses Isaiah's eye-and-heart language to explain unbelief toward Jesus, stating that He blinded their eyes and hardened their heart. Isaiah's supplied wording says the people closed their eyes; the connection preserves this difference rather than substituting one form for the other.

Isaiah 6:11
And I said, How long, O Lord? And He said: Until cities lie waste, without inhabitants, and houses, without people, and the land is left desolate.
  Jeremiah 4:25-29 — parallel depopulation of cities under divine judgment: Isaiah's answer to how long describes empty cities, houses without people, and a desolate land; Jeremiah depicts no human present, ruined cities, and every city abandoned in the Lord's announced judgment.

Isaiah 6:12
And after these things God will remove people far away; and those who remain will multiply upon the land.
  Deuteronomy 28:63-64 — parallel removal of the people far from their land: Isaiah says God will remove people far away; Deuteronomy warns of removal from the land and scattering among nations from end to end of the earth. This supports the removal clause; it does not supply Isaiah's following statement that those remaining will multiply.

Isaiah 6:13
And yet a tenth remains upon it; and again it will be for plunder, as a terebinth, and as an acorn when it falls from its husk.
  Amos 5:3 — related tenth remaining after judgment: Isaiah speaks of a tenth remaining, then exposed again to plunder; Amos describes a hundred remaining from a thousand and ten from a hundred. The shared remnant fraction is specific, while Amos does not repeat Isaiah's further plunder or tree imagery.

Isaiah 7:1
In the days of Achaz, son of Joatham, son of Oziah, king of Judea, Raasson king of Aram and Phakee son of Romelias, king of Israel, went up against Jerusalem to wage war on her, but they could not besiege her.
  2 Kings 16:5 — parallel account of the Aramean and Israelite attack under Achaz: Both name Raasson and Phakee coming against Jerusalem in the reign of Achaz/Ahaz. The supplied accounts differ in describing the siege: Isaiah says they could not besiege her, while Kings says they besieged Ahaz but could not wage the battle successfully.

Isaiah 7:2
It was reported to David’s house, “Aram has come to agreement with Ephraim.” His soul and his people’s soul were distraught, as when a tree in a wood is shaken by wind.
  2 Chronicles 28:5-8 — historical counterpart to the threat from Aram and Ephraim: Isaiah reports alarm at Aram's agreement with Ephraim; Chronicles describes Ahaz delivered into the hands of Syria's king and Israel's king, followed by major losses. This supports the two-front pressure in his reign without claiming that Chronicles repeats the agreement or tree simile.

Isaiah 7:3
The Lord said to Isaiah, “Go out to meet Achaz, you and the remaining Jasub, your son, toward the pool of the upper road of the fuller’s field,
  2 Kings 18:17 — related pool and fuller's-field setting in a later crisis: Isaiah is sent to Achaz toward the pool of the upper road of the fuller's field; the Assyrian envoys later stand at the upper pool's aqueduct on the road of the Fuller's Field under Hezekiah. Both join a pool, road, and fuller's field, with different kings and visitors and differently phrased location descriptions.

Isaiah 7:4
and you will say to him, ‘Take care to be quiet, and do not fear; do not let your soul grow weak before these two smoking firebrand logs. For whenever the wrath of My anger comes, I will heal again.
  Exodus 14:13-14 — parallel summons to courage and quiet before military danger: Isaiah tells the threatened king to be quiet and not fear; Moses tells frightened Israel to take courage and keep silence as the Lord fights for them. The shared response to military danger does not identify the two crises.

Isaiah 7:5
And the son of Aram and the son of Romelias, because they counseled an evil counsel concerning you, saying,
  Psalms 2:1-3 — related hostile rulers' plotting: Isaiah introduces the hostile counsel of the Aramean and Israelite rulers; the psalm depicts rulers gathering and devising opposition to the Lord and His anointed. Both expose hostile political counsel, without identifying Achaz with the psalm's anointed figure.

Isaiah 7:6
“We will go up into Judea and, after speaking with them, turn them toward ourselves and make Tabeel’s son king over her,”
  2 Kings 23:34 — contrasting attempts at foreign installation of a Judean king: Isaiah reports a plan to install Tabeel's son, which the next verse declares will fail; Kings records Pharaoh Nechao actually installing Eliakim in Judah. Both concern foreign control of Judah's succession, but involve different candidates and opposite outcomes.

Isaiah 7:7
thus says the Lord Sabaoth: “This counsel will certainly not endure, nor will it happen.
  Psalms 32:10-11 — parallel frustration of human political counsel by God: The Lord declares that the coalition's counsel will not endure or occur; the psalm says He scatters nations' counsels and nullifies rulers' plans, whereas His own counsel remains.

Isaiah 7:8
But the head of Aram is Damascus; yet within another sixty-five years Ephraim’s kingdom will cease from being a people,
  2 Kings 17:5-6 — later historical counterpart to Ephraim's political collapse: Isaiah announces that Ephraim's kingdom will cease from being a people; Kings recounts Assyria's capture of Samaria and deportation of Israel. The historical loss of kingdom and population corresponds, without claiming that this target establishes the stated sixty-five-year interval.

Isaiah 7:9
and the head of Ephraim is Somoron, and the head of Somoron Romelias’s son. If you do not believe, you will certainly not understand.”’”
  Hebrews 11:3 — parallel dependence of understanding upon faith: Isaiah says that without believing the hearers will not understand; Hebrews says that by faith we understand the ages to have been formed by God's word. Both connect faith and understanding, though their objects are political warning and creation respectively.

Isaiah 7:10
And the Lord spoke again to Achaz, saying,
  Isaiah 1:1 — same person: Isaiah 7:10 addresses Achaz, and Isaiah 1:1 names Achaz among the kings of Judea during whose reign Isaiah saw his vision. Both refer to the same king Achaz of Judah.

Isaiah 7:11
Ask for yourself a sign from the Lord your God, in the depth or in the height.
  2 Kings 20:8-11 — related invitation and request for a confirming sign: Achaz is invited to ask for a sign from the Lord; Hezekiah later asks Isaiah for a sign of the Lord's promise and receives the returning shadow. The shared royal encounter with Isaiah and a divine sign contrasts with Achaz's following refusal.

Isaiah 7:12
And Achaz said, I will certainly not ask, nor will I put the Lord to the test at all.
  Deuteronomy 6:16 — verbal appeal to the prohibition on testing the Lord: Achaz says he will not put the Lord to the test, matching the law's prohibition on testing Him. Isaiah's following rebuke prevents treating this verbal resemblance as approval of Achaz's refusal of an expressly offered sign.

Isaiah 7:13
And he said, Hear now, O house of David: is it a small thing for you to trouble people? And how is it that you trouble the Lord?
  Exodus 16:7-8 — related human opposition reaching the Lord Himself: Isaiah asks why David's house troubles the Lord as well as people; Moses says the congregation's murmuring against the human leaders is against God. Both expose a divine dimension in opposition directed through God's representatives, in different historical settings.

Isaiah 7:14
Therefore the Lord Himself will give you a sign: behold, the virgin will conceive and bear a son, and you will call his name Emmanuel.
  Matthew 1:22-23 — explicit virgin-and-Emmanuel quotation applied to Jesus' birth: Matthew introduces the virgin conceiving and bearing a son named Emmanuel as a prophetic fulfillment in the birth of Jesus, reproducing Isaiah's distinctive sign and name.

Isaiah 7:15
Curds and honey he will eat; before he knows or chooses evil, he will choose the good.
  Isaiah 7:21-22 — same-chapter recurrence of the child's diet in the remnant's food: The child eats curds and honey; later in the same chapter the remaining people eat butter and honey because of abundant milk. The repeated dairy-and-honey diet links the child sign with the chapter's description of life in the land.

Isaiah 7:16
For before the child knows good or evil, he refuses wickedness, to choose the good; and the land which you fear because of the two kings will be forsaken.
  Deuteronomy 1:39 — parallel description of children before knowledge of good and evil: Isaiah places the sign before the child knows good or evil, while describing his refusal of wickedness; Deuteronomy describes young children who do not yet know good or evil and will inherit the land. The developmental description corresponds without equating the separate land promises.
  2 Kings 16:9 — historical counterpart concerning the removal of one feared king: Isaiah announces the forsaking of the land feared because of two kings; Kings records Assyria capturing Damascus and killing Raasson, one of those kings. The target supports that particular ruler's removal, not both kings or the child's age chronology.

Isaiah 7:17
But God will bring upon you, upon your people and upon your father’s house days that have not yet come since the day He removed Ephraim from Judah—the king of the Assyrians.”
  2 Chronicles 28:20-21 — parallel Assyrian distress for Achaz's house: Isaiah announces trouble associated with the king of the Assyrians not experienced since Ephraim's removal from Judah; Chronicles describes Assour's king striking Ahaz and bringing no help despite gifts. Both turn Assyrian involvement into trouble for Judah's king.

Isaiah 7:18
On that day the Lord will whistle for flies—what rules a part of Egypt’s river—and for the bee that is in the Assyrians’ country,
  Deuteronomy 1:44 — related bee imagery for hostile military pursuit: Isaiah portrays the Assyrian force as a bee summoned by the Lord; Deuteronomy likens the Amorites' pursuit to bees. The insect image makes hostile pressure vivid in both, while the armies and events are distinct.

Isaiah 7:19
and they will all come and rest in the country’s ravines, in holes of the rocks, in the caves, in every fissure and in every tree.
  Isaiah 2:19 — shared image: Isaiah 7:19 has the summoned swarm rest in holes of the rocks, in the caves and in every fissure, and Isaiah 2:19 speaks of caves, clefts in the rocks and holes in the earth. Both list the same rock refuges, though in Isaiah 2:19 people flee into them from the Lord's terror.

Isaiah 7:20
On that day the Lord will shave with the great and drunken razor that is beyond the river of the king of the Assyrians—the head and the hair of the feet—and He will remove the beard.
  Ezekiel 5:1-4 — parallel shaving of head and beard as judgment imagery: Isaiah's great and drunken razor removes head hair and beard in an Assyrian judgment image; Ezekiel uses a sword sharper than a barber's razor to shave his head and beard for a siege sign. The target clarifies a comparable enacted judgment image without changing Isaiah's razor into a hired one.

Isaiah 7:21
On that day a person will raise a heifer from the cattle and two sheep,
  Isaiah 7:25 — same oracle: Isaiah 7:21 says a person will raise a heifer from the cattle and two sheep, and Isaiah 7:25 says the ruined land will become pasture for sheep and a trampling place for cattle. Both verses of the oracle describe the emptied land given over to small-scale herding of cattle and sheep.

Isaiah 7:22
and because they produce very much milk, everyone left upon the land will eat butter and honey.
  Deuteronomy 32:13-14 — related provision of honey and dairy produce: Isaiah links abundant milk with the remaining inhabitants' diet of butter and honey; the song in Deuteronomy remembers God's provision of honey, butter of cattle, and milk of sheep. The specific foods correspond, while the historical settings differ.

Isaiah 7:23
On that day every place where there are a thousand vines worth a thousand shekels will become fallow ground and thorn.
  Proverbs 24:30-31 — related vineyard image of cultivation becoming wasteland: Isaiah's valuable vineyard becomes fallow ground and thorn; Proverbs compares a person lacking understanding to a vineyard that becomes abandoned wasteland if left alone. The shared ruined-cultivation image does not turn Proverbs' person-as-vineyard comparison into a literal vineyard visit.

Isaiah 7:24
With missile and arrow they will enter there, because all the land will be fallow ground and thorn.
  Isaiah 5:6 — verbal echo: Isaiah 7:24 says all the land will be fallow ground and thorn, and Isaiah 5:6 says thorns will rise in the abandoned vineyard as in fallow ground. Both describe cultivated land in Judah reverting to fallow ground and thorns under judgment.

Isaiah 7:25
Every mountain that is plowed will be plowed, and fear will certainly not come there; for from fallow ground and thorn it will become pasture for sheep and a trampling place for cattle.
  Isaiah 5:17 — related sheep and cattle imagery in transformed land: Isaiah 7 describes sheep pasture and cattle trampling ground emerging from fallow thorn-land; Isaiah 5 describes lambs feeding in deserted places and plundered people pastured like bulls. The pastoral imagery corresponds while preserving the figurative people in 5:17.

Isaiah 8:1
The Lord said to me, “Take a large new sheet and write on it with a human stylus: ‘To make plunder of spoils swiftly; for it is at hand.’”
  Habakkuk 2:2-3 — parallel command to inscribe a prophetic message: Isaiah is told to write the approaching plunder message on a large new sheet; Habakkuk is told to write a vision clearly on a tablet, with its appointed realization in view. Both give a durable written form to an impending prophetic message.

Isaiah 8:2
“Make reliable men witnesses for Me, Uriah and Zechariah son of Barachias.”
  Jeremiah 39:10-14 — related witnessed prophetic documentation: Isaiah is told to appoint reliable witnesses for the written sign; Jeremiah writes and seals a purchase document before witnesses and orders its preservation as part of a prophetic transaction. The documents serve different purposes, but both use witnesses to attest a tangible prophetic act.

Isaiah 8:3
I approached the prophetess; she became pregnant and bore a son. The Lord said to me, “Call his name Plunder-quickly, Spoil-swiftly,
  Hosea 1:3-4 — parallel child given a divinely appointed judgment name: Isaiah's prophetess bears a son named by the Lord for approaching plunder; Hosea's wife bears a son whom the Lord names Jezreel in connection with impending judgment. Both turn a prophet's child's name into a public warning.

Isaiah 8:4
because before the child knows how to call father or mother, he will take Damascus’s power and Samaria’s spoils before the king of the Assyrians.”
  2 Kings 16:9 — historical counterpart to Assyrian seizure of Damascus: Isaiah names the removal of Damascus's power before Assyria's king; Kings narrates that king's capture of Damascus, deportation, and killing of Raasson. This supports the Damascus clause without claiming proof of the child's speech-development timetable or the Samaria clause.
  Hosea 1:4-5 — parallel near political judgment attached to a child's sign-name: Isaiah connects the newborn sign with impending loss for Damascus and Samaria; Hosea's naming of Jezreel is followed by the announcement that Israel's kingdom and bow will be broken in a little while. The child-sign and near political judgment correspond, while the names and threatened events differ.

Isaiah 8:6
“Because this people does not want Siloam’s water, which flows quietly, but wants to have Raasson and Romelias’s son as king over you,
  Jeremiah 2:13 — related rejection expressed through water imagery: Isaiah describes rejection of quietly flowing Siloam in favor of Raasson and Romelias's son; Jeremiah depicts the people abandoning God, the spring of living water, for broken cisterns. Both expose a disastrous rejected source through water imagery; Jeremiah's explicit identification of the spring as God is not substituted into Isaiah's wording.

Isaiah 8:7
therefore, behold, the Lord brings up against you the river’s strong and abundant water—the king of the Assyrians and his glory. He will ascend every ravine of yours and walk over every wall of yours,
  Jeremiah 26:7-9 — parallel foreign power portrayed as an overwhelming river: Isaiah identifies the strong abundant river with Assyria's king; Jeremiah portrays Egypt rising like a river to cover the land and destroy its inhabitants. The inundating military image corresponds, while the foreign powers are different.

Isaiah 8:8
and he will remove from Judea a person able to lift a head or someone strong enough to accomplish anything. His encampment will fill the breadth of your country. God is with us.”
  2 Kings 18:13 — later historical counterpart to Assyrian invasion of Judah: Isaiah describes Assyrian encampment filling Judah's breadth and removing its capable people; Kings records Sennacherib taking Judah's fortified cities under Hezekiah. This is a concrete historical counterpart to Assyrian penetration of Judah, not proof that every detail names that one campaign.

Isaiah 8:9
Know, nations, and be defeated! Listen, you at the farthest edge of the earth! You who have grown strong, be defeated; for even if you grow strong again, you will again be defeated.
  Psalms 2:10-12 — related warning to nations and rulers under God's power: Isaiah addresses nations and distant peoples whose renewed strength will still end in defeat; the psalm calls kings and judges to instruction and fear of the Lord lest His anger bring destruction. Both subordinate political strength to the Lord's authority.

Isaiah 8:10
Whatever counsel you devise, the Lord will scatter; whatever word you speak will certainly not endure for you, because the Lord God is with us.
  Psalms 32:10-11 — parallel scattering of political counsel by the Lord: Isaiah says the Lord will scatter whatever counsel the nations devise and their word will not endure; the psalm likewise says He scatters nations' counsels and nullifies rulers' plans while His own counsel remains.
  Romans 8:31 — parallel confidence grounded in God being with His people: Isaiah grounds the failure of hostile counsel in God being with us; Paul asks who can stand against us if God is for us. The shared divine-presence argument supports confidence against opposition in different covenantal settings.

Isaiah 8:12
‘Never say hard; for everything this people says is hard.’ But do not fear its fear, and do not be troubled.
  1 Peter 3:14 — reuse of the command not to fear their fear: After Isaiah's difficult opening warning about what the people call hard, the verse says not to fear its fear or be troubled. First Peter repeats that latter command when addressing suffering for righteousness; it does not interpret the opening as a conspiracy saying.

Isaiah 8:13
Sanctify the Lord Himself, and He will be your fear.
  1 Peter 3:15 — related command to sanctify the Lord: Isaiah commands sanctifying the Lord Himself as one's fear; First Peter commands sanctifying Christ as Lord in the heart. The latter explicitly applies the sanctifying command to Christ while adding the heart and readiness-to-answer setting.

Isaiah 8:14
If you trust in Him, He will become a sanctuary for you, and you will not encounter Him as the stumbling of a stone or as the falling of a rock. But Jacob’s house is in a trap, and those settled in Jerusalem in a pit.
  Romans 9:32-33 — reuse of stumbling-stone language in a composite scriptural citation: Isaiah contrasts trusting sanctuary with encountering a stumbling stone or falling rock; Romans uses the stone of stumbling and rock of offense to explain Israel's stumbling because its pursuit was as though from works, not from faith. The citation develops the stone image without erasing Isaiah's protection for those who trust.
  1 Peter 2:6-8 — Christological use of the stone and differing responses: First Peter applies the stone-of-stumbling wording to disobedience toward Christ, while distinguishing the honor of believers. Isaiah likewise distinguishes those trusting the Lord from the trapped house, though its supplied wording presents sanctuary and avoidance of stumbling conditionally.

Isaiah 8:15
Therefore many among them will be powerless, fall and be broken; people who are secure will draw near and be captured.”
  Luke 20:17-18 — related falling and breaking in the stone context: After Isaiah's rock-and-stumbling imagery, many fall and are broken; Jesus says that everyone falling upon the rejected stone will be shattered. The shared fall-and-break consequence links the adjacent stone contexts without identifying every group or event.

Isaiah 8:16
Then those sealing up the law so that it may not be learned will be revealed.
  Luke 11:52 — parallel exposure of obstructed access to divine instruction: Isaiah announces the revealing of those sealing the law so it may not be learned; Jesus condemns law experts for taking away the key of knowledge and hindering entry. Both expose people obstructing others' access to instruction, using different images.

Isaiah 8:17
He will say, “I will wait for God, who turned His face away from Jacob’s house, and I will put my trust in Him.”
  Hebrews 2:13 — quotation of the declaration of trust: Hebrews quotes the declaration of trust immediately before quoting Isaiah's children saying, placing both in its argument about the Son and His brothers. The target matches Isaiah's trust clause, not the additional statement about God's hidden face.

Isaiah 8:18
Behold, I and the children God gave me—and it will be for signs and wonders in Israel’s house, from the Lord Sabaoth, who dwells on Mount Zion.
  Hebrews 2:13 — explicit quotation of the speaker and God-given children: Hebrews repeats 'I and the children whom God gave Me' within its account of the Son's solidarity with those He sanctifies. This directly takes up Isaiah's speaker-and-children sign language in a new stated application.

Isaiah 8:19
If they say to you, “Seek those who speak from the earth and the belly-speakers, the empty-talkers who speak from the belly,” should not a nation turn to its God? Why do they seek the dead concerning the living?
  Deuteronomy 18:10-12 — parallel rejection of consulting mediums and the dead: Isaiah challenges seeking belly-speakers and the dead concerning the living instead of turning to God; Deuteronomy prohibits mediums and inquiry of the dead among practices detestable to the Lord.
  1 Chronicles 10:13-14 — narrative instance of seeking a medium instead of the Lord: Chronicles explains Saul's death by disobedience, inquiry through a spirit medium, and failure to seek the Lord. That concrete contrast corresponds to Isaiah's question why a people consults the dead instead of its God; Chronicles also expressly says Samuel answered Saul.

Isaiah 8:20
For He gave law for help, so that they might speak unlike this utterance, concerning which there are no gifts to give for it.
  Psalms 118:92-94 — Thematic parallel: divine law as sustaining help: Isaiah explicitly says that God gave law for help, in contrast with the utterance discussed after consulting the dead. The psalmist says meditation on God's law kept him from perishing in humiliation, that the ordinances gave him life, and asks for salvation while seeking them.
