The connection is the law's beneficial, sustaining function; the psalm does not resolve Isaiah's unusual clause about gifts or replace it with another wording.

Isaiah 8:21
A harsh famine will come upon you; when you hunger, you will be grieved and speak evil of the ruler and the cult images, and they will look up toward heaven above,
  Exodus 22:27 — verbal echo: Isaiah says the hungry will speak evil of the ruler and the cult images, and Exodus 22:27 commands, You shall not revile gods, neither shall you speak evil of the rulers. Both pair speaking evil of rulers with reviling divine objects.

Isaiah 8:22
and they will look down to the earth, and behold, affliction and confinement and darkness, constricted perplexity and darkness so that they cannot see,
  Amos 5:18-20 — parallel inescapable darkness in the judgment scene: Isaiah describes affliction, confinement, and darkness preventing sight; Amos warns that the Lord's day brings darkness without brightness and escape from one danger into another. The shared oppressive darkness expresses judgment rather than relief.

Isaiah 8:23
and the one in confinement will not remain perplexed for a time. “Do this first, do it quickly, country of Zebulun, land of Naphtali, way of the sea, and the rest who dwell by the sea and beyond the Jordan, Galilee of the nations, the regions of Judea.”
  Matthew 4:13-16 — explicit geographical quotation applied to Jesus in Galilee: Matthew identifies Jesus' settlement in Capernaum with Isaiah's Zebulun, Naphtali, sea-way, beyond-Jordan, and Galilee-of-the-nations language. The quotation continues into the light saying, while the geographic phrases are those supplied here in Isaiah 8:23.

Isaiah 9:1
You people who walk in darkness, behold a great light; you who dwell in a land and shadow of death, a light will shine upon you.
  Matthew 4:14-16 — explicit quotation and fulfillment: Matthew expressly introduces Isaiah's words as fulfilled when Jesus settles in Galilee, repeating the great light for people in darkness and death's shadow.

Isaiah 9:2
The multitude of the people whom Thou hast brought down in Thy gladness, they will rejoice before Thee, as people rejoice at harvest, and as those who divide the spoils.
  Psalms 125:5-6 — shared harvest image for joy: Isaiah compares the people's rejoicing with joy at harvest; the psalm likewise pictures reapers returning with exultation and their sheaves.
  Psalms 118:162 — shared spoil image for rejoicing: Isaiah also compares gladness with dividing spoils; the psalm compares joy in God's sayings with finding much spoil. The occasions of joy differ.

Isaiah 9:3
For the yoke laid upon them has been taken away, and the rod upon their neck; for the Lord has shattered the rod of their oppressors, as in the day against Midian.
  Judges 7:20-25 — explicit historical comparison with Midian's defeat: Isaiah names the day against Midian as the pattern for breaking oppression; Judges records the Lord turning Midian's camp against itself and Israel's pursuit of its rulers.

Isaiah 9:4
For every robe gathered by deceit, and every garment, they will repay with restitution; and they will wish that they had been burned with fire.
  Exodus 22:8 — legal counterpart of restitution for wrongfully acquired property: Isaiah requires restitution concerning robes gathered by deceit; Exodus includes a disputed garment among property for which the convicted wrongdoer must repay double. The fire wish is additional in Isaiah.

Isaiah 9:5
For a child was born to us, a son also was given to us, whose rule was laid upon his shoulder; and his name is called Messenger of Great Counsel. For I will bring peace upon the rulers, peace and health to him.
  Luke 2:11-14 — related birth announcement joined with peace: Both join the birth of a child with an announcement of peace. Luke names the newborn Savior, Christ the Lord; Isaiah calls the child Messenger of Great Counsel.

Isaiah 9:6
Great is his rule, and his peace has no limit, upon the throne of David and his kingdom, to establish it and uphold it in righteousness and judgment, from now on and for ever. The zeal of the Lord Sabaoth will do these things.
  Luke 1:32-33 — close Davidic throne and unending reign: Isaiah promises enduring rule upon David's throne; the angel likewise promises Jesus David's throne and a kingdom without end.
  Psalms 71:1-7 — parallel royal righteousness, judgment and abundant peace: Isaiah joins the ruler's lasting peace with righteousness and judgment; the psalm likewise petitions for a king's righteous judgment, enduring life and abundant peace.

Isaiah 9:7
The Lord sent death against Jacob, and it came upon Israel,
  Amos 4:10 — close divine sending of death upon Israel: Isaiah says the Lord sent death against Jacob and Israel; Amos likewise has God declare that He sent death among His people.

Isaiah 9:8
and all Ephraim’s people and those settled in Samaria will know, saying in insolence and a lofty heart,
  Amos 6:7-8 — parallel judgment of Ephraim and Jacob's arrogance: Isaiah introduces Ephraim and Samaria's insolent, lofty speech; Amos announces Ephraim's captivity and God's abhorrence of Jacob's arrogance.

Isaiah 9:9
“Bricks have fallen, but come, let us hew stones, cut down sycamores and cedars, and build ourselves a tower.”
  Genesis 11:3-4 — related collective tower-building boast with bricks and stone: Isaiah's speakers propose hewn stones and a tower after bricks fall; Genesis likewise records a collective tower-building proposal using brick for stone and seeking a name. The construction details differ.

Isaiah 9:11
Syria from the sun’s rising and the Greeks from the sun’s setting, who devour Israel with their whole mouth. For all these things the anger did not turn away, but the hand is still high.
  Psalms 78:6-7 — close devouring of Israel by foreign nations: Isaiah pictures Syria and the Greeks devouring Israel; the psalm says nations devoured Jacob and desolated his place. The psalm leaves those nations unnamed.

Isaiah 9:12
The people did not turn back until they were struck, and they did not seek the Lord.
  Amos 4:9-10 — related divine blows and failure to turn toward God: Isaiah says the people did not turn back until struck and did not seek the Lord; Amos joins divine blows with the repeated statement that Israel still did not turn to Him. Their stated timing differs.

Isaiah 9:13
The Lord removed from Israel head and tail, great and small, in one day,
  Isaiah 9:14 — same-chapter explanation of head and tail: The removal of Israel's head and tail is immediately explained by identifying leadership with the elder and those showing favoritism, and the tail with the prophet teaching lawless things.

Isaiah 9:14
the elder and those who show favoritism—this is the leadership—and the prophet teaching lawless things—this is the tail.
  Jeremiah 23:13-14 — parallel condemnation of lawless prophets: Isaiah identifies a prophet teaching lawlessness as the tail; Jeremiah likewise condemns Samaria's prophets for misleading Israel and Jerusalem's prophets for supporting evil conduct.

Isaiah 9:15
Those blessing this people will lead astray, and they lead astray in order to swallow them up.
  Jeremiah 23:16-17 — close misleading assurances given to the disobedient: Isaiah says those blessing the people lead them astray; Jeremiah warns against prophets who promise peace and freedom from evils to those rejecting God's word.

Isaiah 9:16
Therefore God will not rejoice over their young men, and He will not pity their orphans or widows, because all are lawless and evil, and every mouth speaks unjust things. For all these things the anger did not turn away, but the hand is still high.
  Jeremiah 13:13-14 — parallel withholding of pity in comprehensive judgment: Isaiah denies pity even to orphans and widows amid general lawlessness; Jeremiah similarly denies sparing or pitying a people whose rulers, families, priests and prophets face destruction. The named groups differ.

Isaiah 9:17
Lawlessness will burn like fire and be consumed by fire like dry grass; it will burn in the forest’s thickets and consume together everything around the hills.
  James 3:5-6 — shared forest-fire image for destructive unrighteousness: Isaiah likens lawlessness to fire consuming thickets; James likens the unrighteous tongue to a small fire setting a great forest ablaze.

Isaiah 9:18
Through the fury of the Lord’s anger the whole earth has been burned, and the people will be as though burned by fire. A person will not pity his brother,
  Jeremiah 21:12-14 — parallel divine anger consuming like fire: Isaiah describes the earth and people burned through the Lord's fury; Jeremiah warns that His anger will kindle an unquenchable fire consuming the woodland and its surroundings. The stated extent differs.

Isaiah 9:19
but will turn to the right because he will hunger, and eat from the left; a person eating the flesh of his own arm will certainly not be filled.
  Deuteronomy 28:53-55 — related hunger expressed through eating human flesh: Isaiah describes hunger unrelieved by eating one's own arm; Deuteronomy warns that besieged people will eat their children's flesh. The flesh and circumstances are specified differently.

Isaiah 9:20
For Manasseh will eat from Ephraim, and Ephraim from Manasseh, because together they will besiege Judah. For all these things the anger did not turn away, but the hand is still high.
  Galatians 5:15 — shared mutual-devouring image for communal hostility: Isaiah portrays Manasseh and Ephraim eating from one another; Paul warns a community biting and devouring one another that mutual consumption may result. Isaiah specifically names Israelite tribes.

Isaiah 10:1
Woe to those writing wickedness! For when they write, they write wickedness,
  Psalms 93:20-21 — parallel injustice exercised through authoritative commands: Isaiah condemns writing wickedness, explained in 10:2 as denying justice; the psalm likewise describes a lawless throne shaping toil by command and condemning innocent blood.

Isaiah 10:2
turning aside the judgment of the poor, seizing the justice of My people’s needy, so that a widow becomes their prey and an orphan their plunder.
  Deuteronomy 24:17-18 — legal prohibition corresponding to injustice against widows and orphans: Isaiah condemns seizing the vulnerable people's justice and making widows and orphans prey; Deuteronomy explicitly forbids perverting their judgment and taking a widow's garment as pledge.

Isaiah 10:3
What will they do on the day of visitation? For affliction will come to you from far away. To whom will you flee for help, and where will you leave your glory,
  Zephaniah 1:14-18 — parallel inability to escape the approaching judgment day: Isaiah asks where help or glory can be secured on the day of visitation; Zephaniah describes the approaching day of affliction and denies that silver or gold can rescue anyone. Isaiah's glory is not equated with those metals.

Isaiah 10:4
so as not to fall into captivity? For all these things the anger did not turn away, but the hand is still high.
  Isaiah 9:20 — repeated literary refrain of continuing anger: Both units close a description of national punishment with the same refrain: the anger has not turned away and the hand is still high. The preceding punishments differ.

Isaiah 10:5
Woe to the Assyrians! The rod of My anger and wrath is in their hands.
  Habakkuk 1:6-10 — parallel foreign military power employed in divine judgment: Isaiah places the rod of divine anger in Assyrian hands; Habakkuk has God raise Chaldean warriors who seize other people's dwellings and captives. The nations are distinct.

Isaiah 10:6
I will send My anger against a lawless nation, and I will command My people to take spoils and plunder, trample the cities and reduce them to dust.
  Joshua 8:1-2 — parallel divine command to attack a city and take spoil: Isaiah says God will command His people to take spoil and plunder cities; Joshua records God's command to attack Ai and take livestock as spoil. Joshua supplies a different military setting, not an identification of Isaiah's people.

Isaiah 10:7
But he did not consider it this way and has not reckoned it this way in his soul; rather, his mind will turn aside, even to destroy not a few nations.
  Zechariah 1:15 — parallel distinction between divine purpose and a nation's destructive intent: Isaiah distinguishes the ruler's intention to destroy many nations from the preceding divine commission; Zechariah likewise distinguishes God's limited anger from nations attacking to bring evils.

Isaiah 10:9
he will say, “Did I not take the country above Babylon and Chalanneh, where the tower was built? I also took Arabia, Damascus and Samaria.
  Isaiah 36:18-19 — parallel Assyrian boast about conquered countries: The Assyrian ruler lists earlier conquests, including Samaria; the later Assyrian challenge likewise appeals to the failure of countries' gods to rescue them, explicitly naming Samaria.

Isaiah 10:10
As I took these in my hand, I will also take all the dominions. Wail, carved images in Jerusalem and Samaria!
  Isaiah 36:18-20 — parallel imperial claim over dominions and their gods: Isaiah's ruler claims he will take all dominions and summons Jerusalem's and Samaria's images to wail; the later speech similarly claims no nation's god can rescue its land from Assyrian hands.

Isaiah 10:11
For as I did to Samaria and her handmade objects, so I will also do to Jerusalem and her idols.”
  Isaiah 36:19-20 — close comparison of Samaria's fate with threatened Jerusalem: Both Assyrian speeches use Samaria's inability to be rescued as the basis for threatening Jerusalem, expressly challenging the protection offered by their gods or images.

Isaiah 10:12
When the Lord has finished doing all things on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem, He will bring judgment upon the great mind—the ruler of the Assyrians—and upon the height of the glory of his eyes.
  Isaiah 37:23-29 — parallel judgment upon the Assyrian ruler's presumption: Isaiah promises judgment upon the ruler's great mind and lofty glory; the later oracle rebukes his reproach and raging and promises to turn him back with muzzle and bridle.

Isaiah 10:13
For he said, “By strength I will act, and by the wisdom of understanding. I will remove nations’ boundaries, plunder their strength and shake inhabited cities.
  Isaiah 37:24-26 — parallel Assyrian boasting about personal power in conquest: Isaiah's ruler boasts that he will remove boundaries and shake cities by strength and understanding; the later speech boasts of chariots and feats of conquest, answered by God's claim to have arranged the devastation.

Isaiah 10:14
I will seize the whole inhabited world in my hand like a nest, and lift it like abandoned eggs. There is no one who will escape me or contradict me.”
  Habakkuk 2:5-8 — parallel conqueror's appetite for all nations: Isaiah's ruler claims the inhabited world as easily seized as abandoned eggs; Habakkuk portrays a boastful man gathering all nations with an appetite like death. Habakkuk's image is not a nest.

Isaiah 10:15
Will an ax be glorified without the one cutting with it, or a saw be raised without the one drawing it? Likewise, if someone lifts a rod or a piece of wood.
  Romans 9:20-21 — related craft analogy rebuking human presumption: Isaiah denies the tool's glory apart from its wielder; Paul denies the formed object's standing to answer its maker and invokes the potter's authority. The objects and their relationships differ.

Isaiah 10:16
And it will not be so; rather, the Lord Sabaoth will send dishonor against your honor, and against your glory a burning fire will burn.
  Psalms 82:15-17 — parallel fiery wrath joined with dishonor: Isaiah promises dishonor against honor and fire against glory; the psalm likewise joins pursuing wrath with consuming-fire imagery and a petition that enemies' faces be filled with dishonor.

Isaiah 10:17
Israel’s light will become fire, and He will sanctify him in burning fire and consume the woodland like grass. On that day,
  Deuteronomy 4:24 — related consuming fire in divine judgment: Isaiah describes Israel's light becoming fire that consumes woodland like grass; Deuteronomy calls the Lord a consuming fire in its warning against covenant unfaithfulness.

Isaiah 10:18
the mountains, hills and forests will be extinguished, and it will consume from soul to flesh. The one fleeing will be like someone fleeing from a burning flame.
  Psalms 82:15-16 — close woodland and mountain fire as judgment imagery: Isaiah describes mountains, hills and forests consumed in fire; the psalm likewise compares God's pursuit in wrath with fire burning woodland and mountains.

Isaiah 10:19
Those left from them will be a number, and a child will write them down.
  Amos 5:3 — parallel counted remainders after devastation: Isaiah portrays those left as a number a child will write down; Amos specifies thousands reduced to hundreds and hundreds to tens. Amos supplies numbers but no child recorder.

Isaiah 10:20
On that day Israel’s remainder will no longer continue, and Jacob’s survivors will certainly no longer trust those who wronged them; rather, they will trust God, Israel’s Holy One, in truth,
  Hosea 14:3-4 — close rejection of Assyrian reliance in turning toward God: Isaiah's survivors will cease trusting their oppressors and trust Israel's Holy One; Hosea commands return to God and declares that Assur will not save them.

Isaiah 10:21
and Jacob’s remainder will be toward a mighty God.
  Hosea 3:5 — Restoration parallel: Israel turning toward its God: Isaiah places Jacob's remainder toward a mighty God, immediately after the promise that the survivors will trust Israel's Holy One instead of those who wronged them. Hosea says Israel's sons will return and seek the Lord their God after their long deprivation. Both envisage a renewed orientation of Israel toward God; Hosea adds seeking David their king and does not supply the title mighty God.

Isaiah 10:22
Even if Israel’s people becomes like the sea’s sand, their remnant will be saved; for He is completing and cutting short a word in righteousness,
  Romans 9:27-28 — explicit quotation of the saved remnant: Paul expressly cites Isaiah's contrast between Israel numerous as sea sand and the remnant being saved, continuing with the word completed and cut short.

Isaiah 10:23
because God will carry out a shortened word in the whole inhabited world.
  Romans 9:28 — explicit quotation of the shortened divine word: Romans repeats the statement that the Lord carries out a word on earth, completing it and cutting it short; Isaiah specifies the whole inhabited world.

Isaiah 10:24
Therefore thus says the Lord Sabaoth: “Do not fear the Assyrians, My people dwelling in Zion, because he will strike you with a rod; for I bring a blow upon you to see Egypt’s way.
  Isaiah 37:6-7 — close reassurance against fear of Assyria: The Lord tells Zion not to fear the Assyrians; the later message through Isaiah likewise tells Hezekiah not to fear their envoys' words and announces the ruler's return and death.

Isaiah 10:25
For yet a little while, and the wrath will cease, but My anger will be against their counsel.”
  Isaiah 54:7-8 — parallel short duration of divine anger: Isaiah says wrath will cease after a little while; the later promise likewise contrasts a short abandonment and little anger with great, everlasting mercy. The anger against hostile counsel is additional here.

Isaiah 10:26
God will rouse action against them according to Midian’s blow in a place of affliction, and His anger on the way by the sea, toward the way by Egypt.
  Judges 7:22-25 — explicit historical comparison with God's blow against Midian: Isaiah recalls Midian's defeat as a pattern of divine action; Judges describes the Lord turning the camp's swords against itself and Israel capturing Midian's rulers.
  Exodus 14:27-31 — historical counterpart of divine action by the Egyptian sea: Isaiah also invokes God's anger on the way by the sea toward Egypt; Exodus records the Egyptians overwhelmed in the sea and Israel rescued by the Lord's great hand.

Isaiah 10:27
On that day his fear will be removed from you and his yoke from your shoulder, and the yoke will be destroyed from your shoulders.
  Jeremiah 37:8 — close destruction of an oppressive yoke: Isaiah promises removal and destruction of the yoke from the shoulders; Jeremiah likewise promises God will smash the yoke from the neck and end service to foreigners.

Isaiah 10:28
For he will come to the city of Angai, pass into Magedo, and deposit his equipment in Machmas,
  1 Samuel 13:5 — same place: Isaiah describes an invader depositing his equipment in Machmas, and 1 Samuel 13:5 has the foreigners' invading army camp at Michmash. Both use the same town as an invader's staging point, spelled Machmas and Michmash.
  Ezra 2:27 — same place: Ezra 2:27 lists the men of Machmas among the returning Israelites, the same town named in Isaiah 10:28 with the same spelling.

Isaiah 10:30
Daughter Gallim. Laisa will listen, Anathoth will listen.
  Jeremiah 1:1 — same place: Isaiah 10:30 names Anathoth among towns on the invader's route, and Jeremiah 1:1 locates Anathoth in the land of Benjamin. Both refer to the same Benjaminite town.
  Joshua 21:18 — same place: Joshua 21:18 lists Anathoth among the cities with allotted lands, the same town named in Isaiah 10:30.

Isaiah 10:32
today along the way, to remain; with the hand offer encouragement, the mountain, Daughter Zion, and the hills in Jerusalem.
  Isaiah 10:24 — same oracle: Isaiah 10:32 calls for encouragement to Daughter Zion and Jerusalem's hills, and Isaiah 10:24 tells My people dwelling in Zion not to fear the Assyrians. Both address Zion during the same Assyrian advance.
  Isaiah 37:22 — parallel wording: Both name Daughter Zion together with Jerusalem. Isaiah 10:32 speaks of 'the mountain, Daughter Zion, and the hills in Jerusalem', and in Isaiah 37:22 'Virgin daughter Zion despised and sneered at you; daughter Jerusalem shook her head over you.' Both belong to Isaiah's oracles on the Assyrian threat to Jerusalem.

Isaiah 10:33
For behold, the Master, the Lord Sabaoth, throws the glorious ones into confusion with strength; those lofty in insolence will be shattered, and the high brought low,
  Ezekiel 31:10-14 — parallel downfall of exalted power in a tree portrayal: Isaiah promises the shattering of the insolently lofty before Lebanon's fall; Ezekiel's Assyrian tree, used in an address to Pharaoh, is handed over for destruction after rising high.

Isaiah 10:34
and the high will fall by a sword, while Lebanon will fall with the high.
  Ezekiel 31:15-18 — close Lebanon imagery joined with downfall and the sword: Isaiah joins Lebanon's fall with the high falling by a sword; Ezekiel likewise describes Lebanon's trees descending with those wounded by the sword in its account of imperial downfall.

Isaiah 11:1
And a shoot will come from the root of Jesse, and a blossom will rise from the root.
  Acts 13:22-23 — related descent from Jesse through David: Isaiah announces a shoot from Jesse's root; Acts explicitly identifies David as Jesse's son and Jesus as the Savior brought from David's seed according to promise.

Isaiah 11:2
And the Spirit of God will rest upon him: the spirit of wisdom and understanding, the spirit of counsel and might, the spirit of knowledge and godliness.
  John 1:32-34 — close Spirit resting and remaining upon the promised figure: Isaiah says God's Spirit will rest upon the shoot from Jesse; John testifies that the Spirit descended and remained upon Jesus. Isaiah additionally enumerates the Spirit's qualities.

Isaiah 11:3
The spirit of the fear of God will fill him. He will not judge according to appearance, nor reprove according to report;
  John 7:24 — close rejection of judgment by appearance: Isaiah's figure will not judge according to appearance; Jesus likewise commands judgment according to righteousness rather than appearance.

Isaiah 11:4
but he will judge the cause of the lowly, and reprove the lowly of the earth; and he will strike the earth with the word of his mouth, and with breath through his lips he will slay the ungodly.
  Psalms 71:2-4 — close royal judgment for poor and lowly people: Isaiah describes judging the cause of the lowly; the royal psalm likewise asks that the king judge the poor in justice and save the needy person's children.
  2 Thessalonians 2:8 — close slaying of the lawless by breath from the mouth: Isaiah says the figure will slay the ungodly with breath through his lips; Paul describes the Lord Jesus killing the lawless one with the breath of His mouth.

Isaiah 11:5
His loins will be girded with righteousness, and his sides bound with truth.
  Ephesians 6:14 — close righteousness and truth portrayed as bodily equipment: Isaiah binds righteousness and truth around the figure's loins and sides; Paul fastens truth around the believers' waist and adds a breastplate of righteousness. Their arrangement differs.

Isaiah 11:6
And the wolf will feed with the lamb, and the leopard will rest with the young goat; and the calf and the bull and the lion will feed together, and a little child will lead them.
  Isaiah 65:25 — close wolf and lamb feeding together: Both portray wolves and lambs feeding together in the restored order. The later verse does not repeat this verse's leopard, young goat or child leading the animals.

Isaiah 11:7
And the ox and the bear will feed together, and their young will be together; and the lion and the ox will eat straw together.
  Isaiah 65:25 — close lion eating straw like an ox: Both portray the lion eating straw together with, or like, the ox. The later verse does not repeat the bear and shared young described here.

Isaiah 11:8
And an infant will stretch out his hand over the hole of asps, and over the nest of their young.
  Luke 10:19 — related protection from dangerous serpents: Isaiah pictures an infant reaching over asps' holes, within 11:9's promise of no harm; Jesus promises His messengers authority over snakes and protection from harm. The people and actions differ.

Isaiah 11:9
And they will certainly do no harm, nor will they be able at all to destroy anyone upon My holy mountain; for the whole earth has been filled with the knowledge of the Lord, as abundant water covers the seas.
  Habakkuk 2:14 — close earth filled with divine knowledge compared with covering water: Both announce the earth filled with knowledge of the Lord or His glory and compare this with covering water. Isaiah specifically names the seas.
  Isaiah 65:25 — close absence of harm upon God's holy mountain: Both expressly deny harm or destruction upon God's holy mountain, alongside the portrayal of formerly dangerous animals living peacefully.

Isaiah 11:10
On that day there will be Jesse’s root and the one rising to rule nations; nations will hope in him, and his rest will be honor.
  Romans 15:12 — explicit quotation of Jesse's root and the nations' hope: Paul expressly quotes Isaiah's root of Jesse rising to rule nations and becoming the object of their hope. Isaiah additionally describes his rest as honor.

Isaiah 11:11
On that day the Lord will again show His hand, to be zealous for the remaining remnant of the people left from the Assyrians, Egypt, Babylonia, Ethiopia, the Elamites, the sun’s rising and Arabia.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-5 — parallel renewed divine care for a people dispersed among nations: Isaiah enumerates nations from which the remaining people come under God's renewed action, leading into 11:12's gathering; Deuteronomy promises renewed mercy and gathering from the nations of dispersion.

Isaiah 11:12
He will raise a signal to the nations and gather Israel’s lost ones; He will gather Judah’s scattered ones from the earth’s four wings.
  Matthew 24:31 — shared gathering from the four quarters and furthest reaches: Isaiah gathers Israel and Judah from the earth's four wings; Matthew describes angels gathering the chosen from four winds and the heavens' extremities. Matthew does not name Israel and Judah here.

Isaiah 11:13
Ephraim’s jealousy will be removed, and Judah’s enemies will perish. Ephraim will not envy Judah, and Judah will not afflict Ephraim.
  Ezekiel 37:19-22 — close reconciliation of Ephraim and Judah: Isaiah ends Ephraim's jealousy and Judah's affliction of Ephraim; Ezekiel joins Ephraim and Judah in one staff and promises they will no longer be two kingdoms.

Isaiah 11:14
They will fly in foreigners’ ships toward the sea; together they will plunder those from the sun’s rising and Idumea. Upon Moab they will first lay their hands, and Ammon’s sons will be the first to obey.
  Obadiah 1:18-21 — parallel united Israelite victory over Edom: After Judah and Ephraim's reconciliation, Isaiah depicts joint victory including Idumea; Obadiah likewise joins Jacob's and Joseph's houses against Esau and his mountain. The wider lists of territories differ.

Isaiah 11:15
The Lord will lay Egypt’s sea waste and bring His hand upon the river with a violent wind. He will strike seven ravines so that one may pass through it in sandals.
  Exodus 14:21-22 — close wind-driven opening of a passage across waters: Isaiah describes God's hand and violent wind opening passage through waters; Exodus records Moses stretching his hand and the Lord's violent south wind making the sea dry. Isaiah additionally names seven ravines.

Isaiah 11:16
There will be a passage for My remaining people in Egypt, and it will be for Israel as on the day it came out of Egypt’s land.
  Exodus 14:29-31 — explicit historical comparison with Israel's departure from Egypt: Isaiah names the day Israel left Egypt as the pattern for the remnant's passage; Exodus records Israel's dry crossing and rescue from the Egyptians.

Isaiah 12:1
On that day you will say, “I will bless Thee, Lord, because Thou wast angry with me, didst turn Thy wrath away and have mercy on me.
  Psalms 84:3-4 — close thanksgiving for divine wrath turned away: Isaiah thanks God because anger was turned away and mercy given; the psalm likewise recalls forgiven sins, stopped wrath and anger turned away.

Isaiah 12:2
Behold, my God, my Savior, is the Lord; I will trust in Him and be saved in Him, and I will not fear, because the Lord is my glory and my praise, and He became my salvation.”
  Psalms 117:14 — close confession that the Lord has become salvation: Isaiah calls the Lord his glory and praise and says He became his salvation; the psalm likewise calls Him strength and hymn and says He has become salvation.
  Psalms 26:1-3 — close freedom from fear through the Lord's saving protection: Isaiah trusts the saving Lord and refuses fear; the psalm likewise calls Him Savior and protector, asks whom to fear, and maintains hope even amid war.

Isaiah 12:3
You will draw water with gladness from salvation’s springs.
  John 4:10-14 — related saving water described as a spring: Isaiah promises joyful drawing from salvation's springs; Jesus promises water becoming a spring into eternal life within its recipient. The setting and manner of receiving the water differ.

Isaiah 12:4
On that day you will say, “Sing hymns to the Lord, cry out His name, announce His glorious deeds among the nations, remember that His name was exalted.
  Psalms 104:1-3 — close summons to call God's name and proclaim His works among nations: Both direct the hearers to invoke the Lord's name and announce His works among the nations, joining that announcement with song and praise of His name.

Isaiah 12:5
Sing the Lord’s name, because He has done exalted things; announce these throughout the earth.
  Psalms 97:1-3 — parallel song for divine wonders made known throughout the earth: Isaiah commands song because of God's exalted deeds and their worldwide announcement; the psalm likewise calls for song for His wonders and declares salvation known to the earth's ends.

Isaiah 12:6
Exult and be glad, you who dwell in Zion, because Israel’s Holy One has been exalted in her midst.”
  Zephaniah 3:14-17 — close Zion rejoicing because God is within her: Both summon Zion or Jerusalem to gladness and ground that joy in the presence of Israel's God within her.

Isaiah 13:1
The vision that Isaiah son of Amos saw against Babylon.
  Jeremiah 27:1-3 — parallel prophetic oracle against Babylon: Both introduce a divine oracle against Babylon and proceed to describe her capture and devastation. Isaiah names his vision; Jeremiah introduces the Lord's spoken word.

Isaiah 13:2
Raise a signal on a level mountain, lift your voice to them, do not fear, beckon with the hand: “Open, rulers!”
  Jeremiah 28:12 — close military summons to raise a signal: Isaiah's Babylon vision commands a signal on a level mountain; Jeremiah's Babylon oracle commands a signal on her walls alongside guards and weapons. The specified sites differ.

Isaiah 13:3
I command, and I lead them; they are consecrated, and I lead them. Giants are coming to fulfill My wrath, rejoicing and acting insolently together.
  Jeremiah 28:27-28 — close consecration of forces for judgment against Babylon: Isaiah describes God commanding and leading consecrated forces to fulfill His wrath; Jeremiah likewise commands consecrating nations against Babylon. Jeremiah names the Medes rather than Isaiah's giants.

Isaiah 13:4
A sound of many nations upon the mountains, like many nations; a sound of kings and gathered nations. The Lord Sabaoth has commanded an armed nation,
  Jeremiah 27:9 — close gathering of nations for the assault on Babylon: Isaiah describes sounds of kings and gathered nations and names the Lord's command to an armed nation; Jeremiah likewise has God rouse assemblies of nations to deploy against Babylon and capture her.

Isaiah 13:5
to come from a distant land, from the farthest edge of heaven’s foundation—the Lord and His armed fighters—to destroy the whole inhabited world.
  Jeremiah 27:41-42 — close armed force arriving from afar: Both describe an armed force from a distant extremity within an oracle against Babylon. Isaiah extends the devastation to the inhabited world; Jeremiah explicitly directs the force against daughter Babylon.

Isaiah 13:6
Wail, for the Lord’s day is near, and shattering will come from God.
  Joel 1:15 — close lament over the approaching day of the Lord: Both call for lament because the Lord's day is near. Isaiah describes shattering from God; Joel describes the day coming like suffering.

Isaiah 13:7
Therefore every hand will slacken, and every human soul grow cowardly;
  Jeremiah 27:43 — close disabled hands in terror of Babylon's fall: Isaiah says every hand will slacken and every soul grow cowardly; Jeremiah likewise describes Babylon's king hearing the invaders' report and his hands becoming disabled.

Isaiah 13:8
the envoys will be troubled, and labor pains will hold them like those of a woman giving birth. They will lament to one another and be distraught, and change their face as to flame.
  Jeremiah 27:43 — close birth-pain image for terror at Babylon's fall: Isaiah's troubled envoys are seized by labor pains like a woman giving birth; Jeremiah applies that same comparison to Babylon's distressed king. Isaiah additionally describes faces changing as to flame.

Isaiah 13:9
For behold, the Lord’s incurable day comes, of wrath and anger, to make the whole inhabited world deserted and destroy its sinners from it.
  Zephaniah 1:15-18 — close day of wrath bringing destruction upon sinners: Both describe the Lord's day as wrath and destruction, link the judgment with human sin, and extend its destructive scope across the earth.

Isaiah 13:10
For heaven’s stars, Orion and all heaven’s array will not give light; it will grow dark as the sun rises, and the moon will not give its light.
  Matthew 24:29 — close prophetic darkening of sun and moon: Both portray the sun and moon losing light alongside a disruption of the stars. Isaiah's stars cease shining, including Orion; Matthew's stars fall from heaven.

Isaiah 13:11
I will ordain evils for the whole inhabited world, and for the ungodly their sins; I will destroy the insolence of the lawless and lower the insolence of the proud.
  Jeremiah 27:31-32 — parallel divine judgment lowering insolence: Isaiah promises the destruction of lawless insolence and lowering of the proud; Jeremiah likewise addresses insolent Babylon and declares that her insolence will weaken and fall.

Isaiah 13:12
Those left will be more precious than unrefined gold, and a human being more precious than the stone from Souphir.
  Lamentations 4:2 — shared comparison of human worth with gold: Isaiah calls the remaining person more precious than gold; Lamentations calls Zion's sons precious and exalted in gold while lamenting their treatment as earthen vessels. The valuations move in different directions.

Isaiah 13:13
For heaven will be enraged, and the earth shaken from its foundations through the fury of the Lord Sabaoth’s anger, on the day when His wrath comes upon it.
  Jeremiah 28:29 — close earth shaking under divine judgment: Isaiah joins the earth's shaking from its foundations with the Lord's fury; Jeremiah likewise says the earth shook when the Lord's purpose rose against Babylon.

Isaiah 13:14
Those left will be like a fleeing little gazelle and a wandering sheep, with no one to gather them, so that each turns back to his own people and hastens to his own country.
  Jeremiah 27:16 — close flight to one's own people and country from Babylon: Both describe people turning or fleeing to their own people and land during Babylon's devastation. Isaiah adds fleeing-gazelle and wandering-sheep images; Jeremiah names the Greek sword.

Isaiah 13:15
Whoever is captured will be defeated, and those gathered together will fall by a sword.
  Jeremiah 28:3-4 — parallel fallen people in the assault on Babylon: Isaiah announces defeat of those captured and death by sword for those gathered; Jeremiah likewise describes wounded and pierced people falling in the Chaldeans' land during the assault.

Isaiah 13:16
They will dash their children before them, plunder their houses and take possession of their wives.
  Psalms 136:8-9 — close dashing of Babylon's children in retribution: Isaiah predicts children dashed before their parents in Babylon's judgment; the psalm addresses daughter Babylon and pronounces happy the one dashing her infants against a rock. Their speech forms differ.
  Jeremiah 6:11-12 — parallel seizure of homes and wives in national judgment: Isaiah also announces houses plundered and wives taken; Jeremiah likewise describes houses, fields and wives transferred to others. Jeremiah's surrounding warning concerns Jerusalem rather than Babylon.

Isaiah 13:17
Behold, I am rousing the Medes against you, who do not value silver or have need of gold.
  Jeremiah 28:11 — close divine rousing of the Medes against Babylon: Isaiah expressly announces God rousing the Medes; Jeremiah likewise says the Lord roused the Medes' king to destroy Babylon. Isaiah additionally denies their interest in silver or gold.

Isaiah 13:18
They will shatter young men’s arrows and certainly not pity your children; their eyes will not spare the children.
  Deuteronomy 28:49-50 — close invading force without mercy for the young: Isaiah denies the invading force's pity for children; Deuteronomy likewise warns of a distant invading nation without mercy for the young. Deuteronomy addresses Israel, while Isaiah's oracle concerns Babylon.

Isaiah 13:19
Babylon, which is called glorious by the king of the Chaldeans, will be as when God overturned Sodom and Gomorrah.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — explicit historical comparison with Sodom and Gomorrah: Isaiah names God's overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah as Babylon's pattern; Genesis records the fire and sulfur and the overthrow of those cities and their surrounding country.
  Jeremiah 27:40 — close parallel application of Sodom's overthrow to Babylon: Both Babylon oracles explicitly compare her devastation with God's overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah. Jeremiah further draws out the absence of human residents.

Isaiah 13:20
She will not be inhabited for everlasting time, nor will people ever enter her through many generations. Arabs will certainly not pass through her, and shepherds will certainly not rest in her.
  Jeremiah 27:39-40 — close promise that Babylon will remain uninhabited: Both deny future habitation in Babylon. Isaiah specifically excludes Arabs passing through and shepherds resting there; Jeremiah speaks generally of no human residing there.

Isaiah 13:21
Wild beasts will rest there, and the houses fill with sound; sirens will rest there, and demons dance there.
  Jeremiah 27:39 — close uncanny inhabitants of abandoned Babylon: Isaiah fills the deserted place with wild beasts, sirens and dancing demons; Jeremiah likewise mentions apparitions on the islands and daughters of sirens living in her, while denying human habitation. The inhabitants differ in detail.

Isaiah 13:22
Donkey-centaurs will dwell there, and hedgehogs make nests in their houses. It comes quickly and will not delay.
  Isaiah 34:11-15 — close ruined territory occupied by donkey-centaurs and nesting hedgehogs: Both name donkey-centaurs dwelling in a devastated place and hedgehogs nesting there. Isaiah 34's oracle concerns Idumea, while this oracle concerns Babylon.
  Habakkuk 2:3 — related assurance that an announced coming will not linger: Isaiah closes Babylon's desolation with an assurance of speedy arrival without delay; Habakkuk likewise assures that the awaited one will come and not linger. The announced subjects differ.

Isaiah 14:1
The Lord will pity Jacob and again choose Israel; they will rest upon their land, and the resident foreigner will be added to them and added to Jacob’s house,
  Zechariah 1:16-17 — close renewed mercy and choice of God's people or city: Isaiah promises renewed pity and choice of Jacob and Israel; Zechariah likewise promises renewed mercy upon Zion and renewed choice of Jerusalem.
  Ezekiel 47:22-23 — close inclusion of resident foreigners in Israel's restored land: Isaiah adds the resident foreigner to Jacob's house as Israel rests upon its land; Ezekiel likewise gives sojourners a share in the tribal inheritance alongside native Israelites.

Isaiah 14:2
and nations will take them and bring them into their place; they will inherit and multiply upon God’s land as male and female slaves. Those who took them captive will be captives, and those who ruled them will be ruled.
  Obadiah 1:17 — close reversal of possession and captivity: Isaiah promises that former captors will become captives and rulers be ruled; Obadiah similarly says Jacob's house will possess those who possessed them.

Isaiah 14:3
On that day God will give you rest from pain, your wrath and the harsh slavery in which you served them,
  Exodus 6:5-7 — parallel divine release from oppressive slavery: Isaiah promises rest from harsh slavery and pain; Exodus likewise has God hear Israel's enslaved groaning and promise rescue from Egyptian domination and oppression.

Isaiah 14:4
and you will take up this lament against Babylon’s king and say on that day, “How the one who made demands has rested, and the taskmaster rested!
  Habakkuk 2:5-8 — parallel lament or taunt directed against a conqueror: Isaiah introduces a lament over Babylon's king and the taskmaster's rest; Habakkuk introduces a parable and riddle against the man gathering nations, announcing that the plunderer will be plundered.

Isaiah 14:5
God shattered the sinners’ yoke, the rulers’ yoke;
  Jeremiah 37:8 — close divine shattering of an oppressive yoke: Isaiah says God shattered sinners' and rulers' yokes; Jeremiah likewise promises that God will smash the yoke and end service to foreigners.

Isaiah 14:6
having struck a nation in wrath with an incurable blow, striking a nation with a blow of anger that did not spare,
  Nahum 3:19 — Judgment imagery: a national blow without healing: Within the lament against Babylon's king, Isaiah describes a nation struck by an incurable, unsparing blow. Nahum describes crushing without healing and an inflamed wound, while hearers clap over the one whose evil continually reached others. The royal-judgment passages use bodily injury without healing to portray devastating violence; Isaiah's nation receiving the blow is not identified with Nahum's addressee, and the direction of the injury differs.

Isaiah 14:7
he rested confidently. The whole earth cries out with gladness,
  Nahum 3:19 — parallel rejoicing at an oppressor's downfall: Isaiah describes the whole earth crying with gladness as the taskmaster rests; Nahum describes those hearing the report clapping over the one whose evil had continually reached them.

Isaiah 14:8
and Lebanon’s trees rejoiced over you, and Lebanon’s cedar: ‘Since you have fallen asleep, the one cutting us has not come up.’
  Psalms 95:12-13 — shared personification of trees rejoicing at judgment: Isaiah's Lebanon trees rejoice because the cutter no longer comes after the king's fall; the psalm likewise portrays woodland trees exulting before the Lord as He comes to judge.

Isaiah 14:9
Hades below grew bitter on meeting you. All the giants who ruled the earth rose together for you, those who raised all the nations’ kings from their thrones.
  Ezekiel 32:21-22 — close underworld scene with giants addressing a fallen power: Isaiah depicts Hades receiving the fallen ruler among giants and kings; Ezekiel likewise gives giants speech from the pit and places Assyria's assembly among the dead there.

Isaiah 14:10
They will all answer and say to you, ‘You too were captured as we were, and were numbered among us.’
  Ezekiel 32:28-32 — close inclusion of a ruler among previously fallen powers: Isaiah's dead rulers number Babylon's ruler among themselves; Ezekiel likewise says Pharaoh too will lie among the wounded and describes rulers already there. In Ezekiel the prediction is spoken by God.

Isaiah 14:11
Your glory went down to Hades, your abundant gladness. They will spread decay beneath you, and your covering is a worm.
  Sirach 10:10-11 — close royal mortality ending among worms: Isaiah sends royal glory into Hades and describes decay and a worm as the king's bedding; Sirach likewise moves from a king dying to the dead person's inheritance of creeping things, beasts and worms.

Isaiah 14:12
How the dawn-bearer who rises in the morning fell from heaven! The one dispatching to all the nations was shattered to earth.
  Luke 10:18 — shared fall-from-heaven image with distinct referents: Isaiah's taunt, introduced against Babylon's king in 14:4, depicts the dawn-bearer falling from heaven; Jesus describes Satan falling from heaven like lightning. The image is compared without identifying the two figures.

Isaiah 14:13
You said in your mind, ‘I will ascend to heaven, set my throne above heaven’s stars, and sit on a high mountain, upon the high mountains toward the north.
  Obadiah 1:3-4 — close proud aspiration to heavenly height among the stars: Isaiah's speaker plans a throne above heaven's stars; Obadiah likewise rebukes a proud heart imagining a nest among the stars and promises descent to the ground.

Isaiah 14:14
I will ascend above the clouds; I will be like the Most High.’
  Ezekiel 28:2 — parallel ruler's aspiration or claim to divine status: Babylon's king aspires to be like the Most High; Tyre's ruler says he is a god and is reminded that he is human. The claims differ in wording and degree.

Isaiah 14:15
But now you will descend to Hades and to the earth’s foundations.
  Matthew 11:23 — close reversal from heavenly exaltation to Hades: Isaiah answers the king's intended ascent with descent to Hades; Jesus similarly contrasts Capernaum's prospective exaltation to heaven with descent to Hades. The addressees are different.

Isaiah 14:16
Those who see you will marvel at you and say, ‘Is this the person who provoked the earth, shook kings,
  Jeremiah 27:23 — parallel astonishment at Babylon's shattered world power: Isaiah's observers question the fallen person who provoked the earth and shook kings; Jeremiah likewise exclaims over Babylon, the shattered hammer of the whole earth.

Isaiah 14:17
who made the whole inhabited world deserted and pulled down the cities, who did not release those in captivity?’
  Jeremiah 27:33-34 — close refusal to release captives followed by divine intervention: Isaiah describes the ruler who would not release captives; Jeremiah likewise says Israel and Judah's captors refused to release them and names their strong Redeemer.

Isaiah 14:18
All the nations’ kings fell asleep in honor, each in his own house;
  Isaiah 14:19 — same-chapter contrast between honored royal death and an outcast corpse: The statement that kings fall asleep in honor, each in his own house, is directly contrasted in the following verse with this ruler thrown out among the dead.

Isaiah 14:19
but you will be thrown into the mountains like an abhorred dead man, with many dead pierced by swords, descending to Hades. As a garment stained with blood will not be clean,
  Jeremiah 22:18-19 — parallel dishonored disposal of a king's body: Isaiah's ruler is cast away as an abhorred corpse; Jeremiah similarly denies royal mourning to Jehoiakim and describes his body thrown beyond Jerusalem's gate with a donkey's burial. The kings and locations differ.

Isaiah 14:20
so neither will you be clean, because you destroyed My land and killed My people. You will certainly not endure for everlasting time, evil offspring.
  Psalms 36:28-29 — close destruction of ungodly offspring: Isaiah's concluding address denies enduring life to evil offspring; the psalm likewise declares that the ungodly's offspring will be destroyed, contrasting the righteous inheriting the land.

Isaiah 14:21
Prepare your children to be slaughtered because of your father’s sins, so that they do not rise, inherit the earth and fill the earth with cities.”
  Exodus 20:5-6 — related judgment extending ancestral sins to descendants: Isaiah directs preparing children for slaughter because of the addressee's father's sins; Exodus likewise speaks of fathers' sins repaid upon later generations, specifying those who hate God.
  Deuteronomy 24:16 — contrast with the legal prohibition of death for another generation's sin: Isaiah's oracle links the children's slaughter with the addressee's father's sins; Deuteronomy forbids sons dying for fathers and assigns death to each person's own sin. The oracle and legal settings differ.

Isaiah 14:22
“I will rise against them,” says the Lord Sabaoth, “and destroy their name, remnant and offspring”—thus says the Lord—
  Psalms 108:13-15 — close removal of offspring, name and remembrance: Isaiah announces destruction of name, remnant and offspring; the psalm likewise petitions for children destroyed, a name erased and remembrance removed from the earth. The psalm is a petition rather than this divine announcement.

Isaiah 14:23
“and make Babylonia deserted, so that hedgehogs dwell there; she will become nothing. I will make her a muddy abyss for destruction.”
  Zephaniah 2:13-15 — parallel ruined imperial capital inhabited by hedgehogs: Isaiah makes Babylonia deserted with hedgehogs and a muddy abyss; Zephaniah likewise places hedgehogs in ruined Nineveh. Nineveh is described as waterless rather than muddy.

Isaiah 14:24
Thus says the Lord Sabaoth: “As I said, so it will be, and as I counseled, so it will endure,
  Isaiah 46:10-11 — close certainty that God's spoken counsel will be accomplished: Both join God's speech and counsel with their certain accomplishment: what He has planned will stand, and what He has spoken He brings about.

Isaiah 14:25
to destroy the Assyrians from My land and from My mountains. They will become a trampling place; their yoke will be removed from them, and their glory removed from the shoulders.”
  Isaiah 37:33-38 — narrative counterpart of deliverance from Assyria: Isaiah promises Assyria's removal and the end of its yoke; the later narrative records Jerusalem's protection, the Assyrian army's destruction and the king's departure and death.

Isaiah 14:26
This is the counsel the Lord has counseled concerning the whole inhabited world, and this the high hand over all the world’s nations.
  Psalms 32:10-11 — parallel divine counsel prevailing over the nations: Isaiah presents the Lord's counsel and high hand over the world's nations; the psalm likewise contrasts scattered national counsels with the Lord's enduring counsel.

Isaiah 14:27
For what the holy God has counseled, who will scatter? And who will turn back the high hand?
  Isaiah 43:13 — close impossibility of reversing God's action: Isaiah asks who can turn back God's high hand; the later declaration likewise denies rescue from His hands and asks who can turn back what He does.

Isaiah 14:28
In the year King Achaz died, this utterance came:
  2 Kings 16:20 — historical record of the king's death used to date the oracle: Isaiah dates the utterance to King Achaz's death; Kings records Ahaz's death, burial and succession by Hezekiah. The king's name is rendered differently in the two passages.

Isaiah 14:29
“Do not rejoice, all you foreigners, because the yoke of the one striking you was shattered; for from snakes’ seed asp offspring will come, and their offspring will emerge as flying snakes.
  Proverbs 24:17-18 — parallel warning against rejoicing at an adversary's fall: Isaiah warns foreigners not to rejoice that their striker's yoke was broken, because dangerous offspring will follow; Proverbs likewise forbids rejoicing at an enemy's fall but gives a different reason.

Isaiah 14:30
The poor will be fed through him, and poor men will rest in peace; but he will destroy your seed with famine and destroy your remnant.
  Psalms 131:15 — close promise of food for the poor: Isaiah promises that the poor will be fed and rest in peace; the psalm likewise promises that Zion's poor will be satisfied with loaves. Isaiah also announces famine against the addressees' offspring.

Isaiah 14:31
Wail, gates of cities! Let troubled cities cry out, all the foreigners, because smoke comes from the north, and there is no surviving.
  Jeremiah 6:1-2 — parallel city warning of devastation approaching from the north: Isaiah summons cities to wail because smoke comes from the north; Jeremiah likewise warns Jerusalem and Benjamin of northern evils and great crushing. The addressed communities differ.

Isaiah 14:32
What will the nations’ kings answer? That the Lord founded Zion, and through Him the humble among the people will be saved.”
  Psalms 86:5 — close declaration that God founded Zion: Isaiah's answer affirms that the Lord founded Zion; the psalm likewise explicitly says that the Most High Himself founded her. Isaiah additionally promises salvation for the humble.

Isaiah 15:1
The utterance against Moab’s country. In a night Moab’s country will perish, for in a night the wall of Moab’s country will perish.
  Jeremiah 31:1-4 — parallel oracle of Moab's collapse: Isaiah announces Moab's country and wall perishing in a night; Jeremiah likewise announces the loss of Moab's cities and Moab's shattering, without that overnight timing.

Isaiah 15:2
Grieve over yourselves, for Debon too will perish. Where your altar is, there you will go up to weep. Over Nabau of Moab’s country, wail: baldness upon every head, every arm cut.
  Jeremiah 31:18 — close announcement of Dibon's devastation: Isaiah says Debon too will perish; Jeremiah addresses resident Dibon with descent from glory, crushing and ruined fortification. Both locate this within Moab's judgment.
  Jeremiah 31:37 — close bodily mourning throughout Moab: Both describe baldness or shaving upon every head during Moab's mourning. Isaiah additionally names cut arms; Jeremiah names shaved beards, beating hands and sackcloth.

Isaiah 15:3
In her squares put on sackcloth and beat yourselves in mourning; upon her roofs and in her lanes all of you wail with weeping.
  Jeremiah 31:37-38 — close mourning upon Moab's rooftops and squares: Isaiah places sackcloth and lament in squares, roofs and lanes; Jeremiah likewise combines sackcloth and beating hands with Moab's rooftops and squares.

Isaiah 15:4
For Hesebon and Eleale have cried out; their voice was heard as far as Iassa. Therefore Moab’s loins cry out; her soul will know.
  Jeremiah 31:34 — close cries from Heshbon and Elealeh: Both Moab oracles place cries at the cities rendered Hesebon/Heshbon and Eleale/Elealeh. Isaiah also says the voice reaches Iassa, which this Jeremiah unit does not name.

Isaiah 15:5
Moab’s heart cries within her as far as Segor, for she is a three-year-old heifer. On Louith’s ascent they will go up toward you weeping; along Aroniim’s road destruction and shaking cry out.
  Jeremiah 31:5 — close Moabite road imagery of weeping and shattering: Both describe upward movement with weeping and a road marked by a cry of destruction. Isaiah names Louith and Aroniim; Jeremiah names Alaoth and Horonaim in its comparable description.

Isaiah 15:6
Nemrim’s water will be deserted, and her grass cease; for there will be no green grass.
  Jeremiah 31:34 — related devastation of named Moabite waters: Isaiah names Nemrim's deserted water and vanished grass; Jeremiah names Nebrim's water becoming a burning. Both set these waters within Moab's devastation, with different stated effects.

Isaiah 15:8
For the cry has reached Moab’s border at Agallim, and her wailing extends to Ailim’s well.
  Isaiah 15:2-5 — same lament: Within the same lament over Moab, the cry of Moab is described as reaching named places: Hesebon and Eleale cry out and their voice was heard as far as Iassa, and Moab's heart cries as far as Segor. Isaiah 15:8 continues this image with the cry reaching Moab's border at Agallim and the wailing extending to Ailim's well.

Isaiah 15:9
Remmon’s water will fill with blood; for I will bring Arabs upon Remmon and remove Moab’s offspring, Ariel and Adama’s remainder.
  2 Kings 3:22-24 — contrast between predicted blood-filled water and water mistaken for blood: Isaiah announces Remmon's water filled with blood; Kings describes Moab seeing fiery-red water like blood and wrongly concluding that the opposing kings killed one another. The events are not identified.

Isaiah 16:2
For you will be like a fledgling removed from a bird taking flight, Daughter Moab. Then, Arnon,
  Jeremiah 31:28 — related bird imagery for Moab's displacement: Isaiah compares daughter Moab with a fledgling removed from a flying bird; Jeremiah describes Moab's people leaving cities and becoming like doves nesting among rocks. The bird situations differ.

Isaiah 16:3
take further counsel. Make a shelter of mourning for her continually. In midday darkness they flee; they were distraught. Do not be carried away.
  Amos 8:9-10 — shared conjunction of midday darkness and mourning: Isaiah places flight in midday darkness beside the command for a shelter of mourning; Amos likewise joins daylight darkening at midday with mourning and lament. Amos expressly attributes the darkening to God.

Isaiah 16:5
A throne will be set right with mercy, and one will sit upon it with truth in David’s tent, judging, seeking judgment and hastening righteousness.
  Jeremiah 23:5-6 — close righteous Davidic rule: Isaiah promises a throne in David's tent with a ruler seeking judgment and righteousness; Jeremiah likewise promises a righteous rising for David and a king performing judgment and righteousness.
  Psalms 88:15 — parallel throne characterized by mercy, truth and righteous judgment: Isaiah describes a Davidic throne set right with mercy and a ruler acting truthfully in judgment; the psalm attributes righteousness, judgment, mercy and truth to God's throne and presence. The throne's occupant differs.

Isaiah 16:6
We heard Moab’s insolence—exceedingly insolent. You raised up your arrogance. Your divination is not so,
  Jeremiah 31:29-30 — close report of Moab's excessive insolence: Both report hearing Moab's great insolence and rebuke his arrogance. Jeremiah adds the lifted heart and God's knowledge of his works; Isaiah additionally rebukes his divination.

Isaiah 16:7
not so. Moab will wail, for all in Moab’s country will wail. You will ponder for Deseth’s residents and not be ashamed.
  Jeremiah 31:31 — close countrywide Moabite wailing: Isaiah says all Moab's country will wail; Jeremiah likewise summons wailing over Moab from every side. Their added statements about particular residents differ.

Isaiah 16:8
Hesebon’s plains will mourn, and Sebama’s vine. You who devour the nations, trample her vineyards as far as Iazer; you will certainly not reach them. Wander in the wilderness! Those sent out were abandoned, for they crossed the wilderness.
  Jeremiah 31:32 — close lament concerning the vine and Iazer or Jazer: Isaiah names Sebama's vine, Iazer and threatened vineyards; Jeremiah names Sibmah's vine, Jazer and destruction of its fruit. Isaiah's wilderness crossing and Jeremiah's branches crossing the sea remain distinct.

Isaiah 16:9
Therefore I will weep like Iazer’s weeping for Sebama’s vine. He felled your trees, Hesebon and Eleale, because I will trample upon your grain harvest and vintage, and all will fall.
  Jeremiah 31:32 — close first-person weeping for the vine and its harvest: Both speakers compare their weeping for Sebama or Sibmah's vine with weeping for Iazer or Jazer, joining it with the loss of harvest produce.

Isaiah 16:10
Gladness and exultation will be removed from your vineyards; in your vineyards they will certainly not rejoice or tread wine into the vats, for it has ceased.
  Jeremiah 31:33 — close loss of Moab's harvest joy and grape treading: Both remove joy from Moab's vineyards or land and describe the cessation of grape treading and its celebration. Jeremiah still mentions wine upon the winepresses.

Isaiah 16:11
Therefore my belly will sound like a lyre for Moab, and my inward parts like a wall that you renewed.
  Jeremiah 31:36 — close inward grief for Moab compared with musical instruments: Isaiah's belly sounds like a lyre for Moab; Jeremiah's heart hums like flutes for Moab. The organ and instrument differ, while the unusual comparison is shared.

Isaiah 16:12
It will turn to your shame, because Moab labored at the altars and will enter her handmade objects to pray; it will certainly not be able to rescue him.
  Jeremiah 31:35 — close failure of Moab's idolatrous worship to avert destruction: Isaiah says Moab's altar labor and prayer to handmade objects cannot rescue; Jeremiah likewise announces Moab's destruction as he ascends an altar and burns incense to his gods.

Isaiah 16:13
This is the utterance the Lord spoke against Moab, whenever He spoke it.
  Jeremiah 31:1 — parallel identification of a divine utterance against Moab: Isaiah identifies the preceding utterance as the Lord's word against Moab; Jeremiah likewise introduces its Moab oracle as what the Lord said.

Isaiah 16:14
Now I say, “In three years, years of a hired worker, Moab’s glory will be dishonored with all its abundant wealth, and a very small, unhonored remnant will be left.”
  Isaiah 21:16-17 — close fixed term measured by a hired worker's years: Both measure glory's collapse by a hired worker's years: three for Moab, one for Kedar. Isaiah 16 describes a small remnant; Isaiah 21 specifies few remaining arrows.

Isaiah 17:1
The utterance against Damascus. Behold, Damascus will be removed from the cities and become a ruin,
  2 Kings 16:9 — historical judgment parallel: Isaiah announces Damascus's removal as a city and its ruin. Kings records the Assyrian capture and deportation of Damascus and the killing of its king, supplying a concrete narrative counterpart to the city's downfall.

Isaiah 17:2
abandoned for everlasting time, a bed and resting place for flocks, with no one pursuing.
  Zephaniah 2:13-15 — shared desolation image: Isaiah pictures the abandoned city as a resting place for flocks. Zephaniah similarly turns Nineveh from a confident city into a wasteland where flocks and wild animals dwell; the cities differ, but their urban desolation becomes animal pasture in both descriptions.

Isaiah 17:3
There will no longer be a stronghold for Ephraim to flee to, nor a kingdom in Damascus, and the Syrians’ remainder will perish; for you are no better than Israel’s sons and their glory. Thus says the Lord Sabaoth.
  2 Kings 16:9 — historical judgment parallel: Isaiah says Damascus will lose its kingdom and the Syrian remainder will perish. Kings records Damascus's capture and the death of Raasson, matching the removal of the city's royal power without supplying every detail of Isaiah's oracle.
  2 Kings 17:5-6 — historical judgment parallel: Isaiah includes the loss of Ephraim's defensive refuge alongside Damascus's downfall. Kings describes Samaria's siege, capture and Israel's deportation, a narrative counterpart concerning the northern kingdom's lost security.

Isaiah 17:4
On that day Jacob’s glory will fail, and the fat parts of his glory will be shaken.
  Hosea 4:7 — judgment theme: Isaiah announces the failure of Jacob's glory and the shaking of its abundance. Hosea likewise answers the people's increased sin by turning their glory into dishonor; both describe the reversal of Israel's former distinction.

Isaiah 17:5
It will be as when someone gathers a standing harvest and reaps seed from the ears in his arm, as when someone gathers an ear in a firm ravine,
  Isaiah 24:13 — literary harvest parallel: The stripping of a standing harvest in Isaiah 17:5 introduces the small remainder described in the following verse. Isaiah 24:13 likewise pictures judgment among the nations through olive gleaning and the end of grape harvest, using agricultural removal to portray diminished populations.

Isaiah 17:6
and a stalk is left in it, or like olive berries, two or three on a lofty top, or four or five left on their branches. Thus says the Lord, Israel’s God.
  Isaiah 24:13 — literary remnant image: Isaiah 17 specifies the few olive berries left on high branches after gathering. Isaiah 24 again uses the gleaning of an olive tree and a finished grape harvest for what remains among the nations; both passages make the remnant visible through the same harvesting image.

Isaiah 17:7
On that day a person will trust in the One who made him, and his eyes will look toward Israel’s Holy One,
  Isaiah 31:1-3 — contrast in trust: Isaiah 17 anticipates people looking to their Maker and Israel's Holy One. Isaiah 31 condemns those who trust Egypt's horses and chariots instead of that same Holy One, contrasting the restored allegiance with the misplaced reliance.

Isaiah 17:8
and they will certainly not trust the altars or their hands’ works, which their fingers made, nor look at their trees or abominations.
  Isaiah 2:8 — literary reversal: Isaiah 17 anticipates abandoning trust in altars and objects made by hands and fingers. Isaiah 2 describes the earlier condition in which the land was filled with handmade abominations and people bowed before what their fingers made.
  Isaiah 27:9 — removal of idolatry: Isaiah 17 describes people ceasing to regard handmade altars, trees and abominations as objects of trust. Isaiah 27 connects Jacob's sin being removed with altar stones reduced to dust and trees and idols cut down, giving a corresponding picture of idolatry's removal.

Isaiah 17:9
On that day your cities will be abandoned as the Amorites and Hivites abandoned theirs before Israel’s sons, and they will be deserted,
  Joshua 24:11-13 — historical recollection: Isaiah expressly compares the coming abandonment of cities with the Amorites and Hivites yielding before Israel. Joshua names those peoples among Israel's opponents and recalls God delivering their cities and land to Israel.

Isaiah 17:10
because you abandoned God your Savior and did not remember the Lord your Helper. Therefore you will plant a faithless planting and faithless seed;
  Deuteronomy 32:15-18 — covenant indictment parallel: Isaiah explains the disaster by the people abandoning God their Savior and forgetting their Helper. Deuteronomy's song likewise says Jacob forsook God his Maker and Savior and forgot the God who nourished him.

Isaiah 17:12
Woe, the multitude of many nations! Like a surging sea, so you will be troubled, and the back of many nations will roar like water.
  Psalms 64:8 — shared sea-and-nations image: Isaiah joins the roar and disturbance of many nations with surging water. The Psalm also places troubled nations beside the sea's hollow and the sound of its waves; the connection is this shared juxtaposition, without claiming that either text describes the same event.

Isaiah 17:13
Many nations are like abundant water, like much water violently rushing down. He will drive him away and pursue him far off, like chaff-dust when people winnow before wind, and like wheel-dust carried by a squall.
  Psalms 82:14-16 — shared judgment image: Isaiah's nations are pursued like windblown chaff and wheel-dust carried by a squall. The Psalm asks God to make hostile rulers like a wheel and straw before wind and pursue them with His storm, closely matching the combined images of pursuit and wind-driven debris.

Isaiah 17:14
Toward evening there will be mourning; before morning it will be gone. This is the portion of those who plundered you, an inheritance for those who took possession of you.
  2 Kings 19:35-36 — narrative illustration: Isaiah places mourning toward evening and its disappearance before morning within a pronouncement concerning plunderers. Kings records the Assyrian army being struck at night and its dead discovered early in the morning, illustrating a sudden overnight reversal without identifying it as the oracle's sole possible referent.

Isaiah 18:1
Woe, wings of a land of ships, beyond Ethiopia’s rivers,
  Zephaniah 3:10 — verbal echo: Both texts use the distinctive geographic phrase 'Ethiopia's rivers' for the distant southern region: Isaiah speaks of a land 'beyond Ethiopia's rivers', and Zephaniah of 'the far reaches of Ethiopia's rivers', from which sacrifices will be brought.

Isaiah 18:2
the one sending hostages by sea and papyrus letters over the water! For swift messengers will go to a lofty nation and a foreign and harsh people—who is beyond it?—a hopeless and trampled nation. Now the land’s rivers,
  Isaiah 18:7 — same people: Later in the same oracle the same distant nation is described again with the paired terms 'trampled nation' and a reference to its land's river. Isaiah 18:2 calls it 'a hopeless and trampled nation' while 18:7 has 'a hoping and trampled nation', a notable difference in the first adjective.

Isaiah 18:3
all are like an inhabited country; their country will be inhabited as when a signal is raised from a mountain, and it will be audible like a trumpet’s voice.
  Isaiah 11:12 — shared signal image: Isaiah 18 describes a mountain signal and an audible trumpet in its picture of an inhabited land. Isaiah 11 raises a signal to the nations to gather dispersed Israel and Judah; both use a conspicuous public signal within a prophecy concerning peoples and their land.

Isaiah 18:4
For thus the Lord said to me, “There will be security in My city, like the light of midday heat, and it will be like a dew cloud on a harvest day.”
  Isaiah 4:5-6 — protective weather imagery: Isaiah 18 associates security in God's city with midday light and a dew cloud at harvest. Isaiah 4 portrays Zion shaded by cloud and sheltered from heat and harsh weather, linking the city's divine protection with atmospheric imagery.

Isaiah 18:5
Before harvest, when the bloom is complete and the unripe grape blossoms as its flower forms unripe fruit, He will remove the small grape clusters with sickles, remove the vine branches and cut them down,
  Joel 4:13 — vineyard judgment imagery: Isaiah pictures sickles cutting off grape growth before the harvest ripens. Joel commands sickles at the vintage as the nations' evils multiply. Both use cutting and gathering a vineyard for judgment, while the premature removal in Isaiah differs from Joel's full winepress.

Isaiah 18:6
and leave them together for heaven’s birds and the earth’s wild beasts. Heaven’s birds will gather upon them, and all the earth’s wild beasts will come upon him.
  Jeremiah 7:33 — judgment aftermath parallel: Isaiah leaves the cut-down subject to heaven's birds and the earth's wild beasts. Jeremiah explicitly gives the people's dead as food to those same two groups, presenting a corresponding image of exposed remains after judgment.

Isaiah 18:7
At that time gifts will be brought to the Lord Sabaoth from a people afflicted and plucked, and from a great people from now on and for everlasting time—a hoping and trampled nation, which is in a part of its country’s river—to the place where the Lord Sabaoth’s name was called, Mount Zion.
  Zephaniah 3:10 — prophetic worship parallel: Isaiah anticipates gifts reaching the Lord at Zion from the people associated with Ethiopia's rivers. Zephaniah likewise anticipates sacrifices brought to God from the far reaches of those rivers, joining the same distant region with worship offered to Him.
  Psalms 67:30-32 — prophetic worship parallel: Isaiah brings distant people's gifts to the place of the Lord's name on Mount Zion. The Psalm links gifts at God's Jerusalem sanctuary with envoys from Egypt and Ethiopia hastening its hand toward God, placing foreign approach and worship in the same setting.

Isaiah 19:1
A vision of Egypt. Behold, the Lord sits upon a light cloud and will come into Egypt; Egypt’s handmade objects will be shaken before Him, and their heart overcome within them.
  Exodus 12:12 — judgment on Egypt's gods: Isaiah's vision has the Lord enter Egypt and its handmade objects shake before Him. Exodus records the Lord passing through Egypt and executing vengeance against its gods; both portray His intervention as exposing the impotence of Egypt's objects of worship.
  Psalms 17:10-12 — divine advent imagery: Isaiah depicts the Lord coming to Egypt upon a light cloud. The Psalm depicts God's descent and flight with darkness and cloud around Him. The shared connection is heavenly weather surrounding His powerful arrival, not identical vehicles or destinations.

Isaiah 19:2
Egyptians will be roused against Egyptians; each will fight his brother and each his neighbor, city against city and district against district.
  Isaiah 9:19-20 — civil-strife parallel: Isaiah 19 sets Egyptians against brothers, neighbors, cities and districts within Egypt. Isaiah 9 pictures Manasseh and Ephraim consuming one another even while joining against Judah; both portray internal conflict as part of a nation's judgment.

Isaiah 19:3
The Egyptians’ spirit will be troubled within them, and I will scatter their counsel. They will inquire of their gods, their statues, those speaking from the earth and the belly-speakers.
  Isaiah 8:19 — divination parallel: The troubled Egyptians turn to statues, speakers from the earth and belly-speakers. Isaiah 8 names the same kinds of speakers and contrasts consulting them with a nation's turning to its God, clarifying the misplaced religious recourse.

Isaiah 19:4
I will hand Egypt into the hands of harsh human masters, and harsh kings will rule them. Thus says the Lord Sabaoth.
  Ezekiel 29:19-20 — prophetic judgment parallel: Isaiah announces Egypt's delivery into harsh human rule without naming the masters. Ezekiel explicitly gives Egypt and its wealth to Nebuchadnezzar as his army's wages; both present foreign domination as divinely permitted judgment, without equating all of Isaiah's unnamed rulers with one king.

Isaiah 19:5
The Egyptians will drink water beside the sea, but the river will fail and dry up.
  Ezekiel 30:12 — prophetic judgment parallel: Isaiah announces the Egyptian river's failure and drying. Ezekiel's Egypt oracle likewise makes the rivers desolate as the country is destroyed, connecting the judgment of Egypt with the collapse of its river system.

Isaiah 19:6
Rivers and the river’s canals will fail, and every gathering of water dry up, in every marsh of reed and papyrus,
  Ezekiel 30:12 — prophetic judgment parallel: Isaiah extends the water failure to rivers, canals and marshes. Ezekiel likewise includes Egypt's desolate rivers in the destruction of the land and what fills it; the comparison concerns the damaged water system, not a claim that Ezekiel names every plant in Isaiah.

Isaiah 19:7
and all the green marsh growth around the river and everything sown by the river will dry up, destroyed by wind.
  Joel 1:10-12 — crop-devastation parallel: Isaiah's riverbank growth and sown crops dry up and perish. Joel likewise describes failed grain, a dried vine and dried field trees, connecting judgment with the disappearance of agricultural abundance.

Isaiah 19:8
Fishermen will groan, and all casting a hook into the river will groan; those casting seines and those using casting nets will mourn.
  Ezekiel 47:9-10 — contrast in river livelihood: Isaiah's failing river leaves hook-and-net fishers mourning. Ezekiel's healing river produces abundant fish and places for fishers and their nets, providing a specific contrast between devastated and restored fishing livelihoods.

Isaiah 19:9
Shame will seize those working split flax and those working fine linen,
  Ezekiel 27:7 — economic context: Isaiah names the shame of Egypt's flax and fine-linen workers. Ezekiel identifies embroidered fine linen from Egypt among Tyre's valuable furnishings, showing the particular Egyptian textile trade whose workers Isaiah places under judgment.

Isaiah 19:11
Tanis’s rulers will be fools; the king’s wise counselors—their counsel will become foolish. How will you say to the king, “We are sons of the discerning, sons of kings from the beginning”?
  Isaiah 29:14 — wisdom brought to nothing: Isaiah 19 calls Tanis's rulers fools and exposes the king's wise counselors' foolish counsel. Isaiah 29 similarly announces the destruction of the wise people's wisdom and concealment of the discerning people's understanding under divine judgment.

Isaiah 19:12
Where are your wise men now? Let them announce to you and say what the Lord Sabaoth has counseled against Egypt.
  Isaiah 44:25 — limits of human counsel: Egypt's wise men are challenged to announce what the Lord has counseled against their nation. Isaiah 44 describes God turning the discerning backward and making their counsel foolish, likewise exposing the limits of human wisdom before the Lord's own counsel.

Isaiah 19:13
Tanis’s rulers failed, and Memphis’s rulers were exalted; they will lead Egypt astray tribe by tribe.
  Ezekiel 30:13-14 — parallel Egypt oracle: Isaiah names the failure of Tanis's rulers and the misguidance associated with Memphis's rulers. Ezekiel's Egypt oracle likewise targets Memphis's magnates and announces fire upon Tanis, connecting the same Egyptian centers with judgment on their power.

Isaiah 19:14
For the Lord mixed for them a spirit of wandering; they led Egypt astray in all their works, as someone wanders while drunk and vomiting.
  Isaiah 28:7 — misguided leadership imagery: Isaiah 19 likens Egypt's confused guidance to a drunken person's wandering. Isaiah 28 describes priest and prophet wandering and losing their senses through wine and sikera; both use intoxication to depict those who should guide others becoming unreliable.

Isaiah 19:15
There will be no work for the Egyptians that will produce a head and tail, a beginning and end.
  Isaiah 9:13-15 — head-and-tail judgment image: Isaiah 19 denies Egypt productive work from head to tail and beginning to end.
