Isaiah 9 also uses head and tail for a judgment extending through the people's leadership, identifying elders and false prophets; the repeated image expresses comprehensive failure rather than a literal anatomical action.

Isaiah 19:16
On that day the Egyptians will be like women in fear and trembling before the Lord Sabaoth’s hand, which He Himself will bring upon them.
  Nahum 3:13 — shared defeat image: Isaiah describes Egypt trembling before the Lord's hand through the ancient battle image of being like women. Nahum uses the same image for Nineveh's people as its gates open to enemies. The connection records the two texts' portrayal of military vulnerability.

Isaiah 19:17
The Judeans’ country will become a terror to the Egyptians; whenever anyone names her to them, they will fear because of the counsel the Lord has counseled against her.
  Exodus 15:14-16 — fear among the nations: Isaiah says the naming of Judah will terrify Egypt because of the Lord's counsel. The Exodus song likewise describes other nations hearing and trembling before the strength of God's arm as His people approach; both connect foreign fear with divine action or counsel, though the nations and occasions differ.

Isaiah 19:18
On that day five cities in Egypt will speak the Canaanite language and swear by the Lord’s name. One city will be called City-Asedek.
  Zephaniah 3:9 — language and allegiance parallel: Isaiah's Egyptian cities speak the Canaanite language and swear by the Lord's name. Zephaniah joins changed speech among peoples with calling upon that name and serving Him, linking a linguistic change with renewed religious allegiance without identifying City-Asedek.

Isaiah 19:19
On that day there will be an altar to the Lord in the Egyptians’ country and a pillar to the Lord by its border,
  Joshua 22:26-29 — boundary witness parallel: Isaiah places an altar and a border pillar for the Lord within Egypt, called a sign in the following verse. Joshua's border dispute is answered by an altar serving as a witness to shared belonging to the Lord. Joshua expressly excludes sacrificial use for that witness altar, so the comparison concerns public testimony rather than identical ritual rules.

Isaiah 19:20
and it will be an everlasting sign to the Lord in Egypt’s country, because they will cry to the Lord on account of their oppressors. The Lord will send them a man who will save them; judging, he will save them.
  Judges 3:9-10 — deliverer pattern: Egypt cries to the Lord because of oppressors, and He sends a man who saves by judging. Judges likewise has Israel cry out and receive Othniel as a savior who judges and delivers, making Egypt's promised rescue resemble Israel's earlier deliverance pattern.

Isaiah 19:21
The Lord will be known to the Egyptians, and the Egyptians will know the Lord on that day. They will make sacrifices, vow vows to the Lord and pay them.
  Zechariah 14:16-19 — worship among the nations: Isaiah anticipates Egyptians knowing the Lord and offering worship to Him. Zechariah includes Egypt among the nations accountable to come and worship the Lord at Jerusalem, with a warning for refusal; both expressly place Egypt within the sphere of worship of Israel's God.

Isaiah 19:22
The Lord will strike the Egyptians with a great blow and heal them with healing. They will turn back to the Lord, and He will listen to them and heal them.
  Hosea 6:1-3 — striking and healing parallel: Isaiah joins God's striking Egypt with healing, Egypt's return and His answering them. Hosea likewise calls for return to the Lord because the One who strikes will heal and restore, sharing the sequence of discipline, return and restoration.

Isaiah 19:23
On that day there will be a road from Egypt toward the Assyrians. Assyrians will enter Egypt, and Egyptians go toward the Assyrians, and the Egyptians will serve the Assyrians.
  Isaiah 11:16 — passage and restoration parallel: Isaiah 19 opens a road between Egypt and Assyria. Isaiah 11 promises a passage for God's remaining people in Egypt, compared with the exodus. Both visions associate Egypt with renewed movement through a divinely announced passage; Isaiah 11:16 does not itself name the Assyrian end of the road.

Isaiah 19:24
On that day Israel will be third among the Assyrians and Egyptians, blessed upon the earth,
  Genesis 12:3 — blessing beyond Israel: Isaiah places Israel alongside Egypt and Assyria in blessing upon the earth. God's promise to Abram similarly extends blessing beyond his own household to all the earth's tribes, supplying a broader covenantal setting for the blessing of these named foreign peoples.

Isaiah 19:25
which the Lord Sabaoth blessed, saying, “Blessed be My people in Egypt and among the Assyrians, and My inheritance Israel.”
  Romans 9:24-26 — divine people-language: Isaiah calls people in Egypt and among the Assyrians God's people while retaining Israel as His inheritance. Romans discusses calling both Jews and Gentiles and cites the declaration that former outsiders become My people. The comparison concerns God's people-language in these differing settings: Romans explicitly names Jews and Gentiles, whereas Isaiah names people in Egypt and among the Assyrians.

Isaiah 20:2
then the Lord spoke to Isaiah, saying, “Go, remove the sackcloth from your waist and untie the sandals on your feet.” He did so, going naked and without sandals.
  Ezekiel 4:1-3 — prophetic sign act: Isaiah obeys a command to remove his sackcloth and sandals as part of a warning about captivity. Ezekiel is commanded to depict and enact a city's siege as a sign for Israel. In both, a commanded visible act conveys a coming national disaster; the actions and recipients differ.

Isaiah 20:3
The Lord said, “As Isaiah My servant has gone naked and without sandals for three years, there will be signs and wonders for the Egyptians and Ethiopians;
  Ezekiel 24:24 — prophet as portent: God explicitly makes Isaiah's unusual appearance a sign for Egypt and Ethiopia. Ezekiel is likewise called a portent whose conduct anticipates what the people will experience, sharing the function of a prophet's lived action as warning.

Isaiah 20:4
for thus the king of the Assyrians will lead Egypt’s and the Ethiopians’ captivity, young men and elders, naked and without sandals, exposed as Egypt’s shame.
  Isaiah 47:2-3 — captivity and exposure imagery: Isaiah 20 portrays Egypt's and Ethiopia's captives led away exposed and without sandals. Isaiah 47 orders Babylon to uncover herself and announces visible shame, using bodily exposure for the humiliation of another powerful nation under judgment.

Isaiah 20:5
The Egyptians will be ashamed in defeat because of the Ethiopians, in whom the Egyptians had trusted, for they were their glory.
  Isaiah 30:3-5 — failed foreign reliance: Egypt is ashamed when Ethiopia, its trusted glory, fails. Isaiah 30 likewise says reliance on Egypt will yield shame instead of assistance. The countries occupy different positions in the two warnings, but both expose the disgrace of a foreign protector that cannot deliver.

Isaiah 20:6
Those dwelling on this island will say, ‘Behold, we had trusted that we could flee to them for help, but they could not be saved from the king of the Assyrians. How, then, shall we be saved?’”
  Isaiah 31:1-3 — failed foreign reliance: Isaiah 20's island residents see that their hoped-for refuge cannot escape Assyria and question their own safety. Isaiah 31 condemns dependence on Egyptian military help and says helpers and helped will fail together, explaining the vulnerability of such borrowed security.

Isaiah 21:1
The vision of the wilderness. As a squall might pass through a wilderness, coming from a wilderness, from a land—a fearsome
  Jeremiah 4:13 — approaching-war imagery: Isaiah introduces a frightening vision through a squall coming across the wilderness. Jeremiah likewise describes an approaching enemy and his chariots as cloud and storm, sharing the imagery of a destructive force sweeping in upon its hearers.

Isaiah 21:2
and harsh vision was announced to me. The faithless person deals faithlessly, and the lawless person acts lawlessly. The Elamites are against me, and Persian envoys come against me. Now I will groan and comfort myself.
  Daniel 5:30 — historical oracle parallel: Isaiah's harsh vision names Elamites and Persian envoys before announcing Babylon's fall in verse 9. Daniel records the Chaldean kingship passing to the Medes and Persians. The connection is the Persian presence within the downfall of Babylon, not an identification of every figure in Isaiah's vision.

Isaiah 21:3
Therefore my loins filled with weakness, and labor pains seized me like a woman giving birth. I did wrong by not hearing; I strove not to see.
  Isaiah 13:8 — shared birth-pain image: Isaiah 21 describes the seer's weakness and labor pains like a woman giving birth. Isaiah 13 uses the same birth-pain comparison for those troubled by the Lord's approaching judgment, portraying overwhelming distress through the same bodily image.

Isaiah 21:4
My heart wanders, and lawlessness submerges me; my soul stands in fear.
  Jeremiah 4:19-21 — prophetic anguish parallel: Isaiah's heart wanders and his soul stands in fear within the harsh vision. Jeremiah likewise describes his raging soul and torn heart as he hears war and the land's crushing, giving a corresponding account of a prophet's inward anguish over announced disaster.

Isaiah 21:5
Prepare the table; drink, eat! Rise, rulers; prepare shields!
  Daniel 5:1-4 — narrative setting parallel: Isaiah's Babylon vision places eating and drinking beside a summons to rulers to prepare shields. Daniel's account likewise begins with Babylon's king and companions banqueting before the kingdom's loss. This connects the settings of feasting under impending overthrow without asserting that Isaiah identifies Daniel's particular banquet.

Isaiah 21:6
For thus the Lord said to me: “Go, station a watcher for yourself, and report whatever you see.
  Habakkuk 2:1-3 — prophetic watch parallel: Isaiah is told to station a watcher and report what is seen. Habakkuk likewise takes a watch position to receive God's response and is told to write the vision clearly, joining vigilance with the communication of revelation.

Isaiah 21:7
I saw mounted riders, two horsemen, a donkey rider and a camel rider. Listen with great attention,
  Isaiah 21:9 — same-chapter literary recurrence: The watcher's sight of mounted riders and two horsemen is followed by the appearance of a rider with a two-horse team and the announcement of Babylon's fall. The repeated mounted figure connects the vision's observed sign with its reported message.

Isaiah 21:8
and call Uriah to the Lord’s watchtower.” He said, “I stood continually by day, and at the camp I stood all night,
  Habakkuk 2:1-3 — prophetic watch parallel: Isaiah's watcher describes remaining at his post by day and through the night. Habakkuk similarly stands at his watch and is instructed to wait for the vision's appointed arrival, sharing persistent attentiveness rather than immediate visible fulfillment.

Isaiah 21:9
and behold, he himself comes, a rider of a two-horse team.” He answered and said, “Babylon has fallen, and all her statues and handmade objects were shattered to the ground.”
  Jeremiah 28:8 — Babylon-fall proclamation: Isaiah's report announces that Babylon has fallen and its handmade objects have been shattered. Jeremiah likewise proclaims that Babylon suddenly fell and was shattered. The retained connection is the explicit announcement of the same city's collapse; Jeremiah's verse does not repeat Isaiah's detail about statues.

Isaiah 21:10
Hear, you who remain and suffer; hear what I heard from the Lord Sabaoth. Israel’s God announced it to us.
  Isaiah 48:20 — message to afflicted survivors: Isaiah addresses those who remain and suffer, asking them to hear God's announcement after the report of Babylon's fall. Isaiah 48 expressly addresses flight from Babylon and proclaims that the Lord rescued Jacob, giving a related message of deliverance to those affected by Babylon's power.

Isaiah 21:11
The vision of Idumea. One calls to me from Seir: “Guard the battlements.”
  Isaiah 21:6 — same-chapter watch commission: The Idumea vision begins with a call from Seir to guard the battlements. Earlier in the chapter a watcher is stationed and commanded to report, so the repeated watch commission provides a literary link between these distinct prophetic scenes without treating their locations as identical.

Isaiah 21:12
“I watch morning and night. If you seek, seek, and dwell beside me;
  Isaiah 21:8 — same-chapter watch recurrence: The answer in the Idumea vision speaks of watching morning and night. The earlier watcher likewise stands continually by day and through the night, repeating the motif of sustained vigilance across the chapter's scenes.

Isaiah 21:14
Bring water to meet the thirsty, you dwelling in Thaiman’s country; meet the fugitives with loaves,
  Isaiah 58:7 — care for the displaced: Isaiah 21 calls for water for the thirsty and loaves for fugitives. Isaiah 58 similarly commands sharing bread with the hungry and bringing homeless poor people into one's house, making material aid to displaced and needy people the specific point of comparison.

Isaiah 21:15
because of the multitude of those fleeing, the multitude of those wandering, the multitude of the sword, the multitude of ready arrows, and the multitude of those fallen in war.
  Jeremiah 4:29 — flight from battle parallel: Isaiah describes people fleeing the sword, ready arrows and the casualties of war. Jeremiah pictures inhabitants abandoning towns and seeking hiding places at the sound of a horseman and drawn bow, describing the same concrete effect of armed attack on people's flight.

Isaiah 21:16
For thus the Lord said to me: “Yet one year, like a hired worker’s year, and the glory of Kedar’s sons will fail,
  Isaiah 16:14 — fixed-term judgment formula: Isaiah 21 gives Kedar one year measured like a hired worker's year before its glory fails. Isaiah 16 uses the same hired-worker measure for three years before Moab's glory is dishonored. The repeated formula is explicit, while the peoples and durations remain distinct.

Isaiah 21:17
and the remaining arrows of Kedar’s strong sons will be few, because the Lord, Israel’s God, has spoken.”
  Jeremiah 25:15 — archery and national power: Isaiah reduces the remaining arrows of Kedar's strong sons to a few. Jeremiah calls for Elam's bow, described as the beginning of its dominion, to be shattered, likewise expressing a foreign nation's loss of power through the defeat of its archery; the two nations are not identified with each other.

Isaiah 22:3
All your rulers have fled; those captured are harshly bound, and the strong among you have fled far away.
  2 Kings 25:4-7 — narrative illustration: Isaiah portrays Jerusalem's rulers fleeing and captives being harshly bound. Kings later records the king's flight from besieged Jerusalem, pursuit, capture and binding with fetters, providing a concrete narrative parallel without establishing that every detail of the oracle concerns that one siege.

Isaiah 22:4
Therefore I said, “Leave me; I will weep bitterly. Do not insist on comforting me over the shattering of the daughter of my race.”
  Lamentations 2:11 — lament over the people: Isaiah insists on bitter weeping over the shattering of the daughter of his people. Lamentations likewise joins tears and inward anguish to the shattering of the daughter of the people, sharing both the personified community and the reason for the lament.

Isaiah 22:5
For there is a day of disturbance, destruction, trampling and wandering from the Lord Sabaoth in Zion’s ravine. They wander from small to great; they wander upon the mountains.
  Lamentations 1:15 — trampling of Judah: Isaiah announces a day of destruction and trampling from the Lord in Zion's ravine. Lamentations describes the Lord setting a time to crush the city's strong men and treading a winepress for Daughter Judah, sharing the portrayal of Jerusalem's humiliation under divine judgment.

Isaiah 22:6
The Elamites took quivers—mounted men upon horses and an assembled battle line.
  Jeremiah 25:15 — Elam's archery: Isaiah identifies Elamites with quivers in the assembled battle force. Jeremiah likewise identifies Elam's bow as an expression of its dominion, providing a specific correspondence between the named people and its military archery rather than equating the two campaigns.

Isaiah 22:7
Your chosen ravines will be filled with chariots, and the horsemen will block your gates,
  2 Kings 25:1-2 — siege parallel: Isaiah pictures Jerusalem's ravines filled with chariots and its gates blocked by horsemen. Kings describes Babylon's force encircling and besieging Jerusalem, providing a narrative counterpart to the city's military enclosure without claiming that Kings specifies Isaiah's chariots.

Isaiah 22:9
and uncover the hidden things of the houses in David’s citadel. They saw that they were many and that the old pool’s water had been diverted into the city,
  2 Chronicles 32:30 — Jerusalem waterworks: Isaiah's view of David's citadel includes water from the old pool diverted into the city. Chronicles records Hezekiah blocking the upper outlet of Gihon and directing its waters toward the City of David, a specific narrative parallel concerning the city's redirected water supply.

Isaiah 22:10
and that Jerusalem’s houses had been pulled down to fortify the city’s wall.
  Jeremiah 40:4 — defensive demolition parallel: Isaiah says Jerusalem's houses were pulled down to fortify its wall. Jeremiah likewise names the city's houses and royal houses torn down for siegeworks and defenses, matching the conversion of domestic buildings into defensive materials.

Isaiah 22:11
You provided water for yourselves between the two walls, inside the old pool, and did not look to the One who made her from the beginning or see the One who founded her.
  2 Chronicles 32:3-8 — contrast in defensive reliance: Isaiah condemns arranging water between the walls while ignoring the city's Maker. Chronicles also describes water measures and wall-building against a siege, but explicitly urges trust in the Lord rather than arms of flesh. The comparison distinguishes the engineering measures from the reliance placed upon them.

Isaiah 22:12
The Lord Sabaoth called on that day for weeping, lamentation, shaving and putting on sackcloth,
  Joel 2:12-13 — call to penitential mourning: Isaiah says the Lord called for weeping, lamentation and sackcloth. Joel likewise calls for return to God with fasting, weeping and mourning, adding that the heart rather than merely garments should be torn; both address the response sought when judgment approaches.

Isaiah 22:13
but they made gladness and exultation, slaughtering calves and sacrificing sheep to eat meat and drink wine, saying, “Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die!”
  1 Corinthians 15:32 — quotation reused in argument: Isaiah's revelers say, Let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die, instead of answering the call to mourn. Paul repeats that sentence while testing the consequences of denying resurrection, using the same expression of immediate gratification under death's horizon.

Isaiah 22:14
These things are revealed in the Lord Sabaoth’s ears: “This sin will not be forgiven you until you die.”
  1 Samuel 3:14 — judicial refusal of expiation: Isaiah announces that this sin will not be forgiven before the offenders die. Samuel receives an oath that Eli's household wrongdoing will never be expiated by incense or sacrifices. Both give a solemn sentence that ordinary expectation of expiation cannot cancel, while their wording and scope differ.

Isaiah 22:15
Thus says the Lord Sabaoth: “Go into the chamber to Somnas the treasurer, and say to him,
  2 Kings 18:18 — court official correspondence: Isaiah addresses Somnas as treasurer before announcing the transfer of stewardship to Eliakim. Kings places Somnas as scribe beside Eliakim son of Chelkias, the steward, preserving the two named officials while distinguishing the offices reported in the two passages.

Isaiah 22:16
What are you doing here, and what do you have here, that you quarried yourself a memorial here, made yourself a memorial on high and inscribed yourself a dwelling in rock?
  Matthew 27:60 — rock-cut memorial parallel: Isaiah challenges Somnas over the memorial he quarried for himself and his rock-cut dwelling. Matthew likewise describes Joseph's own tomb cut in rock. The physical parallel concerns a self-prepared rock-cut memorial and Joseph's own rock-cut tomb, preserving their distinct terms; Isaiah's criticism of Somnas is not transferred to Joseph.

Isaiah 22:17
Behold, the Lord Sabaoth will cast out and crush a man and remove your robe,
  Isaiah 22:21 — same-chapter reversal of investiture: The Lord's removal of the official's robe in verse 17 is answered by Eliakim being clothed in that robe in verse 21. The repeated garment makes the transfer of office visible within the same oracle.

Isaiah 22:18
and your glorious crown, and throw you into a great and immeasurable country. There you will die; He will make your fine chariot dishonored and your ruler’s house a trampling place,
  Jeremiah 20:6 — judgment on an official: Isaiah threatens the addressed official with removal to a distant country where he will die and lose his honor. Jeremiah likewise tells an official and his household that they will go captive and that he will die and be buried in Babylon, sharing the personal sentence of exile and death abroad.

Isaiah 22:19
and you will be removed from your stewardship and your standing.
  Isaiah 22:21 — same-chapter transfer of office: The removal of the present official from his stewardship is matched by the placing of that stewardship in Eliakim's hands. The two verses express the loss and grant of the same authority within the oracle.

Isaiah 22:20
On that day I will call My servant Eliakim, son of Chelkias,
  2 Kings 18:18 — identified official: Isaiah names the incoming servant as Eliakim son of Chelkias. Kings names Eliakim with the same father and identifies him as steward, providing a precise personal and official correspondence.

Isaiah 22:21
clothe him in your robe, give him your crown, and put the power and your stewardship in his hands. He will be like a father to those dwelling in Jerusalem and those dwelling in Judah.
  Genesis 45:8 — paternal civil authority: Eliakim's stewardship will make him like a father to Jerusalem and Judah's inhabitants. Joseph likewise describes God making him as a father to Pharaoh and ruler over Egypt. Both use paternal language for divinely appointed civil responsibility rather than biological parenthood.
  2 Kings 18:18 — official correspondence: Isaiah grants the stewardship to the Eliakim named in the preceding verse. Kings explicitly identifies Eliakim son of Chelkias as steward. The correspondence verifies the named office, without claiming that Kings repeats the robe, crown or paternal imagery.

Isaiah 22:22
I will give him David’s glory, and he will rule, with no one contradicting.
  1 Chronicles 29:23-25 — Royal parallel: granted glory with general submission: Isaiah promises Eliakim David's glory and rule with no one contradicting. Chronicles describes Solomon occupying David's throne, receiving obedience from Israel and submission from the rulers, while the Lord gives him exceptional royal glory. Both connect granted Davidic honor with accepted rule. Eliakim and Solomon remain distinct figures, and no key or opening-and-shutting language is supplied to Isaiah.

Isaiah 22:23
I will establish him as a ruler in a trustworthy place, and he will become a throne of glory for his father’s house.
  Isaiah 22:25 — same-chapter reversal: Verse 23 establishes the ruler in a trustworthy place and promises a throne of glory. Verse 25 repeats the trustworthy-place description but announces shaking, falling and removal of the attached glory, creating an explicit reversal within the oracle.

Isaiah 22:24
Every glorious person in his father’s house, from small to great, will trust in him, and they will hang upon him.
  Isaiah 22:25 — same-chapter dependency and loss: The household's glorious people trust in and hang upon the appointed figure. The following judgment removes the glory upon that figure as he falls, connecting the dependents' reliance with the vulnerability of the office supporting them.

Isaiah 22:25
On that day—thus says the Lord Sabaoth—the person established in a trustworthy place will be shaken and fall, and the glory upon him will be removed, because the Lord has spoken.”
  Isaiah 22:23 — same-chapter reversal: The person established in a trustworthy place is now shaken and falls. The earlier verse uses that same establishment language for the ruler's promised position and glory, so the ending must be read against the security announced there.

Isaiah 23:1
The vision of Tyre. Wail, ships of Carthage, because she has perished, and they no longer come from the Kitians’ land; she has been led captive.
  Ezekiel 27:25-27 — parallel Tyre lament: Isaiah calls Carthage's ships to wail over Tyre's destruction and captivity. Ezekiel places Carthaginian traders among Tyre's maritime company and depicts that company's catastrophic fall in the sea. Both connect Tyre's collapse with the shipping network that depended on her.

Isaiah 23:2
Whom have the island-dwelling traders of Phoenicia become like, crossing the sea
  Ezekiel 27:8-9 — maritime community parallel: Isaiah addresses Phoenician island traders crossing the sea. Ezekiel names Sidon, Arvad and Byblos within Tyre's network of rowers, pilots and advisers, with ships coming across the sea. The target supplies a concrete portrait of the coastal maritime community addressed by Isaiah.

Isaiah 23:3
upon much water, seed of traders? Like a harvest being brought in are the nations’ traders.
  Ezekiel 27:33 — international commerce parallel: Isaiah compares the nations' traders to a harvest being brought in. Ezekiel describes Tyre's abundant sea commerce filling nations and enriching their kings. Both portray the gathering and abundance of international trade; Isaiah's harvest comparison is not treated as a statement specifically about Egyptian grain.

Isaiah 23:5
When it becomes known to Egypt, pain will seize them concerning Tyre.
  Ezekiel 27:35 — parallel reactions to Tyre's fall: Isaiah says news concerning Tyre will bring pain to Egypt. Ezekiel's lament describes island inhabitants becoming gloomy and their kings weeping over Tyre. The affected regions differ, but both oracles portray Tyre's downfall causing grief beyond the city itself.

Isaiah 23:6
Go away to Carthage! Wail, residents of this island!
  Isaiah 23:1 — same-chapter literary refrain: The call to wail addresses island residents being sent toward Carthage, repeating the opening appeal to Carthage's ships. The recurrence extends the lament over Tyre from the ships named at the oracle's opening to the island community now addressed.

Isaiah 23:7
Was this not your insolence from the beginning, before she was handed over?
  Ezekiel 28:2-5 — parallel indictment of Tyrian pride: Isaiah recalls Tyre's longstanding insolence before she was handed over. Ezekiel exposes her ruler's exalted heart, divine pretensions and pride in wealth gained through trade. The target concentrates in the ruler the commercial city's arrogance condemned by Isaiah.

Isaiah 23:8
Who planned these things against Tyre? Is she weaker, or has she no strength? Her merchants are glorious, rulers of the earth.
  Ezekiel 27:33 — commercial power parallel: Isaiah describes Tyre's merchants as glorious rulers of the earth while asking who planned these things against her. Ezekiel says her commerce enriched all the earth's kings. Both explain the extraordinary international influence of Tyre through the wealth of her merchants.

Isaiah 23:9
The Lord Sabaoth planned it, to dissolve all the insolence of the glorious and dishonor everything glorious upon the earth.
  Isaiah 2:11-17 — literary judgment theme: The Lord plans Tyre's humiliation to dissolve the insolence of the glorious and dishonor earthly glory. Isaiah 2 likewise announces the humbling of human height, proud people, towers and splendid ships so that the Lord alone is exalted. Tyre's judgment fits this wider pattern of overturned human grandeur.

Isaiah 23:10
Work your land, for ships no longer come from Carthage.
  Ezekiel 27:25-27 — collapse of maritime commerce: Isaiah tells the inhabitants to work their land because ships no longer arrive from Carthage. Ezekiel's maritime lament includes Carthaginian traders before the ship-like city's shattering and the fall of its company. Both describe the collapse of sea commerce; Ezekiel does not give Isaiah's instruction about farming.

Isaiah 23:11
Your hand no longer has strength at sea, the hand that provoked kings. The Lord Sabaoth commanded concerning Canaan, to destroy her strength.
  Zechariah 9:2-4 — parallel judgment on coastal power: Isaiah attributes the loss of strength at sea to the Lord's command against Canaan's power. Zechariah names Tyre and Sidon, then announces the Lord's striking Tyre's strength into the sea despite her fortifications and wealth. Both make divine judgment decisive over the coast's maritime strength.

Isaiah 23:12
They will say, “You shall no longer continue in insolence and in wronging Sidon’s daughter.” Even if you go to the Kitians, there too you will have no rest,
  Deuteronomy 28:64-65 — exile without rest parallel: Isaiah warns that even flight to the Kitians will bring no rest. Deuteronomy likewise describes scattered people finding no rest among foreign nations. The shared condition is refuge failing to provide security; the covenant warning addresses Israel, whereas Isaiah's immediate oracle concerns the coastal population.

Isaiah 23:13
and into the Chaldeans’ land: this too has been devastated by the Assyrians. There too you will have no rest, because her wall has fallen.
  Isaiah 23:12 — same-chapter refrain of failed refuge: The warning of no rest in the Chaldeans' devastated land repeats the preceding warning of no rest among the Kitians. Repeating the same outcome for two possible destinations closes off the expectation that another foreign refuge will escape the announced calamity.

Isaiah 23:14
Wail, ships of Carthage, because your fortress has perished!
  Ezekiel 27:29-32 — parallel maritime lament: Isaiah commands Carthage's ships to wail because their fortress has perished. Ezekiel portrays rowers leaving their ships, maritime people standing ashore and raising a bitter cry, followed by a lament over Tyre. Both turn the trading fleet into mourners for the fallen city.

Isaiah 23:15
On that day Tyre will be left for seventy years, like a king’s time, like a person’s time. After seventy years, Tyre will be like a prostitute’s song:
  Jeremiah 25:11-12 — seventy-year judgment parallel: Isaiah assigns Tyre seventy years of being left before her condition changes. Jeremiah also marks a period of national humiliation and service by seventy years, followed by divine action. The shared bounded duration is explicit, but the two passages concern different subjects and do not establish an identical chronology.

Isaiah 23:17
After seventy years God will visit Tyre, and she will again be restored to her former state and become a trading center for all the inhabited world’s kingdoms.
  Ezekiel 27:3 — restored international commerce: Isaiah promises that Tyre will return to her former state as a trading center for the world's kingdoms. Ezekiel identifies Tyre as the sea's entrance and trading place for peoples from many islands. That portrait makes concrete the international commercial role Isaiah says will be restored.

Isaiah 23:18
Her trade and her wage will be holy to the Lord. It will not be collected for them; instead, all her trade will be for those dwelling before the Lord, to eat and drink and be filled, as a contribution, a memorial before the Lord.
  Isaiah 60:5-7 — foreign wealth consecrated to the Lord: Isaiah dedicates Tyre's trade and wages to the Lord and to the support of those dwelling before Him. Isaiah 60 likewise brings sea and national wealth toward Zion, culminating in acceptable offerings and the glorification of God's house. Both redirect international prosperity toward the Lord's service.

Isaiah 24:1
Behold, the Lord is destroying the inhabited world; He will make it desolate, uncover its face and scatter those dwelling in it.
  Zephaniah 1:2-3 — worldwide judgment parallel: Isaiah announces the Lord's devastation of the inhabited world and scattering of its residents. Zephaniah similarly announces the removal of humanity and other creatures from the earth's face. Both use comprehensive earthly destruction to introduce divine judgment, without restricting Isaiah's statement to one named city.

Isaiah 24:2
The people will be like the priest, the slave like the master, the maidservant like the mistress; the buyer will be like the seller, the lender like the borrower, and the debtor like the one to whom he owes a debt.
  Hosea 4:9 — shared people-and-priest formula: Isaiah places people and priest under the same impending condition before listing other social pairs. Hosea likewise says the people will be like the priest in a sentence of recompense. Both remove the expectation that priestly position exempts someone from the people's judgment.
  Ezekiel 7:12-13 — commercial distinctions under judgment: Isaiah says buyer and seller will share the same condition when judgment comes. Ezekiel warns the buyer not to rejoice and the seller not to lament when the appointed day arrives, because ordinary exchange no longer offers security. Both suspend the usual contrast between commercial gain and loss.

Isaiah 24:3
The earth will be utterly destroyed, and the earth utterly plundered, for the Lord’s mouth has spoken these things.
  2 Peter 3:6-7 — world judgment by divine declaration: Isaiah grounds the earth's destruction in what the Lord's mouth has spoken. Peter similarly places the present world under a judgment reserved by God's word, recalling the earlier world's destruction by water. The connection is divine speech and world judgment; Peter specifies water and fire, which this Isaiah unit does not.

Isaiah 24:4
The earth mourned, the inhabited world was destroyed, and the earth’s high ones mourned.
  Hosea 4:3 — shared mourning-earth image: Isaiah describes the earth and its high inhabitants mourning under devastation. Hosea likewise makes the land mourn and diminish together with its inhabitants because of their offenses. Both present the land as participating in the consequences of human wrongdoing.

Isaiah 24:5
The earth acted lawlessly because of those dwelling in it, because they transgressed the law and changed the ordinances, an everlasting covenant.
  Leviticus 26:15-16 — covenant violation and judgment: Isaiah explains the earth's lawlessness through transgressed law and changed ordinances and covenant. Leviticus connects refusal of God's judgments and commandments, dissolving His covenant, with ensuing distress. The shared covenantal pattern is explicit, while Isaiah's wider earthly scope is not reduced to every detail of Leviticus's penalties.

Isaiah 24:6
Therefore a curse will consume the earth, because those dwelling in it sinned. Therefore those dwelling on the earth will be poor, and few people will be left.
  Deuteronomy 28:62 — curse and diminished population: Isaiah says a curse follows the inhabitants' sin and leaves few people. Deuteronomy likewise warns that disobedience will leave the formerly numerous people few in number. Both connect culpable refusal of God with the drastic reduction of a population.

Isaiah 24:7
Wine will mourn, the vine will mourn, and all who gladden the soul will groan.
  Joel 1:10-12 — vine and harvest lament: Isaiah makes wine and vine mourn while those who bring gladness groan. Joel likewise describes ruined agricultural produce and a dried vine, saying human beings have put joy to shame. Both join vine and wine imagery with troubled human gladness, while Joel's stated causal wording is preserved.

Isaiah 24:8
The joy of tambourines has ceased; the impudence and wealth of the ungodly have ceased; the lyre’s sound has ceased.
  Ezekiel 26:13 — music silenced by judgment: Isaiah lists the end of tambourine joy and the lyre's sound among judgment's effects. Ezekiel's sentence upon Tyre likewise ends its musicians and the audible sound of psalteries. The shared image is a formerly festive community made musically silent by destruction.

Isaiah 24:9
They were ashamed and did not drink wine; sikera became bitter to those drinking it.
  Lamentations 3:14-19 — Lament imagery: drinking turned to bitterness: Isaiah replaces the surrounding wine-associated gladness with shame and sikera bitter to its drinkers. The lamenting speaker describes public mockery and being filled with bitterness and made drunk with gall, followed by the loss of peace and good things. Both portray affliction through bitter drinking rather than enjoyment; the comparison does not identify sikera with gall or claim the same drink or occasion.

Isaiah 24:10
Every city was deserted; he will shut a house so that none may enter.
  Jeremiah 4:29 — abandoned settlements parallel: Isaiah describes every city deserted and a house shut against entry. Jeremiah similarly depicts inhabitants fleeing at the sound of horseman and bow until every city is abandoned and no one dwells in them. Both portray judgment through empty urban habitation, though Jeremiah names the immediate military cause.

Isaiah 24:11
Wail over wine everywhere! All the earth’s gladness has ceased.
  Joel 1:5 — wine and lost gladness parallel: Isaiah commands lament over wine because the earth's gladness has ceased. Joel calls wine drinkers to weep and lament because gladness and joy have been removed from their mouths. The combination of wine, commanded mourning and lost joy makes the correspondence specific.

Isaiah 24:12
Cities will be left deserted, and abandoned houses will perish.
  Isaiah 6:11-12 — literary desolation parallel: Isaiah 24 leaves cities deserted and abandoned houses perishing. The answer to Isaiah's earlier question about duration likewise describes cities without inhabitants, houses without people and a desolate land. Both define the severity of judgment through the disappearance of settled human life.

Isaiah 24:13
All these things will happen on the earth, among the nations: as when someone gleans an olive tree, so they will glean them, even when the grape harvest has ceased.
  Isaiah 17:6 — literary gleaning image: Isaiah 24 compares the nations under judgment to an olive tree gleaned after harvest. Isaiah 17 spells out the same image as a few berries remaining on high branches. The earlier description clarifies how a gleaned tree can portray the small remainder after devastation.

Isaiah 24:14
These will cry aloud, and those left upon the earth will rejoice together with the Lord’s glory. The sea’s water will be troubled;
  Isaiah 12:4-6 — remnant praise parallel: Those left on the earth rejoice with the Lord's glory in Isaiah 24. Isaiah 12 calls Zion's inhabitants to sing, announce His glorious deeds and rejoice because Israel's Holy One is exalted among them. Both place communal praise of the Lord's glory within a setting of salvation after distress.

Isaiah 24:15
therefore the Lord’s glory will be in the sea’s islands; the Lord’s name will be glorious—O Lord, Israel’s God!
  Isaiah 42:10-12 — island praise parallel: Isaiah 24 places the Lord's glory and glorious name in the sea's islands. Isaiah 42 explicitly summons islands, sea travelers and distant inhabitants to glorify His name and announce His deeds. Both extend the Lord's praise beyond the land to maritime peoples.

Isaiah 24:16
From the earth’s wings we heard wonders: “Hope for the devout!” And they will say, “Woe to those who reject, those who reject the law!”
  Proverbs 11:7 — contrasting destinies of the devout and impious: Isaiah's report couples hope for the devout with woe for rejecters of the law. Proverbs likewise preserves hope for the righteous while the impious person's boast perishes. Both contrast hope for the righteous with calamity for the ungodly; Proverbs specifically frames its contrast at death.

Isaiah 24:17
Fear, pit and trap are upon you who dwell on the earth!
  Jeremiah 31:43 — shared fear-pit-trap formula: Isaiah announces fear, pit and trap upon the earth's inhabitant. Jeremiah uses the same three dangers, in a different order, against resident Moab. The tightly shared formulation depicts inescapable danger, while the addressed population differs.

Isaiah 24:18
Whoever flees the fear will fall into the pit, and whoever climbs from the pit will be caught by the trap, because windows from heaven have opened and the earth’s foundations will be shaken.
  Jeremiah 31:44 — parallel escape-and-capture sequence: Both passages say that fleeing fear leads into a pit and climbing from the pit leads into a trap. Jeremiah directs the sequence against Moab in her visitation year, whereas Isaiah places it within upheaval of earth and heaven. The successive failed escapes are a close verbal and structural parallel.
  Genesis 7:11 — opened-heaven judgment image: Isaiah connects the failed escapes with heaven's opened windows and shaken earthly foundations. Genesis describes the opening of heaven's floodgates when the flood begins. Both use openings above as a sign of catastrophic judgment; Isaiah's unit is not thereby identified as a second narration of Noah's flood.

Isaiah 24:19
The earth will be utterly agitated, and the earth will be utterly at a loss;
  Hebrews 12:26-27 — cosmic shaking parallel: Isaiah describes the earth utterly agitated and at a loss. Hebrews discusses God's shaking of earth and heaven and the removal of created things that can be shaken. Both make shaking an image of the created order's inability to remain secure before divine action; no direct quotation of this Isaiah verse is claimed.

Isaiah 24:20
The earth has tilted and will be shaken like a fruit-watcher’s shelter, like someone drunk and hungover. It will fall and will never be able to rise, for lawlessness has prevailed upon it.
  Psalms 59:4 — shaken-earth image with differing outcomes: Isaiah pictures the earth tilting and shaking before an irreversible fall under lawlessness. The psalm also speaks of God shaking and fracturing the earth, but pleads for its healing. The shared physical image supports the comparison while the plea for repair and announced final collapse remain distinct.

Isaiah 24:21
God will bring His hand against heaven’s array and against the earth’s kings.
  Isaiah 34:4 — judgment reaching heaven parallel: Isaiah 24 directs God's hand against heaven's array as well as earthly kings. Isaiah 34 depicts heaven rolling up and its stars falling. Both extend the imagery of divine judgment to the heavenly order, without requiring a particular identification of the array with named spiritual beings.

Isaiah 24:22
They will gather and shut them into a fortress and a prison; after many generations there will be a visitation of them.
  2 Peter 2:4 — confinement before divine reckoning: Isaiah describes confinement in a fortress and prison followed much later by visitation. Peter describes sinful angels confined in gloom and kept for judgment. Both present restraint before subsequent divine reckoning; Isaiah's visitation is not called release, and its prisoners are not equated with Peter's angels.

Isaiah 24:23
The brick will melt and the wall will fall, because the Lord will reign in Zion and in Jerusalem and will be glorified before the elders.
  Micah 4:7 — divine kingship on Zion: Isaiah says the Lord will reign in Zion and Jerusalem and be glorified before the elders. Micah likewise promises that the Lord will reign over the gathered remnant on Mount Zion. Both place the Lord's rule on Zion beyond the surrounding scenes of affliction and judgment.

Isaiah 25:1
Lord my God, I will glorify Thee and sing Thy name, because Thou hast done wondrous things, an ancient, true plan. May it be so, Lord!
  Psalms 97:1 — praise for divine wonders: Isaiah promises to glorify God and sing His name because He has done wondrous things. The psalm likewise calls for a new song because the Lord has done wonders and worked salvation. In both, accomplished divine wonders are the stated reason for singing praise.
  Isaiah 46:10 — divine counsel accomplished: Isaiah's praise names God's wondrous action as an ancient, true plan. Isaiah 46 declares that God's counsel will stand and that He will do all He has planned, announcing outcomes before they happen. Both connect trustworthy divine planning with events brought to completion.

Isaiah 25:2
For Thou hast made cities a mound, fortified cities so that their foundations fall. The city of the ungodly will certainly never be rebuilt.
  Ezekiel 26:14 — irreversible city ruin parallel: Isaiah celebrates the reduction of fortified cities and says the ungodly city will never be rebuilt. Ezekiel similarly pronounces that Tyre will become bare rock and will not be rebuilt. The shared feature is irreversible urban ruin; Isaiah's unnamed city is not identified as Tyre solely from that resemblance.

Isaiah 25:3
Therefore the poor people will bless Thee, and cities of wronged people will bless Thee.
  Psalms 68:33-34 — praise of the delivered poor: Isaiah says poor people and wronged communities will bless God. The psalm calls the poor to rejoice because the Lord hears the needy and does not despise His bound ones. Both make the response of the disadvantaged central to praise for divine help.

Isaiah 25:4
For Thou didst become a helper to every lowly city and a shelter to those discouraged by need. Thou wilt rescue them from evil people, a shelter for the thirsty and a breath for wronged people.
  Psalms 9:10 — refuge for the needy: Isaiah calls God a helper and shelter for lowly, needy and wronged people. The psalm likewise names the Lord a refuge for the needy and helper in distress. The correspondence concerns God's particular protection of the vulnerable rather than an unspecified claim of help.

Isaiah 25:5
They will bless Thee like fainthearted people thirsting in Zion because of ungodly people, to whom Thou didst hand us over.
  Psalms 106:5-8 — thanksgiving amid thirst and distress: Isaiah portrays fainthearted, thirsty people blessing God amid oppression, following the promise of His shelter. The psalm recounts hungry, thirsty people whose strength fails, their cry and rescue, and a call to acknowledge God's mercies. Both bring thanksgiving out of the distress pictured through thirst and weakness.

Isaiah 25:6
The Lord Sabaoth will act for all the nations upon this mountain. They will drink gladness, drink wine and anoint themselves with perfume.
  Matthew 8:11 — international celebration parallel: Isaiah promises gladness and wine for all nations on the Lord's mountain. Jesus speaks of many arriving from east and west to recline with the patriarchs in the kingdom. Both envision people beyond a single local community sharing a celebratory divine welcome; Isaiah's native wording about wine and perfume is preserved.

Isaiah 25:7
On this mountain, hand all these things over to the nations, for this plan concerns all the nations.
  Isaiah 2:2-4 — mountain and nations parallel: Isaiah says the plan on this mountain concerns all nations. Isaiah 2 likewise gathers nations to the Lord's mountain for His instruction and judgment. The connection is the mountain as the setting of God's purpose for the nations; this source unit is not described as mentioning a veil or shroud.

Isaiah 25:8
Death swallowed, having prevailed; and again God removed every tear from every face. He removed the people’s reproach from all the earth, for the Lord’s mouth has spoken.
  Isaiah 65:19 — ending of tears parallel: Isaiah 25 says God removes every tear from every face and takes away His people's reproach. Isaiah 65 likewise promises that weeping and crying will no longer be heard in the restored Jerusalem. Both describe the removal of communal sorrow by God, without altering Isaiah 25's distinctive preceding statement about death.

Isaiah 25:9
On that day they will say, “Behold, our God, in whom we hoped and exulted; and we will rejoice in our salvation.”
  Psalms 117:24-27 — rejoicing in divine salvation: Isaiah's people recognize their God and rejoice in the salvation for which they hoped. The psalm calls for rejoicing in the Lord's day, cries for salvation and acknowledges that the Lord God has shone upon His people. Both join communal joy, divine recognition and salvation in worship.

Isaiah 25:10
For God will give rest upon this mountain, and Moab’s land will be trampled as people tread a threshing floor with wagons,
  Micah 4:12-13 — threshing as national judgment: Isaiah compares Moab's trampling to the treading of a threshing floor. Micah gathers hostile nations like sheaves and commands Zion to thresh them. Both employ the crushing action of threshing to picture defeat of hostile peoples, while their named agents and immediate targets differ.

Isaiah 25:11
and he will release his hands, just as he himself brought low for destruction; he will bring low his insolence over what he laid his hands on,
  Isaiah 16:6-7 — judgment of Moab's arrogance: Within the oracle against Moab, Isaiah 25 announces the bringing down of insolence. Isaiah 16 likewise names Moab's excessive insolence and arrogance before its country-wide lament. Both connect Moab's humiliation with the pride that preceded it.

Isaiah 25:12
and he will bring low the height of your wall’s refuge, and it will descend to the ground.
  Isaiah 26:5 — literary fortified-city reversal: Isaiah 25 brings the height of the wall's refuge down to the ground. The following song likewise says God brings down those dwelling high and casts fortified cities to the ground. The repeated movement from elevated defenses to ground level links the adjoining judgment descriptions.

Isaiah 26:1
On that day they will sing this song in Judah’s land, saying, “Behold, a fortified city! He will make our salvation a wall and an encircling wall.
  Isaiah 60:18 — walls and salvation image: Isaiah 26 makes salvation a wall and encircling wall for the fortified city. Isaiah 60 similarly names the restored city's walls Salvation. Both portray the community's saving security through city defenses, rather than treating physical fortification alone as the source of safety.

Isaiah 26:2
Open the gates; let a people enter, keeping righteousness and keeping truth,
  Psalms 117:19-20 — gates admitting the righteous: Isaiah commands the gates to open for a people keeping righteousness and truth. The psalm asks for the gates of righteousness to open and declares that the righteous enter the Lord's gate. Both connect admission through the gates with the character of the entering worshipers.

Isaiah 26:3
holding fast to truth and keeping peace. For in Thee
  Philippians 4:6-7 — peace and Godward trust: Isaiah describes people holding truth and keeping peace, with their hope in God expressed across this verse and the next. Philippians joins prayerful reliance upon God with His peace guarding hearts and thoughts. Both associate Godward reliance with peace; Isaiah's people keeping peace are not silently rewritten as God guarding their minds.

Isaiah 26:4
they hoped, Lord, for ever, the great, everlasting God,
  Isaiah 12:2 — confession of trust: Isaiah 26 says the people hoped forever in the great everlasting God. Isaiah 12 likewise confesses trust in God the Savior and freedom from fear because He is salvation. Both express reliance upon the Lord within a communal song of deliverance.

Isaiah 26:5
Thou who didst humble and bring down those dwelling on high. Thou wilt cast down fortified cities and bring them to the ground,
  Isaiah 25:12 — literary overthrow of high defenses: Isaiah 26 brings high dwellings and fortified cities down to the ground. Isaiah 25 has just brought the high refuge of a wall to the ground. Their repeated contrast between defensive height and humiliating descent links the song with the preceding judgment oracle.

Isaiah 26:6
and the feet of the meek and lowly will trample them.
  Malachi 3:21 — trampling after divine judgment: Isaiah gives the meek and lowly feet that trample the fallen defenses described immediately before. Malachi promises those who fear God's name that they will trample the lawless as ashes beneath their feet. Both show the Lord's people participating in the downfall announced by divine judgment, with different objects underfoot.

Isaiah 26:7
The way of the devout became straight, and the way of the devout is prepared.
  Hosea 14:10 — straight ways of the devout: Isaiah describes the devout person's way as straight and prepared. Hosea likewise calls the Lord's ways straight and distinguishes the righteous who walk in them from the impious who grow weak in them. Both use the straight path for the course of a life ordered toward God.

Isaiah 26:8
For the Lord’s way is judgment. We hoped in Thy name and in the remembrance
  Psalms 134:13-14 — name, remembrance and judgment parallel: Isaiah joins the Lord's way of judgment with hope in His name and remembrance. The psalm similarly places the Lord's enduring name and memorial immediately beside His judgment of His people. The same cluster ties divine remembrance to confidence in His dealings with the community.

Isaiah 26:9
which our soul desires. From night my spirit rises early toward Thee, God, because Thine ordinances are light upon the earth. Learn righteousness, you dwelling upon the earth!
  Psalms 118:62 — nocturnal devotion to divine judgments: Isaiah describes the spirit rising toward God from night and calls His ordinances light. The psalmist likewise rises at midnight to give thanks for God's righteous judgments. Both connect seeking God during the night with attention to His righteous ordering of life.

Isaiah 26:10
For the ungodly has ceased; he will certainly not learn righteousness upon the earth or practice truth. Let the ungodly be removed, so that he may not see the Lord’s glory.
  Jeremiah 4:22 — failure to learn righteousness: Isaiah says the ungodly will not learn righteousness or practice truth. Jeremiah likewise indicts leaders who do not know God: they are skilled in evil but have not learned to do well. Both describe moral failure as a refusal or failure to learn the good rather than a lack of worldly ability.

Isaiah 26:11
Lord, Thine arm is high, and they did not know; when they know, they will be ashamed. Zeal will seize an undisciplined people, and now fire will consume the opponents.
  Hebrews 10:27 — verbal echo of consuming judgment: Isaiah announces fire consuming the opponents after their failure to recognize the Lord's raised arm. Hebrews likewise warns of a fury of fire about to consume the adversaries. The close fire-and-opponents formulation presents divine judgment in both settings; Hebrews applies the warning in its own context of deliberate sin.

Isaiah 26:12
Lord our God, give us peace, for Thou hast repaid everything to us.
  Numbers 6:26 — prayer for the Lord's peace: Isaiah addresses the Lord directly with the plea to give His people peace. The priestly blessing likewise asks the Lord to lift His face upon the people and give peace. The correspondence rests on the requested divine gift, not on replacing Isaiah's statement about recompense with wording about God doing all human works.

Isaiah 26:13
Lord our God, acquire us! Lord, we know no other besides Thee; we name Thy name.
  Hosea 13:4 — exclusive acknowledgment of God: Isaiah confesses knowing no other besides the Lord and naming His name. Hosea likewise declares that Israel is to know no god except the Lord and that no other saves. The confession answers the same exclusive allegiance that Hosea states as God's claim upon His people.

Isaiah 26:14
The dead will certainly not see life, nor will physicians ever raise them. Therefore Thou didst bring calamity, destroy and remove every male of theirs.
  Isaiah 26:19 — same-chapter contrast in resurrection power: Isaiah first says that physicians cannot raise the dead. Later in the same song the dead rise and tombs awaken under healing dew from God. Reading the statements together distinguishes the failure of human healing power from the life-giving divine action subsequently promised.

Isaiah 26:15
Add evils to them, Lord; add evils to all the earth’s glorious people!
  Isaiah 2:12-17 — judgment on human exaltation: The prayer asks for calamity upon the earth's glorious people. Isaiah 2 likewise announces the Lord's day against the high and elevated, bringing human height down so that the Lord alone is exalted. Both confront earthly eminence with divine abasement rather than treating prominence as protection.

Isaiah 26:16
Lord, in affliction I remembered Thee; in slight affliction Thy discipline was for us.
  Psalms 77:34-35 — remembering God under discipline: Isaiah remembers the Lord in affliction and recognizes His discipline. The psalm recounts people seeking God and remembering their helper and redeemer when He struck them. Both connect distress with renewed remembrance of God; the psalm's following warning about insincere hearts is not suppressed or applied automatically to Isaiah's speaker.

Isaiah 26:17
Like a woman in labor nearing childbirth and crying out in her pang, so we became toward Thy beloved because of fear of Thee, Lord.
  John 16:21-22 — birth-pain image for communal distress: Isaiah compares the people's fearful condition to a woman crying in labor. Jesus likewise uses a woman's birth anguish when explaining His disciples' sorrow and coming joy. Both make labor pain a picture of communal suffering, while John's explicit promise of seeing Jesus supplies its distinct narrative setting.

Isaiah 26:18
We conceived, labored and gave birth; we produced the spirit of Thy salvation upon the earth, but those dwelling upon the earth will fall.
  Isaiah 66:8 — literary childbirth and restoration image: Isaiah 26 says the people conceived, labored and produced the spirit of God's salvation upon the earth. Isaiah 66 likewise depicts Zion laboring and bearing her children, asking about a nation born at once. Both use completed childbirth to picture a collective outcome; the native phrase about salvation is retained.

Isaiah 26:19
The dead will rise, those in the tombs will be awakened, and those in the earth will rejoice, for the dew from Thee is healing to them; but the earth of the ungodly will fall.”
  John 5:28-29 — resurrection from tombs parallel: Isaiah announces that the dead rise and those in tombs awaken. Jesus likewise says that those in tombs will hear His voice and come out to resurrection. Both expressly describe the dead emerging from burial, while John distinguishes resurrection of life from resurrection of judgment.
  Daniel 12:2 — awakening of the dead parallel: Isaiah describes dead people in the earth awakening and rejoicing under God's healing dew. Daniel likewise announces the rising of those sleeping in the earth, with everlasting life and shame among the outcomes. The shared awakening image makes this a direct resurrection parallel without erasing their differing descriptions of judgment.

Isaiah 26:20
Go, My people, enter your inner rooms, shut your door, hide for a little, a very little while, until the Lord’s wrath passes.
  Exodus 12:22-23 — sheltering indoors through divine wrath: Isaiah commands the people to enter inner rooms, shut their door and hide until the Lord's wrath passes. Exodus similarly forbids leaving the house until morning as the Lord passes through to strike Egypt and protects the marked doorways. Both locate temporary refuge inside while destructive judgment passes outside.

Isaiah 26:21
For behold, the Lord brings wrath from the holy place upon those dwelling on the earth. The earth will uncover its blood and will not cover those killed.
  Genesis 4:10 — blood and the earth as witness: Isaiah says the earth will uncover its blood and no longer cover those killed when the Lord brings wrath. Genesis gives Abel's blood a voice crying to God from the earth after his murder. Both deny that bloodshed hidden in the ground can escape divine acknowledgment.
  Job 16:18 — uncovered blood parallel: Isaiah promises that the earth will uncover blood and cease concealing the slain. Job appeals directly to the earth not to cover his flesh's blood. Both give exposed blood the function of testimony, one as a prophetic announcement and the other as a sufferer's plea.

Isaiah 27:1
On that day God will bring the holy, great and strong sword against the dragon, the fleeing serpent, against the dragon, the crooked serpent, and will kill the dragon.
  Psalms 73:13-14 — divine victory over dragons: Isaiah announces God's killing the dragon with His great strong sword. The psalm likewise celebrates God's crushing the heads of dragons upon the water and the dragon's heads. Both use the defeat of a formidable dragon to portray divine power; no further identification of the creature is required by the comparison.
  Job 26:13 — divine killing of the dragon: Isaiah's sword judgment targets the fleeing and crooked serpent described as a dragon. Job likewise credits God with killing the rebellious dragon by command. The shared decisive divine victory is explicit, although the dragon's descriptors and the means pictured differ.

Isaiah 27:2
On that day, a beautiful vineyard; a desire to lead a song against her.
  Isaiah 5:1-7 — literary vineyard-song parallel: Isaiah 27 introduces a beautiful vineyard and a song against her. Isaiah 5 likewise opens a vineyard song, then turns its cultivation story into a charge against Israel's failed righteousness. Both use the vineyard-song form for a communal address with judgment in its context, rather than presuming an uncomplicated celebration of a guarded vineyard.

Isaiah 27:3
“I am a strong city, a besieged city; in vain I will water her, for she will be captured by night, and by day the wall will fall.
  Isaiah 5:5-6 — literary failure of vineyard defenses: Following the vineyard-song introduction, Isaiah 27 places a besieged city, watering in vain, night capture and a falling wall together. Isaiah 5 likewise removes the vineyard's fence and wall, leaving it trampled and without rain. Both depict failed protection in a vineyard oracle; the native watering in vain is not replaced by continual safe watering.

Isaiah 27:5
the residents in her will cry. “Let us make peace with Him; let us make peace.”
  Isaiah 57:18-19 — peace sought and granted after affliction: Isaiah 27's residents cry to make peace with Him amid the preceding burning and rejection. Isaiah 57 answers an earlier account of divine discipline with healing, comfort and peace upon peace. Both place restored peace in relation to the Lord after affliction, one as the residents' plea and the other as divine assurance.

Isaiah 27:6
Those coming, Jacob’s children—Israel will sprout and flower, and the inhabited world will be filled with his fruit.
  Hosea 14:6-8 — agricultural image of Israel's restoration: Isaiah promises that Jacob's children and Israel will sprout, flower and fill the inhabited world with fruit. Hosea likewise promises Israel blossom, roots, spreading branches and the fruitfulness of olive and vine. Both describe the people's renewal through flourishing plants rather than through military expansion.

Isaiah 27:7
Surely he will not be struck just as he himself struck, and be killed just as he himself killed?
  Obadiah 1:15 — Rhetorical comparison: receiving as one has inflicted: Isaiah asks whether the unnamed figure will be struck and killed just as he himself struck and killed. Obadiah declares that the addressee will receive recompense corresponding to what he did. The shared comparison sets received harm beside inflicted harm, but Isaiah poses a question beginning Surely he will not, whereas Obadiah makes an affirmative announcement. The comparison neither settles Isaiah's pronouns nor turns the question into Obadiah's assertion.

Isaiah 27:9
Therefore Jacob’s lawlessness will be removed, and this is his blessing: when I remove his sin, when they make all altar stones cut down like fine dust. Their trees will certainly not remain, and their idols will be cut down like a forest far away.
  Romans 11:26-27 — verbal correspondence in removal of sin: Isaiah links removal of Jacob's lawlessness with God's declaration about removing his sin. Romans likewise names the turning away of ungodliness from Jacob and concludes its written citation with God taking away their sins. The shared wording concerns Jacob's purification; Isaiah's word blessing and Romans' word covenant are preserved distinctly.
  2 Kings 23:14-15 — narrative illustration of destroyed cult objects: Isaiah joins removal of Jacob's sin with altar stones reduced to fine dust and the cutting down of trees and idols. Kings recounts Josiah shattering pillars, destroying groves and reducing Bethel's altar stones to dust. The acts give a concrete narrative counterpart to the dismantling of illicit worship described by Isaiah.

Isaiah 27:10
The inhabited flock will be released like an abandoned flock; it will be pasture for a long time, and there they will rest.
  Isaiah 17:2 — literary pasture-and-rest image: Isaiah 27 describes a released, abandoned flock with prolonged pasture and rest. Isaiah 17 describes an abandoned city becoming a resting place for flocks. Both bring flock, abandonment and rest together within judgment oracles; the native flock wording in Isaiah 27 is not silently rewritten as a fortified city.

Isaiah 27:11
After a time there will be nothing green in her because it has dried. Women coming from a spectacle, come! For this is a people without understanding; therefore their Maker will certainly not pity them, nor will the One who formed them ever have mercy.
  Isaiah 1:3-4 — uncomprehending people under judgment: Isaiah 27 explains the Maker's refusal of pity by the people's lack of understanding. Isaiah 1 likewise says Israel does not know and the people do not understand, then names their abandonment and provocation of the Lord. Both connect failure to recognize the One who formed or raised them with the ensuing indictment.

Isaiah 27:12
On that day the Lord will fence off from the River’s canal to Rhinocorura; but you, gather Israel’s sons one by one.
  Deuteronomy 30:3-4 — gathering dispersed Israel: Isaiah marks the Lord's boundary from the River's canal to Rhinocorura and calls for Israel's sons to be gathered one by one. Deuteronomy promises God's gathering of Israel from even the remotest dispersion. Both envision the scattered people collected again; Isaiah's command and exact place name remain distinct from Deuteronomy's promise.

Isaiah 27:13
On that day they will sound the great trumpet. Those lost in the Assyrians’ country and those lost in Egypt will come and bow to the Lord upon the holy mountain in Jerusalem.
  Isaiah 11:11-12 — gathering from Assyria and Egypt: Isaiah 27 brings the lost from Assyria's country and Egypt to worship at Jerusalem. Isaiah 11 likewise names Assyria and Egypt among the places from which the Lord gathers His remaining people and the scattered of Israel. The repeated locations and gathering of the dispersed make the connection specific.
  Matthew 24:31 — trumpet and gathering parallel: Isaiah sounds the great trumpet as dispersed people come to worship the Lord at Jerusalem. Matthew likewise joins a great trumpet with the gathering of the chosen from the four winds. The shared trumpet-and-gathering pattern is explicit, while Matthew's angels and Isaiah's named countries retain their different settings.

Isaiah 28:1
Woe to the crown of insolence, Ephraim’s hired men; the flower fallen from glory on the summit of the fertile mountain, those drunk without wine!
  Isaiah 29:9 — close image of intoxication without wine: Ephraim's hired men are described as drunk without wine; the later warning likewise describes a hangover from neither strong drink nor wine. The repeated denial of an ordinary intoxicant links the images without identifying the two audiences.

Isaiah 28:2
Behold, the Lord’s wrath is strong and harsh, like falling hail with no covering, falling violently. Like a great mass of water dragging a country, He will give the land rest with His hands.
  Isaiah 30:30 — close portrayal of wrath through violent hail and water: Both depict the Lord's forceful intervention through falling hail and violent water. Isaiah 28 additionally describes the land being given rest by His hands; Isaiah 30 emphasizes His voice and arm.

Isaiah 28:3
Underfoot will be trampled the crown of insolence, Ephraim’s hired men.
  Lamentations 5:16 — related humiliation pictured through a lost crown: Ephraim's insolent crown is trampled underfoot; the lament declares that the crown has fallen from the community's head because of sin. Both use the crown's degradation to express communal humiliation, with different actions and communities.

Isaiah 28:4
The flower fallen from the hope of glory, on the high mountain’s summit, will be like an early fig: whoever sees it will want to swallow it before taking it into his hand.
  Nahum 3:12 — close figure of fortifications or glory as easily swallowed figs: Isaiah's fading glory is like an early fig someone wants to swallow before taking it in hand; Nahum depicts fortresses as fig trees whose fruit falls into the eater's mouth when shaken. Nahum does not call its figs early.

Isaiah 28:5
On that day the Lord Sabaoth will be the crown of hope, woven of glory, for My people who remain;
  Isaiah 62:3 — related crown imagery joining the Lord and His restored people: Here the Lord becomes a glorious crown for His remaining people; Isaiah 62 calls the restored city a beautiful crown in the Lord's hand. The shared royal adornment image has deliberately different directions.

Isaiah 28:6
they will be left with a spirit of judgment, for judgment and strength, as He prevents destruction.
  Isaiah 11:2-4 — related spiritual endowment for strength and judgment: The remaining people receive a spirit of judgment and strength; the shoot from Jesse receives God's Spirit of counsel and might and judges the lowly. The latter describes one ruler rather than identifying him with the group in this verse.

Isaiah 28:7
For these have been led astray by wine and wandered because of sikera. Priest and prophet lost their senses because of wine, reeled from sikera’s intoxication and went astray. This is an apparition.
  Leviticus 10:9-11 — contrast between priestly sobriety and drunken failure: Isaiah describes priests and prophets losing their senses through wine and strong drink. Leviticus forbids Aaron and his sons those drinks while serving and connects the rule with distinguishing holy from profane and teaching Israel.

Isaiah 28:8
A curse will consume this counsel, for this counsel is for greed.
  Micah 3:11-12 — parallel judgment upon religious counsel corrupted by gain: A curse consumes counsel directed toward greed, following Isaiah's description of priests and prophets. Micah names paid priestly answers and prophecy for silver before announcing Jerusalem's devastation; the forms of gain and judgment remain distinct.

Isaiah 28:9
To whom did we announce evils, and to whom did we announce a message—you weaned from milk, torn from a breast?
  Hebrews 5:12-14 — related milk imagery for recipients of instruction: Isaiah addresses the recipients of a message as people weaned from milk; Hebrews describes learners who still need milk rather than solid food. Both use infancy and feeding to describe an instructional audience, but their stated stages differ.

Isaiah 28:10
Expect affliction upon affliction, hope upon hope, yet a little, yet a little,
  Isaiah 28:13 — same-chapter repetition of the affliction-and-hope refrain: The refrain affliction upon affliction, hope upon hope, and yet a little returns as what the Lord's utterance will become for the people. The later verse supplies the falling, breaking and capture associated with the repeated words.

Isaiah 28:11
because of contemptuous lips, through another tongue, for they will speak to this people,
  1 Corinthians 14:21-22 — explicit scriptural use of speech through other tongues: Paul cites speech to this people through other tongues and other lips, followed by their refusal to listen, and uses it when discussing tongues as a sign. Isaiah supplies the other-tongue wording and continues with refusal to hear in the next verse.

Isaiah 28:12
saying to it, “This is the rest for the hungry, and this is the shattering.” But they did not want to listen.
  Matthew 11:28-30 — related offer of rest to people in need: Isaiah's reported speech names rest for the hungry, but the people refuse to listen; Jesus invites those laboring and burdened to receive His rest. Isaiah's additional language about shattering is not attributed to Jesus' invitation.

Isaiah 28:13
The utterance of the Lord God will become for them affliction upon affliction, hope upon hope, yet a little, yet a little, so that they go and fall backward, come into danger, be shattered and captured.
  Isaiah 8:15 — close sequence of falling, breaking and capture: Both warnings join falling, being broken and being captured. Isaiah 28 connects this outcome with the repeated utterance of affliction and hope; Isaiah 8 places it after the stone-and-trap warning.

Isaiah 28:14
Therefore hear the Lord’s word, afflicted men and rulers of this people in Jerusalem:
  Isaiah 1:10 — parallel summons for rulers to hear the Lord's word: Both directly command rulers to hear the Lord's word. Isaiah 28 identifies Jerusalem's rulers, while Isaiah 1 addresses its rulers as rulers of Sodom; the different descriptions are retained.

Isaiah 28:15
Because you said, “We made a covenant with Hades and agreements with death; if a rushing storm passes, it will certainly not come upon us. We made falsehood our hope and will be sheltered by deceit,”
  Isaiah 30:12-13 — close warning against hope placed in falsehood: Both passages describe reliance on falsehood before announcing the failure of that security. Here the speakers claim shelter through a covenant with death and Hades; Isaiah 30 uses the sudden collapse of a wall.

Isaiah 28:16
therefore thus says the Lord: “Behold, I will place in Zion’s foundations a costly, chosen, precious cornerstone, in her foundations, and whoever trusts in it will certainly not be ashamed.
  1 Peter 2:6 — explicit quotation of Zion's precious cornerstone: First Peter quotes the laying of a chosen, precious cornerstone in Zion and the assurance that the believer will not be ashamed. Its surrounding passage applies the stone imagery to Christ and the community built around Him.
  Romans 9:32-33 — combined stone citation joining trust and stumbling: Romans preserves the stone placed in Zion and the believer's freedom from shame, while combining them with stumbling-stone language. This differs from the costly cornerstone description here and serves Paul's contrast between faith and reliance on works.

Isaiah 28:17
I will make judgment a hope, and My mercy will be weights; those trusting deceit do so in vain, for a storm will certainly not pass you by—
  Proverbs 10:25 — related storm judgment exposing false security: Isaiah warns those trusting deceit that the storm will not pass them by; Proverbs says the impious disappears when a storm passes while the righteous is saved. The shared storm image concerns the failure of ungodly security; Proverbs does not repeat Isaiah's weights metaphor.

Isaiah 28:18
might it also remove your covenant with death? Your hope toward Hades will certainly not remain. If a rushing storm comes, you will become its trampling ground.
  Ezekiel 13:10-14 — parallel overthrow of false protection by a destructive storm: Isaiah questions the covenant with death and declares that hope toward Hades will fail when the rushing storm makes its claimants a trampling ground. Ezekiel exposes false peace through a coated wall torn down by downpour and violent wind. The covenant and wall are different images of unreliable protection.

Isaiah 28:19
When it passes, it will take you; morning after morning it will pass during the day, and at night there will be evil hope. Learn to listen!
  Isaiah 26:8-9 — related night-and-morning language joined to learning under judgment: Isaiah 28 describes the recurring storm by morning, day and night and commands people to learn to listen. Isaiah 26 names the Lord's way as judgment, speaks of seeking Him from night into early morning, and commands the earth's inhabitants to learn righteousness.

Isaiah 28:21
Like a mountain of ungodly people he will rise and be in Gabaon’s ravine. With wrath he will do his deeds, a work of bitterness; his wrath will act strangely, and his bitterness is foreign.”
  Joshua 10:10-14 — related divine intervention at Gabaon or Gibeon: Isaiah locates an extraordinary wrathful act in Gabaon's ravine; Joshua narrates the Lord's intervention, hail and defeat at Gibeon. Joshua separately names Aijalon's ravine, so the comparison does not equate every geographical detail.

Isaiah 28:22
And you, do not rejoice, nor let your bonds grow stronger, because I heard from the Lord Sabaoth completed and shortened deeds, which He will do upon all the earth.
  Isaiah 10:22-23 — close announcement of a completed and shortened judgment: Both passages announce a completed or shortened divine action across the earth or inhabited world. Isaiah 28 speaks of deeds and warns against stronger bonds; Isaiah 10 speaks of a word carried out in righteousness.

Isaiah 28:23
Give ear and hear my voice; pay attention and hear my words.
  Proverbs 4:20 — close instructional summons to attend with the ear: Both speakers ask the hearer to attend to their words and give the ear to instruction. Isaiah introduces the farming illustration; Proverbs addresses a son and continues with keeping the words in the heart.

Isaiah 28:24
Will the plowman plow all day? Or will he prepare seed before working the ground?
  Jeremiah 4:3 — related preparation of ground before sowing: Isaiah asks whether seed is prepared before the ground is worked; Jeremiah commands Judah to break up fallow ground and not sow among thorns. Both join preparing soil with sowing, while Jeremiah directs the agricultural image toward repentance.

Isaiah 28:25
When he has leveled its surface, does he not then sow a little black cumin and cumin, and again sow wheat, barley and emmer within your borders?
  Isaiah 28:24 — same-chapter answer concerning the sequence of cultivation: The preceding question concerns plowing and preparing seed; this verse describes leveling the surface and then sowing named crops. The connection is the question-and-answer movement within the agricultural illustration.

Isaiah 28:26
You will be disciplined by your God’s judgment and will rejoice.
  Psalms 93:12-13 — parallel blessing associated with divine instruction and discipline: Isaiah promises discipline through God's judgment and rejoicing; the psalm calls the person disciplined by the Lord and taught His law happy. The psalm adds relief from evil days, while Isaiah uses the surrounding cultivation illustration.

Isaiah 28:28
will be eaten with bread. For I will not be angry with you for ever, nor will the sound of My bitterness trample you.
