  Psalms 102:9 — close assurance that divine anger will not last forever: Isaiah declares that God will not remain angry forever or trample with bitterness; the psalm likewise says He will not maintain wrath forever. The psalm does not repeat the bread and cultivation language surrounding Isaiah's promise.

Isaiah 28:29
These wonders too came from the Lord Sabaoth. Take counsel; raise up futile consolation!
  Isaiah 30:7 — verbal echo: Both Isaiah texts use the phrase of futile consolation: Isaiah 28:29 ends 'raise up futile consolation!' and Isaiah 30:7 says 'This consolation of yours is futile.'
  Isaiah 8:18 — verbal echo: Both texts speak of wonders coming from the Lord Sabaoth: 'These wonders too came from the Lord Sabaoth' and 'signs and wonders in Israel's house, from the Lord Sabaoth'.

Isaiah 29:1
Woe, city Ariel, against which David fought! Gather produce year after year, for you will eat with Moab.
  2 Samuel 5:6-9 — historical counterpart to David's attack on Jerusalem: Ariel is named as the city against which David fought and is identified with Jerusalem later in the oracle. Second Samuel narrates David's attack on Jerusalem, capture of Zion and settlement there; it does not supply Isaiah's statement about eating with Moab.

Isaiah 29:3
I will surround you like David, set a palisade around you and set towers around you,
  Luke 19:43-44 — parallel announcement of Jerusalem enclosed by siege works: Isaiah describes surrounding the city with a palisade and towers; Jesus warns Jerusalem of enemies building a siege barrier and hemming it in on every side. The shared siege pattern is explicit without identifying the two announcements as one event.

Isaiah 29:4
and your words will be humbled into the earth and sink into the earth. Your voice will be like those who speak from the earth, and your voice will weaken toward the ground.
  Isaiah 8:19 — related imagery of a voice speaking from the earth: Jerusalem's humbled voice is compared with people who speak from the earth; Isaiah 8 names earth-speakers when warning against seeking the dead. The former is a comparison for humiliation, while the latter concerns an illicit practice.

Isaiah 29:5
The wealth of the ungodly will be like dust from a wheel, like drifting chaff, and it will be like a moment, immediately,
  Psalms 1:4 — related chaff imagery for the ungodly and their wealth: Isaiah compares the wealth of the ungodly with drifting chaff and wheel-dust; the psalm compares the ungodly themselves with chaff flung away by wind. The shared dispersal image does not erase Isaiah's specific focus on wealth.

Isaiah 29:6
from the Lord Sabaoth. For there will be a visitation with thunder, earthquake and a great sound, a rushing storm and a consuming flame of fire.
  Psalms 17:8-15 — close depiction of divine visitation through storm and earthquake: Both portray divine intervention with earth shaking, thunder and fire, together with violent atmospheric imagery. Isaiah announces visitation amid the oracle about Ariel; the psalm describes the Lord's angry intervention in the speaker's deliverance.

Isaiah 29:7
Like someone dreaming in sleep will be the wealth of all nations who marched against Ariel, all who marched against Jerusalem, and all assembled against her and afflicting her.
  Job 20:7-9 — parallel disappearance compared with a vanished dream: Isaiah likens the hostile nations' wealth to a sleeper's dream; Zophar describes an apparently established ungodly person vanishing like a dream or nighttime apparition. The comparison concerns transience, not acceptance of every claim in Zophar's speech.

Isaiah 29:8
They will be like people drinking and eating in sleep; when they rise, their dream is futile. As a thirsty person dreams of drinking but wakes still thirsty, his soul having hoped in vain, so will be the wealth of all nations who marched against Mount Zion.
  Psalms 72:19-20 — close image of wicked prosperity dissolving upon awakening: Isaiah's thirsty dreamer wakes still thirsty, illustrating the hostile nations' futile wealth. The psalm describes the lawless becoming desolate and their image counted as nothing like an awakening person's dream.

Isaiah 29:9
Grow faint, lose your senses and suffer a hangover, neither from sikera nor from wine,
  Isaiah 51:21-22 — close description of drunkenness not caused by wine: Both explicitly describe intoxication without wine. Isaiah 29 connects it with the following spirit of stupor; Isaiah 51 places it within the cup-of-anger image and promises removal of that cup.

Isaiah 29:10
because the Lord has made you drink a spirit of stupor, and He will close their eyes and those of their prophets and rulers, those who see hidden things.
  Romans 11:7-8 — scriptural echo of a divinely given spirit of stupor: Romans speaks of God giving a spirit of stupor and eyes not to see while discussing Israel's hardened remainder. Isaiah likewise says the Lord made them drink a spirit of stupor and will close eyes, specifically including prophetic and ruling figures.

Isaiah 29:11
All these sayings will be for you like the words of this sealed book. If they give it to a person who knows how to read, saying, “Read these,” he will say, “I cannot read, for it is sealed.”
  Daniel 12:4 — related sealing of a book that restricts access to its words: Isaiah's literate recipient cannot read because the book is sealed; Daniel is commanded to seal a book until the time of completion. Both connect a sealed book with restricted access, but only Daniel specifies that temporal limit.

Isaiah 29:12
This book will be given into the hands of a person who does not know how to read. Someone will say to him, “Read this,” and he will say, “I do not know how to read.”
  Isaiah 29:11 — same-chapter contrast between two impediments to reading: The literate recipient in the preceding verse cannot read a sealed book; this recipient cannot read because he does not know how. The paired scenes distinguish the book's closure from the reader's inability.

Isaiah 29:13
The Lord said, “This people draws near Me; with their lips they honor Me, but their heart is far from Me. They revere Me in vain, teaching human commands and teachings.
  Matthew 15:7-9 — explicit quotation of lip honor and human commandments: Jesus names Isaiah and quotes the contrast between honoring God with lips and a distant heart, followed by vain worship through human teachings. Matthew applies the words in the dispute over traditions and God's commandment.

Isaiah 29:14
Therefore, behold, I will proceed to remove this people; I will remove them, destroy the wise people’s wisdom and hide the discerning people’s understanding.”
  1 Corinthians 1:19 — explicit quotation concerning the destruction of worldly wisdom: Paul quotes God's destruction of the wise people's wisdom and setting aside of discerning understanding. Isaiah connects this with removing the people; Paul places the quotation within his argument about the word of the cross.

Isaiah 29:15
Woe to those making deep counsel, but not through the Lord! Woe to those making counsel in secret! Their deeds will be in darkness, and they will say, “Who has seen us, and who will know us or what we do?”
  Psalms 93:7-9 — parallel exposure of people who think God cannot see their deeds: Isaiah's secret counselors ask who sees or knows them; the psalm reports wrongdoers saying the Lord will not see or understand and answers by invoking the maker of eyes and ears. Both challenge supposed concealment from God.

Isaiah 29:16
Will you not be reckoned as the potter’s clay? Will the formed thing say to its former, “You did not form me,” or the work to its maker, “You did not make me intelligently”?
  Romans 9:20-21 — close potter-and-formed-object challenge applied to God: Both ask whether what is formed can answer back to its maker, using clay and a potter. Isaiah's objections concern being formed and made intelligently; Romans asks why the object was made this way and develops the potter's authority over vessels.

Isaiah 29:17
Is it not yet a little while until Lebanon is changed like Mount Chermel, and Mount Chermel is reckoned as forest?
  Isaiah 32:15 — close transformation language concerning Carmel and forest: Both include Carmel being reckoned as forest. Isaiah 29 first describes Lebanon changing like Carmel; Isaiah 32 first says Carmel becomes wilderness. The comparison preserves those distinct supplied transformations.

Isaiah 29:18
On that day the deaf will hear a book’s words, and the blind people’s eyes in darkness and mist will see.
  Matthew 11:4-5 — related restoration of blind sight and deaf hearing: Isaiah promises that deaf people will hear a book's words and blind eyes will see; Jesus reports blind people receiving sight and deaf people hearing among His works. Matthew does not include Isaiah's particular reference to the book.

Isaiah 29:19
The poor will exult with joy because of the Lord, and the hopeless among people will be filled with gladness.
  Luke 6:20-21 — related promise of joy for poor and distressed people: Isaiah promises the poor joy in the Lord and gladness for the hopeless; Jesus blesses the poor and promises that present weeping will become laughter. The shared reversal concerns poverty and distress, with different descriptions of the recipients.

Isaiah 29:20
The lawless one has failed, the arrogant one has perished, and those acting lawlessly for evil have been destroyed,
  Psalms 36:35-38 — parallel disappearance of arrogant ungodly power: Isaiah declares the failure of the lawless and the disappearance of the arrogant; the psalm describes an ungodly person raised like Lebanon's cedars who can no longer be found, followed by the destruction of the lawless together.

Isaiah 29:21
and those who cause people to sin by a word. They will make all who reprove at the gates a stumbling block, and have turned the righteous aside through injustices.
  Amos 5:10-12 — close condemnation of hostility to gate-reproof and corrupt justice: Both join mistreatment of someone reproving at the gates with turning aside vulnerable or righteous people in judgment. Amos additionally names payments; Isaiah names people causing sin by a word.

Isaiah 29:22
Therefore thus says the Lord concerning Jacob’s house, which He set apart from Abraham: “Jacob will not now be ashamed, nor will Israel now change his face;
  Isaiah 45:17 — close promise that restored Israel will not be ashamed: Isaiah 29 promises that Jacob will not now be ashamed or Israel change his face; Isaiah 45 promises Israel salvation without shame or abasement. The latter expressly gives the assurance an everlasting scope.

Isaiah 29:23
but when their children see My works, because of Me they will sanctify My name, sanctify Jacob’s Holy One and fear Israel’s God.
  Psalms 21:24 — parallel honoring of God by Jacob's and Israel's descendants: Isaiah's children sanctify Jacob's Holy One and fear Israel's God after seeing His works; the psalm summons all Jacob's seed to glorify the Lord and all Israel's seed to fear Him. The same ancestral names frame reverent praise.

Isaiah 29:24
Those wandering in spirit will know understanding; grumblers will learn obedience, and stammering tongues will learn to speak peace.”
  Isaiah 32:4 — close restoration of understanding and stammering speech: Both connect renewed understanding or attentive minds with stammering tongues learning to speak peace. Isaiah 29 also describes grumblers learning obedience; Isaiah 32 describes the heart of the weak paying attention.

Isaiah 30:1
“Woe, rebellious children,” thus says the Lord, “you made a plan not through Me and agreements not through My spirit, to add sins upon sins,
  Hosea 8:4 — parallel political action undertaken without God: Isaiah condemns plans and agreements made without God or His Spirit; Hosea says they reigned for themselves rather than through God and did not make their rule known to Him. The political acts differ, but their exclusion of God is explicit.

Isaiah 30:2
you who go down to Egypt, but they did not ask Me, to be helped by Pharaoh and sheltered by Egyptians.
  Deuteronomy 17:16 — related warning against returning to Egypt for military reliance: Isaiah condemns going to Egypt for Pharaoh's help without asking God; Deuteronomy forbids a ruler to return the people to Egypt in order to multiply horses. The legal rule and prophetic rebuke address related Egyptian dependence without identical wording.

Isaiah 30:3
For Pharaoh’s shelter will become your shame, and reproach for those trusting in Egypt.
  Ezekiel 29:6-7 — parallel failure of Egypt as Israel's support: Isaiah says reliance on Pharaoh and Egypt will bring shame; Ezekiel depicts Egypt as a reed staff that shatters when Israel leans on it and injures those relying on it. Both expose Egyptian support as harmful rather than secure.

Isaiah 30:4
For there are leaders in Tanis, evil messengers; they will labor in vain
  Isaiah 19:11-13 — close criticism of Tanis's leadership: Isaiah 30 names leaders in Tanis and evil messengers laboring vainly; Isaiah 19 calls Tanis's rulers fools and describes failed counsel that leads Egypt astray. The common place and failure of its leaders support the comparison.

Isaiah 30:5
toward a people that will not benefit them, neither for assistance nor for profit, but for shame and reproach.”
  Jeremiah 2:36-37 — close warning of shame from reliance on Egypt: Isaiah warns that the approached people will provide shame instead of assistance; the surrounding verses identify Egypt. Jeremiah explicitly predicts shame from Egypt and says the Lord has rejected that hope.

Isaiah 30:6
The vision of the four-footed creatures in the wilderness. In affliction and hardship, a lion and lion cub from there, asps and offspring of flying asps; they carried their wealth on donkeys and camels to a nation that will not benefit them with help, but with shame and reproach.
  Deuteronomy 8:15 — related perilous wilderness journey among serpents: Isaiah places the wealth-bearing journey amid wilderness hardship, asps and flying-asps' offspring; Deuteronomy recalls Israel led through a fearful wilderness with biting serpents, scorpions and thirst. The journeys and named creatures differ; neither passage supplies all the other's details.

Isaiah 30:7
Egyptians will benefit you with futile and empty things. Announce to them, “This consolation of yours is futile.”
  Psalms 59:13-14 — parallel contrast between futile human assistance and divine help: Isaiah calls Egypt's proposed benefit empty and its consolation futile; the psalm calls human salvation vain while seeking God's help. Isaiah names a particular foreign helper, whereas the psalm states the contrast more generally.

Isaiah 30:8
Now then, sit and write these things on a tablet and in a book, because these things will endure for days of appointed times and for ever.
  Habakkuk 2:2-3 — close instruction to preserve a message on a tablet for its appointed time: Both command a written record on a tablet and associate the message with a future appointed season. Isaiah additionally names a book and enduring preservation; Habakkuk specifies a clear inscription on boxwood and the vision's movement toward its end.

Isaiah 30:9
For this is a disobedient people, lying sons who did not want to hear God’s law,
  Zechariah 7:11-12 — close refusal to hear God's law and prophetic words: Isaiah calls the people disobedient and unwilling to hear God's law; Zechariah describes people refusing to listen and making their hearts disobedient toward the law and Spirit-sent prophetic words. The following Isaiah verse likewise introduces rejected prophets.

Isaiah 30:10
who say to the prophets, “Do not announce to us,” and to those seeing visions, “Do not speak to us. Instead, speak to us and announce another deception,
  Amos 2:11-12 — close command forbidding prophets to prophesy: Isaiah's people tell prophets not to announce their message; Amos explicitly reports the command that prophets must not prophesy. Isaiah goes further by requesting another deception instead.
  1 Kings 22:13-14 — related pressure to replace an unwelcome prophetic message: Isaiah's audience asks for a different deceptive announcement; the messenger to Micaiah asks him to agree with favorable royal predictions. Micaiah's insistence on speaking the Lord's word supplies a contrasting response to pressure.

Isaiah 30:11
and turn us from this way, remove this path from us, and remove Israel’s Holy One from us.”
  Job 21:14-15 — close rejection of God together with His way: Isaiah's speakers ask that the path and Israel's Holy One be removed; Job describes someone telling the Lord to depart and refusing knowledge of His ways. Both associate rejection of God with refusal of His way, without treating Job's reported speech as an instruction.

Isaiah 30:12
Therefore thus says the Lord, Israel’s Holy One: “Because you disobeyed these words and hoped in falsehood, and because you grumbled and came to trust in this word,
  Isaiah 28:15 — close reliance on falsehood as supposed protection: Isaiah 30 condemns disobedience, hope in falsehood and trust in what it calls this word; Isaiah 28 reports people making falsehood their hope and deceit their shelter. Both warnings expose the claimed basis of security before announcing its collapse.

Isaiah 30:13
therefore this sin will be for you like a wall falling immediately in a captured fortified city, whose fall is immediately at hand,
  Ezekiel 13:10-14 — close use of a collapsing wall for deceptive security: Isaiah compares the people's sin to the imminent collapse of a wall; Ezekiel pictures false assurances of peace as a coated wall that God tears down. Both make the wall's fall disclose the failure of what people trusted.

Isaiah 30:14
and its fall will be like the shattering of an earthen vessel, fine pieces from a jar, so that no shard is found among them with which to lift fire or draw a little water.”
  Jeremiah 19:10-11 — close judgment image of an irreparably shattered earthen vessel: Isaiah describes a jar shattered so completely that no useful shard remains; Jeremiah smashes a jar to depict the people and city broken like a vessel that cannot be healed. Their accounts share the vessel and irreparable judgment while differing in the shard detail.

Isaiah 30:15
Thus says the Lord, Israel’s Holy One: “When you turn back and groan, then you will be saved and know where you were. When you trusted in futile things, your strength became futile.” But you did not want to listen,
  Hosea 14:2-5 — related summons to return while relinquishing futile supports: Isaiah connects turning back with salvation and rebukes strength made futile by false trust; Hosea summons Israel to return and reject Assyria, horses and handmade gods. Hosea adds God's promise of healing and openly declared love.

Isaiah 30:16
but you said, “We will flee on horses.” Therefore you will flee. You said, “We will ride swift mounts.” Therefore those pursuing you will be swift.
  Amos 2:14-15 — close failure of swift flight and horses to save: Isaiah answers confidence in horses and swift mounts with flight and swift pursuers; Amos says the runner's flight and the horseman's attempt to save his life will fail. Both deny that speed and mounted strength secure escape.

Isaiah 30:17
At the voice of one, a thousand will flee; at the voice of five, many will flee, until you are left like a mast upon a mountain, like someone carrying a standard upon a hill.
  Deuteronomy 32:30 — close numerical picture of many fleeing before very few: Isaiah says a thousand flee at one voice and many at five; Deuteronomy asks how one pursues a thousand and two put tens of thousands to flight unless God has delivered them up. The numerical disproportion corresponds without harmonizing the second number.

Isaiah 30:18
Again God will wait to pity you, and therefore He will be exalted to have mercy on you, because the Lord our God is a judge. Where will you leave your glory? Blessed are those remaining in Him.
  2 Peter 3:9 — related divine delay directed toward mercy: Isaiah says God waits to show pity and is exalted to have mercy; Second Peter explains the Lord's apparent delay as patience aimed at repentance rather than people's perishing. The latter concerns the promised coming rather than Isaiah's immediate restoration setting.

Isaiah 30:19
For a holy people will dwell in Zion, and Jerusalem wept bitterly, “Have mercy on me!” He will have mercy on you at the sound of your cry; when He saw, He listened to you.
  Psalms 101:17-22 — close hearing of distressed prayer in Zion's restoration: Isaiah describes Jerusalem's bitter plea and God's hearing and mercy; the psalm joins rebuilding Zion with attention to lowly prayer and hearing the groaning of captives, so His name is proclaimed in Zion and Jerusalem.

Isaiah 30:20
The Lord will give you bread of affliction and scant water; those misleading you will certainly no longer approach you, for your eyes will see those misleading you,
  1 Kings 22:27 — close language of bread of affliction: Isaiah names bread of affliction and scant water; the order concerning Micaiah names bread and water of affliction. The expressions connect severe deprivation, while the agents and the source's additional warning about misleaders differ.

Isaiah 30:21
and your ears will hear the words of those behind you who led you astray, saying, “This is the way; let us walk in it, whether right or left.”
  Jeremiah 23:32 — parallel speech from people who lead others astray: The surrounding Isaiah wording identifies the speakers behind the hearers as those who led them astray, even as they announce a way to walk. Jeremiah condemns false dreamers whose spoken lies lead the people astray. This preserves Isaiah's misleaders rather than converting them into faithful teachers.

Isaiah 30:22
You will remove the silver-covered and gold-covered idols, reduce them to fine pieces and winnow them like a menstruating woman’s fluid; like dung you will push them away.
  Exodus 32:20 — close destruction and dispersal of a manufactured idol: Isaiah describes removing silver- and gold-covered idols, reducing them to fine pieces and winnowing them away; Moses grinds the calf fine and scatters it upon water. Exodus additionally describes burning and drinking, which are not imported into Isaiah.

Isaiah 30:23
Then there will be rain for your land’s seed; bread from your land’s produce will be plentiful and rich, and on that day your livestock will graze a fertile, spacious place,
  Leviticus 26:4-5 — close promise of rain, produce and abundant bread: Both join rain for the land with plentiful produce and satisfying bread. Isaiah additionally describes a fertile spacious pasture for livestock; Leviticus links its agricultural abundance with obedience to God's ordinances.

Isaiah 30:24
your bulls and cattle working the earth will eat straw prepared with barley and winnowed.
  Deuteronomy 25:4 — related provision of food for working cattle: Isaiah's cattle that work the earth receive prepared straw and barley; Deuteronomy forbids muzzling an ox while it treads grain. The former describes provision and the latter regulates labor, but both concern feeding cattle engaged in agricultural work.

Isaiah 30:25
On every high mountain and every lofty hill there will be flowing water on that day, when many perish and towers fall.
  Joel 4:18 — related promised water flowing across the restored land: Isaiah promises flowing water on high mountains and hills; Joel describes flowing water at Judah's outlets and a spring from the Lord's house. Both place the new flow on the announced day, with different terrain and sources of water.

Isaiah 30:26
The moon’s light will be like the sun’s light, and the sun’s light will be sevenfold on the day when the Lord heals His people’s fracture; He will heal the pain of your wound.
  Isaiah 60:19-20 — related transformation of light in the people's restoration: Isaiah 30 intensifies the moon's and sun's light on the day God heals His people; Isaiah 60 makes the Lord the everlasting light and promises an end to mourning. The latter does not repeat the sevenfold solar measure or treat the two descriptions as identical.

Isaiah 30:27
Behold, the Lord’s name comes after a long time, wrath burning. His lips’ utterance comes with glory, an utterance full of anger, and the anger of His wrath will consume like fire.
  Jeremiah 5:14 — related divine utterance accompanied by consuming fire: Isaiah joins the Lord's angry utterance with wrath consuming like fire; Jeremiah receives God's words as fire in his mouth, with the people as wood to be devoured. Both associate divine speech with consuming fire; Jeremiah explicitly makes the words themselves fire.

Isaiah 30:28
His spirit will come like water sweeping through a ravine, reaching the neck, and will divide to trouble nations in futile wandering. Delusion will pursue them and seize them in their faces.
  Isaiah 8:7-8 — related divine judgment portrayed as an overwhelming flood: Isaiah 30 likens God's spirit to water sweeping through a ravine to the neck; Isaiah 8 describes the Lord bringing a mighty river identified with Assyria's king, whose encampment fills the country. Isaiah 8 does not contain the neck detail in this supplied wording.

Isaiah 30:29
Must you always rejoice and always enter My holy places like festival-keepers, like people rejoicing as they enter with a flute to the Lord’s mountain, to Israel’s God?
  Psalms 41:5 — related festive approach to God's holy place: Isaiah asks about continual festival-like entry with rejoicing and a flute to the Lord's mountain; the psalm speaks of passing to God's house with exultation, thanksgiving and a celebrating sound. The question is not converted into the psalmist's declaration of intention.

Isaiah 30:30
God will make His voice’s glory heard and show His arm’s wrath with fury, anger and consuming flame. He will strike violently with lightning, like water and hail falling together violently.
  Psalms 17:13-15 — close divine voice accompanied by hail, fire and lightning: Isaiah joins God's glorious voice with consuming flame, lightning, water and hail; the psalm likewise joins hail and fire with the Lord's thunderous voice and multiplied lightning. These are related descriptions of His forceful intervention.

Isaiah 30:31
For at the Lord’s voice the Assyrians will be defeated by the blow with which He strikes them.
  2 Kings 19:35-36 — historical counterpart to the Lord's defeat of Assyria: Isaiah announces the Assyrians' defeat by the Lord's voice and blow; Kings narrates the Lord's angel striking their camp and Sennacherib withdrawing. The narrative supplies the angelic action rather than explicitly repeating the voice image.

Isaiah 30:32
It will be all around him, from where he hoped for help, on which he himself relied; they will turn and fight him with flutes and a lyre.
  2 Chronicles 20:21-23 — related battle scene joined with music: Isaiah describes opponents turning to fight with flutes and a lyre; Chronicles places appointed singers and thanksgiving before the enemy forces turn upon one another. The campaigns and musical descriptions differ, so this is a related conjunction of battle and music rather than an identification.

Isaiah 30:33
For you will be called to account ahead of time. Has it also been prepared for you to rule a deep ravine, with wood laid out, fire and much wood? The Lord’s wrath is like a ravine burning with sulfur.
  Genesis 19:24-25 — parallel divine judgment described with fire and sulfur: Isaiah compares the Lord's wrath with a ravine burning with sulfur; Genesis narrates sulfur and fire from the Lord overthrowing Sodom and Gomorrah. The ravine and cities remain distinct, and Isaiah's unnamed location is not replaced with either city.

Isaiah 31:1
Woe to those going down to Egypt for help, trusting in horses and chariots because there are many, and in horses, a very great multitude, but not trusting Israel’s Holy One or seeking God!
  Psalms 19:8-9 — close contrast between chariot-and-horse confidence and reliance on God: Isaiah rebukes trust in Egypt's many horses and chariots instead of Israel's Holy One; the psalm contrasts reliance on chariots and horses with being magnified in the Lord's name. Both explicitly oppose military equipment to divine reliance.
  Deuteronomy 17:16 — related royal prohibition behind Egyptian horse reliance: Deuteronomy forbids multiplying horses by returning the people to Egypt; Isaiah condemns going down to Egypt for help and trusting its numerous horses. The law supplies a specific Egypt-and-horses prohibition, while Isaiah adds the failure to seek God.

Isaiah 31:2
He Himself, wise, brought evils upon them; His word will certainly not be set aside. He will rise against evil people’s houses and against their futile hope,
  Isaiah 46:10-11 — close assurance that divine counsel and word stand: Isaiah 31 says God's word will not be set aside as He acts against evil; Isaiah 46 declares that all His counsel will stand and that He has spoken and acted. The different announced actions share the certainty of His effective word.

Isaiah 31:3
an Egyptian human being and not God, horses’ flesh, with no help. The Lord will bring His hand against them; the helpers will grow weary, and all will perish together.
  Jeremiah 17:5-7 — close rejection of fleshly human support in place of God: Isaiah contrasts Egypt's human being and horses' flesh with God and denies their help; Jeremiah curses hope placed in a human and the flesh of his arm when the heart leaves the Lord. Jeremiah also states the contrasting blessing of trust in God.

Isaiah 31:4
For thus the Lord said to me: “As a lion or lion cub cries over prey it has caught and shouts over it until the mountains fill with its voice, and they are defeated and terrified by the greatness of its wrath, so the Lord Sabaoth will descend to march upon Mount Zion, upon her mountains.
  Hosea 5:14 — related portrayal of the Lord as a fierce lion: Isaiah compares the Lord's descent upon Zion's mountains with a lion crying over prey; Hosea portrays the Lord as a lion to Judah's house that seizes beyond rescue. The passages share the fierce predator image without equating the purposes of the two interventions.

Isaiah 31:5
Like flying birds, so the Lord will shield Jerusalem, rescue, preserve and save.”
  Matthew 23:37 — close avian image of protection for Jerusalem: Isaiah compares the Lord shielding and saving Jerusalem with flying birds; Jesus describes His wish to gather Jerusalem's children as a hen gathers chicks under her wings. Matthew also records the city's unwillingness, which is absent from Isaiah's assurance here.

Isaiah 31:6
Turn back, you planning a deep and lawless plan.
  Isaiah 55:7 — close summons for lawless planners to return: Isaiah 31 addresses those planning a deep lawless plan and commands them to turn back; Isaiah 55 tells the lawless man to forsake his counsels and return to the Lord for mercy. Both connect repentance with abandoning wrongful plans.

Isaiah 31:7
For on that day people will renounce their silver and golden handmade things, which their hands made.
  Isaiah 2:20 — close abandonment of handmade silver and gold idols: Both announce a day when people reject the silver and gold objects they made. Isaiah 2 names casting them toward worthless ones and bats; Isaiah 31 emphasizes renouncing the works of their hands.
  Exodus 20:23 — legal counterpart to rejection of silver and gold gods: Exodus explicitly forbids making gods of silver or gold; Isaiah announces renunciation of the silver and golden handmade objects. The command identifies the prohibited manufacture, while the prophecy describes abandoning its products.

Isaiah 31:8
Assour will fall. Neither a man’s sword nor a human’s sword will devour him, and he will flee, but not from a sword. The young men will be defeated,
  2 Kings 19:35-36 — historical counterpart to Assyria defeated beyond human weaponry: Isaiah announces Assour falling without a human sword and fleeing; Kings narrates the Lord's angel striking the Assyrian camp and Sennacherib withdrawing. The target concerns the camp's supernatural defeat, not the later separate account of the king's death by his sons.

Isaiah 31:9
for they will be enclosed by a rock as by a palisade and defeated, and the fugitive will be captured. Thus says the Lord: “Blessed is the one who has seed in Zion and household members in Jerusalem.”
  Psalms 127:5-6 — related blessing of family life in Zion and Jerusalem: Isaiah blesses the person with seed in Zion and household members in Jerusalem; the psalm joins blessing from Zion, Jerusalem's good things and seeing children's children. The familial and geographical elements correspond without repeating the preceding military imagery.

Isaiah 32:1
For behold, a righteous king will reign, and rulers will rule with judgment.
  Jeremiah 23:5-6 — close promise of a righteous king exercising judgment: Isaiah announces a righteous king and rulers judging rightly; Jeremiah announces a righteous rising for David, a king doing judgment and righteousness. Jeremiah expressly supplies the Davidic connection and the name Josedek, which this Isaiah verse does not name.

Isaiah 32:2
The man will conceal his words and hide as from rushing water; he will appear in Zion like a rushing river, glorious in a thirsty land.
  Isaiah 41:18 — related image of abundant water in a thirsty land: The man's appearance in Zion is compared with a rushing river glorious in a thirsty land; Isaiah 41 promises rivers, springs and watercourses where land is thirsty. The comparison concerns the water image, not the source's separate statements about concealed words and hiding.

Isaiah 32:3
They will no longer trust in human beings, but will give their ears to listen.
  Psalms 145:3-5 — close refusal to place trust in human beings: Isaiah says the people will cease trusting human beings and listen; the psalm commands against trusting rulers and human beings without salvation and blesses hope in God. The psalm adds human mortality as its reason.

Isaiah 32:4
The heart of the weak will pay attention and listen, and stammering tongues will quickly learn to speak peace.
  Isaiah 29:24 — close renewed understanding and stammering tongues speaking peace: Both associate changed understanding with stammering tongues learning to speak peace. Isaiah 32 speaks of weak hearts paying attention; Isaiah 29 additionally describes wandering spirits and grumblers learning obedience.

Isaiah 32:5
They will no longer tell the fool to rule, and your attendants will no longer say, “Be silent.”
  Ecclesiastes 10:6 — contrast concerning a fool's elevation to authority: Isaiah promises that people will no longer tell the fool to rule; Ecclesiastes observes the evil of a fool being placed at great heights. The prophecy reverses a kind of misordering described by the wisdom observation.

Isaiah 32:6
For the fool will speak foolish things, and his heart will think futile things, carrying out lawless acts and speaking deception toward the Lord, scattering hungry souls and making thirsty souls empty.
  Psalms 13:1-3 — close connection of foolish heart, corrupt speech and wrongdoing: Isaiah describes the fool's heart thinking futile things and speaking deception toward the Lord amid lawless acts; the psalm joins the fool's heart denying God with corrupt practices and deceitful tongues. Isaiah additionally specifies harm to hungry and thirsty souls.

Isaiah 32:7
For evil people’s counsel will devise lawless things, to destroy the lowly with unjust words and scatter the lowly people’s words in judgment.
  Exodus 23:6-8 — legal contrast to unjust words that destroy the lowly: Isaiah condemns counsel that destroys lowly people through unjust words in judgment; Exodus forbids perverting a poor person's case, unrighteous charges and gifts that corrupt righteous words. The law directly opposes the judicial abuse described.

Isaiah 32:8
The devout made wise plans, and this counsel will remain.
  Proverbs 19:21 — related permanence of wise or divine counsel: Isaiah says the devout person's wise counsel will remain; Proverbs contrasts many human reasonings with the Lord's counsel remaining forever. The holders of the counsel differ; the comparison does not identify every human plan with God's counsel.

Isaiah 32:9
Wealthy women, rise and hear my voice! Daughters in hope, hear my words!
  Isaiah 3:16-24 — related warning to affluent daughters before humiliation: Isaiah 32 addresses wealthy women and daughters living in hope before announcing grief and sackcloth; Isaiah 3 addresses Zion's exalted daughters and describes the removal of luxurious adornment. The shared setting is confident women confronted with the loss of comfort.

Isaiah 32:10
Remember the days of a year in pain with hope; the vintage has been exhausted, the sowing has ceased and will certainly no longer come.
  Joel 1:10-12 — close lament over exhausted vintage and failed cultivation: Isaiah announces the end of the vintage and sowing; Joel describes damaged grain, dried wine, a perished harvest and a dried vine. Both describe ruined agricultural provision; Joel also says human beings put joy to shame.

Isaiah 32:11
Be dismayed, grieve, you confident women; undress, become naked and wrap sackcloth around your waists,
  Joel 1:13 — close call to mourning through sackcloth: Isaiah calls confident women to grieve, undress and wrap sackcloth around their waists; Joel commands priestly ministers to lament and sleep in sackcloth because offerings have ceased. The recipients differ, but the prescribed mourning garment and agricultural context correspond.

Isaiah 32:12
and beat your breasts because of the desirable field and fruitful vine.
  Joel 1:11-13 — close breast-beating and lament over damaged fields and vines: Isaiah commands breast-beating over the desirable field and fruitful vine; Joel laments the failed field harvest and dried vine and commands priests to beat their breasts. The same mourning action is tied to agricultural loss, without equating the two addressed groups.

Isaiah 32:13
My people’s earth will grow thorn and grass, and joy will be removed from every house. Wealthy city,
  Isaiah 5:5-7 — close growth of thorns upon the people's devastated land: Isaiah 32 says the people's earth will grow thorns and grass as joy leaves houses; the vineyard oracle announces abandonment and thorns and identifies the vineyard with Israel's house. The latter supplies a vineyard parable rather than the city's houses.

Isaiah 32:14
houses abandoned! They will leave the city’s wealth and desirable houses; villages will become caves for ever, wild donkeys’ delight, pastures for shepherds,
  Jeremiah 9:10 — parallel abandoned settlements becoming animal habitat: Isaiah depicts abandoned houses and villages becoming caves and wild donkeys' delight; Jeremiah announces Jerusalem as a dwelling of dragons and Judah's cities without inhabitants. The animals and locations retain their distinct descriptions.

Isaiah 32:15
until a spirit from on high comes upon you. Chermel will become wilderness, and Chermel will be reckoned as forest.
  Acts 1:8 — related promise of a spirit coming upon the hearers: Isaiah says a spirit from on high will come upon the people; Jesus promises power when the Holy Spirit comes upon His disciples for witness. Acts does not repeat Isaiah's supplied transformation of Carmel into wilderness and forest.

Isaiah 32:16
Judgment will rest in the wilderness, and righteousness will dwell in Carmel.
  Isaiah 33:5 — close righteousness and judgment filling a restored place: Isaiah 32 places judgment in the wilderness and righteousness in Carmel; Isaiah 33 describes Zion filled with judgment and righteousness. The same paired qualities characterize different named places within the restoration language.

Isaiah 32:17
The works of righteousness will be peace; righteousness will secure rest, and they will be confident for ever.
  James 3:17-18 — close connection between righteousness and peace: Isaiah makes peace the work of righteousness and associates righteousness with enduring rest; James describes a fruit of righteousness sown in peace for peacemakers. The texts connect the same qualities while describing their relation in different directions.

Isaiah 32:18
His people will dwell in a city of peace and live confidently, and they will rest with wealth.
  Ezekiel 34:25-28 — close promise of peaceful dwelling without fear: Isaiah promises a city of peace, confident living and restful wealth; Ezekiel promises peaceful dwelling on the land, sleep in the woods and freedom from terror. The settings differ, but secure habitation is the shared promised result.

Isaiah 32:19
If hail comes down, it will not come upon you. Those dwelling in the forests will be confident like those in the plain.
  Exodus 9:25-26 — historical counterpart to protection from destructive hail: Isaiah promises that descending hail will not come upon the addressed people; Exodus distinguishes hail-struck Egypt from Goshen, where the sons of Israel experienced no hail. The comparison concerns a protected population, not identity of the two storms.

Isaiah 32:20
Blessed are those sowing beside every water, where ox and donkey tread!
  Psalms 1:1-3 — related blessing expressed through growth beside water: Isaiah blesses those sowing beside every water; the psalm calls the person devoted to God's law blessed and likens him to a tree planted beside water outlets. The shared watered-growth image differs from Isaiah's sowers, ox and donkey.

Isaiah 33:1
Woe to those afflicting you—yet no one afflicts you—and the one rejecting you does not reject. The rejecters will be captured and handed over; like a moth upon a cloak, so they will be defeated.
  Isaiah 50:9 — close moth-and-garment image for the defeat of opponents: Isaiah 33 ends by comparing the rejecters' defeat with a moth upon a cloak; Isaiah 50 addresses those who might harm the speaker as growing old like a garment and being devoured like a moth. The recurring garment-and-moth comparison is specific, without resolving the unusual afflicting and rejecting clauses earlier in Isaiah 33:1.

Isaiah 33:2
Lord, have mercy on us, for we have trusted in Thee. The disobedient people’s seed came to destruction, but our salvation is in a time of affliction.
  Psalms 36:39-40 — close salvation in affliction for those who trust the Lord: Isaiah seeks mercy because the speakers trust God and names salvation in affliction; the psalm calls the Lord the righteous people's defender in affliction and says He saves them because they hope in Him. Isaiah separately contrasts a disobedient seed's destruction.

Isaiah 33:3
At the sound of Thy terror, peoples were dismayed by fear of Thee, and nations scattered.
  Psalms 17:14-15 — related divine sound joined with the scattering of opponents: Isaiah describes peoples dismayed and nations scattered at God's terrifying sound; the psalm joins the Lord's thunderous voice with arrows that scatter and lightning that throws opponents into turmoil. The psalm names weapons and lightning that this verse leaves unstated.

Isaiah 33:5
Holy is God, who dwells on high; Zion has been filled with judgment and righteousness.
  Psalms 98:2-5 — close exaltation of the holy God with judgment and righteousness in Zion: Both connect the Lord's holiness and exaltation with Zion and the paired qualities judgment and righteousness. The psalm speaks of what He has done in Jacob; Isaiah describes Zion filled with those qualities.

Isaiah 33:6
They will be handed over under law; our salvation is among treasures. There are wisdom, knowledge and reverence toward the Lord; these are treasures of righteousness.
  Proverbs 2:4-6 — close association of treasure with wisdom, knowledge and fear of the Lord: Isaiah names wisdom, knowledge and reverence toward the Lord among treasures of righteousness; Proverbs tells the hearer to seek wisdom like treasure and promises knowledge of God and understanding of His fear. The treasury image and three associated gifts connect the texts.

Isaiah 33:7
Behold, in your terror they themselves will fear; those you feared will fear you. For messengers will be sent asking for peace, weeping bitterly, pleading for peace.
  Luke 14:31-32 — related delegation seeking peace under threat of war: Isaiah describes fearful messengers pleading and weeping for peace; Jesus' comparison describes a king sending a delegation for peace when facing a stronger force. Luke's scene is illustrative and does not include Isaiah's tears.

Isaiah 33:9
The earth mourned; Lebanon was ashamed; Sharon became marshes. Galilee and Carmel will be clearly visible.
  Isaiah 24:4 — close personification of the earth mourning: Both explicitly say that the earth mourned in an account of devastation. Isaiah 33 adds Lebanon's shame, Sharon becoming marshes and Galilee and Carmel becoming visible; those details are not replaced by Isaiah 24's description of the inhabited world's destruction.

Isaiah 33:10
“Now I will rise,” says the Lord. “Now I will be glorified; now I will be exalted.
  Psalms 11:6 — close divine declaration that now He will arise: Both place the words now I will arise in the Lord's mouth. Isaiah adds His glorification and exaltation; the psalm specifies the misery and groaning of poor and needy people as the occasion for intervention.

Isaiah 33:11
Now you will see, now you will perceive; your spirit’s strength will be futile. Fire will consume you.
  Psalms 20:10 — parallel consuming fire against opponents under divine wrath: Isaiah warns that the strength of the hearers' spirit will be futile and fire will consume them; the psalm describes enemies made like a fiery furnace as the Lord's anger throws them into turmoil and fire devours them. The target does not repeat Isaiah's statement about their spirit.

Isaiah 33:12
Nations will be burned, like a thorn thrown in a field and burned.
  2 Samuel 23:5-7 — close image of opponents burned like discarded thorns: Isaiah compares the burning of nations with a thorn thrown in a field and burned; David's concluding words describe the lawless through cast-out thorn imagery and their burning in shame. The shared combustible-thorn comparison preserves the different addressed groups.

Isaiah 33:13
Those far away will hear what I did; those drawing near will know My strength.”
  Psalms 65:5-7 — related recognition of God's deeds and power: Isaiah predicts that distant hearers will hear His deeds and those near will know His strength; the psalm invites people to see God's works and describes His power and regard for nations. The psalm supplies sea-and-river examples rather than Isaiah's near-and-far distinction.

Isaiah 33:14
The lawless in Zion departed; trembling will seize the ungodly. Who will announce to you that fire burns? Who will announce to you the everlasting place?
  Hebrews 12:28-29 — related fear before divine fire: Isaiah describes trembling among the ungodly and asks who will announce the burning fire and everlasting place; Hebrews urges reverence and awe because God is a consuming fire. The comparison does not recast Isaiah's question as one about dwelling in everlasting burnings.

Isaiah 33:15
One walking in righteousness, speaking a straight way, hating lawlessness and injustice, shaking his hands free of gifts, making his ears heavy so as not to hear a judgment of blood, and closing his eyes so as not to see injustice,
  Psalms 14:1-5 — close ethical description of the person permitted secure dwelling: Both describe righteous walking, truthful or straight speech and refusal of gifts associated with injustice. The psalm asks who can dwell with God, while Isaiah's following verse promises the described person secure rocky shelter.
  Exodus 23:6-8 — legal counterpart to refusing gifts and bloodshed in judgment: Isaiah's righteous person shakes off gifts and refuses a judgment of blood; Exodus forbids unjust charges, killing the innocent and receiving gifts that corrupt justice. The law supplies concrete prohibitions corresponding to the ethical portrait.

Isaiah 33:16
this one will dwell in a high cave of strong rock. Bread will be given him, and his water is trustworthy.
  Psalms 26:5 — close divine provision of elevated rocky shelter: Isaiah promises a high cave of strong rock for the righteous person; the psalm describes the Lord hiding the speaker in trouble and raising him upon a rock. The psalm uses a tent as well, whereas Isaiah adds assured bread and water.
  1 Kings 17:3-6 — historical example of protected life supplied with bread and water: Isaiah promises bread and trustworthy water in secure shelter; the Lord sends Elijah into hiding, provides water from a wadi and commands ravens that bring bread. The narrative supplies a particular example of divine sustenance rather than identifying Elijah with Isaiah's unnamed person.

Isaiah 33:17
You will see a king with glory, and your eyes will see a land from afar.
  Isaiah 6:1-5 — related sight of a glorious king: Isaiah 33 promises sight of a king with glory; Isaiah 6 describes the Lord enthroned amid glory and explicitly says the prophet's eyes saw the King, the Lord Sabaoth. The later promise does not repeat that full identification in its own sentence.

Isaiah 33:18
Your soul will ponder fear: “Where are the scholars? Where are the counselors? Where is the one counting those being fed,
  1 Corinthians 1:20 — close rhetorical questioning of learned authorities: Isaiah asks where scholars, counselors and the one counting those fed are; Paul asks where the wise person, scribe and debater are when exposing worldly wisdom. The repeated where-questions address learned authority while retaining the different lists.

Isaiah 33:19
a people small and great?” With whom they did not deliberate, nor did he know a deep voice, so that a despised people would not hear; the hearer has no understanding.
  Ezekiel 3:5-6 — shared image: Both texts describe deep speech that hearers cannot understand: Isaiah speaks of not knowing 'a deep voice' and of a hearer who 'has no understanding', while Ezekiel speaks of 'a people of deep speech and heavy tongue' whose words 'you will not understand'. Ezekiel applies this to peoples the prophet is not sent to, a different setting.

Isaiah 33:20
Behold, Zion, the city, our salvation! Your eyes will see Jerusalem, a wealthy city, tents that will certainly not be shaken; her tent’s pegs will never be moved, nor her ropes torn.
  Isaiah 54:2-3 — close restoration imagery of a secure city's tent, ropes and pegs: Isaiah 33 describes Jerusalem as unshaken tents with immovable pegs and unbroken ropes; Isaiah 54 commands widening the tent, lengthening ropes and strengthening pegs for expansion. Stability and enlargement are distinct uses of the shared civic tent image.

Isaiah 33:21
For the Lord’s name is great for you. There will be a place for you, rivers and broad, spacious canals. You will not go this way, nor will a rowing ship go.
  Ezekiel 47:1-5 — related abundant water in a holy setting with restricted passage: Isaiah describes broad rivers and canals where people and rowing ships will not pass; Ezekiel's water from the house becomes a torrent that cannot be crossed. Isaiah's excluded traffic is not given Ezekiel's explicit explanation of violently surging water.

Isaiah 33:22
For my God is great; the Lord will not pass me by. The Lord is our judge, the Lord our ruler, the Lord our king; this One will save us.
  James 4:12 — close divine authority to judge and save: Isaiah calls the Lord judge, ruler, king and Savior; James identifies the one Lawgiver and Judge able to save and destroy. Both attribute judgment and saving power to God, with different additional titles and actions.

Isaiah 33:23
Your ropes tore because it was not strong; your mast tilted. It will not lower the sails or raise a signal until it is handed over for plunder. Then many lame people will take spoil.
  Ezekiel 27:26-27 — related maritime collapse exposing strength to loss: Isaiah describes torn ropes, a tilted mast and a vessel handed over for plunder; Ezekiel portrays a great maritime city shattered at sea, its forces and trading company falling with it. The nautical failures correspond without identifying Isaiah's vessel with Tyre.
  1 Samuel 30:21-25 — related participation in spoil despite limited ability to fight: Isaiah promises spoil even to many lame people; David insists that men too weak to continue the pursuit share the spoils equally with fighters. The bodily limitations differ, but both passages include people who could otherwise be excluded from victory's gains.

Isaiah 33:24
The people dwelling among them will certainly not say, “I am weary,” for their sin has been forgiven.
  Psalms 102:3-5 — related forgiveness joined with relief from bodily frailty: Isaiah links the inhabitants' not saying they are weary with forgiven sin; the psalm joins God's gracious treatment of lawless deeds with healing and renewed youth. The comparison preserves Isaiah's weariness rather than replacing it with the psalm's diseases.

Isaiah 34:1
Draw near, nations, and hear, rulers! Let the earth and those in it hear, the inhabited world and the people in it!
  Psalms 48:2-3 — close universal summons to nations and the world's inhabitants: Both call all nations and the world's inhabitants to hear. Isaiah explicitly includes rulers and the earth; the psalm extends the address through rich and poor and different descriptions of human beings.

Isaiah 34:2
For the Lord’s wrath is against all nations, and anger against their number, to destroy them and hand them over to slaughter.
  Zephaniah 3:8 — close universal outpouring of divine wrath upon nations: Isaiah announces the Lord's wrath and slaughter against all nations; Zephaniah describes His judgment upon assembled nations and kings, pouring out furious wrath as the earth is consumed. The forms of destruction differ, but the international scope and divine anger are explicit.

Isaiah 34:3
Their wounded and dead will be thrown out; their odor will rise, and mountains will be soaked with their blood.
  Joel 2:20 — related rising decay and stench after an enemy's defeat: Isaiah describes exposed dead whose odor rises; Joel describes the expelled northern force's decay and stench rising. Isaiah adds mountains soaked with blood, while Joel supplies the force's movement toward the seas and waterless land.

Isaiah 34:4
Heaven will roll up like a scroll, and all the stars will fall like leaves from a vine, like leaves falling from a fig tree.
  Mark 13:24-25 — close judgment imagery of stars falling from heaven: Isaiah describes heaven rolled like a scroll and stars falling like leaves; Jesus' discourse likewise describes stars falling and heavenly powers shaken. Mark does not reproduce the scroll, vine or fig-leaf comparisons.

Isaiah 34:5
My sword became drunk in heaven. Behold, it will descend upon Idumea and upon the people of destruction, with judgment.
  Deuteronomy 32:41-42 — related divine sword carrying out judgment: Isaiah depicts the Lord's sword descending against Idumea; Deuteronomy describes His sharpened sword and repayment against enemies. Deuteronomy makes the arrows drunk with blood, whereas Isaiah says the sword became drunk in heaven.

Isaiah 34:6
The Lord’s sword was filled with blood, thickened with lambs’ fat and the fat of male goats and rams, for the Lord has a sacrifice in Bosor and a great slaughter in Idumea.
  Zephaniah 1:7-8 — close portrayal of divine judgment as the Lord's sacrifice: Isaiah names the Lord's sacrifice and slaughter in Bosor and Idumea; Zephaniah announces His prepared sacrifice and judgment upon rulers and the king's house. Their sacrificial judgment images concern different named settings and add different details.

Isaiah 34:7
The stout ones will fall with them, and rams and bulls; the earth will become drunk with blood and be filled with their fat.
  Ezekiel 39:17-20 — close sacrificial slaughter imagery joining cattle, fat and blood: Isaiah's rams and bulls fall as the earth becomes drunk with blood and full of fat; Ezekiel's great sacrifice joins rulers and warriors with fattened cattle and drinking blood to drunkenness. Isaiah makes the earth drunk and full; Ezekiel gives the eating and drinking to summoned birds and beasts.

Isaiah 34:8
For it is the Lord’s day of judgment and a year of repayment for Zion’s judgment.
  Isaiah 63:1-4 — close pairing of a day of recompense and a year of divine action: Isaiah 34 announces a day of judgment and year of repayment for Zion; Isaiah 63, also naming Edom and Bosor, announces a day of recompense and year of redemption. The repeated time-pair and Edomite setting connect the oracles without equating repayment with redemption.

Isaiah 34:9
Her ravines will turn to pitch, her earth to sulfur, and her land will burn like pitch
  Genesis 19:24-25 — related destruction of land through sulfur and fire: Isaiah transforms ravines and earth into burning pitch and sulfur; Genesis narrates sulfur and fire overthrowing Sodom, Gomorrah and their surrounding country. Genesis does not include pitch or identify its cities with Idumea.

Isaiah 34:10
night and day. It will not be quenched for an everlasting time, and her smoke will rise upward. For generations and a long time she will be deserted.
  Isaiah 66:24 — close judgment fire that will not be quenched: Isaiah 34 describes enduring unquenched fire and smoke over deserted land; Isaiah 66 depicts the unquenched fire associated with the limbs of people who transgressed against God. The comparison concerns unquenchable judgment, not identical locations or victims.

Isaiah 34:11
Birds, hedgehogs, ibises and ravens will dwell in her; a measuring cord for deserted land will be cast upon her, and donkey-centaurs will dwell in her.
  Zephaniah 2:13-15 — close desolated places occupied by wild creatures: Isaiah's desolated territory houses hedgehogs, ravens and other creatures; Zephaniah depicts Nineveh as a wasteland with hedgehogs, ravens and wild animals in its structures. The respective creature lists and places remain distinct.

Isaiah 34:12
Her rulers will be no more, for her kings, her rulers and her magnates will come to destruction.
  Obadiah 1:8-9 — related destruction of Idumea's leading and powerful people: Isaiah announces the disappearance of the land's rulers, kings and magnates; Obadiah announces the loss of Idumea's wise people and terror among Teman's warriors. The same Edomite setting supports the comparison while the classes of leaders differ.

Isaiah 34:13
Thorny trees will sprout in their cities and her fortifications; she will become a dwelling for sirens and a courtyard for ostriches.
  Hosea 9:6 — close thorns growing in abandoned dwellings: Isaiah describes thorny trees rising in cities and fortifications; Hosea predicts thorns in the people's dwellings after their departure. Isaiah additionally names sirens and ostriches, which Hosea does not supply.

Isaiah 34:14
Demons will meet donkey-centaurs and cry one to another. There donkey-centaurs will rest, for they found rest for themselves.
  Isaiah 13:21-22 — close uncanny inhabitants of ruined places: Both passages place demons and donkey-centaurs in desolated territory. Isaiah 34 describes meeting, calling and resting; Isaiah 13 describes demons dancing and donkey-centaurs dwelling in Babylon. The creatures recur without collapsing the different cities or actions.

Isaiah 34:15
There a hedgehog nested, and the earth kept her children safe. There deer met and saw one another’s faces;
  Isaiah 13:22 — close image of hedgehogs nesting amid desolation: Isaiah 34 describes a hedgehog nesting and its children kept safe by the earth; Isaiah 13 names hedgehogs making nests in abandoned houses. Only Isaiah 34 adds the meeting deer and the protection of the young.

Isaiah 34:16
they passed by in number, and not one of them perished; none sought another, for the Lord commanded them and His spirit gathered them.
  Genesis 7:8-9 — related gathering of animals under divine command: Isaiah attributes the animals' complete gathering to the Lord's command and His spirit; Genesis describes animals entering Noah's ark in pairs as God commanded him. The places, purpose and pairing differ, and Genesis does not identify the gathering agency as the Spirit.

Isaiah 34:17
He Himself will cast lots for them, and His hand apportioned grazing. You will inherit for an everlasting time; for generations of generations they will rest upon her.
  Joshua 18:8-10 — related allotment of land through a lot before God: Isaiah depicts God casting lots and apportioning grazing; Joshua's surveyors describe the land in seven portions before Joshua casts lots before the Lord. The comparison concerns the allotment method, not an identification of the different recipients.

Isaiah 35:1
Rejoice, thirsty wilderness! Let the wilderness exult and flower like a lily,
  Hosea 14:6 — close image of restoration as blossoming like a lily: Isaiah summons the thirsty wilderness to flower like a lily; Hosea promises that Israel will blossom like a lily under God's dew. Both use the same flower for renewed life, with different grammatical subjects and means of watering.

Isaiah 35:2
and the Jordan’s desert places will flower and exult. Lebanon’s glory and Carmel’s honor were given to her, and My people will see the Lord’s glory and God’s height.
  Isaiah 60:13 — close transfer of Lebanon's glory to a restored place: Isaiah 35 gives Lebanon's glory to the flowering wilderness; Isaiah 60 says Lebanon's glory will come in named trees to glorify God's holy place. The repeated glory phrase connects the restoration imagery without making the locations identical.
  Isaiah 40:5 — close promise that the Lord's glory will become visible: Isaiah 35 promises that God's people will see the Lord's glory; Isaiah 40 declares that His glory will be seen and all flesh will see God's salvation. The latter extends the audience and explicitly names salvation as the second object seen.

Isaiah 35:3
Be strong, limp hands and paralyzed knees!
  Hebrews 12:12-13 — close exhortation to strengthen weakened hands and knees: Hebrews repeats the paired drooping hands and weakened knees as an exhortation after its discussion of discipline. Isaiah addresses limp hands and paralyzed knees before announcing God's saving intervention; Hebrews adds straight tracks for the feet.

Isaiah 35:4
Give encouragement, you fainthearted in mind. Be strong; do not fear! Behold, our God repays judgment and will repay; He Himself will come and save us.
  Isaiah 40:9-10 — close announcement of God's coming with recompense and reassurance: Both call the hearers not to fear and announce their God coming with strength or repayment. Isaiah 35 expressly promises salvation; Isaiah 40 places the announcement in good news to Zion and the cities of Judah.

Isaiah 35:5
Then blind people’s eyes will open, and deaf people’s ears will hear.
  Matthew 11:4-5 — related restoration of sight and hearing among Jesus' works: Isaiah promises opened blind eyes and hearing ears for the deaf; Jesus reports to John's messengers that blind people receive sight and deaf people hear. Matthew includes other works and does not explicitly name Isaiah in that reply.

Isaiah 35:6
Then the lame person will leap like a deer, and the tongue of those with difficulty speaking will be clear, for water burst out in the wilderness and a ravine in a thirsty land,
  Acts 3:7-8 — narrative counterpart to the lame person leaping: Isaiah announces a lame person leaping like a deer; Acts describes the healed man leaping, standing and walking after Peter raises him in Jesus' name. Acts does not use the deer comparison or include Isaiah's water imagery.
  Mark 7:32-35 — narrative counterpart to difficult speech becoming clear: Isaiah promises that the tongue of those with difficulty speaking will become clear; Mark narrates a man with difficulty speaking whose tongue is loosened and who speaks correctly after Jesus acts. This connection concerns the speech clause rather than the separate lame-person clause.

Isaiah 35:7
the waterless place will become marshes, and a water spring will be in the thirsty land. There will be birds’ gladness, a dwelling of reeds and marshes.
  Isaiah 41:18 — close transformation of dry land into marshes and flowing water: Both describe wilderness or waterless land becoming marshes and thirsty land receiving abundant water. Isaiah 35 additionally gives the birds a glad dwelling of reeds and marshes, while Isaiah 41 names rivers and springs.

Isaiah 35:8
There will be a clean road, called a Holy Way. No unclean person will ever pass there, nor will there be an unclean way there; the scattered will walk upon it and will certainly not wander.
  Isaiah 52:1 — close exclusion of uncleanness from restored holy passage: Isaiah 35 describes a Holy Way on which no unclean person passes; Isaiah 52 tells holy Jerusalem that no uncircumcised and unclean person will pass through her again. The road and city are distinct settings of the shared exclusion.

Isaiah 35:9
No lion will be there, nor will any evil wild beast ever go up on it or be found there; instead, the redeemed will walk in it.
  Ezekiel 34:25-28 — close safety through removal of harmful wild beasts: Isaiah's redeemed travelers encounter no lion or evil wild beast on the way; Ezekiel promises removal of harmful beasts, safe dwelling and freedom from being eaten by the land's animals. The protected journey and settled land remain distinct.

Isaiah 35:10
Those gathered through the Lord will return and come to Zion with gladness, everlasting gladness above their heads; for praise and exultation are upon their heads, and gladness will overtake them. Pain, grief and groaning have fled.
  Isaiah 51:11 — close recurring promise of joyful return to Zion: Both describe people brought back through the Lord, coming to Zion with everlasting gladness, praise upon their heads and the flight of pain, grief and sighing. The sustained sequence establishes a close verbal counterpart rather than a general shared hope.

Isaiah 36:1
In the fourteenth year of Hezekiah’s reign, Sennacherib king of the Assyrians came up against Judea’s fortified cities and took them.
  2 Kings 18:13 — parallel narrative of Sennacherib's capture of Judah's fortified cities: Both date the Assyrian invasion to Hezekiah's fourteenth year and report Sennacherib capturing Judah's fortified cities. The shared ruler, date and action establish the narrative counterpart.

Isaiah 36:2
The king of the Assyrians sent Rapsakes from Lachis to Jerusalem, to King Hezekiah, with a large force. He stood at the upper pool’s aqueduct, on the road of the fuller’s field.
  2 Kings 18:17 — parallel narrative of the Assyrian delegation at the upper pool: Both send the Assyrian spokesman from Lachish to Jerusalem with a large force and place him at the upper pool's aqueduct beside the Fuller's Field road. Kings additionally names two other envoys; Isaiah names Rapsakes alone here.
  Isaiah 7:3 — related reuse of the pool and Fuller's Field meeting place: The Assyrian envoy stands by the pool and Fuller's Field road where Isaiah was earlier sent to meet Achaz. The earlier encounter brings prophetic reassurance, while this scene begins an Assyrian challenge to Hezekiah; the supplied descriptions of the pool and road are retained.

Isaiah 36:3
Eliakim son of Chelkias, the steward, Somnas the scribe, and Joach son of Asaph, the record-keeper, went out to him.
  2 Kings 18:18 — parallel narrative of the three Judean officials meeting the envoy: Both name Eliakim son of Chelkias, Somnas the scribe and Asaph's son as the officials who come out. Isaiah's Joach and Kings' Joash retain their respective supplied name forms and recording office.

Isaiah 36:4
Rapsakes said to them, “Tell Hezekiah: Thus says the great king, the king of the Assyrians: ‘What do you trust in?
  2 Kings 18:19 — parallel challenge to Hezekiah's confidence: Both introduce the spokesman's demand in the name of the great Assyrian king and ask what confidence Hezekiah relies upon. The question opens the same narrated confrontation.

Isaiah 36:5
Does a battle line arise through counsel or words of lips? Now whom have you trusted, that you disobey me?
  2 Kings 18:20 — parallel challenge concerning lip-words, counsel and wartime trust: Both place counsel and words of lips concerning war beside the demand to identify Hezekiah's trusted support. Isaiah frames the first clause as a question about a battle line; Kings reports a claim about counsel and strength.

Isaiah 36:6
Behold, you trust this broken reed staff, Egypt. Whoever leans on it, it will enter his hand. Such are Pharaoh king of Egypt and all who trust in him.
  2 Kings 18:21 — parallel narrative comparison of Egypt with a broken reed staff: Both identify Egypt as a broken reed that enters the hand of anyone leaning on it and apply the comparison to reliance on Pharaoh. The source's warning is part of the Assyrian envoy's speech.
  Ezekiel 29:6-7 — close Egyptian reed-staff image spoken in a different setting: Ezekiel's divine oracle also calls Egypt a reed staff that shatters when Israel leans on it and causes injury. Isaiah places the image in the Assyrian spokesman's rhetoric; the shared image does not endorse everything else he claims.

Isaiah 36:7
But if you say, “We trust the Lord our God,”
  2 Kings 18:22 — parallel introduction of reliance on the Lord as a possible answer: Both introduce the answer that the Judeans trust the Lord their God. Kings continues with an allegation about Hezekiah's removal of high places and altars, which this brief Isaiah unit does not include.

Isaiah 36:8
now join with my master, the king of the Assyrians, and I will give you two thousand horses, if you can provide riders for them.
  2 Kings 18:23 — parallel taunt offering two thousand horses: Both offer two thousand horses from the Assyrian master on condition that the Judeans supply riders. The identical number and condition make the mockery a specific narrative parallel.

Isaiah 36:9
How can you face even one district governor? Those trusting Egyptians for horses and riders are servants.
  2 Kings 18:24 — parallel challenge about one governor and Egyptian military support: Both ask how Hezekiah can face even one district governor and refer to reliance on Egyptian mounted forces. Kings calls the governor one of the master's least servants; Isaiah's additional sentence calls those trusting Egypt for horses and riders servants.

Isaiah 36:10
Now have we come up without the Lord against this country to wage war upon it?’”
  2 Kings 18:25 — parallel rhetorical claim about the Lord's authorization of the invasion: Both ask whether the Assyrians came against the country without the Lord. Kings adds the spokesman's explicit assertion that the Lord told him to attack; Isaiah's supplied verse ends with the rhetorical question.

Isaiah 36:11
Eliakim, Somnas and Joach said to him, “Speak Syrian to your servants, for we understand, and do not speak Judean to us. Why do you speak within hearing of the people on the wall?”
  2 Kings 18:26 — parallel request to use Syrian rather than Judean: Both have the Judean officials request Syrian, which they understand, and object to speaking Judean within the hearing of the people on the wall. The language choice and concern about the listening population correspond closely.

Isaiah 36:12
Rapsakes said to them, “Has my master sent me to your master or to you to speak these words? Was it not to the people sitting on the wall, so that they eat dung and drink urine together with you?”
  2 Kings 18:27 — parallel siege threat directed toward the people on the wall: Both reject the officials' attempt to restrict the message and threaten the wall's population with eating dung and drinking urine alongside them. The degrading food-and-drink threat is retained as the envoy's speech, not approved instruction.

Isaiah 36:13
Rapsakes stood and cried in a loud voice in Judean: “Hear the words of the great king, the king of the Assyrians:
  2 Kings 18:28 — parallel public proclamation in Judean: Both describe the Assyrian spokesman standing and crying loudly in Judean, commanding the population to hear the great king's words. The public choice of language follows the rejected request for Syrian.

Isaiah 36:14
Thus says the king: ‘Do not let Hezekiah deceive you with words, which will not be able to rescue you,
  2 Kings 18:29 — parallel warning not to rely on Hezekiah's words for rescue: Both tell the people not to accept Hezekiah's reassuring words and deny rescue. Isaiah uses deception language, while Kings warns against being lifted up with words and explicitly mentions rescue from the Assyrian king's hand.

Isaiah 36:15
and do not let Hezekiah tell you, “God will rescue you, and this city will certainly not be handed into the king of the Assyrians’ hand.”
  2 Kings 18:30 — parallel rejection of Hezekiah's promised divine deliverance: Both report and reject the claim that God will rescue the people and prevent the city being handed to Assyria. This records the invader's challenge rather than adopting his denial as the narrative's conclusion.

Isaiah 36:16
Do not listen to Hezekiah. Thus says the king of the Assyrians: If you wish to be blessed, come out to me; each will eat from his vine and fig trees, and you will drink your cistern’s water,
  2 Kings 18:31 — parallel offer of vines, figs and cistern water for surrender: Both invite the population to come out to the Assyrian king and promise household produce and cistern water. Isaiah says to eat from the vine and fig trees; Kings says to drink from the vine and eat from the fig tree.
  Micah 4:4 — contrast between an invader's offer and promised undisturbed dwelling: The Assyrian envoy offers each person's vine and fig trees as an inducement to come out; Micah promises people resting beneath their own vine and fig tree without fear, guaranteed by the Lord. The familiar domestic abundance image is voiced by different authorities for different purposes.

Isaiah 36:17
until I come and take you to a land like your land, a land of grain, wine, bread and vineyards.
  2 Kings 18:32 — parallel promise of removal to a land like Judah: Both promise relocation to a land like the people's own, naming grain, wine, bread and vineyards. Kings adds oil-bearing olives, honey and an assurance of life, which Isaiah's shorter list does not include.

Isaiah 36:18
Do not let Hezekiah deceive you, saying, “Your God will rescue you.” Have the nations’ gods each rescued his own country from the king of the Assyrians’ hand?
  2 Kings 18:32-33 — parallel challenge comparing the Lord's rescue with the nations' gods: Both warn against Hezekiah's claim that God will rescue them and ask whether the nations' gods rescued their own lands from Assyria. Kings spans the transition across two verses; the challenge remains the Assyrian speaker's argument.

Isaiah 36:19
Where is the god of Aimath and Arphath? Where is the god of Seppharim’s city? Were they able to rescue Samaria from my hand?
  2 Kings 18:34 — parallel list of captured cities used to taunt their gods: Both name the gods of Aimath, Arphath or Arphad, and Seppharim or Seppharouain, then challenge their ability to rescue Samaria. The respective native name forms remain unchanged.

Isaiah 36:20
Which of all these nations’ gods rescued his land from my hand, that God should rescue Jerusalem from my hand?’”
  2 Kings 18:35 — parallel final challenge to Jerusalem's divine rescue: Both ask which nations' gods rescued their lands and use that claim to question Jerusalem's rescue by God. This repeats the historical opponent's inference without identifying the Lord with the gods he invokes.

Isaiah 36:21
They were silent, and no one answered him a word, because the king had ordered that no one answer.
  2 Kings 18:36 — parallel obedience to the king's command of silence: Both narrate that the Judeans do not answer the spokesman because their king ordered silence. The explicit command explains the identical public response.

Isaiah 36:22
Eliakim son of Chelkias, the steward, Somnas the army’s scribe, and Joach son of Asaph, the record-keeper, came to Hezekiah with torn tunics and reported Rapsakes’ words.
  2 Kings 18:37 — parallel report to Hezekiah by officials with torn clothing: Both bring the same three officials back to Hezekiah with torn garments to report the Assyrian spokesman's words. Isaiah additionally calls Somnas the army's scribe; the Kings verse names him simply as scribe.

Isaiah 37:1
When King Hezekiah heard, he tore his garments, wrapped himself in sackcloth, and went up to the Lord’s house.
  2 Kings 19:1 — parallel account of Hezekiah's response to the Assyrian report: Both describe Hezekiah hearing the report, tearing his garments, putting on sackcloth and entering the Lord's house. The preceding reports in both narratives concern the Assyrian envoy's words.

Isaiah 37:2
He sent Eliakim the steward, Somnas the scribe, and the elders of the priests, wrapped in sackcloth, to the prophet Isaiah son of Amos.
  2 Kings 19:2 — parallel delegation to Isaiah in sackcloth: Both name Eliakim the steward, Somnas the scribe and the priestly elders sent in sackcloth to Isaiah son of Amos. The specified messengers and purpose identify the same narrative episode.

Isaiah 37:3
They said to him, “Thus says Hezekiah: Today is a day of distress, reproach, reproof, and wrath, for labor has come upon the woman giving birth, but she has no strength to give birth.
  2 Kings 19:3 — close birth-pain image in Hezekiah's message: Both give Hezekiah's description of the crisis as a day of distress and rebuke and compare it to labor without strength to give birth. Isaiah additionally includes wrath in its description.

Isaiah 37:4
May the Lord your God hear Rapsakes’ words, which the king of the Assyrians sent to reproach the living God and to reproach words that the Lord your God has heard. You shall plead with the Lord your God for those who remain.”
  2 Kings 19:4 — parallel appeal for prayer after reproach of the living God: Both ask that God hear the Assyrian envoy's reproach of the living God and request prayer for the remaining people. Their envoy's name appears as Rapsakes and Rhapsakes respectively.

Isaiah 37:5
The king’s servants came to Isaiah,
  2 Kings 19:5 — parallel arrival of Hezekiah's servants before Isaiah: Both record the king's servants coming to Isaiah between Hezekiah's appeal and the prophet's reply. The named king and prophet and matching narrative position make this a particular historical correspondence.

Isaiah 37:6
and Isaiah said to them, “You shall say this to your master: Thus says the Lord: Do not fear the words you heard, with which the king of the Assyrians’ envoys reproached Me.
  2 Kings 19:6 — parallel reassurance against the Assyrian envoys' words: Both instruct the messengers to tell their master not to fear the words spoken by Assyria's representatives against God. Kings calls the representatives young servants, while Isaiah calls them envoys.

Isaiah 37:7
Behold, I will put a spirit into him. On hearing a report he will return to his country, and he will fall by the sword in his own land.”
  2 Kings 19:7 — parallel prediction of the Assyrian king's return and death: Both announce a spirit placed within the king, a report he will hear, his return to his own land and his death there by the sword.

Isaiah 37:8
Rapsakes returned and found the king besieging Lomna. The king of the Assyrians heard that
