  2 Kings 19:8 — parallel encounter with the Assyrian king at Lomna: Both describe the returning Assyrian envoy finding the king fighting or besieging Lomna. Kings also explains that he had heard of the departure from Lachish.

Isaiah 37:9
Tharaka king of the Ethiopians had come out to besiege him. On hearing this he turned back and sent messengers to Hezekiah, saying,
  2 Kings 19:9 — parallel report of Tharaka and renewed messengers to Hezekiah: Both name Tharaka king of the Ethiopians coming against Assyria's king and describe the subsequent sending of messengers to Hezekiah. Isaiah speaks of besieging him; Kings speaks of fighting him.

Isaiah 37:10
“You shall say this to Hezekiah king of Judea: Do not let your God, on whom you rely, deceive you by saying, ‘Jerusalem will certainly not be handed into the king of the Assyrians’ hands.’
  2 Kings 19:10 — parallel challenge to Hezekiah's trust in Jerusalem's deliverance: Both convey the Assyrian warning against trusting the promise that Jerusalem will not be handed to Assyria. Isaiah calls this being deceived, whereas Kings speaks of being lifted up by that assurance.

Isaiah 37:11
Or have you not heard what the kings of the Assyrians did—how they destroyed the whole earth?
  2 Kings 19:11 — parallel Assyrian boast of widespread destruction: Both appeal to what Hezekiah has heard about the Assyrian kings' destruction of other lands. Kings then expressly challenges whether he will be rescued.

Isaiah 37:12
Did the gods of the nations my fathers destroyed rescue them—Gozan, Charran, and Raphes, which are in the country of Themad?
  2 Kings 19:12 — parallel catalogue of peoples whose gods did not rescue them: Both ask whether the gods of peoples destroyed by the speaker's fathers rescued them and name Gozan, Charran and Raphes. The additional geography differs: Isaiah names Themad, while Kings names Edem's sons in Thaesthen.

Isaiah 37:13
Where are the kings of Aimath and Arphath, and of the city of Seppharim, Anag, and Ougaua?”
  2 Kings 19:13 — parallel challenge concerning defeated cities and kings: Both ask where the rulers or cities named Aimath, Arphath or Arphad, Seppharim or Seppharouain, Anag or Ana, and Ougaua or Ava are. The matching challenge preserves the respective supplied name forms.

Isaiah 37:14
Hezekiah took the document from the messengers and opened it before the Lord.
  2 Kings 19:14 — parallel presentation of the Assyrian document before the Lord: Both describe Hezekiah receiving the messengers' document and opening or unfolding it before the Lord. Kings additionally expressly mentions reading the letters and going up to the Lord's house.

Isaiah 37:15
Hezekiah prayed to the Lord, saying,
  2 Chronicles 32:20 — parallel record of Hezekiah praying during the Assyrian crisis: Isaiah introduces Hezekiah's prayer to the Lord; Chronicles likewise records his prayer and cry to heaven during this crisis, also naming Isaiah as praying with him.

Isaiah 37:16
“Lord Sabaoth, God of Israel, seated upon the cherubim, Thou alone art God of every kingdom of the inhabited world. Thou didst make heaven and earth.
  2 Kings 19:15 — parallel confession of the cherubim-enthroned Creator: Both prayers address Israel's God seated upon the cherubim, confess Him alone as God of the earth's kingdoms and declare that He made heaven and earth.

Isaiah 37:17
Hear, Lord; look, Lord, and see the words that Sennacherib sent to reproach the living God.
  2 Kings 19:16 — parallel appeal that God attend to Sennacherib's reproach: Both ask the Lord to hear and see the words Sennacherib sent to reproach the living God. Kings develops the petition with the explicit images of God's ear and eyes.

Isaiah 37:18
For the kings of the Assyrians have truly laid waste the whole inhabited world and their country,
  2 Kings 19:17 — parallel acknowledgment of Assyria's actual devastation: Both prayers acknowledge that Assyria's kings truly devastated other territories before explaining why the destroyed idols were not gods. Isaiah uses the wider expression of the inhabited world and their country.

Isaiah 37:19
and cast their idols into the fire. For they were not gods, but works of human hands, wood and stones, and they destroyed them.
  2 Kings 19:18 — parallel explanation of burned idols as human handiwork: Both explain that the idols cast into fire were not gods but human works made of wood and stone. Their destruction therefore supplies no proof against the living God addressed in the prayer.

Isaiah 37:20
But Thou, Lord our God, save us from their hand, so that every kingdom of the earth may know that Thou alone art God.”
  2 Kings 19:19 — parallel plea for salvation revealing the only God to the kingdoms: Both ask God to rescue His people from Assyria so that the earth's kingdoms may know Him alone as God. The desired deliverance has the same expressly stated purpose.

Isaiah 37:21
Isaiah son of Amos was sent to Hezekiah and said to him, “Thus says the Lord, God of Israel: I heard what you prayed to Me about Sennacherib king of the Assyrians.
  2 Kings 19:20 — parallel answer that Hezekiah's prayer was heard: Both introduce Isaiah's message to Hezekiah with God's declaration that He heard the prayer concerning Sennacherib king of the Assyrians.

Isaiah 37:22
This is the word God spoke concerning him: Virgin daughter Zion despised and sneered at you; daughter Jerusalem shook her head over you.
  2 Kings 19:21 — parallel personified Zion's derision of the Assyrian ruler: Both describe virgin daughter Zion despising and sneering at the ruler and daughter Jerusalem shaking her head over him. The matching female-city imagery conveys the same reversal of his intimidation.

Isaiah 37:23
Whom did you reproach and provoke? Against whom did you raise your voice? Yet you did not lift your eyes on high toward the Holy One of Israel.
  2 Kings 19:22 — parallel reproach challenge with different eye-language: Both ask whom the ruler reproached and against whom he raised his voice. Isaiah says he did not lift his eyes on high toward Israel's Holy One; Kings says he lifted them on high against Him. The different formulations are preserved.

Isaiah 37:24
For through messengers you reproached the Lord. You said, ‘With my many chariots I climbed the mountain heights and the furthest reaches of Lebanon. I cut down its cedar’s height and the cypress’s beauty, and entered the height of a part of the woodland.
  2 Kings 19:23 — parallel boast of chariots and cutting Lebanon's trees: Both recount reproach through messengers and a boast involving many chariots, mountain heights, Lebanon, cedars and cypresses. Isaiah describes the ascent as accomplished; Kings says the speaker shall climb, then describes cutting and arrival.

Isaiah 37:25
I laid a bridge and dried up waters and every gathering of water.’
  2 Kings 19:24 — parallel boast of mastery over waters with differing actions: Both speeches boast of drying waters. Isaiah also says the speaker laid a bridge; Kings instead says he cooled and drank foreign waters and dried rivers with his foot's tread.

Isaiah 37:26
Did you not hear long ago about these things I did? From ancient days I arranged them; now I have shown them: to desolate nations in strongholds and those dwelling in fortified cities.
  2 Kings 19:25 — parallel divine claim to have arranged the conquest: Isaiah attributes the devastation of fortified places to God's earlier arrangement; Kings likewise has God say He formed and brought about the outcome involving fortified cities and exiled fighting men. The formulations of the outcome differ.

Isaiah 37:27
I let the hands go, and they withered and became like dry grass on roofs and like wild grass.
  2 Kings 19:26 — close weakened hands and rooftop-grass imagery: Isaiah joins hands let go with withering and grass on roofs; Kings describes inhabitants weak in hand and likens them to field grass and rooftop grass. The common imagery presents the conquered as unable to resist.

Isaiah 37:28
But now I know your rest, your going out and your coming in.
  2 Kings 19:27 — parallel divine knowledge of the ruler's movements: Both declare God's knowledge of the ruler's rest or sitting, going out and coming in. Kings also includes his anger against God within this sentence.

Isaiah 37:29
Your wrath with which you raged, and your bitterness, have risen to Me. I will put a muzzle in your nose and a bridle in your lips, and turn you back by the way you came.
  2 Kings 19:28 — parallel restraint and return of the enraged Assyrian king: Both connect the ruler's anger rising to God with restraint at nose and lips and return along his incoming road. Isaiah names a muzzle in the nose; Kings names hooks in the nostrils, while both name a bridle in the lips.

Isaiah 37:30
This is your sign: This year eat what you have sown, in the second year what remains, and in the third, after sowing, reap and plant vineyards; you will eat their fruit.
  2 Kings 19:29 — parallel three-year agricultural sign with different first-year wording: Both give a three-year sign culminating in sowing, reaping, planting vineyards and eating fruit. Isaiah's first year eats what has been sown; Kings instead specifies what grows by itself, followed by what sprouts in the second year.

Isaiah 37:31
Those remaining in Judea will grow a root below and produce seed above.
  2 Kings 19:30 — parallel renewed growth of Judah's remnant: Both portray Judah's surviving remnant taking root below and producing above. Isaiah specifies seed above, while Kings specifies fruit upward.

Isaiah 37:32
For those remaining will go out from Jerusalem, and those being saved from Mount Zion. The zeal of the Lord Sabaoth will do these things.
  2 Kings 19:31 — parallel remnant emerging through the Lord's zeal: Both announce a remnant going out from Jerusalem and survivors from Mount Zion, concluding that the Lord's zeal will accomplish it.

Isaiah 37:33
Therefore thus says the Lord against the king of the Assyrians: He will certainly not enter this city or shoot a missile against it; he will certainly not bring a shield against it or surround it with a siegework.
  2 Kings 19:32 — parallel denial of an Assyrian assault entering Jerusalem: Both deny the king entry into the city, the shooting of a missile or arrow, the bringing of a shield and the construction of siegeworks. The specified military actions closely correspond.

Isaiah 37:34
By the way he came he will return. Thus says the Lord.
  2 Kings 19:33 — parallel return by the same road: Both declare that Assyria's king will return by the road he came. Kings repeats the additional denial that he will enter the city.

Isaiah 37:35
I will shield this city to save it, because of Me and because of David My servant.”
  2 Kings 19:34 — parallel divine shielding for God's and David's sake: Both promise that God will shield the city, giving God's own sake and David His servant as the stated reasons. Isaiah additionally explicitly says the shielding is to save it.

Isaiah 37:36
An angel of the Lord went out and killed one hundred eighty-five thousand from the Assyrians’ camp. On rising early in the morning, they found all the bodies dead.
  2 Kings 19:35 — parallel angelic destruction of 185000 Assyrians: Both record the Lord's angel striking one hundred eighty-five thousand in the Assyrian camp and the dead bodies found in the morning. Kings explicitly places the angel's action at night.

Isaiah 37:37
The king of the Assyrians turned back, went away, and lived in Nineveh.
  2 Kings 19:36 — parallel Assyrian withdrawal to Nineveh: Both record the Assyrian king departing and returning to live in Nineveh. Kings expressly names him Sennacherib in this sentence.

Isaiah 37:38
While he was worshiping his cult-image in the house of Nasarach, his sons Adramelech and Sarasar struck him with swords. They escaped safely to Armenia, and his son Asordan reigned in his place.
  2 Kings 19:37 — parallel assassination by the king's sons during worship: Both name Adramelech and Sarasar striking their father with swords in his god's house, escaping, and Asordan succeeding him. Isaiah names the place of escape Armenia; Kings names Ararat, and the sanctuary's name also differs in form.

Isaiah 38:1
At that time Hezekiah became ill to the point of death. The prophet Isaiah son of Amos came to him and said, “Thus says the Lord: Arrange your household’s affairs, for you are dying and will not live.”
  2 Kings 20:1 — parallel warning to the mortally ill Hezekiah: Both describe Hezekiah ill to the point of death and Isaiah son of Amos telling him to arrange his household because he is dying and will not live.

Isaiah 38:2
Hezekiah turned his face toward the wall and prayed to the Lord,
  2 Kings 20:2 — parallel prayer with face turned to the wall: Both record Hezekiah turning his face toward the wall and praying to the Lord after Isaiah's announcement concerning his illness.

Isaiah 38:3
saying, “Remember, Lord, how I walked before Thee with truth, in a true heart, and did what was pleasing before Thee.” Hezekiah wept greatly.
  2 Kings 20:3 — parallel appeal to faithful conduct followed by weeping: Both prayers ask God to remember Hezekiah's truthful walk, heart and conduct pleasing to Him, followed by his intense weeping. Isaiah calls the heart true; Kings calls it full.

Isaiah 38:4
The Lord’s word came to Isaiah, saying,
  2 Kings 20:4-5 — parallel divine message to Isaiah after Hezekiah's prayer: Both place a new word from the Lord to Isaiah after Hezekiah's prayer and before the reassuring response. Kings adds Isaiah's location in the middle courtyard and the instruction to return to the king.

Isaiah 38:5
“Go and tell Hezekiah: Thus says the Lord, God of David your father: I heard the voice of your prayer and saw your tears. Behold, I am adding fifteen years to your time.
  2 Kings 20:5-6 — parallel heard prayer and fifteen additional years: Both answers identify God as the God of David, say He heard the prayer and saw the tears, and promise Hezekiah fifteen further years. Kings additionally gives a third-day return to the Lord's house.

Isaiah 38:6
I will save you from the king of the Assyrians’ hand, and I will shield this city.
  2 Kings 20:6 — parallel promise to save Hezekiah and shield Jerusalem: Both promise rescue from the Assyrian king and shielding of the city. Kings additionally states God's own sake and David His servant as reasons.

Isaiah 38:7
This is your sign from the Lord that God will do this thing:”
  2 Kings 20:9 — parallel introduction of the confirming sign: Both introduce a sign from the Lord that He will carry out His announced word. Kings continues immediately with the shadow's steps, while Isaiah gives the sign's description in the following unit.

Isaiah 38:8
“As for the shadow of the steps the sun descended—the ten steps of your father’s house—I will turn the sun back ten steps.” The sun went up the ten steps which the shadow had descended.
  2 Kings 20:10-11 — parallel ten-step reversal with sun and shadow distinguished: Isaiah describes the sun turned back the ten steps descended by the shadow; Kings records Hezekiah's request and Isaiah's prayer followed by the shadow returning ten steps. The stated subject of the reversal differs.

Isaiah 38:9
A prayer of Hezekiah king of Judea, when he became ill and rose from his illness:
  2 Chronicles 32:24 — parallel identification of Hezekiah's prayer during mortal illness: Isaiah identifies the ensuing prayer with Hezekiah's illness and recovery; Chronicles likewise names his illness to the point of death, prayer, God's hearing and a sign. Chronicles does not reproduce the prayer's words.

Isaiah 38:10
I said, “At the height of my days, at Hades’ gates, I will leave my remaining years behind.”
  Psalms 9:14-15 — related gates-of-death imagery within prayer for life: Hezekiah recalls reaching Hades' gates at the height of his days; the psalm addresses God as raising the speaker from death's gates so that he may proclaim praise. The endangered life and subsequent praise connect the prayers.

Isaiah 38:11
I said, “I will certainly no longer see God’s salvation upon the earth; I will certainly no longer see a person
  Psalms 26:13 — contrast between feared loss and hoped-for sight of God's good: Hezekiah recalls expecting no longer to see God's salvation upon earth; the psalmist believes he will see the Lord's good things in the land of the living. Both frame continued earthly life through seeing divine good, with opposite stated expectations.

Isaiah 38:12
from my family. I left the rest of my life; it went out and departed from me like someone taking down a tent he had pitched. My spirit beside me became like a weaver’s web as she approached to cut it off. On that day I was handed over
  2 Corinthians 5:1-4 — shared mortal-life image of a dismantled tent: Hezekiah likens his departing life to taking down a pitched tent; Paul describes the earthly tent being dismantled and contrasts it with an eternal dwelling from God. Isaiah's additional weaver-web image is not repeated in the target.

Isaiah 38:13
until morning, as to a lion; thus he crushed my bones, for from day to night I was handed over.
  Lamentations 3:4-10 — related crushing affliction described with bones and a lion: Hezekiah describes being handed over as to a lion and his bones crushed; Lamentations describes shattered bones and the afflicter as a lion in hiding. The two laments combine bodily breaking and a predatory image.

Isaiah 38:14
Like a swallow I will cry out, and like a dove I will murmur. For my eyes failed from looking toward heaven’s height, toward the Lord who rescued me
  Psalms 68:4 — close crying and eyes failing while looking toward God: Hezekiah speaks of crying and says his eyes failed from looking heavenward toward the Lord; the psalmist is weary from crying and says his eyes failed from hoping in God. The psalm does not repeat the swallow and dove comparisons.

Isaiah 38:15
and took away my soul’s pain.
  Psalms 114:3-8 — parallel relief of the soul's mortal distress: Hezekiah says the Lord took away his soul's pain; the psalm moves from death's pangs, affliction and pain to the soul's rest and rescue from death. Both give the Lord credit for that relief.

Isaiah 38:16
For, Lord, a report about it was brought to Thee, and Thou didst raise my breath; comforted, I lived.
  Psalms 29:3-4 — parallel divine restoration from threatened death: Hezekiah addresses the Lord who raised his breath so that he lived comforted; the psalmist thanks God for healing him and bringing his soul up from Hades. The supplied expressions of restored life differ.

Isaiah 38:17
For Thou didst choose my soul so that it would not perish, and didst cast all my sins behind me.
  Micah 7:18-19 — parallel removal of sins through spatial casting imagery: Hezekiah says God cast his sins behind him; Micah describes sins thrown into the sea's depths after divine mercy. The positions differ, and Isaiah's wording is behind the speaker, not behind God's back.

Isaiah 38:18
For those in Hades will not praise Thee, nor will the dead bless Thee, nor will those in Hades hope for Thy mercy.
  Psalms 6:6 — close denial of praise from death and Hades: Hezekiah says those in Hades will not praise or hope for God's mercy; the psalm likewise says no one in death remembers God and asks who gives Him thanks in Hades.

Isaiah 38:19
The living will bless Thee, as I do. For from today I will have children who will announce Thy righteousness,
  Psalms 77:4-7 — related future children announcing God's deeds: Hezekiah anticipates children who will announce God's righteousness; the psalm describes another generation and sons yet to be born learning and announcing God's praises and works to their sons. Both extend divine acknowledgment to future offspring.

Isaiah 38:20
Lord of my salvation. I will not cease blessing Thee with a harp all the days of my life before God’s house.”
  Psalms 26:4-6 — parallel lifelong worship associated with God's house: Hezekiah promises harp-blessing throughout his life before God's house; the psalm seeks residence in the Lord's house all life's days and promises song and music in its worship. The particular instrument is specified only in Isaiah.

Isaiah 38:21
Isaiah said to Hezekiah, “Take a cake made of figs, crush it and apply it as a poultice, and you will be healthy.”
  2 Kings 20:7 — parallel medicinal application of a fig cake: Both describe a cake of figs applied for Hezekiah's recovery. Isaiah specifies crushing it into a poultice; Kings expressly names the ulcer to which it is placed.

Isaiah 38:22
Hezekiah said, “This is the sign that I will go up to the house of the Lord God.”
  2 Kings 20:8 — parallel Hezekiah sign statement concerning the Lord's house: Both connect Hezekiah's sign with going up to the Lord's house. Isaiah presents his words as a declaration, while Kings presents a question and specifies healing and the third day.

Isaiah 39:1
At that time Marodach son of Laadan, king of Babylonia, sent letters, envoys, and gifts to Hezekiah, for he heard that he had been ill to the point of death and had recovered.
  2 Kings 20:12 — parallel Babylonian correspondence after Hezekiah's illness: Both name Babylon's king sending letters after hearing of Hezekiah's illness. Isaiah names gifts, envoys and his recovery; Kings calls the accompanying items manaa, and the royal name forms differ.

Isaiah 39:2
Hezekiah rejoiced greatly over them and showed them the store of nekhotha, liquid myrrh, incense, perfumed ointment, silver, gold, all the storerooms of the treasury’s equipment, and everything in his treasuries. There was nothing in his house that Hezekiah did not show.
  2 Kings 20:13 — parallel delighted display of Hezekiah's treasures: Both describe Hezekiah rejoicing over the visitors and showing his stores of silver, gold, fragrant substances and equipment, withholding none of his household treasures. The inventories differ in their particular terms.

Isaiah 39:3
The prophet Isaiah came to King Hezekiah and said, “What are these men saying, and from where have they come to you?” Hezekiah said, “They have come to me from a distant land, from Babylon.”
  2 Kings 20:14 — parallel Isaiah's question about the visitors' origin: Both have Isaiah ask Hezekiah what the visitors said and where they came from, followed by Hezekiah's answer that they came from the distant land of Babylon.

Isaiah 39:4
Isaiah said, “What did they see in your house?” Hezekiah said, “They saw everything in my house. There is nothing in my house they did not see, and also the things in my treasuries.”
  2 Kings 20:15 — parallel admission that all the treasures were shown: Both record Isaiah asking what the visitors saw and Hezekiah admitting that they saw everything in his house, including the treasuries, without anything withheld.

Isaiah 39:5
Isaiah said to him, “Hear the word of the Lord Sabaoth:
  2 Kings 20:16 — parallel introduction of the Babylonian exile warning: Both place Isaiah's instruction to hear the Lord's word immediately before the prediction that Hezekiah's ancestral stores will be taken to Babylon. The introduction belongs to the same identified encounter.

Isaiah 39:6
Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and they will take everything in your house. What your fathers gathered until this day will reach Babylon, and they will certainly leave nothing behind. God also said
  2 Kings 20:17 — parallel prediction of ancestral treasures taken to Babylon: Both predict that everything in Hezekiah's house and accumulated by his fathers will be taken to Babylon. Isaiah says nothing will be left; Kings' concluding clause says no word spoken by the Lord will be left over.

Isaiah 39:7
that they will also take some of your children whom you fathered and make them eunuchs in the house of the Babylonians’ king.”
  2 Kings 20:18 — parallel prediction of royal descendants serving as eunuchs: Both predict that Hezekiah's offspring will be taken and made eunuchs in the Babylonian king's house. The relationship concerns the expressly stated descendants, destination and service.
  Daniel 1:3-5 — related royal descendants brought into Babylonian service: Isaiah predicts royal offspring taken into Babylon's king's house; Daniel records the king's order for young men from Israel's royal family to be educated for his service. Daniel puts them under a chief eunuch but does not expressly state that the young men were made eunuchs.

Isaiah 39:8
Hezekiah said to Isaiah, “The Lord’s word that He spoke is good. Let there indeed be peace and righteousness in my days.”
  2 Kings 20:19 — parallel acceptance of the warning and wish for peace: Both record Hezekiah calling the Lord's word good and asking for peace in his own days. Isaiah additionally includes righteousness in that wish.

Isaiah 40:1
Comfort, comfort My people, says God.
  Isaiah 51:3 — internal promise of comfort for God's people and Zion: God's command to comfort His people is developed in the later promise to comfort Zion and her deserted places, with joy, thanksgiving and praise. Both concern comfort of the community addressed by God.

Isaiah 40:2
You priests, speak to the heart of Jerusalem; comfort her. For her humiliation is fulfilled, her sin is loosed; for from the hand of the Lord she has received double for her sins.
  Lamentations 4:22 — related announcement of release for Jerusalem and Zion: Isaiah comforts Jerusalem because humiliation is fulfilled and sin loosed; Lamentations likewise tells daughter Zion that her lawlessness has ended and she will be deported no longer. The formulations of the release differ.
  Jeremiah 16:18 — close double repayment for sins: Isaiah says Jerusalem received double for her sins from the Lord's hand; Jeremiah likewise announces double repayment for injustices and sins. Isaiah looks back in a message of comfort, while Jeremiah announces the penalty.

Isaiah 40:3
A voice of one crying in the wilderness: Prepare the way of the Lord; make straight the paths of our God.
  Luke 3:4 — explicit quotation identifying the wilderness voice: Luke names the book of Isaiah and quotes the wilderness voice calling for the Lord's way to be prepared and His paths straightened, within its account of John's preaching.
  John 1:22-23 — explicit use of Isaiah's voice saying as John's self-description: Asked who he is, John identifies himself as the wilderness voice and cites Isaiah's instruction to straighten the Lord's way. This is an expressly attributed use of the source's distinctive wording.
  Matthew 3:1-3 — explicit application of the wilderness voice to John the Baptist: Matthew introduces John's wilderness proclamation and says he is the one spoken of through Isaiah, then quotes the command to prepare the Lord's way and straighten His paths.
  Mark 1:2-4 — explicit wilderness-voice citation introducing John's ministry: Mark includes the wilderness voice and preparation of the Lord's way in the citation introduced under Isaiah's name, immediately before John's appearance. Its preceding messenger clause is additional to the wording of this Isaiah unit.

Isaiah 40:4
Every valley will be filled, and every mountain and hill will be brought low; all the crooked places will become straight, and the rough country will become plains.
  Luke 3:5 — explicit quotation of transformed terrain: Luke's Isaiah quotation repeats valleys or ravines filled, mountains and hills lowered, crooked places straightened and rough ways made smooth. These concrete changes correspond to the source's preparation of the way.

Isaiah 40:5
And the glory of the Lord will be seen, and all flesh will see the salvation of God; for the Lord has spoken.
  Luke 3:6 — explicit quotation of all flesh seeing God's salvation: Luke concludes its cited Isaiah passage with all flesh seeing God's salvation. Isaiah additionally says the Lord's glory will be seen and grounds the promise in His spoken word.
  Isaiah 52:10 — internal promise of God's salvation seen universally: Both announce the sight of God's salvation beyond a single community: all flesh here and all earth's ends in Isaiah 52, where God's holy arm is revealed before the nations.

Isaiah 40:6
A voice says, “Cry out!” And I said, “What shall I cry?” All flesh is grass, and all the glory of man is as the flower of grass.
  1 Peter 1:24 — quotation of human flesh and glory as grass and flower: First Peter repeats the comparison of all flesh with grass and its glory with the grass's flower. Its surrounding argument contrasts perishable life with rebirth through God's enduring word.

Isaiah 40:7
The grass has withered, and the flower has fallen;
  1 Peter 1:24 — quotation of withered grass and fallen flower: First Peter repeats the source's paired statements that the grass withered and the flower fell, within the same comparison between mortal humanity and God's enduring word.

Isaiah 40:8
but the word of our God remains for ever.
  1 Peter 1:25 — quotation of the perpetually enduring divine word: First Peter repeats that the Lord's word remains forever and identifies it with the good news proclaimed to its hearers. Isaiah names it the word of our God.
  Matthew 24:35 — related permanence of divine and Jesus' words: Isaiah declares God's word enduring forever; Jesus declares that His words will not pass away even though heaven and earth will. The sayings share enduring speech while naming their speakers differently.

Isaiah 40:9
Go up on a high mountain, you who bring good news to Zion; lift up your voice with strength, you who bring good news to Jerusalem. Lift up; do not fear. Say to the cities of Judah, Behold your God!
  Isaiah 52:7 — internal mountain-borne good news concerning Zion's God: Both join mountains, good news addressed to Zion and proclamation concerning her God. Isaiah 40 says to announce 'Behold your God' to Judah's cities; Isaiah 52 announces peace, salvation and God's reign.

Isaiah 40:10
Behold, the Lord comes with might, and His arm with dominion; behold, His reward is with Him, and His work before Him.
  Isaiah 62:11 — close arrival with reward and work before the arriving Savior: Both announce an arrival with reward and work before Him. Isaiah 40 describes the Lord coming with might and dominion; Isaiah 62 tells daughter Zion that her Savior is arriving.

Isaiah 40:11
Like a shepherd, He will shepherd His flock; and with His arm He will gather lambs, and comfort those that are with young.
  Ezekiel 34:11-16 — parallel divine shepherding and care of vulnerable sheep: Isaiah portrays God shepherding, gathering lambs and comforting those with young; Ezekiel likewise promises God's personal gathering, feeding and care of lost, broken and failing sheep. The particular vulnerable groups differ.

Isaiah 40:12
Who measured the water with His hand, heaven with a span, and all the earth with a handful? Who weighed mountains with a weight and wooded valleys with a balance?
  Proverbs 30:4 — related questions about cosmic mastery through physical images: Isaiah asks who measures waters, heaven and earth and weighs mountains; Proverbs asks who wraps waters, gathers winds and takes hold of earth's ends. Both use bodily or household images to ask about mastery of creation, with different specific images.

Isaiah 40:13
Who knew the Lord’s mind, and who became His counselor to instruct Him?
  Romans 11:34 — explicit reuse of the Lord's mind and counselor questions: Romans repeats the questions about who knew the Lord's mind or became His counselor within praise of God's unsearchable wisdom and judgments.
  1 Corinthians 2:16 — quotation of knowing and instructing the Lord's mind: First Corinthians repeats the question about knowing the Lord's mind so as to instruct Him, then says that its speakers have Christ's mind. The surrounding explanation locates revealed knowledge in God's Spirit.

Isaiah 40:14
With whom did He take counsel, and who instructed Him? Who showed Him judgment, or who showed Him the way of understanding?
  Job 21:22 — complementary presentation of God as the source of understanding: Isaiah asks who instructed God or showed Him understanding; Job asks whether the Lord Himself teaches understanding and knowledge. The question about God's instructor is complemented by the affirmation that God teaches.

Isaiah 40:15
If all the nations were counted like a drop from a bucket and like a scale’s slight tipping, they will also be counted like spittle.
  Wisdom of Solomon 11:22 — close drop-and-scales comparison of creation before God: Isaiah compares the nations with a bucket's drop and a scale's slight tipping; Wisdom compares the whole world before God with slight tipping of scales and a drop of morning dew. The scope and source of the drop differ.

Isaiah 40:16
Lebanon is not sufficient for burning, nor all the four-footed animals sufficient for a whole burnt offering,
  Micah 6:6-8 — related insufficiency of extravagant sacrificial provision: Isaiah says even Lebanon and all the four-footed animals are insufficient for a burnt offering; Micah asks whether thousands of rams and vast rich offerings secure approach to God, then names justice, mercy and walking with Him.

Isaiah 40:17
and all the nations are as nothing and were counted as nothing.
  Psalms 38:6 — parallel human existence counted as nothing before God: Isaiah says all nations are counted as nothing; the psalmist calls his own existence nothing before God and extends vanity to every living person. The national and individual scopes differ.

Isaiah 40:18
To whom did you liken the Lord, and to what likeness did you liken Him?
  Exodus 15:11 — parallel question of God's incomparability: Isaiah asks to whom the Lord has been likened; the song in Exodus asks who is like the Lord among gods and praises His glory and wonders. Exodus answers through praise rather than Isaiah's following discussion of images.

Isaiah 40:19
Did a craftsman make an image, or did a goldsmith melt gold and gild it, fashioning it as a likeness?
  Jeremiah 10:9 — close human manufacture and gilding of an image: Isaiah questions a craftsman's image and a goldsmith's melted and applied gold; Jeremiah likewise describes applied silver, gold and the goldsmiths' hands as artisans' works. Jeremiah additionally describes the images' colored clothing.

Isaiah 40:20
For a craftsman chooses wood that will not rot and skillfully seeks how to set up his image so that it will not shake.
  Jeremiah 10:3-4 — close wooden image secured against movement: Both describe a crafted wooden image set so it will not move or shake. Isaiah emphasizes selecting wood that will not rot; Jeremiah specifies metal adornment and fastening with hammers and nails.

Isaiah 40:21
Will you not know? Will you not hear? Was it not announced to you from the beginning? Did you not know the earth’s foundations?
  Job 38:4-6 — parallel questions about knowledge of earth's foundations: Isaiah asks whether the hearers knew earth's foundations; the Lord asks Job where he was when those foundations were laid and whether he understands their measures and support. The addressees and rhetorical situations differ.

Isaiah 40:22
He holds the earth’s circuit, and its inhabitants are like locusts. He set heaven like a vault and stretched it out like a tent to dwell in.
  Psalms 103:2 — close heaven stretched as a fabric dwelling or covering: Isaiah describes God stretching heaven like a tent to inhabit; the psalm describes Him stretching heaven like a hide curtain. The shared extended-covering image concerns God's making of heaven.

Isaiah 40:23
He gives rulers nothing to rule and made the earth as nothing.
  Daniel 4:31 — historical illustration of God removing a ruler's authority: Isaiah says God gives rulers nothing to rule; Daniel's heavenly announcement takes Nebuchadnezzar's kingdom and authority from him and gives them to another. This illustrates the rulership clause, not Isaiah's additional description of earth as nothing.

Isaiah 40:24
For they will certainly not sow or plant, nor will their root take hold in the earth. He blew on them, and they dried; a storm will carry them away like brushwood.
  Psalms 1:3-4 — related plant-and-wind contrast for insecure human life: Isaiah describes roots failing to establish, drying under God's breath and storm-driven brushwood; the psalm contrasts a rooted fruitful tree with ungodly people like wind-flung chaff. The plant materials and stated causes differ.

Isaiah 40:25
“Now then, to whom did you liken Me, and shall I be exalted?” said the Holy One.
  Psalms 85:8-10 — parallel denial of any likeness to God: The Holy One asks to whom He has been likened; the psalm declares that no god is like the Lord and nothing is like His works and that He alone is the great God. Both make the comparison fail.

Isaiah 40:26
Lift your eyes on high and see: Who displayed all these? He brings out His array by number and will call all by name. From abundant glory and in strength’s might, nothing escaped you.
  Psalms 146:4-5 — close divine numbering and naming of the heavenly array: Isaiah describes God bringing out His array by number and calling all by name; the psalm expressly names the multitude of stars that He numbers and calls by names. Both join this with His great strength.

Isaiah 40:27
Do not say, Jacob—and why did you declare, Israel—“My way was hidden from God, and my God removed judgment and departed”?
  Isaiah 49:14-16 — internal challenge to a complaint of divine abandonment: Jacob and Israel say their way is hidden and their God removed judgment and departed; Zion later says the Lord abandoned and forgot her, and receives His denial of forgetting. Both settings answer the community's perception of neglect.

Isaiah 40:28
And now, did you not know, if you have not heard? God is eternal, the God who fashioned the earth’s extremities. He will not hunger or grow weary, and His understanding cannot be discovered.
  Psalms 146:5 — parallel greatness and unsearchable understanding of God: Isaiah names the eternal Creator whose understanding cannot be discovered; the psalm likewise declares His great strength and understanding beyond number. The psalm does not repeat Isaiah's statements about hunger and weariness.

Isaiah 40:29
He gives strength to the hungry, and grief to those who are not in pain.
  Psalms 145:7 — related divine provision for hungry people: Isaiah says God gives strength to the hungry; the psalm says He gives them food. The correspondence concerns God's aid to the hungry, without equating strength and food as identical wording.
  Luke 6:24-25 — related reversal from present ease to grief: Isaiah also says God gives grief to those not in pain; Jesus warns the rich who have received consolation and those laughing now that mourning and weeping will follow. This comparison addresses the source's second clause rather than replacing it with a familiar weakness saying.

Isaiah 40:30
For younger people will hunger, young men will grow weary, and chosen men will be without strength.
  Psalms 32:16-19 — related limits of human strength beside divine sustaining help: Isaiah describes younger and chosen men becoming hungry, weary and without strength; the psalm denies rescue through a giant's strength or military force and attributes rescue and nourishment in famine to God. The named human groups differ.

Isaiah 40:31
But those enduring for God will exchange their strength and grow wings like eagles. They will run without growing weary and walk without hunger.
  Psalms 102:5 — close renewed strength or youth associated with an eagle: Isaiah promises those enduring for God exchanged strength and wings like eagles; the psalm promises youth renewed like an eagle's after God's filling with good things. The images differ between growing wings and renewed youth.

Isaiah 41:1
“Be renewed toward Me, islands, for rulers will exchange their strength. Let them approach and speak together; then let them announce judgment.
  Isaiah 43:9 — internal summons of nations and rulers to a judicial encounter: Isaiah 41 summons islands and rulers to approach, speak and announce judgment; Isaiah 43 gathers nations and rulers and asks for witnesses and true statements. Both frame the challenge through public announcement and judgment.

Isaiah 41:2
Who roused righteousness from the east, called her to His feet, and she will go? He will set her before nations, astonish kings, and put their swords into the earth and their bows like brushwood driven away.
  Isaiah 45:13 — related divine rousing associated with righteousness: Isaiah 41 describes righteousness roused from the east and called to God's feet, using feminine pronouns; Isaiah 45 describes God raising a king with righteousness and directing his ways. The feminine figure is not replaced by that king's identity.

Isaiah 41:4
Who acted and did these things? He called her, the One calling her from the generations’ beginning: I, God, am first, and for the things coming I am.
  Isaiah 44:6 — internal affirmation of God's priority and continuing existence: Isaiah 41 identifies God as first and present for what is coming; Isaiah 44 declares Him first and after these things, with no God apart from Him. The formulations affirm His unmatched continuity without importing a different title.

Isaiah 41:5
Nations saw and feared; the earth’s extremities drew near and came together,
  Psalms 47:5-7 — parallel gathering of rulers or nations marked by fear: Isaiah describes nations seeing and fearing and earth's extremities drawing together; the psalm describes gathered kings seeing, marveling and being shaken with trembling. The scenes share sight, assembly and fear before divine power.

Isaiah 41:6
each deciding to help his neighbor and brother, and he will say,
  Acts 19:24-27 — Contextual parallel: mutual support among makers of worship objects: Isaiah's neighbor-and-brother help is immediately developed through the craftsman, hammering smith, joining, and objects secured with nails. In Acts, the maker of silver Artemis shrines gathers related craftsmen to defend their work against the teaching that handmade things are not gods. Both scenes place collective support around making worship objects; Acts specifies commercial interests and Artemis, which are not supplied as the identities or motives of Isaiah's workers.

Isaiah 41:7
‘A craftsman grew strong, and a smith striking with a hammer while also beating out the work.’ At one time he will say, ‘The joining is good.’ They strengthened them with nails; they will set them in place, and they will not move.
  Jeremiah 10:3-4 — close crafted images fastened with nails so they will not move: Isaiah describes craftsmen's hammering, joining and strengthening the objects with nails; Jeremiah likewise names human craftsmanship and fastening with hammers and nails so that the objects will not move.

Isaiah 41:8
But you, Israel, My servant Jacob whom I chose, offspring of Abraham whom I loved,
  James 2:23 — related divine affection and friendship associated with Abraham: Isaiah identifies Abraham as the one God loved; James names him a friend of God after recounting his belief reckoned as righteousness. The descriptions correspond without substituting 'friend' for Isaiah's actual wording.
  Deuteronomy 7:6-8 — parallel Israel's divine choice and covenantal love: Isaiah calls Israel and Jacob God's chosen servant and Abraham's offspring; Deuteronomy likewise describes Israel's choice as God's possession, grounded in divine love and His oath to the fathers.

Isaiah 41:9
whom I took hold of from the earth’s extremities, and from its lookout heights called you and said, ‘You are My servant; I chose you and did not abandon you’—
  Deuteronomy 4:34-37 — related divine taking and choosing of Israel: Isaiah describes God taking and calling His chosen servant from earth's extremities; Deuteronomy recalls God taking a nation from another nation and choosing the fathers' offspring in the exodus. The places and events of the taking differ.

Isaiah 41:10
do not fear, for I am with you; do not wander, for I am your God who strengthened you. I helped you and secured you with My righteous right hand.
  Deuteronomy 31:6-8 — close reassurance not to fear because God is present: Isaiah grounds freedom from fear in God's presence, strength and help; Moses likewise tells Israel and Joshua not to fear because the Lord is with them and will not abandon them. Isaiah additionally specifies the righteous right hand.

Isaiah 41:11
Behold, all opposing you will be ashamed and abashed, for all your adversaries will be as though they did not exist and will perish.
  Jeremiah 20:11 — parallel shame of opponents in the presence of divine help: Isaiah promises that opponents will be ashamed and abashed; Jeremiah describes the Lord with him as a strong fighter and his pursuers greatly shamed. Isaiah additionally promises their perishing, which this Jeremiah unit does not state.

Isaiah 41:12
You will seek them but certainly not find the people who insult you. For those warring against you will be as though they did not exist, and will not exist.
  Psalms 36:35-36 — close search finding a formerly powerful enemy absent: Isaiah says those seeking their attackers will not find them, as though they did not exist; the psalmist sees an exalted ungodly person disappear and cannot find his place when searching. The individual and collective scopes differ.

Isaiah 41:13
For I am your God who holds your right hand and says to you, ‘Do not fear,
  Psalms 72:23-24 — close God holding the person's right hand: Isaiah describes God holding the addressee's right hand and speaking reassurance; the psalmist likewise says God grasped his right hand and guided him by counsel. The stated divine assistance is personal and specific.

Isaiah 41:14
Jacob, little Israel. I helped you,’ says God who redeems you, Israel.
  Luke 12:32 — related reassurance to a little community: Isaiah's 'little Israel' is told not to fear in the continuing address from 41:13, because God helped and redeems it; Jesus likewise tells the little flock not to fear because the Father gives the kingdom. The community descriptions and promised gifts differ.

Isaiah 41:15
Behold, I made you like new, saw-toothed wagon wheels for threshing. You will thresh mountains, grind hills fine, and make them like dust.
  Micah 4:12-13 — parallel empowerment for threshing and grinding opponents: Isaiah makes Israel like new saw-toothed wagon wheels that thresh mountains and grind hills; Micah gathers nations like threshing-floor sheaves and equips Zion with iron horns and bronze hooves to grind peoples. Instruments and objects differ within the shared threshing image.

Isaiah 41:16
You will winnow, and wind will take them and a storm scatter them. But you will rejoice among Israel’s holy ones. And they will exult—
  Psalms 1:4 — related winnowing and scattering by wind: After the mountains and hills are ground in 41:15, Isaiah describes winnowing followed by wind and storm scattering; the psalm likens the ungodly to chaff flung away by wind. The source does not itself name chaff in this sentence.
  Psalms 149:1-5 — related rejoicing among Israel's holy ones: Isaiah says the addressee will rejoice among Israel's holy ones; the psalm combines praise in their assembly, Israel's rejoicing and holy ones boasting in glory and rejoicing. Isaiah's plural holy ones is preserved rather than changed into a singular title for God.

Isaiah 41:17
the poor and needy. For they will seek water, and there will be none; their tongue dried from thirst. I am the Lord God; I will listen, the God of Israel, and will not abandon them,
  Psalms 106:4-9 — parallel divine response to waterless thirst and need: Isaiah describes needy people seeking unavailable water and God hearing without abandoning them; the psalm describes hungry, thirsty wanderers in a waterless place who cry to the Lord and are rescued. Both join physical need with answered appeal.

Isaiah 41:18
but will open rivers on mountains and springs amid plains. I will make the wilderness marshes and the thirsty land watercourses,
  Psalms 106:35-37 — close wilderness transformed into abundant water: Isaiah promises rivers, springs and marshes in formerly thirsty land; the psalm likewise turns wilderness into lakes and waterless land into outlets, then settles hungry people there. The particular landscape terms differ.

Isaiah 41:19
and will place cedar, box tree, myrtle, cypress, and white poplar in the waterless land,
  Isaiah 55:13 — internal planting of cypress and myrtle in transformed land: Isaiah 41 includes cypress and myrtle among trees placed in waterless land; Isaiah 55 has cypress replace thorny shrub and myrtle replace fleabane. The named trees and restored growth correspond, while the prior conditions differ.

Isaiah 41:20
so that they may see and know, consider and understand together that the Lord’s hand did all these things and Israel’s Holy One displayed them.
  Job 12:7-10 — close recognition that the Lord's hand made these things: Isaiah intends observers to know that the Lord's hand accomplished the renewed landscape; Job appeals to creatures and earth in asking who does not know that the Lord's hand made these things. Recognition of divine workmanship connects the passages.

Isaiah 41:22
Let them approach and announce to you what will happen. Or say what the former things were, and we will apply our mind and know what the last things are; tell us also the coming things.
  Isaiah 46:9-10 — internal contrast between demanded prediction and God's announced fulfillment: Isaiah 41 challenges the claimants to announce former and coming things; Isaiah 46 identifies God as the One who announced last things before they occurred and whose counsel stands. The later assertion answers the kind of test posed here.

Isaiah 41:23
Announce to us the things coming at the end, and we will know that you are gods. Do good and cause harm, and we will marvel and see together.
  Jeremiah 10:5 — close challenge concerning idols' ability to do good or harm: Isaiah challenges the claimants to do good and cause harm so observers may recognize their divinity; Jeremiah says not to fear handmade gods because they cannot do evil and no good is in them. This addresses the action clause alongside Isaiah's prediction challenge.

Isaiah 41:24
For where are you from, and where is your work from? From the earth! They chose you as an abomination.
  Wisdom of Solomon 15:7-8 — related earthly origin of a manufactured god: Isaiah answers the challenged claimants' origin with earth and calls their choice an abomination; Wisdom describes a potter fashioning a futile god from the same clay as ordinary vessels. The earthly material exposes the supposed god's manufacture.

Isaiah 41:25
But I roused the one from the north and the one from the sun’s rising; they will be called by My name. Let rulers come, and like a potter’s clay, as a potter tramples clay, so you will be trampled.
  Jeremiah 18:4-6 — related peoples portrayed as potter's clay under superior power: Isaiah calls rulers to come and compares the addressees' trampling with clay under a potter; Jeremiah compares Israel in God's hands with clay reshaped by a potter. The shared political use of potter's clay preserves the difference between trampling and reshaping.

Isaiah 41:26
For who will announce the things from the beginning so we may know, and the earlier things so we may say, ‘They are true’? There is no one predicting, nor anyone hearing your words.
  Isaiah 48:3-5 — internal test of announcing events beforehand: Isaiah 41 asks who announced events earlier so their truth could be recognized and finds no predictor; Isaiah 48 says God announced and accomplished former things beforehand so they could not be credited to idols.

Isaiah 41:27
I will give Zion a beginning and comfort Jerusalem for a journey.
  Isaiah 40:1-2 — internal renewed comfort for Jerusalem: Isaiah 41 promises comfort to Jerusalem for a journey; Isaiah 40 commands comfort and speech to Jerusalem's heart because humiliation is fulfilled. The shared named city and promised comfort connect the sayings without substituting a different announcement for 41:27.

Isaiah 41:28
For behold, there is no one from the nations, and none among their idols was an announcer. If I ask them, ‘Where are you from?’ they will certainly not answer Me.
  Psalms 113:12-13 — parallel inability of handmade gods to answer: Isaiah finds no announcer among idols and says they cannot answer God's question about their origin; the psalm calls national idols human handiwork and says their mouths will not speak. The source's examination exposes the speechlessness described by the psalm.

Isaiah 41:29
For there are those making you, and those misleading you do so in vain.”
  Jeremiah 10:14-15 — related futility of makers and manufactured gods: Isaiah identifies makers and futile misleading; Jeremiah describes goldsmiths shamed by false, spiritless carvings and calls their works futile. The surrounding idol challenge supplies the particular setting for both accusations.

Isaiah 42:1
“Jacob is My servant; I will uphold him. Israel is My chosen one; My soul accepted him. I put My spirit upon him; he will bring judgment to the nations.
  Matthew 12:17-18 — explicit servant citation applied to Jesus: Matthew cites Isaiah's chosen servant, divine approval, Spirit and judgment for the nations as fulfilled in Jesus' ministry. Isaiah explicitly names Jacob and Israel in this unit; Matthew's citation does not repeat those names.

Isaiah 42:2
He will not cry out or raise his voice, nor will his voice be heard outside.
  Matthew 12:19 — explicit quotation of the servant's restraint of voice: Matthew's servant citation repeats the refusal to cry out or have His voice heard publicly. Isaiah says outside; Matthew specifies the streets and also denies quarrelling.

Isaiah 42:3
He will not crush a bruised reed or extinguish a smoking flax wick; rather, he will bring judgment in truth.
  Matthew 12:20 — explicit quotation of the bruised reed and smoking wick: Both preserve the bruised reed and smoldering wick and connect this restraint with judgment. Isaiah says judgment in truth; Matthew says justice brought forth to victory.

Isaiah 42:4
He will shine forth and will not be broken until he sets judgment upon the earth, and nations will hope in his name.”
  Matthew 12:21 — explicit quotation of the nations hoping in the servant's name: Matthew concludes its servant citation with nations hoping in His name, matching the source's final clause. It does not repeat this unit's promise that he will shine forth and not be broken.

Isaiah 42:5
Thus says the Lord God, who made heaven and fixed it in place, established the earth and its contents, and gives breath to the people upon it and spirit to those treading it:
  Acts 17:24-25 — close Creator giving life and breath to humanity: Isaiah identifies God as maker of heaven, earth and its contents and giver of breath to its people; Paul likewise names the Maker of the world and Lord of heaven and earth who gives everyone life and breath.

Isaiah 42:6
“I, the Lord God, called you in righteousness; I will hold your hand and strengthen you. I gave you as a covenant for a people, a light for nations,
  Isaiah 49:6 — internal appointment as covenant and light for nations: Both describe God's servant appointed as a covenant for a people or race and a light for nations. Isaiah 49 additionally spells out restoration of Israel's dispersion and salvation to earth's extremity.
  Luke 2:30-32 — related light-for-nations image applied to Jesus: Isaiah appoints the servant a light for nations; Simeon, holding Jesus, calls the salvation he has seen a light for revelation to nations and glory for Israel. Luke's setting gives the image a specified application.

Isaiah 42:7
to open blind people’s eyes, bring those bound out of bonds, and those sitting in darkness out of the prison house.
  Luke 4:18 — related mission of opening blind eyes and releasing captives: Isaiah joins sight for blind people with release from bonds and prison darkness; Jesus reads a mission of recovery of sight and release for captives and the oppressed. Luke's cited passage shares these motifs without being presented as a verbatim quotation of this unit.

Isaiah 42:8
I am the Lord God; this is My name. I will not give My glory to another or My praiseworthy deeds to carved images.
  Isaiah 48:11 — internal refusal to give God's glory to another: Both directly state that God will not give His glory to another. Isaiah 42 additionally says He will not give His praiseworthy deeds to carved images, while Isaiah 48 connects the declaration with His profaned name.

Isaiah 42:9
Behold, the things from the beginning have arrived, and new things I will announce; before they spring up, they were disclosed to you.”
  Isaiah 48:3-5 — internal announcement preceding fulfillment: Isaiah 42 distinguishes former things arrived from new things disclosed before appearing; Isaiah 48 likewise says God announced former events before acting and before they arrived, preventing their attribution to idols.

Isaiah 42:10
Sing a new hymn to the Lord—its beginning: “Glorify His name from the earth’s extremity, you who go down to the sea and sail it, islands and their inhabitants.”
  Psalms 95:1-3 — close new song and praise extending across the earth: Both summon a new song to the Lord and proclamation of His name and glory across the earth and peoples. Isaiah specifically includes seafarers, islands and their inhabitants.

Isaiah 42:11
Rejoice, wilderness and its villages, settlements and those inhabiting Kedar. Those inhabiting Petra will rejoice and cry out from the mountain tops.
  Isaiah 35:1-2 — internal summons for the wilderness to rejoice: Both directly summon wilderness or desert places to rejoicing. Isaiah 42 names Kedar and Petra and their inhabitants; Isaiah 35 describes flowering desert places and the sight of God's glory.

Isaiah 42:12
They will give God glory and announce His praiseworthy deeds in the islands.
  Psalms 95:7-8 — parallel giving of glory to God among peoples: Isaiah predicts giving God glory and announcing His praiseworthy deeds in the islands; the psalm commands families of nations to bring the Lord glory and honor for His name.

Isaiah 42:13
The Lord God of Powers will go out and crush war. He will rouse zeal and cry out mightily against His enemies.
  Exodus 15:3-6 — close Lord shattering war and enemies: Isaiah says the Lord will crush war and cry mightily against His enemies; Exodus calls Him the One who shatters wars and praises His right hand shattering enemies. The supplied wording concerns His breaking war, not a substituted warrior title.

Isaiah 42:14
“I was silent; surely I will not always be silent and hold back? I endured like a woman giving birth; I will astonish and dry up together.
  Psalms 49:21 — parallel divine silence ending in intervention: Isaiah's God says He was silent but will not remain silent and hold back; the psalm likewise moves from God's silence while wrongdoing continued to His announced rebuke. Isaiah's childbirth and drying imagery is additional.

Isaiah 42:15
I will turn rivers into islands and dry up marshes.
  Psalms 106:33-34 — related transformation of waters into dry territory: Isaiah turns rivers into islands and dries marshes; the psalm turns rivers into wilderness and water outlets into thirst. Both describe divine removal or transformation of watery places, with different resulting terrain.

Isaiah 42:16
I will lead the blind on a road they did not know and make them tread paths they did not know. I will turn darkness into light for them and crooked places straight. These things I will do, and I will not abandon them.
  Acts 26:17-18 — related opening of sight and movement from darkness to light: Isaiah promises guidance for blind people and darkness turned to light; Jesus commissions Paul to open people's eyes so they turn from darkness to light and from Satan's authority to God. Acts explicitly describes conversion, while Isaiah also supplies road imagery.

Isaiah 42:17
But they turned backward. Be utterly ashamed, you who trust carved images, who say to cast images, ‘You are our gods.’”
  Psalms 96:7 — close shame of those trusting and worshiping images: Isaiah commands shame upon those trusting carved images and calling cast images their gods; the psalm likewise calls those bowing to carved images and boasting in idols to shame.

Isaiah 42:18
Deaf people, listen; blind people, look up and see!
  Jeremiah 5:20-21 — parallel call for unresponsive people to hear: Isaiah commands deaf people to listen and blind people to look and see; Jeremiah summons Jacob and Judah to hear while describing people with eyes that do not see and ears that do not hear.

Isaiah 42:19
Who is blind except My servants, or deaf except those ruling them? God’s slaves have become blind.
  Matthew 15:12-14 — related failure of spiritual perception among a people and its guides: Isaiah rebukes blind servants of God and deaf rulers; Jesus calls the Pharisees blind guides of blind people. The sensory diagnoses and groups differ, while both expose failed religious perception.

Isaiah 42:20
You saw often but did not keep watch; the ears were opened, but you did not listen.
  Mark 8:17-18 — close rebuke for seeing and hearing without understanding: Isaiah says the hearers saw but did not keep watch and had opened ears but did not listen; Jesus asks His disciples why their eyes and ears do not perceive and why they do not understand.

Isaiah 42:21
The Lord God wished to be justified and to magnify praise. And I saw,
  Romans 3:3-4 — related vindication of God amid human unfaithfulness: Isaiah says the Lord wished to be justified and magnify praise; Paul defends God's faithfulness and applies the saying that God is justified in His words. The comparison concerns divine vindication, preserving the source's actual praise language.

Isaiah 42:22
and the people became plundered and despoiled. For the snare is in the storerooms everywhere and in houses together, where they hid them. They became plunder, and no one rescued the spoil; no one said, ‘Give it back!’
  Ezekiel 34:5-10 — parallel people becoming plunder without rescuing care: Isaiah describes people plundered with no rescuer or demand for their return; Ezekiel describes God's sheep plundered because their shepherds failed to seek them, followed by God's promised rescue. The flock image is Ezekiel's particular formulation.

Isaiah 42:24
Who gave Jacob over to plunder and Israel to those despoiling him? Was it not God, against whom they sinned, refusing to walk in His ways or listen to His law?
  Judges 2:11-15 — parallel God handing disobedient Israel to plunderers: Isaiah identifies God as the One who gave Jacob and Israel to plunder because they sinned and refused His ways; Judges likewise records Israel forsaking Him and His handing them to plunderers and enemies.

Isaiah 42:25
He brought His wrathful anger upon them; war overpowered them, as did those burning them all around. None of them understood, nor did they take it to soul.
  Amos 4:10-11 — parallel severe judgment without the required response: Isaiah describes war and surrounding burning without understanding or taking it to heart; Amos describes sword, camps in fire and a firebrand rescued from burning, yet no return to God. The absent responses are expressed differently.

Isaiah 43:1
But now thus says the Lord God who made you, Jacob, who formed you, Israel: “Do not fear, for I redeemed you. I called you by your name; you are Mine.
  John 10:3-4 — related belonging expressed through calling by name: God calls Israel by name and claims it as His own; Jesus' shepherd figure calls his own sheep by name and leads them. The collective address and the individual sheep in the figure differ.

Isaiah 43:2
Even if you cross through water, I am with you, and rivers will not flood over you. Even if you pass through fire, you will certainly not be burned up; flame will not burn you up.
  Exodus 14:21-22 — historical counterpart to safe passage through water: Isaiah promises God's presence and protection when His people cross waters; Exodus records the Lord opening the sea and Israel walking within it on dry ground between walls of water.
  Daniel 3:49-50 — narrative counterpart to unharmed presence amid fire: Isaiah promises that flame will not burn the people passing through fire; Daniel describes the Lord's angel joining the companions in the furnace and the fire neither touching nor distressing them.

Isaiah 43:3
For I am the Lord your God, Israel’s Holy One, who saves you. I made Egypt your exchange, and Ethiopia and Soene on your behalf.
  Ezekiel 29:19-20 — related Egypt given as divine compensation: Isaiah names Egypt as an exchange on Israel's behalf; Ezekiel describes God giving Egypt to Babylon's king as wages in exchange for service against Tyre. Recipients and grounds differ, and Ezekiel does not supply Isaiah's Ethiopia and Soene clauses.

Isaiah 43:4
Since you became honored before Me, you were glorified, and I loved you. I will give many people on your behalf and rulers for your head.
  Proverbs 11:8 — related another person given in place of a favored recipient: Isaiah promises people and rulers on Israel's behalf; Proverbs describes the righteous escaping a hunt and the impious handed over in his place. Proverbs specifies moral categories that Isaiah does not assign to the exchanged rulers.

Isaiah 43:5
Do not fear, for I am with you. From the east I will bring your offspring, and from the west I will gather you.
  Psalms 106:2-3 — close gathering of the redeemed from east and west: Isaiah promises to bring offspring from the east and gather the people from the west; the psalm calls the redeemed to thanksgiving and says God gathered them from countries including east and west, with north and sea also named.

Isaiah 43:6
I will say to the north, ‘Bring them,’ and to the southwest, ‘Do not hinder. Bring My sons from a distant land and My daughters from the earth’s extremities,
  Isaiah 49:22 — internal return of sons and daughters from abroad: Isaiah 43 commands distant regions to bring God's sons and daughters; Isaiah 49 describes a signal to nations and islands followed by sons brought in the bosom and daughters carried on shoulders. The manner of their return is developed differently.

Isaiah 43:7
all who are called by My name.’ For in My glory I fashioned him, formed him, and made him,
  Jeremiah 14:9 — related community bearing God's name: Isaiah includes all called by God's name in the gathering of His formed people; Jeremiah's community appeals to the fact that God's name has been called over them and asks Him not to forget them. The shared name expresses their belonging to God.

Isaiah 43:8
and I brought out a blind people, and their eyes are likewise blind, and deaf people who have ears.
  Jeremiah 5:20-21 — close sensory blindness and deafness of Jacob's people: Isaiah describes a blind people and deaf people possessing ears; Jeremiah addresses Jacob and Judah as having eyes without seeing and ears without hearing. Both portray the community's failure of perception through the same sensory organs.

Isaiah 43:9
All nations were gathered together, and rulers from them will assemble. Who will announce these things, or who will announce to you the things from the beginning? Let them bring their witnesses, be justified, and speak true things.
  Isaiah 41:22-23 — internal judicial challenge to announce and substantiate events: Isaiah 43 asks assembled nations for announcements and truthful witnesses; Isaiah 41 challenges the claimants to announce former and coming things so their claims to divinity can be tested. Both frame knowledge of events as evidence in the dispute.

Isaiah 43:10
Become witnesses for Me—and I too am a witness, says the Lord God, and the servant whom I chose—so that you may know, believe, and understand that I am. Before Me no other god came to be, nor will there be one after Me.
  Deuteronomy 4:34-35 — parallel knowledge that the Lord is the only God: Isaiah appoints witnesses so they may know and believe God's identity and denies any other god before or after Him; Deuteronomy describes God's acts so Israel may know He is God and there is none beside Him.
  John 13:19 — related belief in 'I am' through disclosed events: Isaiah links witness, knowledge and belief with God's declaration 'I am'; Jesus says He tells events beforehand so that, when they happen, His hearers may believe 'I am.' The speakers and immediate disclosed events are distinguished.

Isaiah 43:11
I am God, and apart from Me there is no one who saves.
  Hosea 13:4 — close declaration of no Savior apart from the Lord: Both identify the speaker as the Lord God and expressly deny anyone who saves apart from Him. Hosea additionally recalls creation and the exodus.
  Acts 4:10-12 — related exclusive claim concerning salvation: Isaiah says no one saves apart from God; Peter names Jesus Christ and says salvation is in no one else and no other saving name is given. Acts makes its particular application to Jesus explicit.

Isaiah 43:12
I announced and saved; I reproached, and there was no foreign one among you. You are witnesses for Me, and I too am a witness, says the Lord God,
  Acts 1:8 — related appointment of a people as witnesses: Isaiah's God calls the people witnesses to His announced and saving acts; Jesus tells His chosen apostles they will be His witnesses from Jerusalem to earth's end. Isaiah additionally declares God Himself a witness.

Isaiah 43:13
still from the beginning. No one rescues from My hands. I will act, and who will turn it back?”
  Deuteronomy 32:39 — close impossibility of rescue from God's hand: Both explicitly deny that anyone can deliver or rescue from God's hands. Isaiah continues by asking who can reverse His action; Deuteronomy names His power to kill, make alive, strike and heal.

Isaiah 43:14
Thus says the Lord God who redeems you, Israel’s Holy One: “For your sake I will send to Babylon and rouse all who flee, and Chaldeans will be bound in ships.
  Jeremiah 27:33-34 — parallel Israel's Redeemer acting against Babylon: Isaiah's Redeemer promises intervention in Babylon for the people's sake; Jeremiah names their strong Redeemer and says He will judge His adversaries and provoke Babylon's inhabitants. The ships and fleeing in Isaiah are not repeated by Jeremiah.

Isaiah 43:15
I am the Lord God, your Holy One, who presented Israel as your king.”
  Isaiah 43:3 — verbal echo: In the same chapter God declares, 'I am the Lord your God, Israel's Holy One', closely matching 'I am the Lord God, your Holy One' here. Isaiah 43:3 continues with God saving Israel, whereas 43:15 adds that God presented Israel as your king.

Isaiah 43:16
Thus says the Lord, who gives a way in the sea and a path in strong water,
  Exodus 14:21-22 — historical counterpart of a path through the sea: Isaiah identifies the Lord as giving a way through sea and strong water; Exodus records His opening the sea so Israel crosses on dry ground. The narrative supplies the particular sea-passage pattern recalled by the description.

Isaiah 43:17
who brought out chariots and a horse and a mighty host: But they fell asleep and will not rise; they were extinguished like an extinguished flax wick.
  Exodus 14:23-28 — historical counterpart of the destruction of chariots and horsemen: Isaiah describes chariots, horse and mighty host brought out and finally extinguished; Exodus describes Pharaoh's horses, chariots and riders entering the sea and being covered, with none remaining. The sleep and wick metaphors are Isaiah's own images.

Isaiah 43:18
“Do not remember the first things or ponder the ancient ones.
  Philippians 3:13-14 — related movement of attention beyond former things: Isaiah commands not remembering or pondering former things before announcing a new divine work; Paul describes forgetting what is behind and stretching toward the goal ahead. The former things and the future object differ.

Isaiah 43:19
Behold, I am doing new things which will now spring up, and you will know them. I will make a way in the wilderness and rivers in the waterless land.
  Isaiah 35:6-8 — internal joining of wilderness water and a new road: Isaiah 43 promises a wilderness way and rivers in waterless land; Isaiah 35 likewise describes water bursting in wilderness, thirsty land becoming springs, and a Holy Way for the scattered.

Isaiah 43:20
The field’s wild beasts will bless Me, sirens and daughters of ostriches, because I gave water in the wilderness and rivers in the waterless land to give drink to My chosen race,
  Psalms 148:7-13 — parallel praise offered by wild creatures: Isaiah says wild beasts, sirens and ostrich daughters will bless God because He gives wilderness water; the psalm summons wild animals, cattle, creeping things and birds to praise His name. The species and the stated grounds differ.

Isaiah 43:21
My people, whom I acquired to recount My praiseworthy deeds.
  1 Peter 2:9 — close acquired people appointed to proclaim divine praise: Isaiah identifies God's acquired people as those who recount His praiseworthy deeds; First Peter likewise calls its addressees a people for God's possession so that they proclaim His excellences.

Isaiah 43:22
I did not call you now, Jacob, nor did I make you toil, Israel.
  Micah 6:3-4 — related divine denial of having burdened Israel: Isaiah's God says He did not make Israel toil; Micah's God asks how He grieved or troubled the people and recalls their redemption. This compares the labor clause without reversing Isaiah's opening statement that God did not call Jacob now.

Isaiah 43:23
The sheep for your whole burnt offering were not for Me, nor did you glorify Me with your sacrifices, nor did I make you toil with frankincense.
  Psalms 49:8-14 — related challenge concerning animal offerings as service to God: Isaiah says the sheep offerings were not for God and the sacrifices did not glorify Him; the psalm refuses supplied calves and goats, declares God's ownership of all animals and calls instead for a sacrifice of praise.

Isaiah 43:24
You did not acquire incense for Me with silver, nor did I desire the fat of your sacrifices; instead, amid your sins and wrongdoings I stood over you.
  Psalms 49:12-13 — parallel rejection of God's supposed need for animal food: Isaiah says God did not desire the sacrifices' fat; the psalm says the world is His and asks whether He eats bulls' flesh or drinks goats' blood. Both challenge the idea that sacrificial food meets a divine need.

Isaiah 43:25
I am, I am the One who wipes out your lawless acts, and I will certainly not remember.
  Jeremiah 38:34 — close promise to forgive and no longer remember sins: Isaiah's God wipes out lawless acts and will not remember; Jeremiah's new-covenant promise likewise joins mercy toward injustices with sins remembered no more.

Isaiah 43:26
But you, remember, and let us be judged. You, state your lawless acts first, so that you may be justified.
  1 John 1:8-9 — related confession of sins and restoration to right standing: Isaiah directs the addressee to state lawless acts so as to be justified; First John connects confession of sins with God's forgiveness and cleansing from unrighteousness. The stated outcomes are related but differently expressed.

Isaiah 43:27
Your first fathers and their rulers acted lawlessly against Me,
  Psalms 105:6-7 — parallel acknowledgment of ancestral lawlessness: Isaiah says the first fathers and their rulers acted lawlessly against God; the psalmist acknowledges sin with the fathers and recalls their failure to understand and remember God's deeds in Egypt. Isaiah does not specify that particular ancestral episode here.

Isaiah 43:28
and the rulers defiled My holy things, and I gave Jacob over to destruction and Israel to reproach.”
  2 Chronicles 36:14-17 — historical counterpart of sanctuary defilement and national judgment: Isaiah links rulers defiling holy things with Jacob handed to destruction and Israel to reproach; Chronicles describes leaders, priests and people defiling the Lord's house, rejecting His messengers and being handed to the Chaldeans.

Isaiah 44:1
“But now listen, My servant Jacob, and Israel whom I chose.
  Isaiah 41:8 — internal repeated naming of God's chosen servant: Both name Israel and Jacob as God's servant and chosen people. The recurring address identifies the people receiving the assurance without importing a different servant identity into this unit.

Isaiah 44:2
Thus says the Lord God who made you and formed you from the womb: You will yet be helped. Do not fear, My servant Jacob and beloved Israel whom I chose.
  Jeremiah 1:4-5 — related divine formation from the womb and choice for service: Isaiah addresses chosen Jacob and Israel as formed from the womb; Jeremiah is told that God knew and consecrated him before his birth and appointed him a prophet. The corporate people and the individual prophet are distinct recipients.

Isaiah 44:3
For I will give water in thirst to those traveling through waterless land. I will place My spirit upon your offspring and My blessings upon your children,
  Joel 3:1-2 — close promise of God's Spirit upon descendants: Isaiah promises His Spirit upon the people's offspring and blessings upon their children; Joel promises an outpouring of His Spirit and prophecy by sons and daughters, extending also to male and female slaves.
  John 7:37-39 — related joining of thirst, water and the Spirit: Isaiah joins water for thirsty travelers with the Spirit placed upon offspring; Jesus invites thirsty people to drink, speaks of living water and, as John explains, means the Spirit believers will receive. John explicitly interprets its water image; Isaiah places the two promises alongside one another.

Isaiah 44:4
and they will spring up like grass amid water and like a willow beside flowing water.
  Psalms 1:3 — parallel flourishing through proximity to water: Isaiah compares the offspring's growth to grass amid water and a willow beside flowing water; the psalm compares the blessed person to a tree beside water outlets with fruit and an unfailing leaf. The plants and recipients differ within the shared growth image.

Isaiah 44:5
One will say, ‘I belong to God’; another will cry out in Jacob’s name; another will inscribe, ‘I belong to God,’ in Israel’s name.”
  Zechariah 13:9 — related confession of belonging to the Lord: Isaiah describes people declaring and inscribing that they belong to God in the names of Jacob and Israel; Zechariah describes the tested remnant answering God's 'My people' with 'The Lord is my God.' Both express acknowledged belonging, without matching the inscriptions.

Isaiah 44:6
Thus says God, Israel’s King, who rescued him, God Sabaoth: “I am first, and I am after these things; apart from Me there is no God.
  Isaiah 48:12 — internal declaration of divine priority and enduring existence: Isaiah 44 says God is first and after these things; Isaiah 48 says He is first and forever. Both assert His encompassing priority and continuance, preserving the supplied wording rather than replacing it with a standard first-and-last formula.

Isaiah 44:7
Who is like Me? Let him stand, call out, and prepare for Me, from when I made humankind for eternity. Let them announce to you the coming things before they arrive.
  Isaiah 46:9-10 — internal divine incomparability demonstrated by announcing events: Both deny a rival like God and connect His uniqueness with announcement of what is coming.
