Isaiah 44 challenges rivals to announce events before arrival; Isaiah 46 says He announces final things beforehand and His counsel stands.

Isaiah 44:8
Do not cover yourselves. Did you not hear from the beginning, and did I not announce it to you? You are witnesses whether there is a God apart from Me; there were none then.”
  Isaiah 43:10-12 — internal witnesses to God's unique identity: Isaiah 44 asks the witnesses whether any God exists apart from Him and recalls His announcements; Isaiah 43 names the people witnesses to His announced and saving acts and denies other gods. This comparison concerns the witness and identity clauses.

Isaiah 44:9
All who fashion and carve are futile, making what pleases them, things that will not help them. But they will be ashamed—
  Wisdom of Solomon 13:10 — parallel futility of hope in crafted gods: Isaiah calls image makers futile and their pleasing products unable to help; Wisdom calls hope in dead things wretched and identifies handmade objects called gods. Both expose the gap between craftsmanship and divine assistance.

Isaiah 44:10
all who fashion a god and carve useless things,
  Wisdom of Solomon 15:7-8 — parallel craftsman fashioning a futile god: Isaiah condemns fashioning a god and carving useless things; Wisdom describes a potter forming a futile god from the same clay used for ordinary vessels. The materials and crafts differ, while the manufactured deity's futility is explicit in both.

Isaiah 44:12
For a craftsman sharpened iron, worked it with an adze, bored it with a drill, and worked it with his strong arm. He will hunger and grow weak and certainly not drink water. Having selected
  Isaiah 40:28-29 — internal contrast of weakened maker and inexhaustible Creator: Isaiah's idol craftsman hungers and grows weak despite his strong arm; Isaiah 40 says God will not hunger or grow weary and gives strength to hungry people. The contrast separates the human maker's dependence from the Creator's sustaining power.

Isaiah 44:13
wood, a craftsman set it by measure and arranged it with glue. He made it like a man’s form and human beauty, to set it in a house.
  Wisdom of Solomon 13:13-15 — close wooden human image installed in a dwelling: Isaiah's woodworker shapes a human form and beauty to stand in a house; Wisdom's craftsman shapes leftover wood as a human image and prepares it a dwelling, setting and securing it in a wall. The technical details differ but the manufactured form and installation correspond.

Isaiah 44:14
The wood he cut from the woodland was what the Lord planted and rain made grow,
  Psalms 103:13-16 — parallel God growing and watering the trees: Isaiah says the wood came from woodland planted by the Lord and grown by rain; the psalm describes God watering mountains and trees filled, including the cedars He planted. Isaiah's subsequent idol manufacture thus uses a resource attributed to God.

Isaiah 44:15
so that it might serve people for burning. Taking some, he warmed himself; after burning it they baked loaves over the pieces. They worked the rest into gods and worship them.
  Wisdom of Solomon 13:12-13 — close division of wood between cooking fuel and an image: Isaiah's people burn some wood for warmth and bread and make the rest into gods; Wisdom's craftsman burns scraps to prepare food and carves a remaining scrap as a human image. Both juxtapose ordinary fuel and a fabricated deity from the same material.

Isaiah 44:16
Half of it they burned in fire, and after burning it they baked loaves over the pieces. Over it he roasted meat, ate, and was satisfied. Warmed, he said, ‘It is pleasant for me, for I am warm and have seen fire!’
  Wisdom of Solomon 13:12 — close burning of wood to prepare food and eat one's fill: Isaiah describes bread and roasted meat prepared over the wood, eaten to satisfaction; Wisdom likewise describes burned scraps used to prepare food and eating one's fill. Isaiah additionally supplies the warmth and the speaker's delighted exclamation.

Isaiah 44:17
He made the rest into a carved god and worships it and prays, saying, ‘Rescue me, for you are my god.’
  Jeremiah 2:27-28 — related critique of expecting rescue from manufactured gods: Isaiah's maker worships the remaining wood and asks his carved god to rescue him; Jeremiah describes address to wood and stone, appeals for salvation in calamity, and challenges whether the manufactured gods can rise and save. The wording exposes dependence on what the people made.

Isaiah 44:18
They did not know enough to understand, because they were darkened from seeing with their eyes and comprehending with their heart.
  Romans 1:21-23 — close darkened understanding in the turn to images: Isaiah describes eyes and heart darkened from perception in the idol-making account; Paul describes futile thoughts and a darkened heart as people exchange divine glory for images of mortal beings. Paul develops the pattern as refusal to honor the known God.

Isaiah 44:19
He did not reckon it in his heart, consider it in his soul, or know by understanding that he burned half in fire, baked loaves over its coals, roasted meat and ate, and made its remainder an abomination; and they worship it.
  Wisdom of Solomon 13:12-17 — parallel failure to reckon with an image's dependence: Isaiah rebukes failure to consider that wood used for cooking became a worshiped abomination; Wisdom likewise traces wood from food preparation to an installed image needing support, yet addressed in prayer. The accounts expose the irrational treatment of dependent material as a deity.

Isaiah 44:20
Know that their heart is ashes, and they wander, and no one can rescue his soul. See—will you not say, ‘A lie is in my right hand’?
  Wisdom of Solomon 15:10-11 — close heart-of-ashes description in idol criticism: Isaiah says their heart is ashes and they wander without rescue; Wisdom likewise calls the idol maker's heart ashes, his hope worthless and his life dishonorable because he failed to know his Maker. The shared image belongs to criticism of manufactured divinity in both contexts.

Isaiah 44:21
“Remember these things, Jacob and Israel, for you are My servant. I formed you as My servant; and you, Israel, do not forget Me.
  Deuteronomy 6:12-13 — parallel command that Israel not forget its God: Isaiah reminds formed Jacob and Israel of servant status and commands Israel not to forget God; Deuteronomy warns not to forget the God who brought them from Egypt and commands service to Him. The direction of remembering is the people's duty in both.

Isaiah 44:22
For behold, I wiped away your lawless acts like a cloud and your sins like dark mist. Turn back to Me, and I will redeem you.”
  Acts 3:19 — close turning back connected with sins wiped away: Isaiah joins sins and lawless acts wiped away with the command to turn back to God and the promise of redemption; Peter likewise commands repentance and turning back so sins may be wiped away. The cloud and mist imagery belongs to Isaiah.

Isaiah 44:23
Rejoice, heavens, because God had mercy on Israel! Sound trumpets, earth’s foundations! Cry out joy, mountains, hills and all their trees! For God redeemed Jacob, and Israel will be glorified.
  Psalms 95:11-13 — close cosmic rejoicing including heavens, earth and trees: Isaiah summons heaven, earth's foundations, mountains and trees to joy over God's mercy and redemption of Israel; the psalm summons heavens, earth and woodland trees to rejoice before God's coming judgment. The shared cosmic praise has distinct stated occasions.

Isaiah 44:24
Thus says the Lord who redeems you and forms you from the womb: “I am the Lord who completes all things. I stretched heaven alone and established the earth. Who else
  Job 9:8 — close declaration that God alone stretched out heaven: Isaiah's God says He stretched heaven alone; Job likewise attributes stretching heaven to God alone. Both exclude a companion maker in this particular act, while their adjoining earth and sea descriptions differ.

Isaiah 44:25
will scatter the signs of spirit-mediums and divinations from the heart, turning the discerning backward and making their counsel foolish,
  1 Corinthians 1:19-20 — parallel God making reputed wisdom foolish: Isaiah describes God turning the discerning backward and making their counsel foolish; Paul says God sets aside understanding and makes the world's wisdom foolish. Paul's quoted wisdom saying is not presented here as a direct quotation of Isaiah 44.

Isaiah 44:26
and establish His servant’s words and make His messengers’ counsel true? He says to Jerusalem, ‘You will be inhabited,’ and to Judea’s cities, ‘You will be rebuilt,’ and her deserted places will spring up;
  Zechariah 1:16-17 — parallel promise of rebuilt Jerusalem and restored cities: Isaiah announces inhabited Jerusalem, rebuilt Judean cities and deserted places springing up; Zechariah announces God's compassionate return to Jerusalem, His house rebuilt and cities overflowing with good things. Both promise restored city life under God's action.

Isaiah 44:27
He says to the abyss, ‘You will be desolate, and I will dry your rivers’;
  Psalms 105:9 — historical counterpart to God's drying of deep waters: Isaiah's God declares the abyss desolate and its rivers dry; the psalm recalls His rebuke drying the Red Sea and His guidance through the deep as wilderness. The psalm supplies a concrete past act corresponding to the divine authority over deep water.

Isaiah 44:28
He tells Cyrus to understand, and says, ‘He will do all My wishes.’ He says to Jerusalem, ‘You will be rebuilt, and I will lay the foundation of My holy house.’”
  Ezra 1:1-3 — historical counterpart of Cyrus commissioned for Jerusalem's temple: Isaiah names Cyrus, says he will do God's wishes and promises Jerusalem and the holy house's foundation; Ezra records God stirring Cyrus and the king's proclamation that God commissioned him to build His house in Jerusalem. Isaiah's actual instruction to Cyrus is to understand.
  Ezra 3:10-11 — narrative counterpart of the temple foundation laid: Isaiah promises that God will lay the foundation of His holy house; Ezra records the builders laying the Lord's house's foundation and priests, Levites and people celebrating it. This connection focuses on the foundation itself rather than Cyrus's proclamation.

Isaiah 45:1
Thus says the Lord God to My anointed Cyrus, whose right hand I grasped so that nations would heed him: “I will shatter kings’ strength, open doors before him, and cities will not be shut.
  Ezra 1:1-4 — named royal commission and narrative counterpart: Isaiah names Cyrus as the ruler whose hand God grasps to subdue nations. Ezra names Cyrus, attributes his kingdoms and Jerusalem commission to the Lord, and records the proclamation enabling the return. Ezra does not repeat the anointing language.

Isaiah 45:2
I will go before you and level mountains. I will shatter bronze doors and break iron bars.
  Psalms 106:15-16 — close verbal parallel in shattered barriers: Both attribute the shattering of bronze gates or doors and the breaking of iron bars to God. Isaiah applies the imagery to the way opened before Cyrus; the psalm praises divine deliverance.

Isaiah 45:3
I will give you dark treasures and open hidden, unseen stores to you, so that you may know that I am the Lord God who calls your name, Israel’s God.
  Isaiah 43:1 — internal divine calling by name: The God who calls Cyrus by name in Isaiah 45 also tells Jacob and Israel that He called them by name in Isaiah 43. The recipients differ; the repeated personal summons belongs to the same God's purposeful action.

Isaiah 45:4
For the sake of My servant Jacob and Israel My chosen one, I will call you by your name and welcome you; yet you did not know Me.
  Isaiah 41:8-9 — internal identification of the chosen servant: Isaiah 45 explains Cyrus's call by reference to God's servant Jacob and chosen Israel. Isaiah 41 explicitly calls Israel and Jacob God's chosen servant and describes taking and calling that servant.

Isaiah 45:5
For I am the Lord God, and there is no god besides Me, and you did not know Me,
  Deuteronomy 4:35-39 — shared declaration of divine uniqueness: Isaiah declares that the Lord is God with none besides Him, despite Cyrus's lack of knowledge. Deuteronomy uses the same exclusive declaration as the knowledge Israel should gain from God's acts.

Isaiah 45:6
so that those from the sun’s rising and those from its setting may know that there is none besides Me. I am the Lord God, and there is no other.
  Malachi 1:11 — shared worldwide recognition from sunrise to sunset: Both use the span from the sun's rising to its setting for recognition of the Lord among peoples. Isaiah emphasizes knowing there is no other God; Malachi emphasizes His glorified name and worship.

Isaiah 45:7
I fashioned light and made darkness; I make peace and create calamities. I am the Lord God, who does all these things.
  Amos 3:6 — divine agency in calamity: Isaiah expressly says that God makes peace and creates calamities. Amos similarly asks whether a calamity occurs in a city without the Lord doing it. Neither connection requires replacing calamities with a claim that God commits moral wrongdoing.
  Genesis 1:1-5 — creation of light and ordering of darkness: Isaiah identifies God as the One fashioning light and making darkness. Genesis narrates God's command bringing light and His division and naming of light and darkness within creation.

Isaiah 45:8
Let heaven above rejoice, and let clouds sprinkle righteousness. Let earth bring forth mercy and bring forth righteousness together. I am the Lord who created you.”
  Psalms 84:11-13 — shared heavenly and earthly growth imagery: Isaiah calls clouds to sprinkle righteousness and earth to produce mercy and righteousness. The psalm likewise joins mercy and righteousness with truth springing from earth, righteousness looking from heaven, and the land yielding fruit.

Isaiah 45:9
What better thing did I fashion like a potter’s clay? Will the plower plow the earth all day? Will the clay say to the potter, ‘What are you doing, since you neither work nor have hands?’
  Romans 9:19-21 — potter and clay objection parallel: Both challenge the formed thing's questioning of its maker through potter-and-clay imagery. Romans develops the potter's authority over vessels; Isaiah also includes the plower question and must not be reduced to Romans's wording.

Isaiah 45:12
I made earth and humanity upon it. With My hand I established heaven; I commanded all the stars.
  Nehemiah 9:6 — creation of earth and the heavenly array: Isaiah attributes earth, its humanity, heaven and the commanded stars to God's hand. Nehemiah's prayer likewise names the Lord alone as maker of heaven and its array, earth and everything in it.

Isaiah 45:13
I raised him as king with righteousness, and all his ways are straight. He will rebuild My city and bring back My people’s captivity, without ransom or gifts,” said the Lord Sabaoth.
  Ezra 1:1-4 — royal return and Jerusalem rebuilding counterpart: Isaiah describes a divinely raised king rebuilding God's city and releasing His people's captivity. Ezra records Cyrus directing God's people to return to Jerusalem and build the temple. It supplies the return-and-building counterpart without independently repeating the no-ransom clause.

Isaiah 45:14
Thus says the Lord Sabaoth: “Egypt toiled, and the Ethiopians’ trade; and the Seboin, tall men, will cross over to you and become your slaves. They will follow behind you, bound with handcuffs, bow to you, and pray in you, because God is in you. They will say, ‘There is no God besides Thee;
  1 Corinthians 14:24-25 — bowing with recognition of God's presence: Isaiah's arriving peoples bow and acknowledge that God is in the one addressed. Paul likewise describes an outsider falling down to worship and declaring that God is truly among the believers. The captive procession and a prophetic gathering are different settings.

Isaiah 45:15
for Thou art God, and we did not know it, Israel’s God, the Savior.’”
  Isaiah 43:10-11 — recognition of Israel's God as Savior: The confession in Isaiah 45 acknowledges Israel's God as Savior after not knowing Him. Isaiah 43 joins knowing and understanding God with His declaration that no one saves apart from Him. The source does not describe a God hiding Himself.

Isaiah 45:16
All who oppose Him will be ashamed and abashed and go away in shame. “Be renewed toward Me, islands.”
  Isaiah 41:11-12 — internal judgment of opponents through shame: Isaiah 45 says God's opponents will be ashamed and abashed. Isaiah 41 uses the same paired shame language for those opposing His people; the stated objects of opposition differ.

Isaiah 45:17
Israel is saved by the Lord with everlasting salvation. They will not be ashamed or ever be abashed for eternity.
  Romans 11:25-27 — Israel's salvation and enduring divine commitment: Isaiah promises Israel salvation by the Lord without final shame. Romans also speaks of Israel being saved, relating that hope to the Deliverer and God's covenant taking away sins; it develops a distinct argument concerning Gentiles and Israel.

Isaiah 45:18
Thus says the Lord who made heaven—He is the God who displayed the earth and made it, who marked it out; He did not make it for emptiness, but to be inhabited—“I am, and there is no other.
  Acts 17:24-26 — creation intended for human habitation: Isaiah says God made the earth to be inhabited rather than empty. Acts identifies the maker of heaven and earth as the One who made humanity to dwell across the earth and appointed the boundaries of dwelling.

Isaiah 45:19
I have not spoken in secret or in a dark place of the earth. I did not say to Jacob’s offspring, ‘Seek a futile thing.’ I am, I am the Lord, speaking righteousness and announcing truth.
  John 18:19-21 — open speech contrasted with secrecy: The Lord in Isaiah denies speaking secretly or in a dark place. Jesus in John's account likewise says He spoke openly and nothing in secret, directing questioners to His hearers. This compares the explicit public-speech claims without equating their immediate settings.

Isaiah 45:20
Gather and come; take counsel together, you who are saved from the nations. Those carrying wood as their carved image did not know, praying as though to gods who do not save.
  Jeremiah 10:3-5 — carried wooden idols unable to benefit: Isaiah exposes people carrying a wooden image and praying to gods unable to save. Jeremiah describes wooden manufactured gods that must be carried because they cannot move and have no good in them.

Isaiah 45:21
If they will announce it, let them draw near so that they may know together who made these things heard from the beginning. Then it was announced to you: I am God, and there is no other besides Me; there is none righteous and saving apart from Me.
  Isaiah 43:9-13 — internal challenge of prediction and unique salvation: Both passages challenge other claimants to announce what was known from the beginning, then declare the Lord's unique identity and saving power. The combination of announcement, witnesses or assembled hearers, and exclusive salvation grounds the connection.

Isaiah 45:22
Turn back to Me, and you will be saved, you from the earth’s extremity. I am God, and there is no other.
  Acts 17:29-31 — universal call to turn to God: Isaiah calls people at the earth's extremity to turn to God and be saved. Acts rejects manufactured divinity and commands all people everywhere to repent, adding the appointed judgment through the risen Man.

Isaiah 45:23
By Myself I swear: Surely righteousness will go out from My mouth; My words will not return, because every knee will bend to Me and every tongue will confess to God,
  Romans 14:10-12 — explicit scriptural citation: Romans introduces as written the declaration that every knee will bend and every tongue confess to God, matching Isaiah's central wording. Paul uses it to explain each person's accountability at God's judgment seat.
  Philippians 2:9-11 — New Testament reuse of universal homage language: Philippians repeats the bending of every knee and confession of every tongue in its account of Jesus's exaltation. Isaiah's universal homage to God supplies the corresponding wording; Philippians explicitly names Jesus Christ as Lord and God the Father as glorified.

Isaiah 45:24
saying, ‘Righteousness and glory will come to Him,’ and all who set themselves apart will be ashamed.
  Isaiah 41:11-12 — internal contrast between divine glory and opponents' shame: Isaiah 45 sets righteousness and glory coming to Him beside shame for those setting themselves apart. Isaiah 41 likewise announces shame and disappearance for adversaries. The link concerns the opponents' shame, without changing Isaiah 45's pronouns into a personal claim of strength.

Isaiah 45:25
From the Lord all the offspring of Israel’s sons will be justified, and in God they will be glorified.”
  Romans 8:28-30 — justification joined to glorification by God: Isaiah joins Israel's offspring being justified from the Lord with being glorified in God. Romans likewise joins God's justifying and glorifying those He calls, within its own account of conformity to His Son.

Isaiah 46:1
Bel fell; Dagon was shattered. Their carved images became a burden for wild and domestic animals. Lift them, bound together, as a load for one who is weary
  1 Samuel 5:2-4 — named idol's fall and destruction: Isaiah's actual text names Dagon as shattered. Samuel narrates Dagon falling before the ark and losing head and hands. The shared named idol and its humiliation support the connection without substituting Nebo into Isaiah or identifying the events as one occurrence.

Isaiah 46:2
and hungry and exhausted, without strength at all; they cannot be saved from war, and they themselves were led captive.
  Isaiah 46:3-4 — same-chapter contrast between helpless burdens and saving care: The burden described across verses 1-2 ends in inability to be saved from war and in captivity. The following address contrasts this with God's own carrying, making and saving of Israel through old age. The reversal of who carries and who can save is explicit in the chapter.

Isaiah 46:3
“Listen to Me, house of Jacob and all Israel’s remnant, you who are carried from the belly and instructed from childhood.
  Psalms 70:17 — instruction from childhood: Isaiah addresses the remnant as instructed from childhood. The psalmist likewise thanks God for teaching him from youth, grounding a lifelong account of God's care.

Isaiah 46:4
Until old age I am, and until you grow very old I am. I bear with you; I made you, and I will release you. I will take you up and save you.
  Psalms 70:18 — divine care into old age: Isaiah promises God's sustaining and saving presence until advanced age. The psalmist asks God not to forsake him in old age and advanced years. One is an assurance to Israel, the other a prayer for continuing support.

Isaiah 46:5
To whom did you liken Me? Look, devise your handiwork, you who wander astray.
  Isaiah 40:18-20 — internal rejection of a manufactured likeness of God: Both ask to whom the Lord could be likened and immediately contrast Him with human handiwork. Isaiah 40 specifies the craftsman, goldsmith and stabilized wooden image that clarify the kind of comparison being challenged.

Isaiah 46:6
Those contributing gold from a pouch will set silver on a balance by weight; after hiring a goldsmith they made handmade objects and bend down to worship them.
  Jeremiah 10:3-5 — manufactured precious-metal idols: Isaiah describes measured gold and silver, a hired goldsmith and worship of the handmade result. Jeremiah similarly describes crafted wood adorned with silver and gold and mechanically fastened in place.

Isaiah 46:7
They lift it onto their shoulders and go along. If they set it down, it stays in its place and will never move. Whoever cries to it, it will certainly not listen or save him from evils.
  Jeremiah 10:5 — immobility and helplessness of carried idols: Both expose images that must be lifted and carried because they cannot move themselves. Isaiah adds their inability to hear or save, while Jeremiah denies their ability to do evil or provide good.

Isaiah 46:8
Remember these things and groan. Repent, you who have wandered astray; turn back in heart.
  Joel 2:12-13 — repentance as a return of the heart: Isaiah calls the wandering to remember, groan, repent and return in heart. Joel likewise summons a whole-hearted return accompanied by mourning and emphasizes the heart rather than merely torn clothing.

Isaiah 46:9
Remember the former things from eternity, for I am God, and there is none besides Me,
  Isaiah 44:6 — internal declaration that no other god exists: The call to remember former things leads to God's declaration that none exists beside Him. Isaiah 44 combines the same exclusion of another god with His identity as first and after these things.

Isaiah 46:10
announcing the last things beforehand, before they came to be—and together they were fulfilled. I said, ‘All My counsel will stand, and I will do everything I have planned,’
  Proverbs 19:21 — enduring divine counsel: Isaiah says God's counsel will stand and His plans be accomplished. Proverbs contrasts many human reasonings with the Lord's counsel remaining for ever.

Isaiah 46:11
calling a bird from the east and from a distant land concerning what I have planned. I spoke and brought it about; I created and acted. I brought him and made his journey prosper.
  Isaiah 48:15 — internal repeated calling and prospering of a journey: Both passages join God's speaking and bringing the commissioned person with making his journey prosper. Isaiah 46 additionally uses the eastern bird image; that image is not supplied to Isaiah 48.

Isaiah 46:12
Listen to Me, you who have lost heart, who are far from righteousness.
  Isaiah 46:13 — same-chapter reversal from far to near righteousness: The address to people who have lost heart and are far from righteousness is immediately answered by God bringing His righteousness near and not delaying salvation. The repeated righteousness and opposing distance terms make the literary connection explicit, without turning lost heart into a claim of stubbornness.

Isaiah 46:13
I brought My righteousness near, and I will not delay the salvation that comes from Me. I have given salvation in Zion to Israel for glory.”
  Isaiah 56:1 — internal nearness of salvation: Isaiah 46 brings righteousness near and says salvation will not delay. Isaiah 56 likewise announces that salvation has drawn near and therefore calls for justice and righteousness; its final clause names the revealing of mercy.

Isaiah 47:1
“Come down and sit on the ground, virgin daughter Babylon. Enter darkness, daughter of the Chaldeans, for you will no longer be called tender and pampered.
  Isaiah 13:19-22 — internal downfall of glorious Babylon: Isaiah 47 brings pampered daughter Babylon down to the ground and darkness. Isaiah 13 likewise moves glorious Babylon into overthrow and permanent desolation. Both reverse the city's prestige, using different images of the resulting condition.

Isaiah 47:2
Take a millstone and grind flour. Uncover your veil, expose your gray hair, pull up your garments to bare your lower legs, and cross rivers.
  Judges 16:21 — grinding as an image of humiliation: Babylon's fall is pictured through the command to take a millstone and grind flour. Judges narrates captive Samson grinding grain in prison after his capture and blinding. The common labor image marks humiliation, without transferring the blinding or prison details to Babylon.

Isaiah 47:3
Your shame will be uncovered, and your reproaches will be visible. I will exact justice from you; I will certainly no longer hand it over to humans.”
  Nahum 3:5-6 — city judgment through exposed shame: Isaiah threatens the uncovering of Babylon's shame in divine justice. Nahum likewise portrays a judged city with its shame and dishonor exposed before nations and kingdoms.

Isaiah 47:4
The One who rescued you said—His name is the Lord Sabaoth, Israel’s Holy One—
  Isaiah 43:14 — internal naming of Israel's rescuer: Both identify the speaking Lord as Israel's Holy One and rescuer or redeemer. Isaiah 43 directly places that identity within action concerning Babylon and the Chaldeans, the city addressed in Isaiah 47.

Isaiah 47:5
“Sit stricken and enter darkness, daughter of the Chaldeans. You will certainly no longer be called a kingdom’s strength.
  Isaiah 13:19-22 — internal loss of Babylon's royal standing: Isaiah 47 says the Chaldean daughter will no longer bear the title of a kingdom's strength. Isaiah 13 describes Babylon's royal glory giving way to uninhabited ruins, a corresponding reversal of its public standing.

Isaiah 47:6
I was provoked against My people; you defiled My inheritance. I gave it into your hand, but you gave them no mercy; you made the elder’s yoke exceedingly heavy.
  Zechariah 1:14-16 — nations exceeding the scope of divine anger: Isaiah says God handed His people over, but their captor gave no mercy and burdened the elderly. Zechariah likewise distinguishes God's anger from the nations' added assault bringing evils, and announces renewed compassion for Jerusalem.

Isaiah 47:7
You said, ‘I will rule for ever.’ You did not consider these things in your heart or remember the last things.
  Lamentations 1:9 — failure to consider the end before a city's fall: Babylon assumes perpetual rule and does not remember the last things. Lamentations similarly says Jerusalem did not remember her last things before being brought exceedingly low. The cities and circumstances differ, while the neglected end is explicit in both.

Isaiah 47:8
Now hear these things, pampered one who sits confident, saying in her heart, ‘I am, and there is no other. I will not sit as a widow or know bereavement.’
  Zephaniah 2:15 — confident city's exclusive self-claim: Both depict a city dwelling confidently and saying in her heart that she alone is and has no counterpart. Zephaniah joins the claim to the city's desolation; Isaiah additionally names Babylon's denial of widowhood and bereavement.

Isaiah 47:9
Now these two will come upon you suddenly, in one day: widowhood and childlessness will come suddenly upon you, amid your sorcery and the overwhelming power of your enchanters,
  Jeremiah 15:8-9 — sudden widowhood and bereavement in judgment: Isaiah pairs widowhood and childlessness arriving suddenly on Babylon. Jeremiah likewise combines multiplied widows, sudden distress for a mother, and the emptying of a woman who bore seven. Sorcery belongs to Isaiah's setting and is not asserted by Jeremiah here.

Isaiah 47:10
in your hope of wickedness. For you said, ‘I am, and there is no other.’ Know that understanding these things and your prostitution will become your shame. You said in your heart, ‘I am, and there is no other.’
  Zephaniah 2:15 — city pride expressed in the repeated I-am claim: Isaiah repeats Babylon's inward claim that she is and there is no other amid wicked confidence. Zephaniah places the same kind of inward exclusive claim in the mouth of the confident city before its fall.

Isaiah 47:11
Destruction will come upon you, and you will certainly not know it—a pit, and you will fall into it. Misery will come upon you, and you will certainly not be able to become clean. Destruction will come upon you suddenly, and you will certainly not know it.
  1 Thessalonians 5:2-3 — unexpected and inescapable destruction: Isaiah repeatedly warns that destruction will arrive suddenly without Babylon knowing it. Thessalonians likewise places sudden destruction after a declaration of security and says the speakers will not escape. The latter concerns the day of the Lord and does not identify Babylon as its sole subject.

Isaiah 47:12
Stand now amid your enchantments and your abundant sorcery, which you learned from youth, if you can be helped.
  Wisdom of Solomon 17:7-8 — failure of magical claims to provide help: Isaiah challenges the long-practiced enchantments and sorcery to help their practitioners. Wisdom similarly exposes magical art and its boasts as helpless, with those promising relief themselves overcome by fear.

Isaiah 47:13
You have toiled in your counsels. Let heaven’s astrologers stand up and save you; let those watching the stars announce to you what is about to come upon you.
  Daniel 2:27-28 — limits of professional divination: Isaiah challenges heavenly observers to announce what is coming and provide rescue. Daniel denies that disclosure of the royal mystery belongs to wise men and sorcerers and attributes revelation to God. The catastrophe and dream are distinct tests of claimed knowledge.

Isaiah 47:14
Behold, all will be burned up like brushwood upon a fire and certainly not rescue their soul from flame. Since you have burning coals, sit upon them!
  Nahum 1:10 — judged opponents compared to combustible brush: Isaiah compares the failed helpers to brushwood burned in fire. Nahum likewise pictures the doomed as a thorn-vine and dry stubble consumed in destruction. Isaiah's taunt about sitting on coals remains its own detail.

Isaiah 48:1
“Hear these things, house of Jacob, you called by Israel’s name, who came forth from Judah, who swear by the name of the Lord God of Israel, remembering without truth or righteousness,
  Jeremiah 4:2 — contrast in truthful oath-taking: Isaiah rebukes those invoking Israel's God without truth or righteousness. Jeremiah gives the positive counterpart: swearing that the Lord lives with truth, judgment and righteousness.

Isaiah 48:2
and cling to the holy city’s name and lean upon Israel’s God; His name is the Lord Sabaoth.
  Jeremiah 7:4-10 — confidence in a holy place without corresponding conduct: Isaiah describes people leaning on Israel's God and the holy city's name after exposing false invocation. Jeremiah likewise rebukes confidence in the temple's name while injustice and false oaths continue, requiring changed conduct.

Isaiah 48:3
I announced the former things beforehand; they went out from My mouth and were heard. Suddenly I acted, and they arrived.
  Isaiah 46:10 — internal announcement followed by accomplishment: Both passages place God's prior announcement alongside the arrival or fulfillment of what was announced. Isaiah 48 emphasizes the sudden action; Isaiah 46 relates fulfillment to His enduring counsel.

Isaiah 48:4
I know that you are stubborn, your neck an iron sinew and your forehead bronze.
  Deuteronomy 9:6-7 — stiff-necked resistance: Isaiah makes the neck an iron sinew as an image of stubbornness. Deuteronomy explicitly calls Israel stiff-necked and recalls continuing wilderness disobedience.

Isaiah 48:5
I announced it to you long ago; before it came upon you I made you hear it. Do not say, ‘My idols did it,’ or say, ‘My carved and cast images commanded me.’
  Isaiah 44:7-8 — internal foretelling against rival divine claims: Isaiah 48 says the prior announcement prevents credit being given to carved or cast images. Isaiah 44 similarly challenges rivals to announce coming things and makes the prior disclosure evidence concerning whether any other god exists.

Isaiah 48:6
You heard everything, but you did not understand. Yet I also made you hear the new things from now on, what is about to happen, and you did not speak.
  Isaiah 42:9 — internal announcement of new things: Both announce new things after speaking of earlier things already heard or arrived. Isaiah 48 emphasizes the hearers' failure to understand; Isaiah 42 emphasizes the Lord's advance disclosure.

Isaiah 48:7
They happen now, not long ago; you did not hear them in earlier days. Do not say, ‘Yes, I know them.’
  Isaiah 48:3-5 — same-chapter contrast in claimed prior knowledge: The earlier verses stress former things announced beforehand, while verse 7 says these things occur now and cannot be claimed as old knowledge. The chapter distinguishes the grounds for knowing God's action and excludes competing claims by idols or hearers.

Isaiah 48:8
You neither knew nor understood; from the beginning I did not open your ears. For I knew you would utterly reject faithfulness, and you would be called lawless even from the belly.
  Psalms 57:4 — faithlessness described from the womb: Isaiah calls the addressed people lawless from the belly and foretells their rejection of faithfulness. The psalm likewise describes sinners as estranged from the womb and speaking lies from the belly.

Isaiah 48:9
For My name’s sake I will show you My anger and bring My glorious deeds upon you, so that I do not destroy you.
  Ezekiel 36:21-23 — preservation for the divine name's sake: Isaiah says God will show anger and bring glorious deeds for His name's sake so as not to destroy the people. Ezekiel likewise bases sparing Israel on His profaned name. The comparison does not replace Isaiah's showing anger with delaying anger.

Isaiah 48:10
Behold, I sold you, but not for silver; I rescued you from a furnace of poverty.
  Deuteronomy 4:20 — rescue from a furnace as an image of affliction: Isaiah names a furnace of poverty from which God rescued the people. Deuteronomy identifies Egypt as the iron furnace from which God brought Israel out. The furnace descriptions differ but both portray rescue from severe subjection.

Isaiah 48:11
For My sake I will act for you, because My name is being profaned. I will not give My glory to another.
  Isaiah 42:8 — internal refusal to give divine glory to another: Both passages explicitly state that God will not give His glory to another. Isaiah 48 places that refusal beside action for His profaned name; Isaiah 42 additionally names carved images in refusing to give His praiseworthy deeds to them.
  Ezekiel 36:22-23 — action for the sanctification of a profaned name: Both explain God's action as undertaken for His own name because His people have profaned it. Ezekiel develops the outcome as the nations knowing the Lord through His sanctification among them.

Isaiah 48:12
Listen to Me, Jacob and Israel whom I call. I am first, and I am for ever,
  Isaiah 44:6 — internal firstness and enduring divine identity: Isaiah 48 identifies the caller of Jacob and Israel as first and for ever. Isaiah 44 likewise names Israel's rescuer as first and after these things, with no other god beside Him.

Isaiah 48:13
and My hand founded the earth, and My right hand established heaven. I will call them, and they will stand together
  Psalms 101:26 — founding earth and making heaven by God's hands: Both describe earth as founded by God and heaven as the work of His hands. Isaiah additionally pictures the created things standing together when He calls.

Isaiah 48:14
and all will gather and hear. Who announced these things to them? Loving you, I carried out your will against Babylon, to remove the Chaldeans’ offspring.
  Isaiah 43:14 — internal action against Babylon for Israel: Isaiah 48 connects love for the addressed people with action against Babylon and the Chaldeans. Isaiah 43 likewise promises action in Babylon for Israel's sake and describes the Chaldeans' confinement. Isaiah 48's statement about carrying out your will is preserved.

Isaiah 48:15
I spoke, I called; I brought him and made his journey prosper.
  Isaiah 46:11 — internal repeated bringing and prospering: Both say God brought the commissioned person and made his journey prosper, joining that outcome to His speech. The repeated formulation connects the commissions without importing an additional name into either verse.

Isaiah 48:16
Draw near to Me and hear these things. From the beginning I did not speak in secret or in a dark place of the earth. When it came to be, I was there; and now the Lord has sent me, and His spirit.”
  Isaiah 61:1 — divine sending with the Spirit mentioned: Isaiah 48 ends with the speaker's sending by the Lord and mention of His Spirit. Isaiah 61 likewise joins the Lord's Spirit upon the speaker with His sending to proclaim good news. The comparison does not decide the grammatical role of the Spirit in Isaiah 48.

Isaiah 48:17
Thus says the Lord who rescued you, Israel’s Holy One: “I am your God. I have shown you how to find the road on which you will walk.
  Psalms 24:4-5 — God the Savior teaching the way: Isaiah identifies the rescuing God as the One showing the road on which His people should walk. The psalm asks God the Savior to make His ways known, teach His paths and guide the petitioner.

Isaiah 48:18
If you had listened to My commands, your peace would have become like a river and your righteousness like a sea wave.
  Psalms 118:165 — obedience to divine instruction and peace: Isaiah's conditional lament says listening to His commands would have brought river-like peace. The psalm positively associates loving God's law with great peace; it does not supply Isaiah's river and sea imagery.

Isaiah 48:19
Your offspring would have become like sand, those born from your belly like the earth’s dust. Even now you will certainly not be destroyed, nor will your name perish before Me.”
  Genesis 22:17 — promised descendants compared with sand: Isaiah describes the offspring that would have multiplied like sand. God's promise to Abraham likewise compares multiplied seed with the sand beside the sea. Isaiah sets the image in an address concerning neglected commands and continuing preservation.

Isaiah 48:20
Leave Babylon, fleeing from the Chaldeans. Announce a voice of rejoicing, and let this be heard; proclaim it to the earth’s extremity. Say, “The Lord rescued His slave Jacob.”
  Zechariah 2:10-11 — command to leave Babylon for Zion: Both summon the people to escape from Babylon. Isaiah explicitly names flight from the Chaldeans and proclamation of Jacob's rescue; Zechariah directs those dwelling in daughter Babylon to escape safely to Zion.

Isaiah 48:21
Even if they thirst, He will lead them through a wilderness and bring water out of a rock for them. A rock will split and water will flow, and My people will drink.
  Exodus 17:5-6 — wilderness water from the rock: Isaiah promises water brought from a split rock for the people to drink in the wilderness. Exodus narrates the commanded striking of Horeb's rock and the promise that water will come out for the people to drink.

Isaiah 48:22
“There is no rejoicing for the ungodly,” says the Lord.
  Isaiah 57:20-21 — internal repeated denial of rejoicing to the ungodly: The declaration that there is no rejoicing for the ungodly recurs at the end of Isaiah 57, following the picture of the unjust tossed by waves and unable to rest. Both supplied texts say rejoicing.

Isaiah 49:1
Listen to me, islands, and attend, nations. “After a long time it will stand,” says the Lord. From my mother’s belly He called my name,
  Jeremiah 1:4-5 — prophetic calling from the womb: Isaiah's speaker says the Lord called his name from his mother's belly while addressing nations. Jeremiah likewise receives a calling described as preceding emergence from the womb and appointment as a prophet to nations.

Isaiah 49:2
and made my mouth like a sharp sword. In the shelter of His hand He hid me; He made me a chosen arrow and sheltered me in His quiver.
  Hebrews 4:12 — sharp sword as an image of effective speech: Isaiah compares the servant's mouth to a sharp sword. Hebrews likewise describes God's active word as sharper than a two-edged sword, developing its penetrating and discerning effect. The comparison concerns speech pictured as a weapon, without equating the speakers.

Isaiah 49:3
He said to me, “You are My slave, Israel, and in you I will be glorified.”
  Isaiah 41:8-9 — internal naming of Israel as God's servant: Isaiah 49 explicitly names the addressed slave Israel. Isaiah 41 likewise names Israel and Jacob as the servant God chose and called. The cross-reference preserves that named identity rather than silently replacing it.

Isaiah 49:4
I said, “I toiled in vain and gave my strength to futility and nothing. Therefore my judgment is with the Lord, and my toil before my God.”
  1 Corinthians 15:58 — labor entrusted to God despite apparent futility: Isaiah's speaker calls his toil vain but locates his judgment and labor before God. Paul urges steadfast work because labor is not empty in the Lord. The latter supplies an assurance corresponding to the former's appeal, without erasing its lament.

Isaiah 49:5
And now thus says the Lord, who formed me from the belly as His slave to gather Jacob and Israel to Him—I will be gathered and glorified before the Lord, and my God will be my strength—
  Jeremiah 1:4-5 — formation from the womb for divine service: Isaiah connects formation from the belly with service gathering Jacob and Israel. Jeremiah similarly connects formation and consecration before birth with a prophetic commission, though his stated audience is the nations.

Isaiah 49:6
and He said to me, “It is a great thing for you to be called My servant, to establish Jacob’s tribes and bring back Israel’s dispersion. Behold, I have appointed you as a covenant for a race, a light for nations, so that you may bring salvation to the earth’s extremity.”
  Acts 13:46-48 — explicit scriptural reuse in the Gentile mission: Paul and Barnabas introduce the appointment as a light for Gentiles and salvation to earth's end as the Lord's command for their mission. This repeats Isaiah's light-and-worldwide-salvation formulation; Isaiah additionally names the restoration of Jacob and Israel.
  Luke 2:29-32 — light and salvation for nations with Israel's glory: Simeon's declaration joins salvation before all peoples, light for nations and glory for Israel. Isaiah similarly combines the servant's work for Israel with appointment as a light bringing salvation to the earth's extremity.

Isaiah 49:7
Thus says the Lord who rescued you, Israel’s God: “Sanctify the one who despises his own life, the one abhorred by nations—the slaves of rulers. Kings will see him and rise; rulers will bow to him because of the Lord, for Israel’s Holy One is faithful, and I chose you.”
  Psalms 71:10-11 — homage from kings and rulers: Isaiah says kings will rise and rulers bow to the chosen figure. The psalm likewise pictures kings presenting gifts and all kings bowing before its royal subject. The connection is the promised homage; the psalm does not supply Isaiah's wording about despising one's own life.

Isaiah 49:8
Thus says the Lord: “At an acceptable time I heard you, and on a day of salvation I helped you. I gave you as a covenant for nations, to establish the earth and inherit a desolate inheritance,
  2 Corinthians 6:1-2 — explicit quotation of the acceptable time: Paul quotes the acceptable time when God heard and the day of salvation when He helped, matching Isaiah's opening promise. Paul applies it to receiving grace now, while Isaiah continues with covenant and inheritance language.

Isaiah 49:9
saying to those in bonds, ‘Come out,’ and telling those in darkness to be revealed. They will be fed along all their roads, with pasture along every path.
  Isaiah 42:6-7 — internal liberation of prisoners in darkness: Both describe the commissioned work as releasing those bound and bringing people out of darkness. Isaiah 49 adds their feeding and pasture; Isaiah 42 places release alongside opening blind eyes.

Isaiah 49:10
They will neither hunger nor thirst, nor will scorching heat or the sun strike them. Rather, the One showing them mercy will comfort them and lead them through springs of water.
  Psalms 22:1-3 — divine shepherding beside sustaining water: Isaiah promises that the merciful guide will lead the people through springs without hunger or thirst. The psalm likewise portrays the Lord shepherding, nourishing beside restful water and guiding along paths. It does not repeat the protection from scorching heat.

Isaiah 49:11
I will make every mountain into a road and every path into pasture for them.
  Isaiah 40:3-4 — internal transformation of difficult terrain into a way: Isaiah 49 makes mountains roads and paths pasture for the returning people. Isaiah 40 likewise prepares God's way by lowering mountains and making rough country level. Each changes the landscape to permit the promised journey.

Isaiah 49:12
Behold, these come from afar, these from the north and these from the sea, and others from the Persians’ land.”
  Jeremiah 38:8-10 — gathering from distant lands and the north: Isaiah lists arrivals from afar, the north, sea and Persians' land. Jeremiah likewise announces gathering from the north and earth's farthest end. The target supports the distant northern gathering, without replacing Persia with another region.

Isaiah 49:13
Rejoice, heavens, and let earth exult! Let mountains burst out in joy and hills in righteousness, for God had mercy on His people and comforted His people’s lowly ones.
  Isaiah 44:23 — internal cosmic rejoicing over Israel's redemption: Both summon heaven, earth and mountains to rejoice because God has shown mercy to His people. Isaiah 44 explicitly names Jacob's redemption; Isaiah 49 adds comfort for the people's lowly ones.

Isaiah 49:14
But Zion said, “The Lord has abandoned me, and the Lord has forgotten me.”
  Psalms 12:2 — lament of being forgotten by God: Zion says the Lord has forgotten and abandoned her. The psalmist likewise asks how long the Lord will forget him and turn His face away. Both preserve the complaint as the sufferer's speech.

Isaiah 49:15
“Will a woman forget her child, failing to have mercy on the offspring of her belly? Even if a woman forgets these, I will not forget you,” said the Lord.
  Psalms 26:10 — divine care exceeding parental care: Isaiah says that even maternal forgetting would not make God forget Zion. The psalm likewise contrasts parental abandonment with the Lord's taking the speaker to Himself. Isaiah poses a maternal possibility; the psalm describes abandonment by both parents.

Isaiah 49:16
“Behold, on My hands I painted your walls, and you are before Me continually.
  Exodus 28:9-12 — Memorial imagery: represented Israel kept before the Lord: To answer Zion's fear of being forgotten, God says He painted her walls on His hands and keeps her continually before Him. Exodus orders Israel's sons' names engraved on stones carried on Aaron's shoulders before the Lord as a memorial. Both use a bodily borne representation to express continuing remembrance before God. The painted walls on God's hands remain distinct from engraved names on priestly shoulder stones.

Isaiah 49:17
You will swiftly be rebuilt by those who pulled you down, and those who laid you waste will depart from you.
  Isaiah 60:10 — internal rebuilding of Zion by unexpected builders: Isaiah 49 promises rebuilding by those who pulled the city down and departure of its destroyers. Isaiah 60 likewise promises that foreigners will build Zion's walls. Both associate restoration with unexpected human builders, but the passages do not establish that those groups are identical.

Isaiah 49:18
Lift your eyes around and see them all. Behold, they gathered and came to you. As I live, says the Lord, you will put them all on and wrap them around you like a bride’s adornment.
  Isaiah 60:4 — internal summons to see gathered children: Both command Zion to lift her eyes around and see those gathered to her. Isaiah 60 explicitly names sons and daughters; Isaiah 49 pictures the gathered people as her bridal adornment.
  Isaiah 61:10 — bridal adornment as an image of restored joy: Isaiah 49 compares the gathered people worn by Zion to a bride's adornment. Isaiah 61 likewise compares God-given clothing of salvation and joy to bridal ornaments. The adornment's immediate referent differs.

Isaiah 49:19
For your deserted, destroyed, and fallen places will now be cramped by inhabitants, and those swallowing you will be removed far from you.
  Zechariah 2:8-9 — Jerusalem filled again with inhabitants: Isaiah says formerly ruined places will be cramped with inhabitants. Zechariah likewise promises fruitful habitation of Jerusalem because of the multitude of people and animals, with God protecting the city.

Isaiah 49:20
For your sons whom you lost will say within your hearing, ‘This place is too cramped for me. Make room for me, so I can settle.’
  Isaiah 54:1-3 — internal enlargement for unexpectedly numerous children: The recovered children in Isaiah 49 complain that their dwelling is too cramped. Isaiah 54 answers the corresponding image of the formerly barren woman's numerous children with commands to widen her tent and spread out.

Isaiah 49:21
You will say in your heart, ‘Who bore these for me? I was childless and widowed. Who raised these for me? I was left alone; where were these of mine?’”
  Isaiah 54:1 — internal astonishment at the deserted mother's children: Isaiah 49 depicts the childless widow wondering who bore and raised the returning children. Isaiah 54 likewise summons the deserted, non-childbearing woman to rejoice because her children are unexpectedly numerous.

Isaiah 49:22
Thus says the Lord: “Behold, I lift My hand toward nations and will raise My signal toward islands. They will bring your sons in their bosom and carry your daughters on shoulders,
  Isaiah 60:4 — internal return of children carried by others: Isaiah 49 promises sons brought in bosoms and daughters carried on shoulders. Isaiah 60 repeats the return of sons from afar and daughters lifted upon shoulders, within Zion's gathering.

Isaiah 49:23
and kings will be your foster fathers and ruling women your nurses. They will bow to you upon the earth’s surface and lick the dust of your feet. You will know that I am the Lord, and you will not be ashamed.
  Psalms 71:9-11 — royal bowing and dust imagery: Isaiah pictures rulers bowing to Zion and licking the dust of her feet. The psalm likewise joins kings bowing with enemies licking dust. The groups assigned the dust-licking action differ, while the homage imagery is shared.

Isaiah 49:24
Will anyone take spoils from a giant? If someone takes captives unjustly, will he be saved?”
  Matthew 12:28-29 — strong man's possessions and the question of plunder: Isaiah asks whether spoils can be taken from a giant. Jesus similarly asks how someone can seize the strong man's possessions without first binding him. The comparison concerns the obstacle to rescue or plunder; Matthew applies it to His work against demons.

Isaiah 49:25
Thus says the Lord: “If someone captures a giant, he will take spoils; one taking from a strong man will be saved. I will judge your case, and I will rescue your sons.
  Luke 11:21-22 — victory over the strong and taking spoils: Isaiah connects taking from a strong man with rescue and God's judgment for His people's sons. Luke likewise describes a stronger attacker defeating an armed strong man and distributing his spoils. Luke's setting does not identify the captives named by Isaiah.

Isaiah 49:26
Those who afflicted you will eat their own flesh, drink their own blood like new wine, and become drunk. All flesh will perceive that I am the Lord who rescued you and upholds Jacob’s strength.”
  Isaiah 60:16 — internal recognition of the saving God after reversal: Both culminate in recognition that the Lord saves or rescues His people. Isaiah 49 reaches that confession through the afflicters' destruction; Isaiah 60 reaches it through the nations' provision and royal wealth. The connection does not transfer self-consuming violence to Isaiah 60.

Isaiah 50:1
Thus says the Lord: “What is your mother’s divorce document with which I sent her away? Or to which debtor have I sold you? Behold, you were sold because of your sins, and I sent your mother away because of your lawless acts.
  Jeremiah 3:6-8 — divorce imagery for a people's unfaithfulness: Isaiah asks for the mother's divorce document and explains the sending away through lawless acts. Jeremiah expressly describes Israel receiving a divorce certificate after unfaithfulness. Isaiah's question and its term debtor remain distinct from Jeremiah's narrative.

Isaiah 50:2
Why did I come when no person was there, call when no one obeyed? Is My hand not strong enough to rescue? Am I not able to deliver? Behold, by My threat I will make the sea desolate and turn rivers to deserts; their fish will dry up for lack of water and die in thirst.
  Isaiah 59:1-2 — internal question about the Lord's saving hand: Both ask whether God's hand lacks power to save. Isaiah 59 answers by locating separation in the people's offenses; Isaiah 50 likewise surrounds the question with sin, failed response and demonstrations of God's power.
  Exodus 7:20-21 — divine judgment leaving a river's fish dead: Isaiah describes waters made dry and fish dying for lack of water. Exodus narrates fish dying when the river becomes blood. The common feature is God's judgment on water with dead fish; the mechanism differs and is not harmonized.

Isaiah 50:3
I will clothe heaven in darkness and make its covering like sackcloth.”
  Exodus 10:21-23 — divinely imposed darkness: Isaiah says God clothes heaven in darkness like sackcloth. Exodus narrates His command for darkness over Egypt. The narrative supplies a concrete counterpart to imposed darkness, without supplying Isaiah's clothing metaphor.

Isaiah 50:4
The Lord gives me a tongue of instruction, to know the right time when a word must be spoken. He appointed morning for me; He added an ear for me to hear,
  John 7:15-16 — instruction attributed to the One who sends: Isaiah's speaker attributes the instructed tongue and listening ear to the Lord. Jesus likewise attributes His teaching to the One who sent Him when hearers question its source. Neither comparison adds a reference to the weary that is absent from Isaiah's supplied wording.

Isaiah 50:5
and the Lord’s instruction opens my ears. I do not disobey or contradict.
  John 8:29 — obedient response to divine instruction: Isaiah's speaker says the Lord's instruction opens his ears and he does not disobey. Jesus similarly says He always does what pleases the One who sent Him. Both state the speaker's obedience, in different settings.

Isaiah 50:6
I have given my back to scourges and my cheeks to slaps. I did not turn my face away from the shame of spitting.
  Matthew 26:67 — spitting and blows upon the face: Isaiah describes offering cheeks to slaps and not turning the face from spitting. Matthew narrates people spitting into Jesus's face, striking and slapping Him. These particular acts supply the narrative parallel.
  Matthew 27:26-30 — flogging and humiliating treatment of Jesus: Isaiah joins a back given to scourges with facial humiliation. Matthew narrates Jesus's flogging, mockery, spitting and blows. The correspondence concerns those named acts without claiming Matthew quotes the verse here.

Isaiah 50:7
The Lord became my helper; therefore I was not abashed. Instead, I set my face like solid rock and knew that I would certainly not be ashamed.
  Luke 9:51 — firmly setting the face toward an ordeal: Isaiah's speaker sets his face like solid rock in confidence in God's help. Luke describes Jesus firmly setting His face toward Jerusalem as His being taken up approaches. The shared expression depicts determination; Luke does not repeat the rock comparison.

Isaiah 50:8
For the One who justified me draws near. Who is bringing a case against me? Let him stand against me together. Who is bringing a case against me? Let him approach me.
  Romans 8:33-34 — God's justification against an accuser's charge: Isaiah pairs the nearness of the One justifying with repeated challenges to anyone bringing a case. Romans likewise asks who can bring a charge or condemn when God justifies, then names Christ's death, resurrection and intercession.

Isaiah 50:9
Behold, the Lord helps me. Who will harm me? Behold, all of you will grow old like a garment, and it will devour you like a moth.
  Job 13:28 — frailty pictured by a moth-eaten garment: Isaiah says the opponents will age like a garment and be consumed as by a moth. Job likewise uses the aging or moth-eaten garment image. Isaiah places it within assurance of God's help against harm.

Isaiah 50:10
Who among you fears the Lord? Let him listen to His servant’s voice. Those walking in darkness have no light; trust in the Lord’s name and lean upon God.
  Psalms 22:4 — trust in God while passing through darkness: Isaiah calls those without light to trust the Lord's name and lean on God. The psalm similarly expresses confidence in God's presence while walking in death's shadow.

Isaiah 50:11
Behold, all of you burn fire and strengthen a flame. Walk by your fire’s light and the flame you kindled. These things came to you because of Me; you will fall asleep in sorrow.
  Isaiah 50:10 — same-chapter contrast between trust and self-kindled light: Verse 10 calls the person in darkness to trust God; verse 11 addresses people walking by the fire they kindled and ends in sorrow. The neighboring commands contrast the source of reliance and the outcome, without adding an account of a literal fire-setting event.

Isaiah 51:1
“Listen to Me, you who pursue what is right and seek the Lord. Look at the solid rock you quarried and the hollow of the pit you dug.
  Isaiah 51:2 — same-chapter repeated summons to look at origins: The command to look at quarried rock and the dug pit is immediately followed by the command to look at Abraham and Sarah, whose one beginning God multiplied. The repeated look frames the ancestral comparison while preserving the active verbs you quarried and you dug.

Isaiah 51:2
Look at Abraham your father and Sarah who labored to bear you. He was one, and I called him, blessed him, loved him, and multiplied him.
  Genesis 12:1-3 — Abraham's call and promised multiplication: Isaiah recalls God calling Abraham when he was one, blessing and multiplying him. Genesis narrates that call with the promise to make Abram a great nation and bless him.
  Hebrews 11:11-12 — Sarah's childbearing and descendants from one man: Isaiah explicitly recalls Sarah's labor and the multiplication of Abraham from one. Hebrews likewise joins Sarah receiving power to conceive with innumerable descendants coming from one aged man.

Isaiah 51:3
I will now comfort you, Zion; I have comforted all her deserted places and will make her deserted places like the Lord’s paradise. They will find joy and exultation in her, thanksgiving and a voice of praise.
  Ezekiel 36:35 — restored wasteland compared to a garden: Isaiah promises to make Zion's deserted places like the Lord's paradise. Ezekiel similarly describes devastated land becoming like a garden of delight and ruined cities standing restored.

Isaiah 51:4
Listen to Me, listen, My people; and kings, give ear to Me. For law will go out from Me, and My judgment as a light for nations.
  Isaiah 2:3-4 — internal outgoing law and judgment for nations: Isaiah 51 announces law going out from God and judgment as a light for nations. Isaiah 2 likewise describes law going out from Zion and the Lord judging between nations.

Isaiah 51:5
My righteousness approaches swiftly, and My salvation will go out like light. Nations will hope in My arm; islands will wait for Me and hope in My arm.
  Isaiah 42:4 — internal hope of nations in divine saving rule: Isaiah 51 says nations and islands will hope in God's arm as salvation approaches. Isaiah 42 likewise joins judgment established on earth with nations hoping in the servant's name. The objects of the hope are expressed differently.

Isaiah 51:6
Lift your eyes to heaven and look at earth below. Heaven was made firm like smoke, but earth will grow old like a garment, and earth’s inhabitants will die like these. My salvation will last for ever, and My righteousness will never fail.
  Psalms 101:26-28 — aging creation contrasted with divine permanence: Isaiah compares earth's aging to a garment while declaring everlasting salvation. The psalm likewise says the created heavens and earth grow old like a garment while God remains unchanged. Isaiah's unusual statement about heaven being made firm like smoke is left intact.

Isaiah 51:7
Listen to Me, you who know judgment, My people with My law in your heart. Do not fear people’s reproach or be overcome by their contempt.
  Matthew 5:11-12 — encouragement under human reproach: Isaiah tells the people with God's law in their heart not to fear human reproach and contempt. Jesus likewise calls His reviled and persecuted followers to rejoice because of their heavenly reward and the prophets' precedent.
  Psalms 118:11 — divine instruction held within the heart: Isaiah identifies the addressed people as having God's law in their heart. The psalmist likewise places God's sayings in his heart so that he may avoid sinning. This connects the inward instruction clause rather than the distinct reproach clause.

Isaiah 51:8
For a garment will be consumed by time, and wool eaten by a moth. But My righteousness will last for ever, and My salvation through generations of generations.”
  Isaiah 50:9 — internal recurrence of the moth-eaten garment: Both use the aging garment and consuming moth for what cannot endure. Isaiah 51 expressly contrasts such decay with God's everlasting righteousness and salvation; Isaiah 50 applies the decay to opponents.

Isaiah 51:9
Awake, awake, Jerusalem, and clothe yourself with the strength of your arm! Awake as at day’s beginning, as an age-old generation. Are you not the one
  Isaiah 52:1 — internal repeated awakening of Jerusalem and Zion: Both summon Jerusalem or Zion to awake and clothe herself with strength. The addressee in Isaiah 51 is Jerusalem, and the comparison does not introduce a dragon or Rahab absent from its actual wording.

Isaiah 51:10
who makes the sea desolate, the abundant water of the abyss, who made the sea depths a crossing road for those rescued
  Exodus 14:21-22 — sea made into a crossing route: Isaiah recalls the sea depths becoming a road for those rescued. Exodus narrates the sea being dried and split so Israel could enter on dry ground between the waters.

Isaiah 51:11
and ransomed? For by the Lord they will be brought back and come to Zion with joy and everlasting exultation. Exultation and praise are upon their heads; joy will overtake them. Pain, sorrow, and sighing fled.
  Isaiah 35:10 — internal repeated return to Zion with enduring joy: Both describe those gathered or brought back through the Lord arriving at Zion with everlasting gladness above their heads, while pain and grief depart. The close cluster of wording supports more than a general association with happiness.

Isaiah 51:12
“I am, I am the One comforting you. Recognize whom you dreaded and feared: a mortal person, a human being who withered like grass.
  Matthew 10:28-31 — divine reassurance against fear of mortal people: Isaiah contrasts God's comfort with fear of a mortal human who withers like grass. Jesus likewise discourages fear of those whose power reaches only the body and assures His followers of the Father's care.

Isaiah 51:13
You forgot God who made you, who made heaven and founded earth, and feared continually, every day, the face of your oppressor’s anger. For as he planned to remove you—where now is your oppressor’s anger?
  Deuteronomy 32:18 — forgetting the God responsible for one's existence: Isaiah rebukes forgetting the God who made the people and founded creation. Deuteronomy likewise rebukes forsaking the God who begot and nourishes Israel. The creation and parental descriptions differ while the charge of forgetfulness is explicit.

Isaiah 51:15
For I am your God, who stirs the sea and makes its waves resound; the Lord Sabaoth is My name.
  Jeremiah 38:36 — sea's resounding waves and the Lord's name: Both identify the Lord by His action causing the sea's waves to resound and give His name as the Lord Sabaoth or Almighty. Jeremiah adds the ordered lights of day and night.

Isaiah 51:16
I will put My words in your mouth and shelter you under My hand’s shadow, by which I established heaven and founded earth; and He will say to Zion, ‘You are My people.’
  Jeremiah 1:9 — placing divine words in the commissioned mouth: Isaiah promises to put God's words in the addressed person's mouth. Jeremiah narrates the Lord touching the prophet's mouth and declaring that He has put His words there.

Isaiah 51:17
Awake, awake, rise, Jerusalem, you who drank the cup of anger from the Lord’s hand. You drank out and emptied the cup of falling, the bowl of anger.
  Jeremiah 32:15-17 — judgment pictured as a cup received from God's hand: Jerusalem has drunk the cup and bowl of anger in Isaiah. Jeremiah receives a cup from the Lord's hand for the nations, whose drinking brings madness before the sword. The shared cup is an image of imposed judgment.

Isaiah 51:18
There was no one to comfort you among all your children you bore, and no one to take your hand among all your sons you raised.
  Lamentations 1:2 — Jerusalem lacking anyone to comfort her: Isaiah says none of Jerusalem's children comfort her or take her hand. Lamentations similarly pictures her weeping with no comforter among those who loved her; the identified circle of failed helpers differs.

Isaiah 51:19
These two confront you—who will grieve with you?—fall and crushing, famine and sword. Who will comfort you?
  Lamentations 2:11-13 — crushed Jerusalem asking who can comfort her: Both describe the daughter's crushing and ask who will comfort her. Lamentations also depicts children failing for want of food, corresponding to Isaiah's named famine and destruction.

Isaiah 51:20
Your sons are helpless, sleeping at every way out like a half-boiled beet, full of the Lord’s anger, exhausted because of the Lord God.
  Lamentations 2:11-12 — helpless children in the city's public places: Isaiah depicts exhausted sons lying at the ways out under the Lord's anger. Lamentations likewise depicts children failing in the city squares and pouring out their lives to their mothers. Isaiah's half-boiled-beet simile is not replaced by another animal or image.

Isaiah 51:21
Therefore listen, humbled one, drunk but not from wine.
  Isaiah 29:9 — internal drunkenness without wine: Both explicitly describe a disordered, weakened condition as drunkenness or hangover without wine. Isaiah 51 places that description in the context of the cup of divine anger.

Isaiah 51:22
Thus says the Lord God who judges His people: ‘Behold, I have taken from your hand the cup of falling, the bowl of anger. You will not drink it again.
  Lamentations 4:21-22 — end of Zion's punishment and transfer of the cup: Isaiah removes the cup of anger from Jerusalem so she will not drink again. Lamentations similarly ends Zion's lawlessness and deportation while announcing that the Lord's cup will pass to Edom.

Isaiah 51:23
I will throw it into the hands of those who wronged and humbled you, who told your soul, “Bend down, so we may pass.” You laid your back level with the earth, outside for passersby.’”
  Lamentations 4:21-22 — oppressors receiving the cup after Zion: Isaiah transfers the cup to those who humiliated Jerusalem. Lamentations likewise announces the cup's arrival at Edom while Zion's deportation ends. Isaiah's account of the bowed back as a walkway remains its own description of abuse.

Isaiah 52:1
Awake, awake, Zion! Clothe yourself with your strength, Zion, and put on your glory, Jerusalem, holy city. No uncircumcised and unclean person will pass through you again.
  Joel 4:17 — holiness of Jerusalem and restricted entry: Isaiah calls Jerusalem the holy city and promises that uncircumcised and unclean people will no longer pass through her. Joel likewise calls Jerusalem holy and says foreigners will no longer pass through; the excluded groups are named differently.

Isaiah 52:2
Shake off the dust and rise; sit, Jerusalem. Strip off the bond around your neck, captive daughter Zion.
  Isaiah 61:1-3 — internal release and reversal of Zion's humiliation: Isaiah commands captive daughter Zion to rise from dust and remove her neck bond. Isaiah 61 announces release for captives and glory and joy in place of mourning for Zion. The target does not specifically repeat the neck bond.

Isaiah 52:3
For thus says the Lord: “You were sold for nothing, and without silver you will be ransomed.”
  1 Peter 1:18-19 — ransom distinguished from payment in silver: Isaiah promises ransom without silver after being sold for nothing. Peter likewise says redemption was not with perishable silver or gold, then names Christ's precious blood. The target supplies its own positive account of redemption's means.

Isaiah 52:4
Thus says the Lord: “My people formerly went down to Egypt to live there as strangers, and they were forcibly led to the Assyrians.
  Genesis 46:5-7 — narrative counterpart to Israel's descent into Egypt: Isaiah recalls the people's earlier migration to Egypt. Genesis narrates Jacob's household and possessions entering Egypt, naming the children and descendants who went with him.
  2 Kings 17:6 — narrative counterpart to deportation to Assyria: Isaiah pairs the earlier stay in Egypt with forcible removal to the Assyrians. Kings records the Assyrian king's capture of Samaria and deportation of Israel, with the places of resettlement.

Isaiah 52:5
And now, why are you here?” Thus says the Lord. “My people were taken for nothing. Be amazed and wail!” Thus says the Lord. “Because of you, My name is continually blasphemed among the nations.
  Romans 2:24 — explicit citation of God's name blasphemed: Romans introduces as written the saying that God's name is blasphemed among the nations because of the addressed people. Isaiah contains that same accusation within its lament over the people's captivity.

Isaiah 52:6
Therefore My people will know My name on that day, because I am the One speaking. I am present
  Exodus 6:6-7 — recognition of the Lord through His promised rescue: Isaiah promises that His people will know His name as the One who speaks and is present. Exodus likewise joins the declaration I am the Lord with rescue and the promise that Israel will know Him as their delivering God.

Isaiah 52:7
like beauty on the mountains, like the feet of one proclaiming a report of peace, like one bringing good news. For I will make your salvation heard, saying, ‘Zion, your God will reign.’”
  Romans 10:14-15 — explicit reuse of the good-news messenger's feet: Romans introduces a scriptural saying about the beautiful feet of those announcing good things within its argument for sent proclaimers. Isaiah joins the feet of a messenger with peace, good news, salvation and God's reign; the supplied wording is closely related rather than identical throughout.
  Nahum 2:1 — close prophetic messenger parallel: Both bring together mountains, the feet of a good-news bearer and the announcement of peace. Nahum directs Judah to celebrate after the hostile passage has ended; Isaiah announces salvation and God's reign to Zion.

Isaiah 52:8
For the voice of those guarding you was raised, and together they will rejoice with their voice. For eyes will see eye to eye when the Lord has mercy on Zion.
  Isaiah 62:6-7 — internal voices of Jerusalem's appointed guards: Isaiah 52 describes the city's guards raising their voices together at the Lord's mercy. Isaiah 62 likewise appoints guards upon Jerusalem's walls who will not be silent as they remember the Lord, in the context of restoration.

Isaiah 52:9
Let Jerusalem’s deserted places break out in joy together, for the Lord had mercy on her and rescued Jerusalem.
  Isaiah 51:3 — internal joy of restored deserted places: Both call attention to Zion or Jerusalem's deserted places receiving divine mercy and renewed joy. Isaiah 51 adds the paradise comparison and thanksgiving; Isaiah 52 specifically names Jerusalem's rescue.

Isaiah 52:10
The Lord will uncover His holy arm before all nations, and all the ends of earth will see the salvation that comes from God.
  Psalms 97:2-3 — God's salvation seen at the ends of earth: Both announce salvation made visible before nations and seen by all earth's ends. The psalm joins that worldwide recognition with remembered mercy to Jacob and Israel.

Isaiah 52:11
Depart, depart; go out from there, and do not touch anything unclean. Go out from her midst; separate yourselves, you who carry the Lord’s vessels.
  2 Corinthians 6:16-18 — scriptural reuse of departure and separation from uncleanness: Paul repeats the command to come out, be separated and touch nothing unclean in his address to believers as God's temple. Isaiah addresses those carrying the Lord's vessels; that immediate temple-service setting is not erased.

Isaiah 52:12
For you will not go out in turmoil or travel in flight, because the Lord will go ahead of you, and the One gathering you is the Lord, Israel’s God.
  Exodus 13:21-22 — God leading the departing people: Isaiah promises that the Lord will go ahead of His people as they depart without turmoil or flight. Exodus narrates God leading the people with the cloud and fire before them. Isaiah's supplied final description names the One gathering them, not a rearguard.

Isaiah 52:13
Behold, My servant will understand, and will be exalted and greatly glorified.
  Philippians 2:7-11 — servant status followed by divine exaltation: Isaiah announces the servant's exaltation and great glory. Philippians likewise joins Christ's taking a slave's form and humbling Himself with God's highly exalting Him. The comparison does not claim Isaiah states every stage of Paul's account.

Isaiah 52:14
As many will be astonished at you— so your form will be without honour among men, and your glory among men—
  Isaiah 53:2-3 — internal repeated description of dishonored appearance: The servant's form and glory without honor in Isaiah 52 recur in Isaiah 53's absence of form, beauty and honor and the sufferer's being held of no account. The continuation develops the same portrait.

Isaiah 52:15
so many nations will marvel at him, and kings will close their mouths. For those to whom he was not proclaimed will see, and those who have not heard will understand.
  Romans 15:20-21 — explicit citation about previously unreached hearers: Paul quotes the seeing of those not previously told and the understanding of those who have not heard, corresponding to Isaiah's final clause. He applies it to proclaiming Christ where He has not been named; Isaiah also describes the nations' wonder and kings' silence.

Isaiah 53:1
O Lord, who has believed our report? And to whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed?
  John 12:37-41 — explicit quotation attributed to Isaiah: John explicitly names Isaiah and quotes both the question about believing the report and the question about the revealed arm of the Lord. He applies the saying to unbelief despite Jesus's signs.
  Romans 10:16-17 — explicit quotation concerning the received report: Paul names Isaiah and repeats the question about who believed the report in his discussion of hearing and obedience to the gospel. This corresponds to the first question of the source verse.

Isaiah 53:2
We proclaimed before him as a child, as a root in thirsty ground. He has no form nor glory; and we saw him, and he had no form nor beauty.
  Isaiah 52:14 — internal repetition of the servant's unhonored appearance: Isaiah 53 says the figure has no form, glory or beauty; Isaiah 52 already describes the form and glory as without honor among people. The connection concerns that repeated description and leaves the unusual opening We proclaimed before him as a child intact.

Isaiah 53:3
But his form was without honour, fallen below all men: a man under affliction, and knowing how to bear sickness. For his face has been turned away; he was dishonoured and held of no account.
