  Psalms 21:7-9 — contempt directed at a suffering figure: Isaiah describes an afflicted figure dishonored and held of no account. The psalm likewise presents a sufferer as an object of human reproach and contempt, mocked by onlookers. Its mockery is not converted into Isaiah's explicit sickness language.

Isaiah 53:4
He bears our sins, and suffers pain for us; yet we reckoned him to be in toil and affliction and distress.
  1 Peter 2:21-24 — bearing others' sins and suffering for them: Isaiah expressly says the figure bears our sins and suffers pain for us. Peter likewise says Christ suffered for the addressed people and bore their sins in His body. The connection follows the actual sin-bearing wording.

Isaiah 53:5
But he was wounded for our iniquities, and has been weakened for our sins. The chastisement of our peace was upon him; by his bruise we were healed.
  1 Peter 2:24 — wounding for sins and healing through the wound: Isaiah links the sufferer's wounds and weakness to the people's sins and says they were healed by his bruise. Peter likewise connects Christ's bearing sins with the declaration that His wound healed the readers.

Isaiah 53:6
We all, like sheep, have gone astray; each person has strayed in his own way. And the Lord delivered him over to our sins.
  1 Peter 2:25 — reuse of the wandering-sheep comparison: Isaiah compares everyone straying in their own way to sheep. Peter repeats the comparison for the readers' former wandering and adds their return to the Shepherd and Overseer of their souls.

Isaiah 53:7
And because he has been afflicted, he does not open his mouth. Like a sheep he was led to slaughter, and as a lamb is silent before its shearer, so he does not open his mouth.
  Acts 8:30-35 — explicit reading and interpretation of Isaiah: Acts identifies the eunuch's reading as Isaiah, quotes the sheep led to slaughter and lamb silent before the shearer, and records Philip beginning with this Scripture to proclaim Jesus. These images match the source's repeated silence.
  Matthew 27:12-14 — narrative counterpart to silence under accusation: Isaiah depicts the afflicted figure not opening his mouth. Matthew narrates Jesus giving no answer to accusations despite Pilate's questioning, supplying a specific instance of silence under hostile testimony.

Isaiah 53:8
In his humiliation his judgment was taken away. Who will declare his generation? For his life is taken from the earth; because of the iniquities of My people he was led to death.
  Acts 8:30-35 — explicit quotation of humiliation and removed judgment: Acts quotes the removal of judgment in humiliation, the question concerning generation, and life taken from the earth, then records the passage being explained in relation to Jesus. Isaiah additionally explains death through the people's iniquities.

Isaiah 53:9
And I will give the wicked in exchange for his burial, and the rich in exchange for his death; for he committed no iniquity, nor was deceit found in his mouth.
  1 Peter 2:22 — reuse of the innocent sufferer's lack of deceit: Peter describes Christ as committing no sin and having no deceit in His mouth, closely matching Isaiah's final clauses. This connection does not turn the source's wicked and rich in exchange wording into an assigned grave with a rich man.

Isaiah 53:10
And the Lord desires to cleanse him of his affliction. If you give an offering for sin, your soul will see seed that lives long. And the Lord desires to take him away
  Isaiah 53:3-6 — same figure: Both passages in the same song speak of the same afflicted figure and of the Lord acting toward him. Earlier he is 'a man under affliction' and 'the Lord delivered him over to our sins'; here 'the Lord desires to cleanse him of his affliction'. The two describe different divine actions toward his affliction.

Isaiah 53:11
from the toil of his soul, to show him light and fashion him with understanding, to justify the righteous one who serves many well; and he will bear their sins.
  Hebrews 9:26-28 — bearing the sins of many: Isaiah's verse ends by saying he will bear their sins. Hebrews likewise describes Christ offered once to bear the sins of many. The target supports the sin-bearing clause without replacing Isaiah's statement about justifying the righteous one who serves many well.

Isaiah 53:12
Therefore he will inherit many, and will divide the spoils of the mighty; for his soul was delivered to death, and he was numbered among the lawless. And he bore the sins of many, and for their sins he was delivered up.
  Luke 22:37 — explicit quotation of being counted with the lawless: Jesus quotes the written saying that He was counted with the lawless and says it must be accomplished in Him. Isaiah explicitly places being numbered among the lawless beside the figure's death and sin-bearing.
  Hebrews 9:28 — bearing many people's sins in an offering involving death: Isaiah says the figure's soul was delivered to death and that he bore the sins of many. Hebrews likewise presents Christ as offered once to bear the sins of many, adding His future appearance for salvation.

Isaiah 54:1
“Rejoice, barren woman who does not give birth! Break out and cry aloud, you who do not labor in childbirth, because the deserted woman’s children are more numerous than those of the woman who has a husband,” for the Lord has spoken.
  Galatians 4:26-28 — explicit citation of the barren woman's many children: Paul quotes the command to the barren woman to rejoice because the deserted woman's children exceed those of the married woman. He places it in his discussion of the Jerusalem above and children of promise.

Isaiah 54:2
“Widen the place of your tent and your curtains. Pitch them; do not spare! Lengthen your ropes and strengthen your pegs.
  Isaiah 49:19-20 — internal enlargement for a growing restored population: Isaiah 54 commands widening the tent, extending ropes and strengthening pegs after promising numerous children. Isaiah 49 pictures the restored children finding the place too cramped and asking for room to settle.

Isaiah 54:3
Spread out still farther to right and left. Your offspring will inherit nations, and you will settle deserted cities.
  Genesis 22:17 — multiplied offspring inheriting others' territory: Isaiah promises offspring inheriting nations and settling deserted cities. Genesis likewise promises Abraham's multiplied seed inheritance of adversaries' cities. The nations and the deserted condition are Isaiah's particular formulations.

Isaiah 54:4
Do not fear because you were put to shame, or be abashed because you were reproached. For you will forget lasting shame and never remember the reproach of your widowhood.
  Isaiah 49:21-23 — internal reversal of Zion's widowhood and shame: Isaiah 54 promises that widowhood's reproach will be forgotten and shame need not cause fear. Isaiah 49 likewise moves from Zion's memory of being childless and widowed to returned children, royal service and the assurance that she will not be ashamed.

Isaiah 54:5
For the Lord is the One making you; the Lord Sabaoth is His name. The One who rescued you is Himself Israel’s God; He will be called upon throughout all earth.
  Zechariah 14:9 — worldwide recognition of the Lord's name: Isaiah identifies Zion's maker and rescuer as the Lord and says He will be called upon throughout the earth. Zechariah similarly places the Lord's kingship and one name over all the earth. Isaiah's source does not call Him husband in this verse.

Isaiah 54:6
“The Lord has not called you like an abandoned, fainthearted wife, nor like a wife hated since youth,” says your God.
  Isaiah 62:4-5 — internal rejection of abandoned status within marriage imagery: Isaiah 54 denies that the Lord calls Zion like an abandoned or hated wife. Isaiah 62 likewise says she will no longer be called Abandoned and compares the Lord's joy over her to a bridegroom's joy. Both reject abandonment in this imagery; the source's negative wording is preserved.

Isaiah 54:7
“For a short time I abandoned you, and with great mercy I will have mercy on you.
  Isaiah 60:10 — internal movement from judgment to mercy toward Zion: Isaiah 54 contrasts a short abandonment with great mercy. Isaiah 60 likewise explains the city's restoration by contrasting God's anger in striking with His mercy in loving.

Isaiah 54:8
In a little anger I turned My face from you, and with everlasting mercy I will have mercy on you,” says the Lord who rescued you.
  Micah 7:18-20 — anger giving way to enduring covenant mercy: Isaiah contrasts little anger and a turned face with everlasting mercy. Micah likewise says God does not retain anger because He desires mercy, and recalls His sworn mercy to the ancestors.

Isaiah 54:9
“This dates for Me from the water in Noah’s time, when I swore to him then concerning the earth not to be angry with you again, nor at a threat against you
  Genesis 9:8-17 — explicit appeal to the promise associated with Noah's flood: Isaiah expressly refers to the water in Noah's time and God's oath as a basis for reassurance. Genesis records the covenant with Noah, his descendants and living creatures that a flood will not again destroy all flesh.

Isaiah 54:10
for mountains to move away; nor will your hills be shifted. So neither will My mercy toward you fail, nor will your covenant of peace ever be removed.” For the Lord said, “I am gracious to you.”
  Ezekiel 37:26 — unremoved and everlasting covenant of peace: Isaiah says the covenant of peace will never be removed and mercy will not fail. Ezekiel likewise promises a covenant of peace explicitly called everlasting. Isaiah's assurance about mountains and hills is preserved without converting it into their removal.

Isaiah 54:11
Humbled and unsettled woman, you were not comforted! Behold, I am preparing carbuncle as your stone and sapphire as your foundations,
  Revelation 21:19 — shared image: Both texts describe a city's foundations made with precious stones including sapphire: Isaiah promises 'sapphire as your foundations', and Revelation lists 'the second sapphire' among the foundations of the city's wall. Isaiah also names carbuncle, which Revelation's list does not include.

Isaiah 54:12
and I will make your battlements jasper, your gates crystal stones, and your enclosing wall choice stones,
  Revelation 21:11 — shared image: Both texts describe a restored city adorned with jasper and crystal: Isaiah promises battlements of jasper and gates of crystal stones, and Revelation describes the city's radiance as 'a jasper stone shining as crystal'.

Isaiah 54:13
and all your sons taught by God, and your children in great peace.
  John 6:44-45 — explicit prophetic citation of divine teaching: Jesus introduces as written in the Prophets the saying that all will be taught by God, corresponding to Isaiah's description of the sons. He applies hearing and learning from the Father to coming to Him.

Isaiah 54:14
You will be built in righteousness. Keep away from injustice, and you will not fear; terror will not approach you.
  Isaiah 1:26-27 — internal restoration of Zion in righteousness: Isaiah 54 promises a city built in righteousness and calls for separation from injustice. Isaiah 1 likewise promises restored judges and the name City of Righteousness, joining Zion's salvation with judgment and mercy.

Isaiah 54:15
Behold, newcomers will approach you through Me and take refuge with you.
  Zechariah 2:14-15 — nations taking refuge with the restored people: Isaiah promises newcomers approaching through God and taking refuge with the addressed city. Zechariah likewise says many nations will take refuge with the Lord, become His people and dwell within Zion. The native Isaiah wording is positive arrival, not hostile gathering.

Isaiah 54:16
Behold, I am creating you, not like a smith blowing coals and bringing out an implement for work. I did not create you for ruin, to destroy
  Wisdom of Solomon 1:13-14 — creation's purpose distinguished from destruction: Isaiah says God is creating the addressed people and did not create them for ruin. Wisdom likewise denies that God made death or delights in destruction and says He created things to exist. Isaiah's contrast with a smith remains distinct from this broader account of creation.

Isaiah 54:17
every perishable implement. I will not grant success against you. Every voice will rise against you in judgment; you will defeat them all, and those accountable to you will be held liable in it. There is an inheritance for those serving the Lord, and you will be righteous before Me,” says the Lord.
  Romans 8:31-34 — divine vindication against hostile legal claims: Isaiah promises defeat of voices raised against the addressed people in judgment and righteousness before God for His servants. Romans similarly asks who can bring a charge or condemn when God justifies. The legal vindication is shared without importing the source's implements into Romans.

Isaiah 55:1
You who thirst, go to the water; and you who have no silver, go and buy wine and rich food, and drink without silver or price.
  John 7:37-39 — invitation to the thirsty to come and drink: Isaiah calls the thirsty to water and offers drink without price. Jesus likewise calls anyone thirsty to come to Him and drink; John explains the saying with reference to the Spirit. The source's wine and rich food remain its own features.
  John 4:10-14 — life-giving water offered as a gift: Isaiah invites those without money to receive water and drink without price. Jesus describes God's gift of living water which He gives and which becomes a spring into eternal life. The connection is the offered sustaining gift, not an identification of ordinary purchased water.

Isaiah 55:2
Why do you pay a price of silver, and give your toil for what does not satisfy? Listen to Me, and you will eat good things, and your soul will delight in good things.
  John 6:27 — labor redirected from inadequate food to lasting provision: Isaiah questions spending money and toil on what fails to satisfy and calls the people to listen and eat good things. Jesus likewise redirects labor from perishing food to the food enduring to eternal life which He gives.

Isaiah 55:3
Listen with your ears, and follow My ways; listen to Me, and your soul will live amid good things. And I will make with you an everlasting covenant: the holy and sure blessings of David.
  Acts 13:32-37 — explicit citation of David's holy and sure blessings: Acts quotes the holy and sure blessings of David within the proclamation of Jesus's resurrection and the fulfilled ancestral promise. Isaiah names those blessings within God's offer of an everlasting covenant.
  Psalms 88:4-5 — covenant sworn to David with enduring offspring: Isaiah associates the everlasting covenant with David's sure blessings. The psalm explicitly describes God's covenant and oath to David, promising his offspring and throne through generations.

Isaiah 55:4
Behold, I have given him as a testimony among the nations, a ruler and commander of nations.
  2 Samuel 22:44 — royal appointment over nations: Isaiah speaks of a figure given as ruler and commander of nations following the Davidic blessings. David's song likewise credits God with preserving him as head of nations. The comparison concerns the royal commission without resolving every referent of Isaiah's him.

Isaiah 55:5
Nations that did not know you will call upon you, and peoples that do not know you will flee to you for refuge, because of your God, the Holy One of Israel, for He has glorified you.
  2 Samuel 22:44-45 — response of previously unknown peoples: Isaiah promises that nations formerly unacquainted with the addressed figure will call and seek refuge. David's song likewise speaks of a people he did not know serving him and foreigners obeying as soon as they hear. The responses are related but not identical.

Isaiah 55:6
Seek God, and when you find Him, call upon Him; and when He draws near to you,
  Deuteronomy 4:29-31 — seeking, finding and returning to God: Isaiah commands seeking God and calling when He is found; the following clause joins His drawing near with the call to return. Deuteronomy likewise promises that the people will seek and find the Lord with their whole heart and return to His voice. Isaiah's wording is not changed to while He may be found.

Isaiah 55:7
let the ungodly forsake his ways, and the lawless man his counsels; and let him return to the Lord, and he will receive mercy; for He will abundantly forgive your sins.
  Ezekiel 18:21-23 — turning from lawlessness and receiving mercy: Isaiah calls the lawless to abandon their ways and counsels, return and receive abundant forgiveness. Ezekiel similarly promises life and non-remembrance of transgressions when the lawless turn from their deeds and practice righteousness and mercy.

Isaiah 55:8
For My counsels are not as your counsels, nor are My ways like your ways, says the Lord.
  Romans 11:33-36 — divine ways and counsel beyond human understanding: Isaiah distinguishes God's counsels and ways from those of the hearers. Romans likewise calls His ways untraceable and asks who has known His mind or served as His counselor. It is a conceptual parallel, not an assertion that Romans cites this verse verbatim.

Isaiah 55:9
But as the heaven is distant from the earth, so is My way distant from your ways, and your thoughts from My mind.
  Psalms 102:11 — heaven's height above earth as a measure of divine greatness: Isaiah uses the distance of heaven from earth to compare God's ways and thoughts with human ones. The psalm uses the same vertical comparison for God's mercy toward those fearing Him. The measured divine qualities differ.

Isaiah 55:10
For as rain or snow comes down from the heaven, and does not return until it has drenched the earth, and made it bring forth and sprout, and given seed to the sower and bread for food,
  2 Corinthians 9:10 — reuse of seed for the sower and bread for food: Isaiah describes rain's fruitful result as seed for the sower and bread for food. Paul repeats that paired provision in naming God as supplier and adds multiplication of the readers' seed and fruits of righteousness.

Isaiah 55:11
so it will be with My word, whatever goes out from My mouth: it will certainly not return until all that I have willed is accomplished; and I will prosper your ways and My commandments.
  Numbers 23:19 — divine speech accomplished in action: Isaiah says the word from God's mouth will accomplish His will before returning. Numbers likewise asks whether God would speak and not act or abide by His speech, distinguishing His reliability from human weakness.

Isaiah 55:12
For you will go out in gladness, and in joy you will be taught; for the mountains and the hills will leap, welcoming you with joy, and all the trees of the field will clap their branches.
  Psalms 97:7-9 — nature personified in joyful response: Isaiah pictures mountains and hills welcoming with joy and trees clapping branches. The psalm likewise makes rivers clap and mountains exult at God's coming. The images support the connection without changing Isaiah's being taught in joy into being led.

Isaiah 55:13
And the cypress will rise in place of the thorny shrub, and the myrtle will rise in place of the fleabane; and the Lord will be for a name and for an everlasting sign, and will not fail.
  Isaiah 41:18-20 — internal planting of named trees displaying God's work: Isaiah 55 replaces thorny growth with cypress and myrtle and says the Lord will be for a name and an everlasting sign. Isaiah 41 likewise places cypress and myrtle among trees in formerly waterless land so that people recognize the Lord's action.

Isaiah 56:1
Thus says the Lord: “Keep justice; do righteousness. For the coming of My salvation has drawn near, and the revealing of My mercy.
  Psalms 97:2-3 — parallel manifestation of God's salvation and mercy: Isaiah announces the nearness of God's salvation and the revealing of His mercy; the psalm declares His salvation made known and His mercy remembered toward Jacob. The announcement and the psalm's accomplished acts have different time frames.

Isaiah 56:2
Happy is the man who does these things, the person holding fast to them, keeping the Sabbaths from profanation and guarding his hands from wrongdoing.”
  Exodus 20:8-11 — legal counterpart to keeping the Sabbath from profanation: Isaiah blesses the person who keeps the Sabbaths from profanation; Exodus commands remembering the Sabbath and keeping it holy through cessation of work. Isaiah also requires guarding the hands from wrongdoing.

Isaiah 56:3
Let no foreigner attached to the Lord say, “Will the Lord then separate me from His people?” And let no eunuch say, “I am a dry tree.”
  Deuteronomy 23:2 — contrast between exclusion from the assembly and the eunuch's promised belonging: Deuteronomy excludes a man with crushed testicles or a cut-off organ from the Lord's assembly; Isaiah tells the eunuch not to call himself a dry tree, before promising faithful eunuchs a place in God's house. The physical descriptions and covenantal settings are preserved rather than treated as identical wording.

Isaiah 56:4
Thus says the Lord: “To the eunuchs who keep My Sabbaths, choose what I wish, and hold fast to My covenant,
  Wisdom of Solomon 3:14 — parallel faithfulness of eunuchs before the Lord: Isaiah names eunuchs who keep the Sabbaths, choose God's wishes and hold His covenant; Wisdom describes a eunuch whose hand does no lawless deed and who conceives no evil against the Lord. Their particular duties differ, while faithfulness defines the favorable description in both.

Isaiah 56:5
I will give them in My house and wall a renowned place better than sons and daughters; I will give them an everlasting name, and it will not fail.
  Wisdom of Solomon 3:14 — close honored portion in God's house for a faithful eunuch: Isaiah promises a renowned place in God's house and wall, better than sons and daughters; Wisdom promises the faithful eunuch a choice favor and delightful portion in the Lord's temple. Isaiah expressly adds an everlasting name, which Wisdom's individual verse does not state.

Isaiah 56:6
And the foreigners attached to the Lord, to serve Him and love the Lord’s name, to become His male and female slaves, and all those keeping My Sabbaths from profanation and holding fast to My covenant—
  1 Kings 8:41-43 — parallel foreigners serving the God whose name is over the temple: Isaiah welcomes foreigners attached to the Lord and loving His name; Solomon's prayer asks God to hear foreigners praying toward the house so all peoples may know and fear His name. Isaiah specifically requires Sabbath observance and covenant fidelity.

Isaiah 56:7
I will bring them into My holy mountain and make them rejoice in My house of prayer. Their whole burnt offerings and sacrifices will be acceptable on My altar, for My house will be called a house of prayer for all nations,”
  Mark 11:17 — explicit citation of the house of prayer for all nations: Mark introduces Jesus' temple saying as written Scripture and repeats the house of prayer for all nations. The subsequent accusation of a robbers' den is additional to this Isaiah unit.
  Matthew 21:13 — explicit house-of-prayer citation in the temple: Matthew records Jesus saying that it is written that His house will be called a house of prayer. The citation preserves that clause while not repeating Isaiah's express phrase for all nations.
  Luke 19:46 — explicit house-of-prayer citation during the temple action: Luke records the scriptural saying that God's house shall be a house of prayer as Jesus drives sellers out. Its cited wording does not repeat Isaiah's sacrifices, holy mountain or all-nations clause.

Isaiah 56:8
said the Lord who gathers Israel’s scattered people, “for I will gather a gathering to him.”
  Psalms 146:2 — close gathering of Israel's dispersed people: Isaiah identifies the Lord as the One gathering Israel's scattered people and promises a gathering; the psalm likewise says the Lord will gather Israel's dispersed people as He builds Jerusalem.

Isaiah 56:9
All you wild beasts, come and eat, all you beasts of the woodland!
  Jeremiah 12:9 — close summons for wild beasts to gather and eat: Isaiah summons wild beasts and woodland beasts to come and eat; Jeremiah summons the field's wild beasts to gather and eat God's abandoned inheritance. Jeremiah names the object of the eating, which this Isaiah summons leaves unstated.

Isaiah 56:10
See: all have been blinded; they did not know how to think. They are all mute dogs, unable to bark, dreaming of a bed, loving to doze.
  Ezekiel 33:6 — analogy between mute dogs and a watchman's failed warning: Isaiah portrays blind, unthinking figures as dogs unable to bark and loving to doze; Ezekiel describes a watchman who sees an approaching sword but fails to sound the trumpet. The failed audible warning is the comparison, without identifying the two figures as the same office.

Isaiah 56:11
The dogs are shameless in appetite, knowing no satisfaction, and they are evil, without understanding. They all followed their own ways, each according to what was his own.
  Ezekiel 34:2-3 — related rebuke of self-serving appetite: Isaiah condemns insatiable dogs who each follow their own ways; Ezekiel rebukes shepherds consuming milk, clothing themselves with wool and slaughtering fattened animals while not feeding God's sheep. The rebukes use different animal images and roles to expose appetite directed toward oneself.

Isaiah 57:1
See how the righteous man perished, and no one considers it in his heart. Righteous men are removed, and no one understands. For the righteous man was taken from the presence of injustice.
  Wisdom of Solomon 4:10-14 — close removal of a righteous person from wickedness misunderstood by observers: Isaiah says a righteous person was taken from injustice while no one understands or takes it to heart; Wisdom describes the pleasing person removed from wickedness while observers neither understand nor consider it. Wisdom develops the protective purpose of the removal.

Isaiah 57:2
His burial will be in peace; he has been removed from the midst.
  Wisdom of Solomon 3:1-3 — parallel peace of the righteous after apparent loss: Isaiah says the righteous person's burial will be in peace; Wisdom says the righteous seem to have suffered destruction in their departure but are in peace. The target discusses their souls in God's hand rather than the burial itself.

Isaiah 57:3
“But you, come forward here, lawless sons, offspring of adulterers and a prostitute!
  Hosea 2:4-7 — close address to children of prostitution: Isaiah calls the hearers offspring of adulterers and a prostitute; Hosea names children of prostitution and describes their mother's adultery and pursuit of lovers. The familial sexual imagery marks the communities' unfaithfulness in both settings.

Isaiah 57:4
In whom did you delight? Against whom did you open your mouth and let loose your tongue? Are you not children of destruction, lawless offspring?
  Psalms 21:14 — related hostile opening of the mouth: Isaiah challenges the hearers' opened mouths and loosened tongues; the psalm portrays assailants opening their mouths like a roaring, predatory lion. The psalm speaks from the afflicted person's position, while Isaiah addresses the aggressors.

Isaiah 57:5
You who appeal to idols beneath leafy trees, slaughtering their children in ravines among the rocks!
  Jeremiah 7:31 — parallel condemnation of child sacrifice in a ravine: Isaiah condemns slaughtering children in ravines among rocks while appealing to idols; Jeremiah names Topheth in Hinnom's ravine, where children were burned contrary to God's command. Jeremiah specifies the location and method more narrowly than Isaiah's unit does.

Isaiah 57:6
That is your portion; this is your lot. To those you poured libations, and to those you brought sacrifices. Will I not therefore be angry over these things?
  Jeremiah 7:18-19 — close illicit libations provoking divine anger: Isaiah recounts libations and sacrifices to the objects of the preceding accusation and asks whether God will be angry; Jeremiah describes drink offerings to foreign gods to provoke Him to anger. Jeremiah also describes the shame returning upon the worshipers.

Isaiah 57:7
On a high and lofty mountain—there is your bed, and there you brought sacrifices up.
  Hosea 4:13 — parallel sacrifices on elevated places amid sexual unfaithfulness: Isaiah places a bed and sacrifices on a high mountain; Hosea describes sacrifices on mountaintops and hills within its account of prostitution and adultery. Hosea additionally names sheltering trees, while Isaiah's unit emphasizes the elevated bed.

Isaiah 57:8
Behind your doorposts you set your memorials. You thought that if you departed from Me you would have something more. You loved those sleeping with you
  Ezekiel 23:17 — related bed imagery for unfaithfulness with foreign lovers: Isaiah accuses the addressee of departure from God and loving those who sleep with her; Ezekiel describes Babylon's sons coming to Jerusalem's personification and a bed of lodgers in prostitution. The target does not reproduce Isaiah's memorials behind doorposts.

Isaiah 57:9
and multiplied your prostitution with them. You increased the number of those far away from you, sent ambassadors beyond your boundaries, turned away, and were humbled as far as Hades.
  Ezekiel 23:16 — close sending of distant messengers in an account of prostitution: Isaiah joins multiplied prostitution with ambassadors sent beyond the boundaries; Ezekiel says the personified city desired the depicted Chaldeans and sent messengers to their land. Isaiah additionally describes humiliation as far as Hades.

Isaiah 57:10
You wore yourself out with your many journeys and did not say, ‘I will stop.’ You kept strengthening yourself because you did these things; therefore you did not implore Me—
  Jeremiah 2:25 — related persistence in exhausting pursuit of strangers: Isaiah describes weariness from many journeys without saying to stop and continuing to strengthen oneself; Jeremiah tells the woman to turn from a rough road and thirst, but she says she will be strong and follows strangers. The surrounding accounts concern unfaithful pursuit.

Isaiah 57:11
you! Whom did you reverently fear, that you lied to Me, did not remember Me, and took Me neither into thought nor into your heart? I see you and overlook you, yet you did not fear Me.
  Isaiah 51:12-13 — internal connection between misplaced fear and forgetting God: Isaiah 57 asks whom the hearers feared so that they lied and forgot God; Isaiah 51 names fear of a mortal oppressor and forgetting the Creator. The latter identifies the feared person more specifically.

Isaiah 57:12
I will proclaim My righteousness and your evils, which will not benefit you.
  Daniel 9:7-10 — Theological contrast: God's righteousness and human wrongdoing: Isaiah distinguishes God's own righteousness from the people's evils, which cannot benefit them. Daniel's confession similarly assigns righteousness and mercy to the Lord, while assigning shame to the people because they sinned, fell away, and disobeyed His law. God's accusation and Daniel's confession occupy different speaking positions but preserve the same contrast; neither text calls the human evils God's righteousness.

Isaiah 57:13
When you cry aloud, let them rescue you in your distress! Wind will take all these, and a storm carry them away. But those holding fast to Me will acquire land and inherit My holy mountain.
  Judges 10:13-14 — close challenge that chosen gods should rescue their worshipers: Isaiah challenges the objects of reliance to rescue the hearers in distress; Judges tells Israel, which abandoned the Lord for other gods, to cry to those gods for rescue in affliction. Isaiah also predicts their removal by wind and storm.
  Psalms 36:9-11 — parallel land inheritance for those holding to the Lord: Isaiah promises land and God's holy mountain to those holding fast to Him; the psalm promises land to those waiting for the Lord and to the meek. The holy mountain is specified only in Isaiah.

Isaiah 57:14
They will say, ‘Clear roads before him, and remove obstacles from My people’s road.’
  Isaiah 62:10 — internal command to clear a road for God's people: Both command preparation of a road for God's people. Isaiah 57 names removing obstacles; Isaiah 62 names scattering stones from the road and raising a signal for nations.

Isaiah 57:15
Thus says the Lord Most High, who dwells in the heights for ever, whose name is Holy among holy ones, the Lord Most High resting among holy ones and giving patience to the fainthearted and life to the brokenhearted:
  Psalms 33:19 — parallel divine help for the brokenhearted: Isaiah's exalted Lord gives life to the brokenhearted and patience to the fainthearted; the psalm says the Lord is near the brokenhearted and saves the humble in spirit. Isaiah locates His resting among holy ones, not explicitly among the brokenhearted.

Isaiah 57:16
‘I will not take vengeance on you for ever or always be angry with you. For spirit will go out from Me, and I made every breath.
  Psalms 102:9 — close denial that divine anger lasts forever: Isaiah says God will not avenge forever or always be angry; the psalm says He will not remain angry to the end or maintain wrath forever. Isaiah additionally grounds the declaration in His making every breath.

Isaiah 57:17
Because of sin I grieved him a little, struck him, and turned My face away from him. He was grieved and went gloomily along his ways.
  Psalms 29:8 — close distress when God turns His face away: Isaiah describes God striking and turning His face away, followed by the person's grief and gloomy path; the psalmist says God turned His face away and he became troubled. Isaiah expressly names sin as the occasion.

Isaiah 57:18
I have seen his ways, healed him, comforted him, and given him true comfort—
  Psalms 146:3 — parallel God healing those in distress: Isaiah describes God seeing the person's ways, healing and comforting him after his grief; the psalm says He heals the brokenhearted and binds their fractures. The target supplies brokenheartedness and fracture imagery rather than Isaiah's repeated comfort.

Isaiah 57:19
peace upon peace to those far away and those near. And the Lord said, ‘I will heal them.’
  Ephesians 2:17 — close proclamation of peace to those far and near: Isaiah announces peace upon peace to those far away and near, with God's promise to heal; Ephesians says Christ proclaimed peace to those far away and those near. Its context explains their reconciliation and shared access to the Father.

Isaiah 57:20
But the unjust will be tossed by waves in this way and will be unable to rest.
  Jude 1:13 — related wave imagery of the ungodly: Isaiah's unjust are tossed by waves and unable to rest; Jude portrays the people it condemns as wild sea waves foaming up their own shames. Being tossed by waves and being compared to waves remain distinct formulations.

Isaiah 57:21
‘There is no rejoicing for the ungodly,’ said the Lord God.”
  Isaiah 48:22 — internal repeated denial of rejoicing for the ungodly: Both conclude with the Lord's declaration that there is no rejoicing for the ungodly. The native wording is rejoicing in both passages, rather than a substituted peace formula.

Isaiah 58:1
“Shout with strength and do not spare! Raise your voice like a trumpet and announce My people’s sins to them, and Jacob’s house their lawless acts.
  Micah 3:8 — close announcement of Jacob's and Israel's sins: Isaiah commands loud proclamation of the people's sins and Jacob's lawless acts; Micah's passage connects strength in the Lord's Spirit with declaring Jacob's impieties and Israel's sins. Isaiah adds the trumpet comparison for the voice.

Isaiah 58:2
Day after day they seek Me and desire to know My ways. Like a people who practiced righteousness and did not abandon their God’s judgment, they now ask Me righteous judgment and desire to draw near to God,
  Ezekiel 33:30-32 — related eagerness for God's words without corresponding conduct: Isaiah describes daily seeking of God and requests for righteous judgment before exposing oppressive fasting; Ezekiel describes people eagerly gathering to hear God's words but refusing to do them. The forms of apparent devotion differ.

Isaiah 58:3
saying, ‘Why did we fast, and Thou didst not see? We humbled our lives, and Thou didst not notice?’ For on your fasting days you pursue your wishes and prod all those under your control.
  Zechariah 7:5-6 — close questioning of whether fasting is truly for God: Isaiah answers a complaint about unnoticed fasting by exposing pursuit of the fasters' wishes and pressure upon those under them; Zechariah asks whether the people's fast was for God and says they eat and drink for themselves. The shared issue is the practice's self-directed purpose.

Isaiah 58:4
If you fast for lawsuits and fights and strike a humble person with fists, why do you fast for Me as today, to make your voice heard in an outcry?
  James 4:1-3 — related condemnation of fighting joined with corrupted devotion: Isaiah questions fasting used for disputes, fighting and striking a humble person; James connects fighting with pleasures and describes prayers that receive nothing because they serve those pleasures. James discusses asking rather than fasting.

Isaiah 58:5
I did not choose this fast, or a day for a person to humble his life. Even if you bend your neck like a ring and spread sackcloth and ashes beneath you, you will not thereby call it an acceptable fast.
  Joel 2:12-13 — contrast between outward mourning gestures and heartfelt turning: Isaiah denies that a bent neck, sackcloth and ashes by themselves make an acceptable fast; Joel calls for fasting with the whole heart and tearing hearts rather than garments. Both resist reducing devotion to its visible gestures.

Isaiah 58:6
‘It is not such a fast that I chose,’ says the Lord. ‘Instead, loosen every bond of injustice, undo the strangling ties of coercive agreements, release the crushed, and tear up every unjust written contract.
  Luke 4:18 — related release of the oppressed as divinely appointed action: Isaiah's chosen fast releases the crushed and breaks unjust bonds and contracts; Jesus reads a mission including sending the oppressed away in release. Luke's wider Isaiah reading is not presented as a verbatim quotation of this entire fasting instruction.

Isaiah 58:7
Break your bread for the hungry and bring homeless poor people into your house. If you see someone naked, clothe him; do not overlook those belonging to your own family.
  Matthew 25:35-40 — parallel feeding, welcoming and clothing those in need: Isaiah commands bread for the hungry, shelter for homeless poor people and clothing for the naked; Matthew's King commends feeding, welcoming strangers and clothing, identifying care for His least brothers with care for Him. Isaiah also expressly names one's own family.
  James 2:15-17 — practical counterpart to providing food and clothing: Isaiah commands actual food and clothing for those lacking them; James rejects a peaceful wish that supplies neither clothing nor daily food and calls faith without works dead. The target explicitly tests a claim to faith through those concrete provisions.

Isaiah 58:8
Then your light will break out early, and your healings quickly rise. Your righteousness will go ahead of you, and God’s glory will enfold you.
  Isaiah 60:1 — internal restored light accompanied by God's glory: Isaiah 58 promises light breaking out and God's glory enfolding the merciful hearer; Isaiah 60 tells Jerusalem to shine because her light has come and God's glory has risen upon her. The settings share light and divine glory with different descriptions of their arrival.

Isaiah 58:9
Then you will cry, and God will hear you. While you are still speaking He will say, “Behold, I am present.” If you remove from yourself a bond, finger-pointing, and a word of grumbling,
  Isaiah 65:24 — close answer from God while the people are still speaking: Both promise God's response while the people are still speaking. Isaiah 58 gives the answer as His presence; Isaiah 65 says He listens even before their cry and asks what it is.

Isaiah 58:10
and give bread to the hungry from your heart and satisfy a humbled life, then your light will rise in darkness, and your darkness be like noon.
  Psalms 111:4-9 — close joining of mercy to the poor with light in darkness: Isaiah promises light in darkness after heartfelt feeding of hungry and humbled people; the psalm describes light rising in darkness for the upright and a righteous person giving to the poor. The specific act of feeding is Isaiah's formulation.

Isaiah 58:11
Your God will be with you continually, and you will be filled as your life desires. Your bones will grow rich; you will be like a drenched garden and a spring whose water never ran out. Your bones will sprout like grass and grow rich, and they will inherit generations upon generations.
  Jeremiah 17:7-8 — related unfailing growth sustained by water and divine help: Isaiah promises a drenched garden and an unfailing spring under God's continuing care; Jeremiah likens trust in the Lord to a tree beside water that does not fear a rainless year or cease bearing fruit. The garden, spring and tree are distinct images of sustained life.

Isaiah 58:12
Your ancient deserted places will be rebuilt, and your foundations will endure through generations upon generations. You will be called Builder of Fences, and you will put an end to the paths between.
  Isaiah 61:4 — close rebuilding of places desolated through generations: Both promise rebuilding ancient deserted places and ruined settlements associated with generations. Isaiah 58 additionally names enduring foundations and the title Builder of Fences, which Isaiah 61 does not repeat.

Isaiah 58:13
If you turn your foot away from the Sabbaths, from doing your wishes on the holy day, and call the Sabbaths delightful, holy to your God, you will not lift your foot for work or speak a word in anger from your mouth,
  Deuteronomy 5:12-15 — legal counterpart to honoring the Sabbath by refraining from work: Isaiah calls for honoring the holy Sabbath and refraining from one's work; Deuteronomy commands keeping the Sabbath holy and doing no work, extending rest to dependents and the resident foreigner. Isaiah additionally addresses angry speech.

Isaiah 58:14
and you will trust in the Lord. He will bring you up to earth’s good things and feed you your father Jacob’s inheritance, for the Lord’s mouth has spoken these things.’”
  Deuteronomy 32:13-14 — related divine elevation and feeding from the land's abundance: Isaiah promises that God will bring the people up to earth's good things and feed Jacob's inheritance; Moses' song recounts God bringing them up upon the earth's strength and feeding field produce and other rich food. The promise and the remembered provision have different time frames.

Isaiah 59:1
Is the Lord’s hand unable to save? Or has His ear grown heavy so that He cannot hear?
  Numbers 11:23 — parallel denial that the Lord's hand lacks power: Isaiah denies that the Lord's hand cannot save; the Lord asks Moses whether His hand will not suffice to provide meat. Both reject an inference of divine inability, in different circumstances.

Isaiah 59:2
Instead, your offenses stand between you and God, and because of your sins He has turned His face away from you so as not to show mercy.
  Micah 3:4 — close wrongdoing causing God to turn His face away: Isaiah attributes the separation and God's turned face to the people's sins; Micah says He will turn His face away and not listen because the rulers acted wickedly. Micah's preceding accusation specifies their abuse of the people.

Isaiah 59:3
For your hands are stained with blood and your fingers with sins. Your lips spoke lawlessness, and your tongue meditates on injustice.
  Isaiah 1:15 — internal bloody hands accompanying rejected prayer: Isaiah 59 names blood-defiled hands while explaining separation from God; Isaiah 1 names hands full of blood as the reason He will not listen to multiplied supplication. The surrounding settings explicitly connect misconduct and the unanswered appeal.

Isaiah 59:4
No one speaks what is just, nor is there true judgment. They trust vain things and speak emptiness, for they conceive trouble and give birth to lawlessness.
  Job 15:35 — related conception and birth imagery for wrongdoing: Isaiah says people conceive trouble and give birth to lawlessness; Eliphaz says the ungodly person conceives pains and his belly bears deceit. The images describe wrongdoing's production; Job's statement remains a claim within Eliphaz's speech.

Isaiah 59:5
They broke open asps’ eggs and weave a spider’s web. Whoever was about to eat their eggs broke one and found a wind-egg, and in it a basilisk.
  Job 8:13-15 — Image parallel: a spider's web and the futility of ungodliness: Isaiah describes lawless people weaving a spider's web, whose inability to provide clothing is explained in the next unit. Bildad depicts the ungodly person's tent becoming a spider's web and his propped-up house failing to stand. In both arguments the web exposes the uselessness of what the ungodly produce or rely on. Job's statement belongs to Bildad's speech; it does not establish that Job is ungodly or explain Isaiah's separate asp-egg and basilisk imagery.

Isaiah 59:6
Their web will not become clothing, nor will they clothe themselves from their works, for their works are works of lawlessness.
  Job 8:14-15 — related useless shelter depicted through a spider's web: Isaiah says the woven web cannot become clothing or covering; Bildad says the ungodly person's tent becomes a spider's web and his house cannot endure. Clothing and housing are different inadequate protections in the two images.

Isaiah 59:7
Their feet run to wickedness, quick to shed blood. Their thoughts are the thoughts of fools; destruction and misery lie in their ways.
  Romans 3:15-16 — close scriptural citation of swift bloodshed and ruined ways: Romans includes swift feet pouring out blood and ruin and misery in their ways within its explicit collection of scriptural charges. These reproduce the distinctive sequence in Isaiah's indictment.

Isaiah 59:8
They do not know the way of peace, and there is no justice in their ways. For the paths they travel are twisted, and they do not know peace.
  Romans 3:17 — close citation of not knowing the way of peace: Romans' scriptural indictment repeats that they did not know the way of peace. Isaiah additionally describes judgment's absence and twisted paths.

Isaiah 59:9
Therefore judgment withdrew from them, and righteousness will not reach them. While they waited for light, darkness came to them; waiting for dawn, they walked in an untimely darkness.
  Jeremiah 13:16 — close expectation of light overtaken by darkness: Isaiah describes people waiting for light but encountering darkness; Jeremiah warns that people will wait for light but find death's shadow and darkness. The two passages use the same frustrated expectation in different settings.

Isaiah 59:10
They will grope along a wall like the blind, and as though without eyes they will grope. They will fall at noon as at midnight; like people dying, they will groan.
  Deuteronomy 28:29 — close blind groping and failure at midday: Isaiah describes groping like blind people and falling at midday as at midnight; Deuteronomy's curse describes groping at midday like a blind person in darkness. Isaiah's description supplies a concrete counterpart to the curse's imagery.

Isaiah 59:11
Like a bear and like a dove they will travel together. We waited for judgment, but there is none; salvation has withdrawn far from us.
  Habakkuk 1:2 — related lament over absent judgment and rescue: Isaiah describes waiting for judgment and salvation that remain absent; Habakkuk cries to the Lord while wronged and asks why He does not listen or save. Habakkuk explicitly cries, while Isaiah's unit uses bears and traveling doves.

Isaiah 59:12
For our lawlessness is great before Thee, and our sins have opposed us. Our lawless deeds are within us, and we have recognized our wrongs:
  Psalms 50:5-6 — close acknowledgment of one's own sin before God: Isaiah's community acknowledges its lawless acts and sins before God; the psalmist knows his lawlessness and confesses sin against God. The shared acknowledgment is communal in Isaiah and personal in the psalm.

Isaiah 59:13
We acted impiously and lied and withdrew from following our God. We spoke unjust things and disobeyed; we conceived and meditated on unjust words from our heart.
  Psalms 77:36-37 — parallel lying speech joined with unfaithfulness to God: Isaiah confesses lying, withdrawal from God and unrighteous words; the psalm describes lying to Him with the tongue and a heart unfaithful to His covenant. Both join deceptive speech with failed loyalty.

Isaiah 59:14
We drove judgment backward, and righteousness has withdrawn far away, for truth was consumed in their ways, and they could not go straight through.
  Habakkuk 1:4 — parallel judgment prevented and distorted by wrongdoing: Isaiah's speakers say they drove judgment backward, while righteousness withdrew far away; Habakkuk says judgment is not completed and goes out distorted as the impious oppress the righteous. Their descriptions portray justice's failure through different movements.

Isaiah 59:15
Truth has been taken away, and they turned their mind away from understanding. The Lord saw, and it did not please Him that there was no judgment.
  Psalms 11:2-3 — related disappearance of truth amid false speech: Isaiah says truth has been taken away; the psalm laments that truths have become few and describes deceitful speech. Isaiah additionally states God's displeasure at the absence of judgment.

Isaiah 59:16
He saw, and there was no man; He considered, and there was no one to help. He defended them with His arm and steadied Himself by mercy.
  Isaiah 63:5 — close internal divine intervention when no helper is found: Both describe God looking, finding no helper and acting through His own arm. Isaiah 59 speaks of His being steadied by mercy; Isaiah 63 says His wrath stood by and His arm rescued them.

Isaiah 59:17
He put on righteousness like a breastplate, placed a helmet of salvation on His head, and wrapped Himself in a garment of vengeance and the cloak,
  Ephesians 6:14-17 — close adaptation of righteousness breastplate and salvation helmet: Isaiah depicts God wearing righteousness as a breastplate and salvation as a helmet; Ephesians tells believers to receive those two named pieces of armor. Isaiah's vengeance garments are not repeated in that instruction.
  Wisdom of Solomon 5:17-20 — parallel divine armor for judgment: Both portray divine combat using righteousness as a breastplate and other moral realities as armor or weapons. Wisdom's helmet is unfeigned judgment, whereas Isaiah's is salvation; Wisdom also makes creation an ally in battle.

Isaiah 59:18
as one who will repay recompense—reproach to His adversaries.
  Psalms 93:1-2 — parallel divine vengeance expressed as repayment: Isaiah promises recompense and repayment against adversaries; the psalm invokes the God of vengeance as earth's Judge to repay the arrogant. The psalm is a petition, while Isaiah announces the action.

Isaiah 59:19
Those from the west will fear the Lord’s name, and those from sunrise the glorious name. For anger from the Lord will come like a violent river; it will come with wrath.
  Malachi 1:11 — close worldwide recognition of the Lord's name: Isaiah joins the west and sunrise with fear of the Lord's name and glory; Malachi says His name is glorified among nations from sunrise to sunset. Malachi adds incense and pure sacrifice; Isaiah describes anger from the Lord coming like a violent river.

Isaiah 59:20
The deliverer will come for Zion’s sake and turn ungodliness away from Jacob.
  Romans 11:26 — explicit citation of the Zion deliverer turning Jacob from ungodliness: Romans introduces the deliverer's coming and the turning away of Jacob's ungodliness as written Scripture. Its coming from Zion differs from Isaiah's coming for Zion, while the Jacob clause closely corresponds.

Isaiah 59:21
“And this is My covenant with them,” said the Lord: “My spirit that is upon you and the words I put into your mouth will never fail from your mouth or your offspring’s mouth,” for the Lord said, “from now on and for ever.”
  Isaiah 51:16 — internal divine words placed and preserved in the people's mouth: Isaiah 59's covenant promises that God's Spirit and words will not fail the mouth or descendants; Isaiah 51 says God puts His words in the mouth and calls Zion His people. The enduring descendant promise belongs specifically to Isaiah 59.
  Romans 11:26-27 — covenant formula in a continuing scriptural citation: After quoting the Zion deliverer, Romans continues with God's covenant with them. Isaiah 59:21 likewise opens with that covenant formula, but develops the enduring Spirit and words; Romans instead speaks of removing sins.

Isaiah 60:1
Shine, shine, Jerusalem, for your light has come, and the Lord’s glory has risen upon you.
  Ephesians 5:14 — related summons to illumination from the Lord: Isaiah commands Jerusalem to be illumined because her light and the Lord's glory have come; Ephesians summons the sleeper to rise and promises Christ will shine upon him. The comparison is an illumination motif, not a claim of identical wording or addressees.

Isaiah 60:2
Behold, darkness and gloom will cover earth over nations, but the Lord will appear upon you, and His glory will be seen over you.
  Exodus 10:22-23 — contrast between surrounding darkness and light for God's people: Isaiah places the Lord's appearing over Jerusalem against darkness covering nations; Exodus places light among Israel against Egypt's thick darkness. The historical plague and Isaiah's restoration vision have distinct settings.

Isaiah 60:3
Kings will walk by your light, and nations by your radiance.
  Luke 2:30-32 — parallel light reaching nations in connection with Israel: Isaiah says nations will walk by Jerusalem's light; Simeon identifies the Child Jesus as salvation prepared for peoples, a light for the nations and glory for Israel. Luke names the Child where Isaiah addresses the illumined city.

Isaiah 60:4
Lift your eyes around you and see your children gathered. Behold, all your sons have come from far away, and your daughters will be lifted upon shoulders.
  Isaiah 49:22 — close internal gathering of children carried from afar: Both describe sons being brought and daughters carried on shoulders. Isaiah 49 specifies nations and islands as the recipients of God's signal; Isaiah 60 calls the city to look at the gathering.

Isaiah 60:5
Then you will see and fear and be astonished in heart, for the wealth of sea, nations, and peoples will turn toward you. There will come to you
  Haggai 2:7-9 — parallel nations' wealth accompanying restored glory: Isaiah describes the sea's wealth and the resources of nations coming to the city; Haggai describes the nations' choice things coming and God's house being filled with glory, naming silver and gold. The house is Haggai's immediate focus.

Isaiah 60:6
herds of camels, and camels from Madiam and Gaipha will cover you. They will all come from Saba, bringing gold and carrying frankincense, and they will proclaim the Lord’s salvation as good news.
  Matthew 2:11 — related distant visitors bringing gold and frankincense: Isaiah describes visitors from Saba bringing gold and frankincense; Matthew's eastern magi bring those gifts to the Child Jesus, with myrrh as an additional gift. Matthew does not identify them as Saba's visitors or mention Isaiah's camels.

Isaiah 60:7
All Kedar’s sheep will gather to you, and Nabaioth’s rams will come to you. They will be offered acceptably upon My altar, and My house of prayer will be glorified.
  Psalms 50:20-21 — parallel acceptable animal offerings in restored Zion: Isaiah describes gathered flocks accepted upon God's altar and His house glorified; the psalm asks for Jerusalem's rebuilding and anticipates delightful offerings and calves upon the altar. The named animals differ.

Isaiah 60:8
Who are these flying like clouds and like doves with their young?
  Hosea 11:11 — Restoration imagery: people compared with doves: Isaiah asks about arrivals flying like clouds and doves with their young, within the gathering of Zion's children from far away. Hosea compares those from Egypt and Assyria's land with a bird and a dove and promises to restore them to their houses. Both restoration scenes employ bird imagery for returning people. Hosea's verb is astonished, not flying, and Isaiah's young are not replaced with windows or a dovecote.

Isaiah 60:9
Islands waited for Me, and Tharsis ships among the first, to bring your children from far away, with silver and gold, because of the Lord’s holy name and because Israel’s Holy One is glorious.
  Psalms 71:10 — related gifts from Tarshish and the islands: Isaiah describes waiting islands and Tharsis's ships bringing children with silver and gold; the psalm describes kings of Tarshish and islands offering gifts. The psalm's royal donors differ from Isaiah's ships carrying returning children.

Isaiah 60:10
Foreigners will build your walls, and their kings will attend you. For because of My anger I struck you, and because of mercy I loved you.
  Nehemiah 2:7-8 — historical counterpart to foreign royal help for Jerusalem's walls: Isaiah describes foreigners building the walls and kings attending; Nehemiah receives a foreign king's authorization for safe passage and timber for the city wall. This is a specific example of royal assistance, not an identification of every promised builder.

Isaiah 60:11
Your gates will be opened continually; day and night they will not be shut, to bring you the nations’ resources and kings being led.
  Isaiah 26:2 — internal opening of city gates for people to enter: Isaiah 60 promises continually open gates for nations' resources and kings; Isaiah 26 commands opening the fortified city's gates for a people keeping righteousness and truth. Only Isaiah 60 specifies day and night openness and incoming wealth.

Isaiah 60:12
For nations and kings who will not serve you will perish, and the nations will be utterly desolated.
  Zechariah 14:16-19 — parallel consequences for nations refusing the restored city's order: Isaiah threatens destruction for nations and kings refusing service; Zechariah describes nations coming to Jerusalem to worship the King and a downfall upon those refusing the festival. The demanded service is specified differently.

Isaiah 60:13
Lebanon’s glory will come to you in cypress, pine, and cedar together, to glorify My holy place.
  2 Chronicles 2:7-8 — related Lebanon timber for the glory of God's house: Isaiah names Lebanon's cypress, pine and cedar for glorifying the holy place; Solomon requests Lebanon's cedar, juniper and pine for a great and glorious house. The species lists overlap without being identical.

Isaiah 60:14
The sons of those who humbled and provoked you will come to you in fear. You will be called the Lord’s City, Zion of Israel’s Holy One.
  Isaiah 49:23 — internal humbled approach to restored Zion: Isaiah 60 describes oppressors' sons coming in fear; Isaiah 49 describes kings and ruling women bowing and licking dust at the city's feet. The groups and postures differ, while the city's restored standing and recognition of the Lord connect the promises.

Isaiah 60:15
Because you became abandoned and hated, with no one helping, I will make you an everlasting exultation, joy for generations upon generations.
  Isaiah 54:7-8 — parallel abandonment reversed by enduring divine favor: Isaiah 60 reverses abandonment with everlasting joy; Isaiah 54 contrasts brief abandonment and anger with great, everlasting mercy. The promised reversal is shared, while joy and mercy are the particular outcomes named.

Isaiah 60:16
You will suck the nations’ milk and eat kings’ wealth. You will know that I am the Lord who saves and delivers you, Israel’s God.
  Isaiah 49:26 — close recognition of the Lord as Israel's rescuer: Isaiah 60 names Israel's God as the One saving and delivering the city; Isaiah 49 names the Lord as rescuer and upholder of Jacob's strength. The recognition follows nations' milk and kings' wealth in the former and enemies' defeat in the latter.

Isaiah 60:17
Instead of bronze I will bring you gold, instead of iron silver, instead of wood bronze, and instead of stones iron. I will appoint your rulers in peace and your overseers in righteousness.
  Isaiah 1:26 — internal righteous government accompanying Zion's restoration: Isaiah 60 promises rulers in peace and overseers in righteousness; Isaiah 1 restores judges and counselors and names Zion the City of Righteousness. The offices and expressions of their proper order differ.

Isaiah 60:18
Injustice will no longer be heard in your land, nor destruction and misery within your borders. Your walls will be called Salvation, and your gates Engraving.
  Isaiah 26:1 — close salvation figured as the restored city's walls: Isaiah 60 names the walls Salvation; Isaiah 26 says salvation will become a wall and encircling wall. Isaiah 60's gates are named Engraving in this edition, not a substituted Praise.

Isaiah 60:19
The sun will not be your light by day, nor will the rising moon light your night. Instead, the Lord will be your everlasting light and God your glory.
  Micah 7:8 — parallel the Lord Himself providing light: Isaiah promises the Lord as everlasting light beyond sun and moon; Micah says the Lord will shine for the speaker sitting in darkness. Micah does not make Isaiah's claim about the heavenly lights.

Isaiah 60:20
For your sun will not set, and your moon will not fail. The Lord will be your everlasting light, and your days of mourning will be fulfilled.
  Isaiah 35:10 — internal end of mourning in the restored Zion setting: Isaiah 60 says the days of mourning will be fulfilled; Isaiah 35 describes those returning to Zion with everlasting gladness while pain, grief and groaning flee. Isaiah 60 additionally speaks of unsetting light.

Isaiah 60:21
Your people will all be righteous, and they will inherit the land for ever, guarding the planting, the works of His hands, for glory.
  Psalms 36:29 — close righteous people inheriting land enduringly: Isaiah says all the people will be righteous and inherit the land forever; the psalm likewise says the righteous will inherit the land and dwell upon it for age upon age.

Isaiah 60:22
The fewest will become thousands, and the least a great nation. I, the Lord, will gather them at the proper time.
  Isaiah 49:19-21 — related reversal from diminished population to abundant descendants: Isaiah 60 promises that the fewest become thousands and the least a great nation; Isaiah 49 describes formerly deserted places becoming cramped with children the bereaved city did not expect. Their measures of increase differ.

Isaiah 61:1
The Lord’s spirit is upon me, because He anointed me. He sent me to bring good news to the poor, to heal the brokenhearted, to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind,
  Luke 4:18 — explicit Isaiah reading of the Spirit-anointed mission: Luke identifies the scroll as Isaiah and records Jesus reading the Spirit's anointing for good news to the poor, captives' release and the blind's sight. The wording also includes sending the oppressed away in release.
  Matthew 11:4-5 — related good news for the poor and sight for the blind in Jesus' works: Isaiah's anointed mission includes good news for the poor and sight for the blind; Jesus answers John's inquiry by naming those two works among the signs being heard and seen. Matthew's list contains other healings as well.

Isaiah 61:2
to announce the Lord’s acceptable year and a day of recompense, to comfort all who mourn,
  Luke 4:19 — explicit citation of the Lord's acceptable year: Jesus' Isaiah reading includes proclamation of the Lord's acceptable year. Luke's recorded reading stops there and does not repeat this source unit's day of recompense and comfort for mourners.

Isaiah 61:3
so that Zion’s mourners may receive glory instead of ashes, an ointment of joy for those who mourn, a robe of glory instead of a spirit of despondency. They will be called Generations of Righteousness, the Lord’s planting, for glory.
  Psalms 29:12 — parallel mourning garments exchanged for gladness: Isaiah exchanges ashes and a discouraged spirit for glory, gladness and a robe of glory; the psalmist says God changed lament into dancing, tore open sackcloth and girded him with gladness. The garments and other exchanged signs differ.

Isaiah 61:4
They will rebuild age-old deserts and raise places formerly desolated. They will renew deserted cities laid waste for generations.
  Isaiah 58:12 — close internal rebuilding of ancient deserted places: Both describe rebuilding ancient deserted places and raising ruins associated with generations. Isaiah 61 expands the picture to deserted cities, while Isaiah 58 additionally names enduring foundations.

Isaiah 61:5
Foreigners will come shepherding your sheep, and people of other nations will be plowmen and vinedressers.
  Isaiah 60:10 — internal foreign participation in the restored community's work: Isaiah 61 assigns foreigners tending flocks, plowing and vineyard work; Isaiah 60 assigns foreigners building walls and kings attending. These are different concrete forms of foreign contribution in the restoration promises.

Isaiah 61:6
But you will be called priests of the Lord, ministers of God. You will consume the nations’ resources and be admired in their wealth.
  Exodus 19:5-6 — parallel priestly identity for God's covenant people: Isaiah promises that the people will be called priests and ministers of God; Exodus promises covenant-keeping Israel a royal priesthood and holy nation. Isaiah additionally describes receiving nations' resources.

Isaiah 61:7
Thus they will inherit the land a second time, and everlasting joy will be over their head.
  Jeremiah 16:15 — parallel renewed possession of the ancestral land: Isaiah speaks of inheriting land a second time; Jeremiah promises restoration to the land God gave the fathers after gathering the dispersed. Jeremiah does not reproduce Isaiah's numerical expression or everlasting joy.

Isaiah 61:8
“For I am the Lord who loves righteousness and hates plunder obtained through injustice. I will give their labor’s yield to righteous people and make an everlasting covenant with them.
  Malachi 1:13 — related divine rejection of plunder: Isaiah says the Lord hates plunder arising from injustice; Malachi rejects sacrifices containing things seized by force. Malachi's offering setting is more specific than Isaiah's statement and should not be inserted into it.

Isaiah 61:9
Their offspring and descendants will be known among nations. Everyone seeing them will recognize them, because they are offspring blessed by God,
  Genesis 26:28-29 — narrative counterpart to outsiders recognizing the Lord's blessing: Isaiah predicts that observers will recognize the descendants as blessed by God; Abimelech's party tells Isaac they clearly saw the Lord with him and calls him blessed by the Lord. The encounter illustrates recognition, not the whole future promise.

Isaiah 61:10
and they will rejoice greatly in the Lord.” Let my soul exult in the Lord, for He clothed me in a garment of salvation and a tunic of joy. Like a bridegroom He placed a turban on me, and like a bride He adorned me with ornaments.
  Psalms 131:16 — parallel clothing with salvation accompanied by rejoicing: The speaker in Isaiah rejoices in God's clothing him with a garment of salvation and tunic of joy; the psalm promises Zion's priests clothing with salvation and her holy ones exultation. Isaiah further develops bridegroom and bride adornment.

Isaiah 61:11
As earth increases its flower and a garden its seeds, so the Lord will make righteousness and exultation spring up before all nations.
  Isaiah 45:8 — internal earth producing righteousness through God's action: Isaiah 61 compares God's making righteousness and exultation spring up with earth and garden growth; Isaiah 45 calls earth to bring forth mercy and righteousness together. The images place righteous flourishing within God's creative action.

Isaiah 62:1
For Zion I will not be silent, and for Jerusalem I will not let up until My righteousness goes out like light and My salvation burns like a torch.
  Isaiah 42:14 — internal declaration that God will no longer remain silent: Isaiah 62 refuses silence for Zion's sake until her restoration appears; Isaiah 42 asks whether God will always remain silent and hold back before describing His intervention. The specific images and objects of the intervention differ.

Isaiah 62:2
Nations will see your righteousness and kings your glory. He will call you your new name, which the Lord will name.
  Isaiah 65:15 — internal promise of a new name from God: Isaiah 62 promises the city a new name named by the Lord; Isaiah 65 says God's servants will be called by a new name. The city and the servants are distinct immediate recipients.

Isaiah 62:3
You will be a crown of beauty in the Lord’s hand and a royal diadem in your God’s hand.
  Wisdom of Solomon 5:16 — related crown and diadem imagery associated with the Lord's hand: Isaiah calls the city a crown of beauty and royal diadem in God's hand; Wisdom says the righteous receive a splendid crown and beautiful diadem from His hand. Being the crown and receiving a crown remain distinct images.

Isaiah 62:4
You will no longer be called Abandoned, nor will your land be called Desert. You will be called My Desire, and your land Inhabited.
  Hosea 2:25 — parallel changed names expressing renewed divine belonging: Isaiah replaces Abandoned and Desert with My Desire and Inhabited; Hosea reverses Not Shown Mercy and Not My People through mercy and belonging. The names differ while each reversal expresses restored relationship with God.

Isaiah 62:5
As a young man lives with a virgin, so your sons will dwell with you. As a bridegroom rejoices over a bride, so the Lord will rejoice over you.
  Zephaniah 3:17 — close divine rejoicing over restored Zion: Isaiah likens God's rejoicing over the city to a bridegroom's delight in his bride; Zephaniah says the Lord will renew her in love and rejoice over her with delight. Zephaniah's comparison is a feast day rather than marriage.

Isaiah 62:6
Upon your walls, Jerusalem, I appointed guards all day and all night, who will never be silent as they remember the Lord.
  Luke 18:7 — related day-and-night remembrance and appeal to God: Isaiah's appointed guards are never silent day or night as they remember the Lord; Luke describes God's chosen ones crying to Him day and night for justice. Luke's surrounding parable explicitly teaches persistent prayer.

Isaiah 62:7
For there is no one like you, if He restores Jerusalem and makes her a source of pride upon earth.
  Zephaniah 3:19-20 — related restoration making the community renowned throughout earth: Isaiah's conditional clause speaks of God restoring Jerusalem and making her a source of pride on earth; Zephaniah promises restored people renown and a boast among earth's peoples. This does not substitute a command to give God no rest for Isaiah's native wording.

Isaiah 62:8
The Lord swore by His right hand and the strength of His arm: “I will no longer give your grain and food to your enemies, nor will foreigners drink your wine for which you toiled.
  Deuteronomy 28:33 — reversal of the curse of foreigners consuming the land's produce: Isaiah promises that enemies and foreigners will no longer consume the people's grain and wine; Deuteronomy's curse says an unknown nation will consume the land's yield and their labors. The reversal concerns who enjoys the produce.

Isaiah 62:9
Instead, those who gather will eat these things and praise the Lord, and those who gather will drink them in My holy dwellings.”
  Psalms 127:2 — parallel enjoying the produce of one's own labor: Isaiah promises that those gathering will eat and drink their produce; the psalm promises eating the labors of one's own hands. Isaiah additionally names praise and drinking in God's holy dwellings.

Isaiah 62:10
Go through My gates and make a road for My people. Scatter the stones from the road; raise a signal for the nations!
  Isaiah 40:3-4 — internal preparation of a cleared road: Isaiah 62 commands road preparation and scattering stones; Isaiah 40 commands preparing the Lord's way and describes crooked and rough places becoming straight and level. The specified obstacles differ.

Isaiah 62:11
For behold, the Lord has made it heard to earth’s farthest end: “Tell daughter Zion, ‘Behold, the savior is arriving for you, bringing His own reward and His work before His face.’”
  Zechariah 9:9 — close announcement to daughter Zion of a saving arrival: Isaiah tells daughter Zion that her Savior comes; Zechariah tells daughter Zion her king comes, righteous and saving. Zechariah specifies the king's gentleness and mount, which Isaiah does not name here.
  Isaiah 40:10 — close internal coming with reward and work before Him: Both announce an arrival with reward and work before the coming figure. Isaiah 62 tells daughter Zion that the savior is arriving; Isaiah 40 names the Lord and emphasizes might and dominion.

Isaiah 62:12
He will call it a holy people redeemed by the Lord; but you will be called a sought-out city, not abandoned.
  Deuteronomy 7:6-8 — parallel people named holy and redeemed by the Lord: Isaiah names the restored community Holy People and Redeemed by the Lord; Deuteronomy identifies Israel as the Lord's holy people and recalls redemption from Egypt's bondage. Isaiah's new city titles are additional.

Isaiah 63:1
Who is this arriving from Edom, with redness of garments from Bosor, so beautiful in His robe, with force and strength? “I speak righteousness and saving judgment.”
  Isaiah 34:5-6 — close internal judgment associated with Edom and Bosor: Isaiah 63 introduces the red-robed arrival from Edom and Bosor; Isaiah 34 locates the Lord's bloody sacrifice and slaughter in Idumea and Bosor. The named territory and city tie the scenes together without making their images identical.

Isaiah 63:2
Why are Thy garments red and Thy clothes as though from a trodden winepress?
  Isaiah 63:3 — literal same-chapter question and answer about red garments: The question compares the red clothing to a trodden winepress; the following answer describes trampling the nations in wrath and bringing their blood to earth. This immediate literary explanation connects the stained garments with judgment.

Isaiah 63:3
“I am full of what has been trodden. No man from the nations is with Me. I trampled them in wrath, crushed them like earth, and brought their blood down to earth.
  Joel 4:12-13 — parallel treading imagery for judgment upon nations: Isaiah's answer about winepress-like garments describes trampling nations in wrath; Joel's judgment of nations calls for treading a full winepress because their evils multiplied. The actors and wording of the treading differ.

Isaiah 63:4
For a day of recompense came upon them, and a year of redemption is here.
  Isaiah 34:8 — close paired day and year of divine recompense: Isaiah 63 pairs a day of recompense with a year of redemption; Isaiah 34 pairs the Lord's day of judgment with a year of repayment for Zion's judgment. Redemption and repayment are distinct descriptions of the year.

Isaiah 63:5
I looked, but there was no helper; I considered, but no one came to aid. My arm rescued them, and My wrath stood by.
  Isaiah 59:16 — close divine action through His own arm when no helper is found: Both describe the absence of a helper followed by God's own arm acting. Isaiah 63 says the arm rescued them and wrath stood by; Isaiah 59 speaks of His being steadied by mercy.

Isaiah 63:6
I trampled them in My anger and brought their blood down to earth.”
  Isaiah 34:7 — internal judgment leaving blood upon the earth: Isaiah 63 says God trampled in anger and brought the victims' blood to earth; Isaiah 34 says the earth becomes drunk with blood in the Idumean judgment. The descriptions share the bloodied earth while using different images.

Isaiah 63:7
I remembered the Lord’s mercy, the Lord’s excellent deeds in everything He repays us. The Lord is a good judge for Israel’s house; He brings upon us according to His mercy and the abundance of His righteousness.
  Psalms 102:2-4 — close remembrance of God's repayments and mercy: Isaiah remembers the Lord's mercy and excellent deeds in what He repays; the psalm commands remembering His repayments and names redemption, mercy and compassion. The psalm's imperative and Isaiah's recollection serve the same thankful remembrance.

Isaiah 63:8
He said, “Are they not My people, children who will surely not reject Me?” And He became their salvation
  Exodus 4:22-23 — parallel Israel described as God's children in a rescue setting: Isaiah calls the people God's children and describes His becoming their salvation; Exodus calls Israel His firstborn son while demanding release from Pharaoh. Collective sonship accompanies God's action for the people in both.

Isaiah 63:9
from every affliction. Neither an envoy nor an angel, but the Lord Himself saved them because He loved and spared them. He Himself redeemed them, took them up, and exalted them throughout all the days of the age.
  Deuteronomy 4:37 — close God's own rescue motivated by love: Isaiah says the Lord Himself saved and redeemed them because He loved them; Deuteronomy says He loved the fathers, chose their offspring and Himself brought them from Egypt. Isaiah expressly contrasts the Lord Himself with an envoy or angel.

Isaiah 63:10
But they disobeyed and provoked His holy spirit. He turned toward hostility against them and Himself fought them.
  Ephesians 4:30 — related warning against offending God's Holy Spirit: Isaiah recounts disobedience that provoked God's holy spirit and brought hostility; Ephesians commands believers not to grieve the Holy Spirit in whom they were sealed for redemption. The narrated consequence and the exhortation differ.

Isaiah 63:11
The one who brought the sheep’s shepherd up from earth remembered days of old. Where is the one who placed the holy spirit among them,
  Hebrews 13:20 — related bringing up of the sheep's shepherd: Isaiah speaks of bringing the sheep's shepherd up from earth; Hebrews names God bringing the great shepherd of the sheep, Jesus, up from the dead. The shared shepherd and bringing-up language does not turn Isaiah's earth into an explicit statement of resurrection.

Isaiah 63:12
who led Moses by the right hand, the arm of His glory? He overpowered water before Him to make Himself an everlasting name.
  Exodus 14:21-22 — historical counterpart to Moses and divine mastery of water: Isaiah recalls God leading Moses and overpowering water; Exodus narrates Moses extending his hand, the Lord driving back the sea and Israel crossing on dry ground. Isaiah condenses the event into divine guidance and glory.

Isaiah 63:13
He led them through the abyss like a horse through a wilderness, and they did not grow weary.
