  Psalms 105:9 — close guidance through the deep compared with a wilderness: Isaiah describes leading them through the abyss like a horse through a wilderness; the psalm says God guided them through the deep as in a wilderness. Isaiah adds that they did not grow weary.

Isaiah 63:14
Like livestock through a plain, a spirit from the Lord came down and guided them. So Thou didst lead Thy people to make Thyself a glorious name.
  Psalms 142:10 — close the Lord's Spirit guiding on level terrain: Isaiah compares the Spirit's guidance to livestock passing through a plain; the psalm declares that God's good Spirit will lead the speaker on level ground. The collective remembrance and personal declaration share the guiding Spirit and terrain image.

Isaiah 63:15
Turn from heaven and see from Thy holy and glorious house. Where are Thy zeal and strength? Where is the abundance of Thy mercy and compassion, since Thou hast held back from us?
  Deuteronomy 26:15 — close appeal for God to look from His holy heavenly house: Isaiah asks God to turn and see from His holy, glorious house in heaven; Deuteronomy asks Him to look from His holy house in heaven and bless Israel. Isaiah's appeal particularly questions withheld compassion.

Isaiah 63:16
For Thou art our Father, because Abraham did not know us and Israel did not recognize us. But Thou, Lord, art our Father. Rescue us! From the beginning Thy name has been upon us.
  Deuteronomy 32:6 — parallel confession of the Lord as Israel's Father: Isaiah twice calls the Lord our Father despite unrecognition by Abraham and Israel; Moses' song calls Him the people's Father who acquired, made and created them. Only Isaiah contrasts Him with named human ancestors.

Isaiah 63:17
Why, Lord, didst Thou make us stray from Thy way and harden our hearts so that we do not fear Thee? Return because of Thy servants, because of the tribes of Thine inheritance,
  Romans 9:18 — related attribution of hardening to God: Isaiah's communal question attributes straying and hardened hearts to God; Romans states that He hardens whom He wills. The lament's request that He return and Romans' argument about mercy retain their distinct settings.

Isaiah 63:18
so that we may inherit a little of Thy holy mountain. Our adversaries trampled Thy sanctuary.
  Psalms 78:1 — close lament over adversaries violating God's sanctuary: Isaiah says adversaries trampled God's sanctuary; the psalm says nations entered His inheritance and defiled His holy temple. Both lament violated sacred space without naming identical attackers here.

Isaiah 63:19
We became as at the beginning, when Thou didst not rule us and Thy name had not been called upon us. If Thou openest heaven, trembling will seize mountains because of Thee, and they will melt,
  Micah 1:3-4 — parallel divine manifestation shaking mountains: Isaiah imagines heaven opened and mountains trembling and melting before God; Micah describes the Lord descending, mountains shaken and valleys melting. The particular terrain that melts differs.

Isaiah 64:1
like wax melting before fire. Fire will burn up the adversaries, and the Lord’s name will become manifest among the adversaries. Nations will be shaken before Thee.
  Psalms 67:3 — close wax melting before fire as an image of judgment: Isaiah compares melting to wax before fire and says adversaries will burn; the psalm asks that sinners perish before God as wax melts before fire. The psalm is a petition, while Isaiah describes the expected manifestation.

Isaiah 64:2
Whenever Thou dost glorious things, trembling will seize mountains because of Thee.
  Habakkuk 3:6 — parallel mountains responding to God's manifestation: Isaiah says glorious divine acts bring trembling upon mountains; Habakkuk describes God standing and looking, followed by the earth shaking and mountains being violently crushed. Their precise descriptions of the mountains' response differ.

Isaiah 64:3
From of old we have not heard, nor have our eyes seen a god except Thee and Thy works, which Thou wilt do for those awaiting mercy.
  1 Corinthians 2:9 — related unseen and unheard divine works in Paul's scriptural citation: Isaiah speaks of not hearing or seeing a god except the Lord and His works for those awaiting mercy; Paul's citation speaks of unseen and unheard things prepared for those loving God. The eye-and-ear pattern corresponds, but the objects and beneficiaries are not worded identically.

Isaiah 64:5
We all became like unclean people; all our righteousness is like a menstruating woman’s rag. We fell away like leaves because of our lawless deeds; thus wind will carry us off.
  Zechariah 3:3-4 — related polluted clothing imagery for lawlessness: Isaiah calls the people's righteousness a menstruating woman's rag; Zechariah portrays Joshua in filthy garments whose removal is explained as removal of lawless deeds. The corporate comparison and the priest's symbolic clothing remain distinct.

Isaiah 64:6
There is no one calling on Thy name or remembering to take hold of Thee, because Thou didst turn Thy face away from us and didst hand us over because of our sins.
  Psalms 13:2-3 — parallel absence of people seeking God: Isaiah laments no one calling God's name or remembering to hold to Him; the psalm describes God looking for anyone seeking Him, followed by all turning aside. Isaiah additionally describes God's turned face and His handing the people over because of sins.

Isaiah 64:7
But now, Lord, Thou art our Father, and we are clay, all the work of Thy hands.
  Jeremiah 18:6 — close people as clay in God's hands: Isaiah calls the people clay and the work of God's hands; Jeremiah explicitly likens Israel to a potter's clay in His hands. The potter is named in Jeremiah, while Isaiah also names God as Father.

Isaiah 64:8
Do not be exceedingly angry with us or remember our sins at the appointed time. And now look, for we are all Thy people.
  Psalms 78:8 — close request that God not remember the people's sins: Isaiah asks God not to remember sins at the appointed time; the psalm asks Him not to remember ancient lawless acts and to bring compassion quickly. Their temporal expressions differ.

Isaiah 64:9
The city of Thy holy place became a desert; Zion became like a desert, Jerusalem a curse.
  Lamentations 1:1-4 — parallel lament over deserted Zion and Jerusalem: Isaiah describes the holy city and Zion as desert and Jerusalem as a curse; Lamentations describes Jerusalem left alone and Zion's roads empty of festival visitors. The deserted city is shared, while the details of its desolation differ.

Isaiah 64:10
The house, our holy place, and the glory our ancestors blessed became fire-burned, and all the glorious things collapsed.
  2 Kings 25:9-10 — historical account of the burning of the Lord's house: Isaiah laments the holy house becoming fire-burned; Kings narrates the chief cook setting fire to the Lord's house and Jerusalem's houses. The historical narrative supplies a concrete temple-burning counterpart to the lament.

Isaiah 64:11
Despite all these things Thou didst hold back, Lord, and keep silent and humble us greatly.
  Psalms 73:10-11 — related complaint that God withholds action after sanctuary destruction: Isaiah says God held back and remained silent despite the preceding devastation; the psalm, after lamenting the burnt sanctuary, asks why He turns His hand away. The complaints use silence and a withdrawn hand respectively.

Isaiah 65:1
“I became manifest to those not seeking Me; I was found by those not inquiring of Me. I said, ‘Here I am,’ to the nation that did not call My name.
  Romans 10:20 — explicit Isaiah citation of God found by those not seeking Him: Romans names Isaiah and repeats God's being found and made known among people who did not seek or ask for Him. Isaiah's additional announcement to a nation not calling His name is not included in that verse's quotation.

Isaiah 65:2
I spread out My hands all day to a disobedient, contradicting people who did not walk in a true way but followed their sins.
  Romans 10:21 — explicit citation of extended hands toward a disobedient people: Romans quotes the all-day stretching out of God's hands toward a disobedient, contrary people and identifies the subject as Israel. Isaiah further describes their untrue path and following sins.

Isaiah 65:3
This people continually provokes Me to My face. They sacrifice in gardens and burn incense on bricks to demons that do not exist,
  Deuteronomy 32:17 — parallel condemnation of sacrifices to demons rather than God: Isaiah condemns sacrifices and incense to demons that do not exist; Deuteronomy says the people sacrificed to demons and not God. Deuteronomy does not repeat Isaiah's explicit statement that these demons do not exist.

Isaiah 65:4
and sleep in tombs and caves for dreams. They eat pig’s flesh and sacrificial broth; all their vessels are defiled.
  Leviticus 11:7-8 — legal counterpart to the accusation of eating pig's flesh: Isaiah includes pig's flesh among defiled practices; Leviticus calls the pig unclean and forbids eating its flesh. Isaiah's tombs, caves and sacrificial broth are additional accusations.

Isaiah 65:5
They say, ‘Far away from me! Do not approach me, for I am clean.’ This is smoke of My wrath; fire burns in it all the days.
  Luke 18:9-14 — related rejection of self-exalting religious separation: Isaiah's speakers tell others to keep away because they are clean; Luke's parable targets people trusting their own righteousness and despising others, illustrated by the Pharisee's contrast with other people. The claimed cleanliness and claimed righteousness are distinct forms of self-exaltation.

Isaiah 65:6
Behold, it is written before Me: I will not keep silent until I repay into their lap
  Psalms 78:12 — close recompense into the offender's lap or bosom: Isaiah announces God will repay into their lap; the psalm petitions Him to repay neighbors' insults into their bosom. The repayment image corresponds, while the psalm additionally specifies sevenfold recompense.

Isaiah 65:7
their sins and their ancestors’ sins,” says the Lord, “who burned incense on mountains and insulted Me on hills. I will repay their works into their lap.”
  Exodus 20:5 — related reckoning with ancestral sins and later generations: Isaiah names the people's sins together with their ancestors' sins; Exodus describes repayment of fathers' sins upon children of those hating God. Isaiah explicitly includes the present people's own deeds rather than attributing the whole charge only to ancestors.

Isaiah 65:8
Thus says the Lord: “As a grape is found in the cluster and people say, ‘Do not ruin it, for the Lord’s blessing is in it,’ so I will act for the sake of the one serving Me; for this one’s sake I will not destroy everyone.
  Genesis 18:26 — parallel sparing a larger group for the righteous within it: Isaiah's preserved grape illustrates not destroying everyone for the sake of the Lord's servant; God tells Abraham He would spare Sodom if fifty righteous people were found within it. The conditional promise is the parallel, not a claim that Sodom was ultimately spared.

Isaiah 65:9
I will bring out the offspring from Jacob and from Judah, and it will inherit My holy mountain. My chosen ones and My slaves will inherit and dwell there.
  Psalms 68:36-37 — close descendants of God's servants inheriting restored Zion: Isaiah promises Jacob's and Judah's offspring inheritance of the holy mountain; the psalm promises salvation for Zion and rebuilt Judean cities possessed by His servants' offspring. Both join restoration, descendants and inherited dwelling.

Isaiah 65:10
There will be shelters for flocks in the woodland, and the ravine of Achor will be a resting place for cattle, for My people who sought Me.
  Joshua 7:24-26 — contrast between Achor's judgment memory and promised pastoral rest: Joshua recounts Achar's judgment in Achor and the place's enduring name; Isaiah promises that ravine as a resting place for cattle belonging to the people seeking God. The same named place moves from a judgment narrative to a promised use for the restored people.

Isaiah 65:11
But you who abandoned Me and forget My holy mountain, preparing a table for the demon and filling a mixed drink for Fortune,
  1 Corinthians 10:20-21 — close rejection of a table and drink associated with demons: Isaiah condemns a table for the demon and mixed drink for Fortune; Paul excludes participation in demons' table and cup alongside the Lord's. Fortune is separately named only in Isaiah.

Isaiah 65:12
I will hand you over to the sword; all of you will fall in slaughter, because I called you and you did not obey, I spoke and you disregarded Me. You did evil before Me and chose what I did not want.”
  Proverbs 1:24-28 — parallel rejected calling followed by calamity: Isaiah's Lord says He called without obedience and will hand the people to the sword; personified Wisdom says she called without obedience and describes coming destruction and an unanswered return call. The speakers and particular judgments differ.

Isaiah 65:13
Therefore thus says the Lord: “Behold, those serving Me will eat, but you will hunger. Those serving Me will drink, but you will thirst. Those serving Me will rejoice, but you will be ashamed.
  Luke 6:20-25 — related opposed outcomes of hunger and satisfaction: Isaiah contrasts God's servants eating and drinking with rebels' hunger and thirst; Luke blesses the presently hungry with future filling and warns the presently filled of hunger. Luke's present and future reversal is not identical to Isaiah's division of the two groups.

Isaiah 65:14
Behold, those serving Me will exult with joy, but you will cry out because of your heart’s pain and wail from a broken spirit.
  Luke 6:21-25 — related contrasting outcomes of joy and mourning: Isaiah contrasts servants' joy with others' heartache and wailing; Luke contrasts future laughter for those weeping now with mourning for those laughing now. The temporal reversal in Luke differs from Isaiah's stated separation of servants and opponents.

Isaiah 65:15
For you will leave your name as a surfeit for My chosen ones, but the Lord will put you to death. Those serving Him will be called a new name,
  Isaiah 62:2 — internal promise that God gives a new name: Isaiah 65 promises a new name for God's servants; Isaiah 62 promises the city a new name from the Lord. This connection concerns the new-name clause, not an alteration of Isaiah 65's native word surfeit.

Isaiah 65:16
which will be blessed upon earth. For they will bless the true God, and those swearing upon earth will swear by the true God. They will forget their former affliction, and it will not rise into their heart.
  Jeremiah 4:2 — related truthful swearing and blessing in relation to the Lord: Isaiah joins blessing the true God with swearing by Him; Jeremiah joins swearing that the Lord lives in truth and righteousness with nations' blessing and praise of God in Jerusalem. Jeremiah's pronouns and phrasing of the blessing are not identical.

Isaiah 65:17
For heaven will be new and earth new, and they will never remember former things, nor will those things enter their heart,
  2 Peter 3:13 — close expectation of new heavens and a new earth: Isaiah promises new heaven and earth; Second Peter says believers await new heavens and earth according to God's promise and specifies righteousness dwelling there. Isaiah adds forgetting former things.

Isaiah 65:18
but they will find joy and exultation in it. For behold, I am making Jerusalem an exultation and My people joy.
  Zephaniah 3:14-17 — parallel Jerusalem and God's people restored to joy: Isaiah says God makes Jerusalem exultation and His people joy; Zephaniah summons daughter Jerusalem to rejoice and describes God's saving presence and delight over her. The divine and communal rejoicing are intertwined in both settings.

Isaiah 65:19
I will exult over Jerusalem and rejoice over My people, and no sound of weeping or crying will ever again be heard in her.
  Isaiah 25:8 — internal removal of weeping in God's saving work: Isaiah 65 promises that weeping and crying will no longer be heard in Jerusalem; Isaiah 25 says God removed every tear from every face. The local city promise and the broader expression retain their different scopes.

Isaiah 65:21
They will build houses and inhabit them themselves; they will plant vineyards and eat their own produce.
  Deuteronomy 28:30 — reversal of the curse of building and planting without enjoyment: Isaiah promises building houses for one's own habitation and planting vineyards for one's own food; Deuteronomy threatens building without dwelling and planting without harvesting. The corresponding acts receive opposite outcomes.

Isaiah 65:22
They will not build for others to inhabit or plant for others to eat. For My people’s days will be like those of the tree of life; they will wear out the works of their labor.
  Amos 9:14-15 — parallel enduring enjoyment of restored homes and crops: Isaiah promises that others will not inhabit the builders' houses or eat the planters' produce; Amos promises restored cities inhabited by the people and vineyards and gardens whose produce they enjoy, without renewed uprooting. Isaiah's tree-of-life comparison is additional.

Isaiah 65:23
My chosen ones will not labor in vain or bear children for a curse, because they are offspring blessed by God, and their descendants will be with them.
  Deuteronomy 28:41 — contrast between blessed descendants remaining and children lost to captivity: Isaiah promises children not born for a curse and descendants remaining with God's blessed offspring; Deuteronomy's curse describes children who will not belong to their parents because of captivity. The comparison concerns family continuity rather than an identical description of childbirth.

Isaiah 65:24
Before they cry out I will listen to them; while they are still speaking I will say, ‘What is it?’
  Isaiah 58:9 — close internal answer while the people are still speaking: Both promise God's answer while the people are still speaking. Isaiah 65 also says He listens before their cry; Isaiah 58 expresses His answer as His presence.

Isaiah 65:25
Then wolves and lambs will graze together, and a lion will eat straw like an ox, while a serpent will eat earth like bread. They will not wrong or destroy on My holy mountain,” says the Lord.
  Isaiah 11:6-9 — close internal peaceful animals on God's holy mountain: Both describe wolves with lambs, lions eating straw with oxen and no destructive harm on God's holy mountain. Isaiah 65 adds the serpent eating earth like bread, which the earlier animal scene does not say.
  Genesis 3:14 — close serpent eating earth: Isaiah's peaceful-animal scene retains the serpent eating earth like bread; Genesis' sentence upon the serpent includes eating earth throughout its life. The shared food does not erase the different settings of curse and future peace.

Isaiah 66:1
Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?
  Acts 7:49 — explicit prophetic citation of heaven as throne and earth as footstool: Acts introduces the prophet's saying and repeats heaven as God's throne, earth as His footstool and the questions about a house and place of rest. The surrounding speech contrasts this with a house made by human hands.
  Matthew 5:34-35 — close throne and footstool language applied to oaths: Matthew's instruction against swearing by heaven or earth names them God's throne and His feet's footstool. Isaiah uses those same images in a question about building God's house, not directly about oaths.

Isaiah 66:2
For My hand made all these things, and all these things are Mine,” says the Lord. “And to whom will I look except the humble and quiet one who trembles at My words?
  Acts 7:50 — close continuation of the citation concerning God's creating hand: Acts continues the prophetic citation by asking whether God's hand made all these things; Isaiah asserts that His hand made them and that they are His. Acts does not repeat this unit's following description of the humble hearer.
  Psalms 50:19 — parallel divine regard for a humbled person: Isaiah says God looks to the humble, quiet person who trembles at His words; the psalm says God will not despise a broken and humbled heart. The psalm places this within its contrast with burnt offerings.

Isaiah 66:3
But the lawless person who sacrifices a calf to Me is like someone killing a dog; the one offering fine flour is like one offering pig’s blood; the one giving frankincense as a memorial is like a blasphemer. These people chose their ways and their abominations, which their soul desired,
  Proverbs 15:8 — parallel rejection of sacrifice offered by the impious: Isaiah likens the lawless person's sacrifices to abhorrent acts; Proverbs calls impious people's sacrifices an abomination to the Lord. Isaiah's first comparison is killing a dog, not a substituted killing of a person.

Isaiah 66:4
and I will choose their mockeries and repay their sins to them, because I called them and they did not obey Me, I spoke and they did not hear. They did evil before Me and chose what I did not want.”
  Isaiah 65:12 — close internal repeated charge of ignoring God's call: Both say God called without obedience, spoke without being heard, and was confronted with chosen evil that He did not want. Their announced punishments differ in expression.

Isaiah 66:5
Hear the Lord’s word, you who tremble at His word! Speak, our brothers, to those who hate us and loathe us, so that the Lord’s name may be glorified and seen in their rejoicing; and those people will be ashamed.
  Luke 6:22 — related hatred directed toward people attending to God: Isaiah addresses those trembling at the Lord's word and names those hating and loathing them; Luke blesses disciples hated and excluded because of the Son of Man. The parallel does not resolve Isaiah's further pronouns concerning rejoicing and shame.

Isaiah 66:6
A sound of shouting from the city, a sound from the temple, the sound of the Lord repaying recompense to His opponents!
  Amos 1:2 — parallel the Lord's voice sounding from Jerusalem: Isaiah announces the Lord's recompense in sounds from the city and temple; Amos describes the Lord speaking from Zion and giving His voice from Jerusalem, with the countryside mourning. The venue and divine voice correspond while their effects differ.

Isaiah 66:7
Before the woman in labor gave birth, before the pain of labor came, she escaped and bore a male.
  Isaiah 54:1 — related unexpected children without ordinary labor: Isaiah 66 describes a woman bearing a male before labor's pain; Isaiah 54 promises many children to the deserted woman who does not labor in childbirth. Both use unexpected childbirth in the restoration imagery, without identical sequences or numbers.

Isaiah 66:8
Who heard such a thing, and who has seen anything like it? Did a land labor in one day, or was a nation born all at once? For Zion labored and bore her children.
  Isaiah 49:20-21 — Within-book literary parallel: Zion astonished at newly given children: Isaiah 66 asks how a nation could be born all at once and declares that Zion labored and bore her children. In Isaiah 49, the formerly childless and widowed Zion asks who bore and raised the unexpectedly numerous children now needing room. Both depict restored Zion as a mother astonished at her children. Chapter 49 does not repeat the one-day timing or establish how the sudden birth occurs.

Isaiah 66:9
‘But I gave this expectation, and you did not remember Me,’ said the Lord. ‘Behold, did I not make the woman who gives birth and the barren woman?’ said God.
  Exodus 4:11 — parallel Creator's claim over contrasting human bodily conditions: Isaiah's God asks whether He made both the childbearing woman and the barren woman; Exodus' Lord asks who made people hard of hearing, deaf, seeing or blind. Each rhetorical question attributes people in differing bodily conditions to the Creator, without equating those conditions.

Isaiah 66:10
Rejoice, Jerusalem, and celebrate in her, all who love her! Rejoice with joy, all who mourn over her,
  Psalms 125:1-3 — parallel rejoicing over Zion's restoration: Isaiah calls Jerusalem's lovers and mourners to joy; the psalm recalls Zion's captivity turned back, comfort and mouths filled with joy. The summons and the remembered restoration have different forms.

Isaiah 66:11
so that you may nurse and be filled from her comforting breast, so that, nursing fully, you may delight in the entrance of her glory.
  Isaiah 60:16 — internal milk imagery for the city's abundance: Isaiah 66 depicts people nursing from Jerusalem's comforting breast; Isaiah 60 depicts Jerusalem drinking nations' milk and eating kings' wealth. The city receives abundance in the earlier image and nourishes her people in the later one.

Isaiah 66:12
For thus says the Lord: ‘Behold, I turn toward them like a river of peace, and like a flooding torrent the glory of nations. Their children will be lifted on shoulders and comforted on knees.
  Isaiah 48:18 — close internal river imagery associated with peace: Isaiah 66 says God turns toward them like a river of peace; Isaiah 48 says their peace would have become like a river had they listened. The later declaration differs from the earlier unrealized conditional statement.
  Isaiah 49:22 — close children carried on shoulders in the gathering: Isaiah 66 describes children lifted upon shoulders and comforted on knees; Isaiah 49 describes nations carrying the city's daughters on shoulders. Isaiah 49 distinguishes sons carried in the bosom, while Isaiah 66 uses children collectively.

Isaiah 66:13
As if a mother were to comfort someone, so I too will comfort you, and in Jerusalem you will be comforted.’
  Psalms 102:13 — complementary parental images for divine care: Isaiah likens God's comfort to a mother's comfort; the psalm likens His compassion to a father's compassion upon children. The parent and the terms comfort and compassion differ, while both comparisons describe God's care.

Isaiah 66:14
You will see, and your heart will rejoice; your bones will spring up like vegetation. The Lord’s hand will be known to those who revere Him, and He will threaten the disobedient.
  Proverbs 17:22 — related rejoicing heart and renewed bodily vigor: Isaiah joins a rejoicing heart with bones springing up like vegetation; Proverbs says a rejoicing heart makes one flourish and contrasts a sorrowful person's dried bones. The links between inner joy, flourishing and bones are expressed through different contrasts.

Isaiah 66:15
For behold, the Lord will come like fire, and His chariots like a storm, to render vengeance in wrath and rejection in a flame of fire.
  2 Thessalonians 1:7-8 — parallel coming in flaming judgment and vengeance: Isaiah describes the Lord coming like fire to render vengeance; Second Thessalonians describes the Lord Jesus revealed from heaven in flaming fire, bringing vengeance. The letter names the Lord Jesus and His powerful angels, while Isaiah names storm-like chariots.

Isaiah 66:16
For by the Lord’s fire all the earth will be judged, and by His sword all flesh; many will be wounded by the Lord.
  Ezekiel 21:8-10 — parallel divine blade turned against all flesh: Isaiah describes all flesh judged by the Lord's sword; Ezekiel describes the Lord drawing His dagger against all flesh and being recognized as its wielder. Ezekiel's stated direction from east to north is not identical to Isaiah's whole-earth scope.

Isaiah 66:17
‘Those purifying and cleansing themselves for the gardens, eating pig’s flesh and abominations and the mouse in the porches, will be consumed together,’ said the Lord,
  Leviticus 11:29-31 — legal counterpart identifying the mouse among unclean creatures: Isaiah condemns eating the mouse among abhorrent practices; Leviticus names the mouse among unclean creeping things and gives a rule about touching their dead bodies. The shared classification does not make the legal touching rule identical to Isaiah's eating accusation.

Isaiah 66:18
‘and I know their works and their reasoning. I am coming to gather all nations and languages, and they will come and see My glory.
  Zechariah 8:22-23 — close gathering of nations and languages around God's presence: Isaiah promises to gather all nations and languages to see His glory; Zechariah describes many nations seeking the Lord in Jerusalem and people from all nations' languages accompanying a Jew because God is with them. Their descriptions of approach differ.

Isaiah 66:19
I will leave signs upon them and send those saved from them to the nations: to Tharsis, Phoud, Loud, Mosoch, Thobel and Greece, and to the distant islands that have not heard My name or seen My glory. They will announce My glory among the nations.
  Romans 15:20-21 — related mission among those not previously hearing the divine message: Isaiah describes God sending saved people to nations and islands that have not heard His name or seen His glory; Paul describes proclaiming Christ where He was not named and quotes a promise to those who had not heard. The letter's quoted wording is not presented as a verbatim citation of this Isaiah unit.

Isaiah 66:20
They will bring your brothers from all nations as a gift to the Lord, with horses and chariots, in mule carriages with sunshades, to the holy city Jerusalem,’ said the Lord, ‘as the Israelites might bring Me their sacrifices with psalms into the Lord’s house.
  Romans 15:16 — related people presented as an offering to God: Isaiah describes brothers from all nations brought as a gift to the Lord; Paul describes priestly gospel service so the offering of the Gentiles becomes acceptable. Isaiah's transport to Jerusalem and sacrifices with psalms differ from Paul's account of sanctification in the Spirit.

Isaiah 66:21
From them I will take priests and Levites for Myself,’ said the Lord.
  Isaiah 61:6 — internal appointment of people to priestly service: Isaiah 66 says God will take priests and Levites from them; Isaiah 61 says its addressees will be called priests and ministers of God. The comparison does not settle the precise antecedent of from them in Isaiah 66.

Isaiah 66:22
‘For just as the new heaven and new earth that I make remain before Me,’ says the Lord, ‘so your offspring and your name will stand.
  Isaiah 65:17 — internal new-creation promise used to assure enduring descendants: Isaiah 65 announces new heaven and earth; Isaiah 66 invokes their remaining before God as the comparison for the people's enduring offspring and name. The latter explicitly draws assurance from the creation promise.

Isaiah 66:23
Month after month and Sabbath after Sabbath, all flesh will come before Me to bow down in Jerusalem,’ said the Lord.
  Zechariah 14:16 — parallel nations coming to Jerusalem to worship the Lord: Isaiah promises all flesh worshiping in Jerusalem month after month and Sabbath after Sabbath; Zechariah describes remaining nations coming annually to worship the King, the Lord Almighty. The schedules and descriptions of the worshipers differ.

Isaiah 66:24
‘They will go out and see the limbs of the people who transgressed against Me, for their worm will not die and their fire will not be quenched. They will be a spectacle to all flesh.’
  Mark 9:48 — close citation of the undying worm and unquenched fire: Mark's warning about Gehenna repeats the worm not dying and fire not being extinguished. Isaiah describes the transgressors' limbs as a spectacle outside, a detail not repeated in Mark's quoted line.

Closed with no supportable cross-reference
Isaiah 3:7: The unit is the refusal to be leader because there is neither bread nor cloak in the house. The leads concern healing and wounds, God's oath, a rejection of a ruler in a different setting, and unrelated mentions of bread or leaders; none shares a refusal of leadership grounded in lacking bread and a cloak.
Isaiah 3:21: The unit lists garments bordered with purple and purple at the center among removed finery. The leads concern rings, earrings, robes and collars, or the purple garment placed on Jesus or Jonathan; none mentions purple-bordered or purple-centered garments or a catalogue of finery removed in judgment.
Isaiah 8:5: The unit is only the formula that the Lord spoke to me yet again. Its sole lead, Isaiah 7:10, shares just the renewed-speech formula with a different addressee (Achaz), which is a generic introduction rather than a substantive connection.
Isaiah 8:11: In this edition the unit says they resist the course of this people's way with a strong hand. The leads describe the Lord's hand on a prophet, warnings not to walk in a way, divine guidance, Jeremiah's compulsion, or building courses; none shares a plural group resisting the people's course with a strong hand.
Isaiah 9:10: The unit says God will dash those rising against Mount Zion upon them and scatter their enemies. The leads narrate Assyrian conquests of Damascus and Israel or Aram's decline, and generic petitions about enemies rising against the speaker; none mentions those rising against Mount Zion or matches this action.
Isaiah 10:8: The unit is the conditional clause, If they say to him, You alone are ruler. The leads give Assyrian taunts about governors, horses and Jerusalem, or titles of king of kings for Nebuchadnezzar; none contains the claim that he alone is ruler.
Isaiah 10:29: Re-reviewed with fresh leads; the proposed link did not survive a second reading: In the shown Zechariah 14:10, 'Rama will remain in its place, from Benjamin's Gate to the place of the First Gate... to the king's wine vats' gives the extent of Jerusalem itself, marked by its gates. It is not the town of Rama north of Jerusalem, so the rationale's identification is unsupported. The shown Isaiah 10:29 also has no Gabe.
Isaiah 10:31: The unit names Madebena and Gibbir, whose residents were distraught, with a call to offer encouragement. Neither place appears in the leads, which give a different town list (Ziklag, Macharim) or unrelated uses of 'distraught' for Hamath and Arpad, Eli, David's house and envoys.
Isaiah 15:7: The unit asks whether Moab will be saved and announces that Arabs will be brought upon the ravine to take her. No text shown names Arabs coming against Moab or a ravine taken; the Moab laments speak of lost acquisitions and other passages concern lions, seizing, spoils or Arabs bringing gifts to Jehoshaphat, so only general words of seizing and saving are shared.
Isaiah 16:1: The unit speaks of being sent like creeping creatures upon the earth and asks whether Mount Zion is a deserted rock. The texts shown concern Moab's lamb tribute, Edom's Rock, sacrificial lambs, creeping things in creation and law, or Zion's deserted places being comforted; none shares the creeping-creature sending or the question about Mount Zion as a deserted rock.
Isaiah 16:4: The unit says Moab's fugitives will sojourn with the addressee and be a shelter from the pursuer, because the alliance was removed and the ruler trampling the earth perished. The texts shown speak of a taskmaster resting, an oppressor's anger, trampling the lawless or Moab trampled, and sheltering under God's wings; none presents Moab's fugitives as shelter or the perishing of a ruler trampling the earth in shared wording.
Isaiah 17:11: The unit pairs wandering on the day of planting with morning sowing that blossoms for harvest on an allotted day, likened to a human father allotting to sons. The texts shown share only common agricultural words (sow, blossom, harvest) used for reaping trouble, fading grass or a fresh shoot; none has the allotment or the father-to-sons comparison.
Isaiah 19:10: The unit describes grief among those warping cloth and all who make beer in Egypt. The texts shown concern Egypt's rivers, canals and marshes during the plagues, watering Egypt's land by foot, or general pain and suffering; none mentions these trades or their grief.
Isaiah 20:1: The unit dates an Assyrian capture of Azotus by Tanathan, sent by Arna king of the Assyrians. The Azotus texts shown concern other events (Uzziah breaking its walls, prophetic threats, the ark at Ashdod), and the Assyrian envoy Tharthan in 2 Kings is sent against Jerusalem, not Azotus, under a king not named Arna; no text gives this campaign or these persons.
Isaiah 21:13: The unit pictures sleeping in the wood at evening on Daidan's road. Texts naming Daidan or Dedan give genealogies, merchants or a list of peoples to be judged, without this road or lodging, and the passage about sleeping in the woods describes secure dwelling under a covenant of peace rather than travellers forced to lodge on Daidan's road.
Isaiah 22:1: The unit opens the utterance of Zion's ravine by asking why all went up to the roofs. The texts shown concern other valleys (Sor, Jehoshaphat, Judgment), mountains around Jerusalem, or Moabite wailing on roofs, and the remaining ones share only the word 'utterance'; none addresses this ascent to the roofs in Zion's ravine.
Isaiah 22:2: The unit describes futile roofs, a city full of crying, and wounded and dead not slain by sword or in war. The texts shown describe cities full of the sword-slain, famine, or feasting before death, or use crying and wounds in unrelated settings; none states that the city's dead did not die by sword or in war.
Isaiah 22:8: The unit says Judah's gates will be uncovered and the city's choice houses looked into on that day. The texts shown concern Solomon's House of the Forest and its shields, armour on David's tower, Rehoboam's shields, the Assyrian envoy at Jerusalem, or gates and houses in unrelated laws and promises; none corresponds to this uncovering of gates and inspection of choice houses.
Isaiah 23:4: The unit has the sea and the sea's strength tell Sidon to be ashamed, saying it did not labor, give birth or rear young men and maidens. The texts shown name Sidon in genealogies and borders or in general judgments on Tyre and Sidon, or use birth and young-men-and-maidens language in unrelated contexts; none shares the sea's denial of bearing and rearing children.
Isaiah 23:16: The unit tells a forgotten prostitute to take a lyre, wander through cities and sing so she may be remembered. The texts shown speak of forgotten friends, a prostitute roaming streets in Proverbs, or lyres in worship and music generally; none combines the forgotten prostitute with lyre-playing for remembrance.
Isaiah 27:4: The unit, with shifting speakers, speaks of seizure, guarding stubble in a field, rejection because of hostility, the Lord doing what He commanded, and a cry of being burned. The texts shown share only general images of fire consuming grass, chaff or thorns, cessation of wrath, or the verb 'seize' in narrative; none matches the stubble-guarding question or this sequence.
Isaiah 27:8: The unit says he will send them away fighting and reproaching, and asks whether God planned with a harsh spirit to destroy them with a spirit of wrath. The texts shown speak of a scorching wind, discipline in judgment, or wrath turned away; none shares the sending away with reproach or the harsh spirit of destruction in common wording.
Isaiah 28:20: The unit says, 'Cramped, we cannot fight, and we ourselves are too weak to be gathered.' The texts shown concern wind carrying people off, useless webs, false trust, or ordinary statements about being unable to fight; none shares the cramped condition or the weakness to be gathered.
Isaiah 28:27: The unit distinguishes processing black cumin with a rod from cumin, neither being crushed harshly or by a wagon wheel. The texts shown use threshing wheels as images of judgment, mention a wagon rolling or tithing cumin, or speak of discipline in judgment; none sets out this differentiated handling of the two herbs.
Isaiah 29:2: The unit has the Lord pressing Ariel hard and claiming her strength and wealth as His. The texts shown describe the Lord destroying strongholds, a mother lacking strength to give birth, Zion's hills, mourning adornment or a sacrifice in Bosor, and other passages use 'press hard' of unrelated persons; none names Ariel or this claim on her strength and wealth.
Isaiah 33:4: The unit compares gathering spoils small and great to gathering locusts, followed by mockery. The texts shown describe plundering spoils in battle, locusts as a devouring force, or recovering spoil 'from small to great'; none uses the locust-gathering comparison for spoils or the accompanying mockery.
Isaiah 33:8: The unit joins deserted roads, the ceasing of fear of the nations, a removed covenant and people not reckoned as human beings. The texts shown describe travellers using twisted roads, Zion's mourning roads, Assyrian boasting or deserted cities refilled; each shares at most a single general element and none the combined statement.
Isaiah 41:3: The unit says an unnamed figure will pursue them and the way of his feet will pass through in peace. The texts shown concern burial in peace, a peaceful house, or the exhortation to seek and pursue peace; none describes this pursuit and peaceful passage, and the figure is not identified in any of them.
Isaiah 41:21: The unit says, from the Lord God and Jacob's King, that 'your judgment draws near' and 'your plans have drawn near'. The texts shown are Job's challenges to answer, Job stating his case, Micah's lawsuit, or uses of 'draw near' about Jacob and Esau; none shares the drawing near of judgment and plans under the title Jacob's King.
Isaiah 42:23: The unit asks who among them will give ear and listen for what is coming. The texts shown are general calls to listen or laments that people did not listen; none shares this question about attending to coming things beyond the common vocabulary of hearing.
Isaiah 44:11: The unit joins the drying up of those from whom they came with the deaf among humankind assembling and standing abashed and ashamed together. The texts shown share only the common formula of being ashamed and abashed, applied to idolaters or enemies; none contains the dried-up origins or the deaf assembling.
Isaiah 45:10: The unit quotes questions put to a father ('What will you father?') and a mother ('What will you bring forth in labor?'). The texts shown concern honouring father and mother, divine fatherhood, a mother's labor pains, or parents bringing matters before elders; none contains these questions about what parents will produce.
Isaiah 45:11: The unit invites the hearers to ask God about His sons and daughters and to command Him concerning the works of His hands. The texts shown use common phrases (Israel's Holy One, the work of Thy hands, children seeing God's works) or general calls to prayer; none shares the invitation to ask and command about God's sons, daughters and works.
Isaiah 47:15: The unit tells Babylon her helpers, with whom she toiled in trade from youth, have each wandered on his own and there is no salvation for her. The texts shown describe fleeing Babylon, self-seeking leaders, Egypt's toil and trade, or toil from youth in a psalm; none shares the failed trading helpers wandering away, beyond single common words.
Isaiah 51:14: The unit says only that as you are saved, it will not stand or linger, with no stated referent. The texts shown speak of captives freed, rescue from Babylon, a waterless pit, or something that will not linger, but none shares a definite referent or this statement, so no specific connection can be fixed.
Isaiah 64:4: The unit joins God's meeting those doing what is just, remembering His ways, God's anger, sin and going astray. The texts shown speak of provoking God's spirit, hoping in His name, God accepting those practising righteousness, or confessions of sin; none shares this combination of meeting the just with anger and straying.
Isaiah 65:20: The unit says no one will be cut off early, the young person will be a hundred years old, and the sinner who dies at a hundred will be accursed. The texts shown speak of a timely death, prolonged days for the obedient, or the ungodly not prolonging days, without the hundred-year life of the young or the accursed sinner at a hundred.
